**6.1.1**  Janamejaya said: How did they wage war, those heroes — the Kurus, the Pāṇḍavas, and the Somakas — and the high-fortuned kings gathered from many lands?

**6.1.2**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: How those heroes — the Kurus, the Pāṇḍavas, and the Somakas — waged war upon Kurukṣetra, that field of austerities, hear it now, O lord of the earth.

**6.1.3**  Descending upon Kurukṣetra, the Pāṇḍavas together with the Somakas, mighty in strength and eager for victory, advanced against the Kauravas.

**6.1.4**  All of them accomplished in Vedic study, all rejoicing in battle, they hoped for victory in the war, or else for death facing the foe in combat.

**6.1.5**  Having advanced against the unassailable army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, they encamped with their troops on the western side, their faces turned eastward.

**6.1.6**  Outside Samantapañcaka, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Kuntī had encampments by the thousands raised according to due rule.

**6.1.7**  The whole earth seemed emptied, with only the young and the aged left behind; it was bereft of horses and of men, stripped of chariots and elephants.

**6.1.8**  As far as the sun shines upon the circle of Jambūdvīpa, over all that expanse was the host assembled, O best of kings.

**6.1.9**  Gathered in one place, men of every class, they overspread a circle many leagues across, covering regions, rivers, mountains, and forests.

**6.1.10**  To all of them, O bull among men, King Yudhiṣṭhira appointed, for them and their mounts, food and provisions of the finest kind.

**6.1.11**  And Yudhiṣṭhira devised for them watchwords of various kinds: 'He who gives this sign is to be known as one of the Pāṇḍavas.'

**6.1.12**  The Kaurava prince assigned to all of them tokens of recognition, signs, and emblems, now that the hour of battle had arrived.

**6.1.13**  Beholding the crests of the Pārthas' banners, the high-minded son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, together with all the rulers of the earth, drew up his ranks against the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.1.14**  With a white parasol held above his head, surrounded by his brothers in the midst of a thousand elephants —

**6.1.15**  beholding Duryodhana so arrayed, all the Pāṇḍava warriors, exultant, blew their great conches and beat their war-drums by the thousands.

**6.1.16**  Then the Pāṇḍavas, looking upon their own exultant army, grew glad of heart, and so too did the valiant Vāsudeva.

**6.1.17**  Then, gladdening their warriors, Vāsudeva and Dhanaṃjaya, those two tigers among men, standing upon their chariot, blew their divine conches.

**6.1.18**  Hearing the blast of both the Pāñcajanya and the Devadatta, the warriors together with their mounts let flow their dung and urine.

**6.1.19**  As other beasts tremble at hearing the roar of a lion, even so was Dhṛtarāṣṭra's host stricken with fear in that hour.

**6.1.20**  A cloud of earthly dust arose, so that nothing could be discerned; and the sun was hidden, veiled by the dust raised by the army.

**6.1.21**  And there the cloud rained down a shower of flesh and blood, drenching all the ranks — a portent strange to behold.

**6.1.22**  Then a low wind arose, driving gravel before it, buffeting those ranks and scattering that dust about.

**6.1.23**  Both armies then, O king, exceedingly eager for battle, stood arrayed upon Kurukṣetra like two storm-tossed oceans.

**6.1.24**  And the meeting of those two armies was a wondrous thing, like the clash of two oceans when the end of an age is come.

**6.1.25**  The whole earth was emptied, with only the young and the aged left behind, by reason of that vast host gathered together by the Kauravas.

**6.1.26**  Then the Kurus, the Pāṇḍavas, and the Somakas made a covenant, and they established the rules of warfare, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.1.27**  'When our war has ceased, let there be goodwill between us once more, as before and as is fitting, and let there be no further treachery.'

**6.1.28**  'When the fighting between us is waged with words, the answering also shall be only with words; one who has withdrawn from the midst of the host is on no account to be slain.'

**6.1.29**  'A chariot-warrior is to be fought by a chariot-warrior, one mounted on an elephant by an elephant, a horseman by a horseman, and a foot-soldier only by a foot-soldier, O Bhārata.'

**6.1.30**  'According to fitness, prowess, ardor, and age, one shall strike only after first giving challenge — never one who trusts unguarded, never one who is in distress.'

**6.1.31**  'One engaged with another, one off his guard, one turned away, one whose weapons are spent, one without armor — none of these is on any account to be slain.'

**6.1.32**  'Not against charioteers, not against draft-animals, not against those who carry weapons to the warriors, nor against those who sound the drums and conches, shall a blow ever be struck.'

**6.1.33**  Having made this covenant, the Kurus, the Pāṇḍavas, and the Somakas fell into utter wonder as they gazed upon one another.

**6.1.34**  And having taken up their stations, those great-souled bulls among men became, with all their troops, of joyful aspect and of good cheer.

**6.2.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, observing the twilights of dawn and dusk, the holy sage, foremost of all the knowers of the Vedas, Vyāsa the son of Satyavatī —

**6.2.2**  the holy grandsire of the Bharatas, who saw with his own eyes what should come to pass in the dreadful war, knower of past, present, and future —

**6.2.3**  to King Vaicitravīrya, grieving, afflicted, brooding then upon the folly of his sons, he spoke this in secret.

**6.2.4**  Vyāsa said: O king, your sons and the other rulers of the earth have run out their allotted time; meeting one another in battle, they will slay each other.

**6.2.5**  As they, whose time is spent, go to their ruin, O Bhārata, understand the turning of time, and do not set your mind to grief.

**6.2.6**  Yet if you wish to behold this battle, O lord of the people, then look — I grant you sight; witness this war.

**6.2.7**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: I have no wish to look upon the slaughter of my kinsmen, O best of brahmin sages; yet by your power may I hear this war in its entirety.

**6.2.8**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When he wished not to see the battle but desired to hear of it, the lord and bestower of boons granted a boon to Saṃjaya.

**6.2.9**  Vyāsa said: This Saṃjaya of yours, O king, shall tell you of this war; nothing in the battle shall be hidden from his sight.

**6.2.10**  Endowed with a divine eye, O king, this Saṃjaya will recount the war to you, and he shall become all-knowing.

**6.2.11**  Whether open or secret, whether by night or by day, even what is conceived in the mind — all of it Saṃjaya will know.

**6.2.12**  Weapons shall not pierce him, nor shall weariness afflict him; this son of Gavalgaṇa shall come forth alive from this war.

**6.2.13**  And I shall spread abroad the fame of these Kurus, O bull of the Bharatas, and of all the Pāṇḍavas; grieve not.

**6.2.14**  This was ordained of old; here you have no cause to grieve, nor can it be held back: where there is righteousness, there is victory.

**6.2.15**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having spoken thus, the holy great-grandsire of the Kurus addressed the mighty-armed Dhṛtarāṣṭra once again.

**6.2.16**  In this war, O great king, there shall be a vast destruction, even as I now mark these omens that portend terror.

**6.2.17**  Hawks and vultures, crows and herons, gathered together with the armies, swoop down at the forest's edge and form their flocks.

**6.2.18**  And the birds, rejoicing, foresee a most terrible battle; the flesh-eaters shall devour the flesh of elephants and horses.

**6.2.19**  Shrieking 'khaṭā, khaṭā' in dread, harbingers of fear, the cranes pass through the midst of the sky toward the southern quarter.

**6.2.20**  At both twilights, of dawn and of dusk, I behold without fail, O Bhārata, the sun at its rising and its setting ringed about with headless trunks.

**6.2.21**  Bordered with white and red, dark-necked, and charged with lightning, three-colored bars of cloud screen the sun at twilight.

**6.2.22**  A day and a night I have beheld in which sun, moon, and stars all blazed alike, with no distinction between day and dark: this shall be for destruction.

**6.2.23**  On the full-moon night of Kārttika the moon was unseen, robbed of its radiance, the color of fire against a sky of one same hue.

**6.2.24**  Heroes shall sleep slain, kings covering the earth, sovereigns and princes, brave men whose arms are like iron bars.

**6.2.25**  In the sky, by night, I mark without ceasing the fierce clamor of a boar and a cat warring together.

**6.2.26**  The images of the gods, too, tremble and laugh, vomit blood from their mouths, sweat, and fall down.

**6.2.27**  War-drums sound though none has struck them, O lord of the people, and the great chariots of the warriors roll forth though none has yoked them.

**6.2.28**  Cuckoos and woodpeckers, blue jays and vultures, parrots, cranes, and peacocks utter dreadful cries.

**6.2.29**  At the reddening of dawn there appear by the hundreds swarms of locusts, as it were warriors bearing weapons and ornaments, armored and mounted upon horses' backs.

**6.2.30**  Both twilights blaze forth, attended by a burning of the quarters of the sky; and there fell a rain of blood and a rain of bones, O Bhārata.

**6.2.31**  And she who is renowned throughout the three worlds, esteemed by the virtuous, Arundhatī — even by her has Vasiṣṭha been set behind.

**6.2.32**  Yonder stands Śanaiścara, O king, afflicting Rohiṇī; the mark upon the moon is turned awry: a great terror shall come to pass.

**6.2.33**  And from a cloudless sky a most dreadful thundering is heard without cease, and from the weeping draft-animals teardrops fall.

**6.3.1**  Vyāsa said: Asses are born of cows, and sons take pleasure with their mothers; the trees in the forest put forth flower and fruit out of their season.

**6.3.2**  Princesses heavy with child bring forth monstrous things — flesh-eating creatures and birds, jackals and beasts of other kinds.

**6.3.3**  Ill-omened beasts are born, three-horned, four-eyed, five-footed, with two organs, two heads, two tails, and fanged.

**6.3.4**  They are born with gaping mouths, uttering inauspicious cries — three-footed, crested, eagle-like, four-fanged, and horned.

**6.3.5**  And likewise are seen other women, even those of the reciters of the Veda, bringing forth eagles and peacocks within your city.

**6.3.6**  A mare gives birth to a calf, O lord of the earth, and a bitch to a jackal; partridges, mynahs, and parrots cry out words of ill omen.

**6.3.7**  Some women bring forth four or five daughters at a birth, and these, the very moment they are born, dance and sing and laugh.

**6.3.8**  The little ones of common folk, still at the breast, dance and sing aloud within the houses of thieves, foretelling a great terror.

**6.3.9**  Others, driven by fate, scrawl figures of armed men; and infants, cudgels in hand, rush at one another, and, eager to fight, fashion mock cities and lay siege to them.

**6.3.10**  Lotuses, blue lilies, and water-lilies spring up upon the trees; fierce winds blow from every side, and the dust never settles.

**6.3.11**  Again and again the earth quakes, and Rāhu has seized the sun; and a white planet, passing beyond Citrā, stands fixed.

**6.3.12**  Most especially does it foretell the undoing of the Kurus, this comet of dreadful aspect, that has mounted Puṣya and there stands still.

**6.3.13**  A great planet shall work fearful ill upon both armies: Aṅgāraka stands retrograde in Maghā, and Bṛhaspati in Śravaṇa.

**6.3.14**  Encroaching upon the lunar mansion Bhāgya, Śukra is afflicted by the son of Sūrya; having mounted into the eastern Proṣṭhapada, O lord of peoples, and then circled round into the northern, he stands waiting in conjunction.

**6.3.15**  A dark planet, blazing, wreathed in smoke and fire, has encroached upon the brilliant mansion of Indra, Jyeṣṭhā, and there it stands.

**6.3.16**  The Pole Star, blazing dreadfully, turns from right to left; and a harsh planet has taken its station between Citrā and Svāti.

**6.3.17**  Moving in retrograde and counter-retrograde course, bright as fire upon Śravaṇa, the Red-bodied one (Mars) has overspread the constellation of Brahmā and there abides.

**6.3.18**  The earth, mantled with every kind of grain and garlanded with fruit, brings forth barley with five heads and rice with a hundred heads.

**6.3.19**  The cows, foremost of all the world, upon whom this whole creation depends — they, giving milk to their calves, ooze blood instead.

**6.3.20**  Swords, fiercely ablaze, leap forth from their scabbards; plainly the weapons foresee the battle now at hand.

**6.3.21**  The radiance of the weapons and of the waters is as the color of fire; there shall be a great destruction of armor and of banners.

**6.3.22**  Toward the quarters of the sky, beasts and birds with flaming mouths cry out, foretelling calamity and announcing a great terror.

**6.3.23**  A bird with but one wing, one eye, and one foot, ranging the sky by night, cries out fiercely in its rage, spewing forth blood again and again.

**6.3.24**  Two planets, copper-red of crest and seeming to blaze, stand there, having veiled over the splendor of the noble Seven Seers.

**6.3.25**  And both planets, abiding for a full year and both ablaze — Bṛhaspati and Śanaiścara (Jupiter and Saturn) — stand close beside the two stars of Viśākhā.

**6.3.26**  In Kṛttikā, the first of mansions, a fierce planet, blazing and stealing away forms with its glare, stands like a smoking comet.

**6.3.27**  Through all three of the earlier mansions, O lord of peoples, Budha (Mercury) repeatedly speeds, engendering exceedingly great fear.

**6.3.28**  A fourteenth-day moon, a fifteenth-day moon, and once even a sixteenth I have known; but this new moon falling on a thirteenth day I have never known before.

**6.3.29**  Both moon and sun eclipsed within a single month, on the thirteenth day and out of their proper season — these two seizures shall bring the peoples to ruin.

**6.3.30**  All the quarters are wrapped in dust, with showers of grit on every side; and dreadful portent-clouds rain down blood in the night.

**6.3.31**  Again, on the fourteenth of the dark fortnight, at midnight, there came a fierce and most terrible rain of flesh, whereat the rākṣasas glutted themselves unsated.

**6.3.32**  The rivers flowed backward against their stream, their waters turned to blood; the wells, foaming over, bellow like bulls; and meteors fall amid thunderclaps, mingled with dry lightning-bolts.

**6.3.33**  And this very day, when the night had passed, at sunrise the sun was struck on every side by four great blazing meteors.

**6.3.34**  And the great seers, as they paid worship to the Sun, declared therein: the earth shall drink the blood of thousands of kings.

**6.3.35**  From Kailāsa and Mandara, and from the mountain Himavat as well, peaks crash down by the thousand with a mighty roar.

**6.3.36**  In the quaking of the earth the great elemental beings make each of the four oceans, severally, heave up their shores, churning them again and again.

**6.3.37**  Fierce winds blow, uprooting trees and dragging gravel along; and the sacred shrine-trees topple down in villages and in cities.

**6.3.38**  The fire, when offered into by the brāhmaṇas, blazes up yellow, red, and blue, its flame veering leftward, smelling of corpses, thick with smoke and harsh of sound; and touch, smell, and taste are all turned awry, O lord of the earth.

**6.3.39**  The banners of the kings smoke and quiver again and again, and shed showers of live coals; and so too do the kettledrums and the war-drums.

**6.3.40**  Upon the tops of palace-spires and at the city gates fierce vultures wheel and settle, taking up a circling course to the left.

**6.3.41**  And the birds cry out shrilly, again and again, "Cooked! cooked!" and roost upon the banner-tips, portending the destruction of the rulers of the earth.

**6.3.42**  Brooding and shaking out their tails, trembling all over, the horses and the elephants, piteous, weep by the thousand.

**6.3.43**  Having heard all this, let your lordship resolve upon what the hour demands, so that this world may not go to utter ruin, O Bhārata.

**6.3.44**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard these words of his father, Dhṛtarāṣṭra spoke thus: This, I think, was ordained long ago; it shall come to pass, there is no doubt.

**6.3.45**  If kṣatriyas are slain in battle according to the law of kṣatriyas, then, attaining the world of heroes, they shall win nothing but bliss.

**6.3.46**  These tigers among men, having yielded up their lives in the great battle, shall win renown here and, in the world beyond, great happiness for a long age.

**6.4.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed by his son Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the sage, lord of poets, O best of kings, knowing the truth of things, sank into deep meditation.

**6.4.2**  Then once more the great ascetic, the proclaimer of Time, spoke these words: Without doubt, O lord of kings, it is Time that draws the world together to its end.

**6.4.3**  And again Time brings forth the worlds; nothing here is everlasting — neither of your kinsmen, nor of the Kurus, nor of your allies and friends.

**6.4.4**  Point out the righteous path, for you are able to hold them back; men call the slaughter of one's own kin a base thing — do not do what is hateful to me.

**6.4.5**  This is Time, born in the shape of your son, O lord of peoples. In the Veda no slaughter is honored; this is in no wise a good.

**6.4.6**  He who would slay his own kindred slays the law of his line and his very own body. By the power of Time you are about to stray from the path, though, while it is yet possible, you might keep to the road.

**6.4.7**  For the destruction of this house, and likewise of the rulers of the earth, this calamity has come in the guise of kingship — let it be cast off, this bringer of misery.

**6.4.8**  By another your wisdom has been undone; show your sons the path of dharma. What use to you, O unassailable one, is a kingdom by which you have come to sin?

**6.4.9**  Guarding your honor, your dharma, and your renown, you shall win heaven. Let the Pāṇḍavas have the kingdom, and let the Kauravas come to peace.

**6.4.10**  As the foremost of brāhmaṇas spoke thus, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, son of Ambikā, skilled in speech, broke in upon his words and went again into the way of discourse.

**6.4.11**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Even as your lordship knows, so do I know; being and non-being are known to me as they truly are. Yet in its own concerns, dear sir, the world is bewildered — and know me too to be of one nature with the world.

**6.4.12**  I beseech you of matchless might: you are our refuge and our steadfast guide. But these of mine are not in my power, O great seer; you ought not to burden me here with the guilt.

**6.4.13**  For you are dharma itself, and purity, honor, renown, constancy, and memory; and you are the venerable grandsire of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas alike.

**6.4.14**  Vyāsa said: O king, son of Vicitravīrya, whatever stirs within your mind, speak it freely as you will; I shall cut away your doubt.

**6.4.15**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Those signs which appear in battle for them that shall be victorious — all of these, O blessed one, I wish to hear of truly.

**6.4.16**  Vyāsa said: When the fire is clear of radiance, its beams rising upward, its flame whirling to the right and free of smoke, and holy fragrances waft from the oblations — this, they say, is the very mark of coming victory.

**6.4.17**  Where conches and drums, deep-voiced and loud-sounding, resound, and the sun and the moon shine with rays unsullied — this, they say, is the very mark of coming victory.

**6.4.18**  Auspicious are the cries of crows behind those who have set out and those about to go, O king: those behind urge them onward, while those in front would hold them back.

**6.4.19**  Where birds of fair-sounding voice — royal swans, parrots, herons, and woodpeckers — wheel to the right in the battle, there the brāhmaṇas declare victory is certain.

**6.4.20**  They whose host is radiant and hard to look upon for its splendor — with ornaments, armor, and standards, and with the golden-hued, serene faces of its men — they conquer their foes.

**6.4.21**  Where the voices and the spirit of the warriors are glad, O Bhārata, and their garlands do not wither, they overcome their enemies in battle.

**6.4.22**  A favorable wind from the south, for one who has entered or is about to enter the field, accomplishes his purpose when at his back, but thwarts it when in his face.

**6.4.23**  They for whom sound, form, taste, touch, and smell show themselves auspicious, and whose warriors are ever glad — for them victory is sure.

**6.4.24**  The winds blow in their favor, and so the clouds and the birds, and the storm-clouds float along behind them, and so too do the rainbows.

**6.4.25**  These, O lord of peoples, are the marks of them that are winning; for them that are about to die they are reversed, O ruler of men.

**6.4.26**  Whether the army be small or great, this is held for certain: the gladness of the host of warriors is, by itself, called a mark of victory.

**6.4.27**  A single man, broken in spirit, breaks even a very great army; and after that one broken man even the bravest warriors give way in turn.

**6.4.28**  And a great host once shattered is most hard to check, like the mighty rush of waters, like a herd of deer struck with panic.

**6.4.29**  In the clash of battle a great host, once broken, can in no way be set in order again; at the mere word "broken!" even the bravest warriors are broken, and at the sight of men frightened and routed the terror swells the more.

**6.4.30**  A great host suddenly shattered, O king, and driven scattering to the quarters by the foe, cannot be made to stand fast, not even by brave men.

**6.4.31**  Having gathered a great army of the four arms, let the wise king, ever vigilant, strive by means of good policy first of all.

**6.4.32**  Victory by stratagem they call the best, by sowing discord the middling; lowest is that victory which is won by battle, O lord of peoples. The pitched clash is a great evil, and therefore it is called the maimed kind of conquest.

**6.4.33**  Brave men who know one another well, full of spirit, resolute and steadfast — even fifty such churn up a mighty host; or even five, six, or seven, who will not turn back, win the day.

**6.4.34**  Garuḍa, son of Vinatā, does not esteem a great multitude, O Bhārata, even on beholding a vast gathering of fine-feathered birds.

**6.4.35**  Victory does not come by the mere multitude of an army, O Bhārata; victory, indeed, is a thing uncertain, and fate is here the final refuge; for even those who win in battle suffer their own losses.

**6.5.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having spoken thus to the wise Dhṛtarāṣṭra, Vyāsa departed; and Dhṛtarāṣṭra too, having heard it, sank into meditation.

**6.5.2**  Having brooded for the space of a moment, and sighing again and again, he questioned Saṃjaya of disciplined soul, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.5.3**  Saṃjaya, these rulers of the earth, heroes who delight in battle, strike one another down with weapons high and low.

**6.5.4**  The kings, having flung away their very lives for the earth's sake, do not cease from the slaughter, swelling the realm of Yama.

**6.5.5**  Coveting earthly sovereignty, they cannot endure one another. The earth, I think, must hold many a virtue; tell me of it, Saṃjaya.

**6.5.6**  Many thousands, many myriads, hundreds of millions, and tens of millions of the world's heroes are gathered together in Kurujāṅgala.

**6.5.7**  And the measure of the lands and of the cities from which these have come together, Saṃjaya, I wish to hear of truly.

**6.5.8**  You are endowed with the lamp of divine understanding and with the eye of knowledge, by the grace of that brāhmaṇa-seer Vyāsa of measureless splendor.

**6.5.9**  Saṃjaya said: As far as my understanding reaches, O greatly wise one, I shall tell you of the earth's qualities; look on them with the eye of the scriptures. Homage to you, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.5.10**  Beings here are of two kinds: the moving and the unmoving. Of the moving, the womb is threefold — those born of eggs, of sweat, and of the womb-membrane.

**6.5.11**  Of all moving beings, O king, the womb-born are the foremost; and of the womb-born, the highest are humankind and the beasts that there are.

**6.5.12**  Bearing manifold forms, of these there are fourteen divisions: seven that dwell in the wilderness and seven of them that dwell in villages.

**6.5.13**  Lions, tigers, and boars, buffaloes and elephants, and bears, and monkeys too—these seven, O king, are reckoned the creatures of the wild.

**6.5.14**  The cow, the goat, man, the ram, the horse, the mule, and the ass—these seven are declared by the good to be the domestic animals.

**6.5.15**  These, O king, are the fourteen animals, tame and wild, named in the Vedas, O guardian of the earth, upon which the sacrifices are established.

**6.5.16**  Among the domestic kinds man is foremost, and the lion among the dwellers of the wild; yet of all beings alike the living is by one upon another.

**6.5.17**  The immovable beings are said to be those that spring from the soil, and of them there are five kinds: trees, shrubs, creepers, climbing vines, the pithless-barked plants, and the grasses.

**6.5.18**  These come to nineteen, lacking one; with the five great elements they are reckoned twenty-four—the Gāyatrī, honored by the world.

**6.5.19**  He who truly knows this holy Gāyatrī, endowed with every quality, O best of the Bharatas, does not perish from the worlds.

**6.5.20**  For upon the earth is everything born, and upon the earth does everything perish; the earth is the foundation of beings, the earth indeed is their last resort.

**6.5.21**  His who holds the earth is the whole world, all that moves and stands still; greedy for it, kings slay one another.

**6.6.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: The names of the rivers and of the mountains, Saṃjaya, and of the peoples too, and of others that dwell upon the earth—

**6.6.2**  and the measure of the earth as well, O knower of measures, in its entirety—relate it all to me fully, Saṃjaya, and the forests too.

**6.6.3**  Saṃjaya said: These five great elements, O great king, in sum, the wise declare to be equal, by which all things in the world subsist.

**6.6.4**  Earth, water, and wind, fire, and ether as well—each surpassing the next in qualities; and among them earth is chief.

**6.6.5**  Sound, and touch, and form, and taste, and odor for the fifth—these are declared the qualities of earth by the seers who know the truth.

**6.6.6**  Four qualities are in water, O king—odor is not found therein; sound, and touch, and form; then fire has three qualities; sound and touch belong to wind; and in ether sound alone.

**6.6.7**  These five qualities, O king, abide in the five great elements throughout all the worlds, upon which the worlds are established.

**6.6.8**  They do not encroach upon one another when balance prevails; but when they fall into disproportion among themselves, then the embodied rise up clothed in bodies—not otherwise.

**6.6.9**  In due succession they perish, and in due succession are born—all of them beyond measure; such is their lordly form.

**6.6.10**  Here and there are seen the constituents born of the five elements; of these men by reasoning set forth the measures.

**6.6.11**  Those states that are beyond thought one should not establish by reasoning; and that which lies beyond the primal natures is the mark of the unthinkable.

**6.6.12**  I shall declare to you the continent Sudarśana, O delight of the Kurus; circular it is, O great king, that continent, shaped like a wheel.

**6.6.13**  Covered over with river-waters and with mountains like clouds, with cities of varied form and with pleasant peopled lands,

**6.6.14**  with trees laden with flower and fruit, abounding in wealth and grain, and girt about on every side by the salt sea.

**6.6.15**  And as a man might behold his own face in a mirror, so does the continent Sudarśana appear in the orb of the moon.

**6.6.16**  In two of its parts is the holy fig tree, and in two parts the great hare, with the sowing of all healing herbs swelling it on every side; and beyond that the waters are to be known—this is told in brief.

**6.7.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: The continent has been told in brief; now tell its extent, Saṃjaya. As far as this expanse of earth appears within the figure marked by the hare, declare its measure, and then you shall speak of the fig tree.

**6.7.2**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed by the king, Saṃjaya spoke these words: Stretching eastward, O great king, are these six jeweled mountains, plunging on both sides into the eastern and the western seas.

**6.7.3**  Himavān and Hemakūṭa, and Niṣadha the foremost of mountains, and Nīla made of lapis, and Śveta with the gleam of silver, and the mountain named Śṛṅgavān, bound about with every metal.

**6.7.4**  These mountains, O king, are frequented by Siddhas and Cāraṇas; the span between them is many thousands of yojanas.

**6.7.5**  There are the holy peopled lands, those regions, O Bhārata; in them dwell living creatures of every kind and all manner of births.

**6.7.6**  This here is the region Bhārata; beyond it is Haimavata; and beyond Hemakūṭa they declare the region Harivarṣa.

**6.7.7**  To the south of Nīla and to the north of Niṣadha stretches eastward, O great king, the mountain named Mālyavān.

**6.7.8**  Beyond Mālyavān is the mountain Gandhamādana, and circular between the two stands Meru, the golden mountain.

**6.7.9**  With the radiance of the morning sun, like fire without smoke, it descends, it is said, sixteen thousand yojanas below.

**6.7.10**  And high it rises eighty-four thousand yojanas, O lord of the earth, standing above, within, and athwart, embracing the worlds.

**6.7.11**  At its sides these four continents are set, O lord: Bhadrāśva and Ketumāla, and Jambūdvīpa, O Bhārata, and the Northern Kurus, the refuge of those who have earned merit.

**6.7.12**  There the bird Sumukha, the son of Suparṇa it is said, fell to pondering when he beheld the crows turned golden:

**6.7.13**  "Since Meru makes no distinction among birds—the highest, the middling, and the lowest—therefore I shall forsake it."

**6.7.14**  Around it the sun, the lord of lights, ceaselessly circles, and the moon with its constellations, and the wind, keeping it ever on their right.

**6.7.15**  That mountain, O great king, is endowed with heavenly flowers and fruits, encompassed all about with fair mansions wrought of jāmbūnada gold.

**6.7.16**  There the hosts of gods, O king, the Gandharvas, Asuras, and Rākṣasas, together with throngs of Apsarases, sport upon that mountain everlastingly.

**6.7.17**  There Brahmā and Rudra and Śakra, the lord of the gods, assemble and worship with manifold sacrifices of abundant gifts.

**6.7.18**  Tumburu and Nārada, Viśvāvasu, Hāhā, and Hūhū, those foremost of immortals, draw near and praise them with hymns, O lord.

**6.7.19**  The seven great-souled seers, and Kaśyapa the lord of creatures, go there—good fortune be yours—ever at each holy juncture of the moon.

**6.7.20**  Upon its very summit dwells Uśanas, son of Kavi, with the Daityas, O lord of the earth; his are these jewels, his these jeweled mountains.

**6.7.21**  From him the lord Kubera enjoys a fourth part, and of that he gives a fraction of the wealth to men.

**6.7.22**  On its northern side is a heavenly grove of karṇikāra trees, blossoming in every season, auspicious and lovely, springing up amid a lattice of rocks.

**6.7.23**  There Paśupati himself, surrounded by his heavenly attendant spirits, the lord, the source of beings, sports with Umā at his side.

**6.7.24**  He wears a garland of karṇikāra flowers hanging down to his feet, alight with his three eyes as with three risen suns.

**6.7.25**  Him the Siddhas of fierce austerity, firm in their vows and speaking truth, behold; for by the ill-conducted Maheśvara cannot be seen.

**6.7.26**  From the summit of that mountain, O lord of men, a stream like milk, broad enough to be spanned by thirty arm-lengths, with a voice like a terrible thunderbolt,

**6.7.27**  holy, frequented by the holiest, the fair Gaṅgā Bhāgīrathī falls with unceasing rush into the fair lake of the moon; and by her was made that holy lake, vast as the ocean.

**6.7.28**  Her, unbearable even to the mountains, Maheśvara of old bore upon his head for a hundred thousand years.

**6.7.29**  On the western side of Meru is Ketumāla, O lord of the earth, and there too the great Jambū grove, like the garden Nandana.

**6.7.30**  The span of life there is ten thousand years, O Bhārata, and the men are golden-hued, and the women like Apsarases.

**6.7.31**  Free from sickness, freed from grief, ever glad of heart are the men born there, with the gleam of refined gold.

**6.7.32**  On the peaks of Gandhamādana, Kubera with the Rākṣasas, surrounded by throngs of Apsarases, rejoices, the lord of the Guhyakas.

**6.7.33**  At the farther foothills of Gandhamādana, the outlying tracts beyond, the utmost span of life is eleven thousand years.

**6.7.34**  There the men are dark, O king, full of vigor and mighty in strength, and the women all are hued like lotus-petals, fair to look upon.

**6.7.35**  Beyond Nīla is Śveta, and beyond Śveta the Hairaṇyaka land; the region named Airāvata is beyond that, past Śṛṅgavān.

**6.7.36**  Bow-shaped, O great king, are the two regions, southern and northern; and Ilāvṛta is the middle one—so there are five regions in all.

**6.7.37**  Each farther region beyond these surpasses in excellences: in length of life and health, in righteousness, in pleasure, and in wealth,

**6.7.38**  endowed are the beings in those regions, O Bhārata. Thus, O great king, is this earth heaped about with mountains.

**6.7.39**  And Hemakūṭa, vast indeed, is the mountain named Kailāsa, where King Vaiśravaṇa rejoices together with the Guhyakas.

**6.7.40**  To the north of Kailāsa, near the mountain Maināka, stands the great heavenly mountain Hiraṇyaśṛṅga, made of jewels.

**6.7.41**  At its side is the great, heavenly, fair lake of golden sands, the lovely lake named Bindusaras, where King Bhagīratha, beholding the Gaṅgā Bhāgīrathī, dwelt for many years.

**6.7.42**  There are sacrificial posts of jewels and altars too of gold; and having sacrificed there, the thousand-eyed one of great renown attained perfection.

**6.7.43**  There the eternal lord of beings, of keen splendor, having created all the worlds, is worshipped, surrounded by the assembled spirits—Nara and Nārāyaṇa, Brahmā, Manu, and Sthāṇu for the fifth.

**6.7.44**  There the goddess of the threefold path first took her stand; descending from the world of Brahmā, she divides into seven streams:

**6.7.45**  Vasvokasārā, Nalinī, and Pāvanā, and Sarasvatī, and Jambūnadī, and Sītā, and Gaṅgā, and Sindhu for the seventh.

**6.7.46**  Unthinkable, of heavenly resolve—such is the ordinance of the Lord—where they perform the sacrificial session at the close of a thousand ages.

**6.7.47**  And here and there Sarasvatī becomes now seen, now unseen; these are the seven heavenly Gaṅgās, famed throughout the three worlds.

**6.7.48**  On Himavān dwell the Rākṣasas, on Hemakūṭa the Guhyakas, serpents and Nāgas on Niṣadha, and on Gokarṇa those rich in austerity.

**6.7.49**  The Śveta mountain is called the home of gods and Asuras; the Gandharvas on the Niṣadha mountain, the brahmā-seers on Nīla, O king; and Śṛṅgavān, O great king, is the haunt of the fathers.

**6.7.50**  Thus, O great king, these are the seven regions, portion by portion, wherein the beings have settled, both the moving and the fixed.

**6.7.51**  Their prosperity, manifold, is seen to be both divine and human, impossible to reckon up, yet to be believed by one who would prosper.

**6.7.52**  As for this heavenly hare-shape about which you ask me, O king—at the hare's sides are two regions, both south and north, and its two ears are Nāgadvīpa and Kaśyapadvīpa.

**6.7.53**  Its head is copper-hued, O king—the splendid Malaya mountain; this is seen as the second part of the continent, set in the form of a hare.

**6.8.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Now describe to me, Saṃjaya, the northern and the eastern side of Meru, in full, O great of mind, and the mountain Mālyavān.

**6.8.2**  Saṃjaya said: To the south of Nīla and on the northern side of Meru, O king, are the holy Northern Kurus, frequented by the Siddhas.

**6.8.3**  There the trees bear honeyed fruit and are ever laden with blossom and fruit; their flowers are sweetly fragrant and their fruits are full of juice.

**6.8.4**  Some trees there yield the fruit of every desire, O lord of men; and others there, O ruler of men, are the trees called the milk-bearers.

**6.8.5**  These ever pour forth milk endowed with the six flavors and like to nectar; and within their fruits are brought forth garments and ornaments as well.

**6.8.6**  The whole earth there is made of jewels, with fine golden sand; everywhere it is pleasant to the touch and free of mire, O lord of men.

**6.8.7**  All the men born there have fallen from the world of the gods, endowed with equal beauty and equal virtues, in fair times and foul alike.

**6.8.8**  They are born as couples, and the women are like the apsarases; and they drink the milk of those milk-bearing trees, milk that is like nectar.

**6.8.9**  Each couple, being born together, grows up as equals, alike in beauty and virtue and likewise alike in garb; and each is devoted to the other, even as the cakravāka pair, O mighty one.

**6.8.10**  Free of sickness and bereft of sorrow, their minds ever joyful, they live, O great king, for ten thousand years and ten hundred years besides, and they never forsake one another.

**6.8.11**  There are birds named bhāruṇḍas, sharp-beaked and of great strength; these carry off the dead and cast them into mountain caverns.

**6.8.12**  The Northern Kurus, O king, have thus been described to you in brief; now I shall tell of the eastern flank of Meru in due order.

**6.8.13**  Its eastern domain is Bhadrāśva, O lord of peoples, where lies the forest of Bhadraśāla and the great tree called Kālāmra.

**6.8.14**  And the Kālāmra, O great king, is fair and ever laden with blossom and fruit, a tree a yojana in height, frequented by siddhas and cāraṇas.

**6.8.15**  The men there are fair, possessed of splendor and great strength; and the women are of the hue of the white lotus, lovely and pleasing to behold.

**6.8.16**  Bright as the moon, moon-hued, their faces like the full moon, their limbs cool as the moon, skilled in dance and song.

**6.8.17**  Their lifespan there is ten thousand years, O bull of the Bharatas; nourished on the juice of the Kālāmra, they remain forever in their youth.

**6.8.18**  To the south of Nīla and to the north of Niṣadha stands the great and everlasting Jambū tree named Sudarśana.

**6.8.19**  Yielding the fruit of every desire, holy, frequented by siddhas and cāraṇas; from its name the eternal Jambūdvīpa is so called.

**6.8.20**  A thousand and a hundred yojanas, O bull of the Bharatas, is the height of that king of trees, touching the heavens, O lord of men.

**6.8.21**  A thousand cubits, and fifteen hundred more, is the girth of that tree, whose fruits are of varied juices.

**6.8.22**  Falling upon the earth, those fruits make a mighty sound, and they release, O king, a juice that resembles silver.

**6.8.23**  The fruit-juice of that Jambū, becoming a river, O lord of men, circles Meru in reverence and flows on to the Northern Kurus.

**6.8.24**  The people there drink that juice with delight forever, O lord of men; and when that fruit-juice is drunk, old age does not afflict them.

**6.8.25**  There is the gold called Jāmbūnada, the adornment of the gods; and the men born there are of the hue of the youthful sun.

**6.8.26**  Likewise upon the peak of Mālyavān there blazes the bearer of oblations, the fire of time named Saṃvartaka, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.8.27**  And on the eastern peak of Mālyavān lies the eastern boundary-rock; Mālyavān stands fifty thousand yojanas in extent.

**6.8.28**  The men born there shine like fine silver; all have fallen from the world of Brahmā, and all are expounders of the sacred word.

**6.8.29**  Performing austerities, they become men of upward-flowing seed; and for the protection of all beings they enter into the sun.

**6.8.30**  Sixty thousand of them, and sixty hundred besides, go before Aruṇa, surrounding the maker of day.

**6.8.31**  For sixty thousand years and sixty hundred years besides, scorched by the heat of the sun, they enter at last the orb of the moon.

**6.9.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: The names of the regions and of the mountains, O Saṃjaya, tell me truly, and who dwell upon the mountains.

**6.9.2**  Saṃjaya said: To the south of Śveta and to the north of Nīla is the region named Ramaṇaka, and there men are born.

**6.9.3**  Endowed with pure lineage, all most fair to look upon, and given over to delight, are the men born there.

**6.9.4**  For ten thousand years and fifteen hundred years besides they live, O great king, their minds ever joyful.

**6.9.5**  To the south of Śṛṅgavān and to the north of Śveta is the region named Hairaṇvata, where flows the river Hairaṇvatī.

**6.9.6**  Followers of the yakṣas, O great king, wealthy and fair to behold, of great strength they ever are there, O king, with joyful minds.

**6.9.7**  Eleven thousand years, O lord of men, is the measure of their life, and fifteen hundred years besides they live.

**6.9.8**  The peaks of Śṛṅgavān are three, O lord of men: one there is made of jewels, and one is wondrously of gold.

**6.9.9**  And one is made of every gem, adorned with mansions; there dwells forever the self-luminous goddess Śāṇḍilī.

**6.9.10**  To the north of Śṛṅgavān, at the shore of the sea, O lord of men, is the region named Airāvata, beyond that very Śṛṅgavān.

**6.9.11**  There the sun does not scorch, nor do those men grow old; and the moon with its constellations is veiled, as though become a single radiance.

**6.9.12**  Lustrous as the lotus, lotus-hued, their eyes like lotus-petals, and fragrant as lotus-leaves are the men born there.

**6.9.13**  Unmoving, sweet of fragrance, taking no food, masters of their senses, all are fallen from the world of the gods and free of passion, O king.

**6.9.14**  Thirteen thousand years, O lord of men, is the measure of life that those men live, O best of the Bharatas.

**6.9.15**  And to the north of the ocean of milk the lord Hari, Vaikuṇṭha, dwells upon a chariot wrought of gold.

**6.9.16**  That vehicle is eight-wheeled, yoked with elemental beings, swift as thought, fire-hued, of great speed, adorned with Jāmbūnada gold.

**6.9.17**  He is the lord of all beings and the all-pervading, O bull of the Bharatas; he is contraction and expansion, the doer and the cause of doing.

**6.9.18**  Earth, water, and space, wind and fire, O king—he is the sacrifice of all beings, and the devourer of oblations is his mouth.

**6.9.19**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed by Saṃjaya, the great-minded king Dhṛtarāṣṭra fell to brooding upon his sons, O lord of men.

**6.9.20**  Having pondered, O great king, he spoke again these words: Without doubt, O son of a sūta, Time draws in the world and creates all anew; nothing here abides forever.

**6.9.21**  Nara and Nārāyaṇa, the all-knowing, the sustainer of all beings—him the gods call Vaikuṇṭha, and the Vedas call that lord Viṣṇu.

**6.10.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: This land of Bhārata, where this host has gathered in its frenzy, where this son of mine, Duryodhana, is greedy beyond all measure—

**6.10.2**  Where the sons of Pāṇḍu too are covetous, and where my mind clings fast—of this tell me the truth, for you are skilled, O Saṃjaya.

**6.10.3**  Saṃjaya said: The Pāṇḍavas are not covetous of it—hear my words, O king; covetous of it are Duryodhana and also Śakuni, the son of Subala.

**6.10.4**  And other kṣatriyas besides, lords of many lands, who are covetous of the land of Bhārata and cannot endure one another.

**6.10.5**  Here I shall describe to you, O Bhārata, the land of Bhārata, dear to the god Indra and to Manu, son of Vivasvat,

**6.10.6**  and to Pṛthu, son of Vena, O king, and to the great-souled Ikṣvāku, to Yayāti and Ambarīṣa, to Māndhātṛ and Nahuṣa,

**6.10.7**  and likewise to Mucukunda, to Śibi son of Uśīnara, to Ṛṣabha and to Ila, and to the king Nṛga as well,

**6.10.8**  and to others, O great king, mighty kṣatriyas all—to every one of them, O king of kings, was the land of Bhārata dear, O Bhārata.

**6.10.9**  That land I shall declare to you as I have heard it, O subduer of foes; hear me as I speak, O king, what you ask of me.

**6.10.10**  Mahendra, Malaya, Sahya, Śuktimān, and Ṛkṣavān too, Vindhya and Pāriyātra—these seven are the principal mountains.

**6.10.11**  Near these, O king, are mountains by the thousand, well-known, massive, vast, with peaks of many hues.

**6.10.12**  And others beyond these, unknown, small, and inhabited by lowly folk; āryas and mlecchas, O Kaurava, and men mingled with both, O mighty one.

**6.10.13**  They drink of many rivers: the Gaṅgā, the Sindhu, the Sarasvatī, the Godāvarī and the Narmadā, and the great river Bāhudā,

**6.10.14**  the Śatadru and the Candrabhāgā, and the great river Yamunā, the Dṛṣadvatī and the Vipāśā, the Vipāpā and the Sthūlavālukā,

**6.10.15**  the river Vetravatī, and the stream Kṛṣṇaveṇā, the Irāvatī and the Vitastā, the Payoṣṇī and the Devikā too,

**6.10.16**  the Vedasmṛti, the Vetasinī, the Tridivā and the Iṣkumālinī, the Karīṣiṇī, the Citravahā, and the stream Citrasenā,

**6.10.17**  the Gomatī and the Dhūtapāpā, and the great river Vandanā, the Kauśikī, the Tridivā, the Kṛtyā, the Vicitrā, and the Lohatāriṇī,

**6.10.18**  the Rathasthā and the Śatakumbhā, and the Sarayū, O lord of men, the Carmaṇvatī, the Vetravatī, the Hastisomā, and the Diś as well,

**6.10.19**  the Śatāvarī and the Payoṣṇī, and beyond them the Bhaimarathī, the Kāverī and the Culukā, and the Vāpī and the Śatabalā too,

**6.10.20**  the Nicīrā and the Mahitā, and the Suprayogā, O ruler of men, the Pavitrā, the Kuṇḍalā, the Sindhu, the Vājinī, the Puramālinī,

**6.10.21**  the Pūrvābhirāmā and the Vīrā, the Bhīmā and the Oghavatī as well, the Palāśinī, the Pāpaharā, the Mahendrā, and the Pippalāvatī,

**6.10.22**  the Pāriṣeṇā and the Asiknī, the Saralā and the Bhāramardinī, the Puruhī, the Pravarā, the Menā, the Moghā, and the Ghṛtavatī too,

**6.10.23**  the Dhūmatī and the Atikṛṣṇā, the Sūcī and the Chāvī, O Kaurava, the Sadānīrā and the Adhṛṣyā, and the great river Kuśadhārā,

**6.10.24**  the Śaśikāntā and the Śivā, and the Vīravatī as well, the Vāstu, the Suvāstu, the Gaurī, the Kampanā, and the Hiraṇvatī,

**6.10.25**  the Hiraṇvatī, the Citravatī, and the stream Citrasenā, the Rathacitrā, the Jyotirathā, the Viśvāmitrā, and the Kapiñjalā,

**6.10.26**  the Upendrā and the Bahulā, the Kucarā, the Ambuvāhinī, the Vainandī, the Piñjalā, the Veṇṇā, and the great river Tuṅgaveṇā,

**6.10.27**  the Vidiśā and the Kṛṣṇaveṇṇā, and the Tāmrā and the Kapilā too, the Śalu, the Suvāmā, the Vedāśvā, and the great stream Harisrāvā,

**6.10.28**  the Śīghrā and the Picchilā, and the stream Bhāradvājī, the stream Kauśikī, the Śoṇā, the Bāhudā, and then the Candanā,

**6.10.29**  the Durgā and the Antaḥśilā, the Brahmamedhyā, the Bṛhadvatī, the Carakṣā and the Mahirohī, and likewise the Jambunadī too,

**6.10.30**  the Sunasā, the Tamasā, the Dāsī, the Trasā, another, the Varāṇasī, the Loloddhṛtakarā, and the great river Pūrṇāśā.

**6.10.31**  The Mānavī and the Vṛṣabhā too, great rivers, O lord of men, the ever-wholesome Nirāmayā, the Vṛtyā, the Mandagā, and the slow-flowing Mandavāhinī.

**6.10.32**  The Brahmāṇī and the great Gaurī, and the Durgā too, O Bhārata, the Citropalā, the Citrabarhā, the Mañju, and the Makaravāhinī.

**6.10.33**  The Mandākinī, the Vaitaraṇī, and the Kokā, a great river, the Śuktimatī, the Araṇyā, the Puṣpaveṇī, and the Utpalāvatī.

**6.10.34**  The Lohityā and the Karatoyā, and likewise the Vṛṣabhaṅginī, the Kumārī, the Ṛṣikulyā, and the Brahmakulyā too, O Bhārata.

**6.10.35**  The most holy Sarasvatīs and all the Gaṅgās, O honored one — all of them mothers of the world, and all of them of mighty power.

**6.10.36**  And rivers beyond reckoning besides, by the hundreds and the thousands. Thus, O king, have these streams been recounted, as memory serves.

**6.10.37**  Now learn from me, as I declare them, the peoples that come next. There are the Kurus and Pāñcālas, the Śālvas, the Mādreyas, and the Jāṅgalas.

**6.10.38**  The Śūrasenas and the Kaliṅgas, the Bodhas and the Maukas as well, the Matsyas, the Sukuṭyas, the Saubalyas, the Kuntalas, the Kāśis, and the Kosalas.

**6.10.39**  The Cedis, the Vatsas, and the Karūṣas, the Bhojas, the Sindhus, and the Pulindakas, the Uttamaujas, the Daśārṇas, and the Mekalas together with the Utkalas.

**6.10.40**  The Pāñcālas and the Kauśijas, the Ekapṛṣṭhas, the Yugaṃdharas, the Saudhas, the Madras, and the Bhujiṅgas, the Kāśis and the Aparakāśis.

**6.10.41**  The Jaṭharas and the Kukkuśas, and the Sudāśārṇas too, O Bhārata, the Kuntis and the Avantis, and the Aparakuntis as well.

**6.10.42**  The Govindas, the Mandakas, the Ṣaṇḍas, the Vidarbhas, the Anūpavāsikas, the Aśmakas, the Pāṃsurāṣṭras, the Goparāṣṭras, and the Panītakas.

**6.10.43**  The Ādirāṣṭras and the Sukuṭṭas, and the Balirāṣṭra entire, the Vānarāsyas, the Pravāhas, the Vakras, the Vakrabhayas, and the Śakas.

**6.10.44**  The Videhas and the Māgadhas, the Suhmas and the Vijayas likewise, the Aṅgas, the Vaṅgas, the Kaliṅgas, and the Yakṛllomans as well.

**6.10.45**  The Mallas, the Sudeṣṇas, the Prāhūtas, and the Māhiṣas and Kārṣikas, the Vāhīkas, the Vāṭadhānas, the Ābhīras, and the Kālatoyakas.

**6.10.46**  The Aparāndhras and the Śūdras, the Pahlavas and the Carmakhaṇḍikas, the Aṭavīśabaras too, and the dwellers of the desert lands, O honored one.

**6.10.47**  The Upāvṛts, the Anupāvṛts, the Surāṣṭras, and the Kekayas likewise, the Kuṭṭas, the Aparāntas, the Dvaidheyas, the Kākṣas, and the Sāmudraniṣkuṭas.

**6.10.48**  And the Andhras in great number, O king, and the Antargiris likewise, the Bahirgiris, the Aṅgas, the Maladas, the Māgadhas, and the Mānavarjakas.

**6.10.49**  The Mahyuttaras, the Prāvṛṣeyas, and the Bhārgavas, O lord of men, the Puṇḍras, the Bhārgas, the Kirātas, the Sudoṣṇas, and the Pramudas likewise.

**6.10.50**  The Śakas, the Niṣādas, the Niṣadhas, and likewise the Ānartas and the Nairṛtas, the Dugūlas, the Pratimatsyas, the Kuśalas, and the Kunaṭas as well.

**6.10.51**  The Tīragrāhas, the Taratoyas, the Rājikas, the hosts of the Rasyakas, the Tilakas, the Pārasīkas, the Madhumantas, and the Prakutsakas.

**6.10.52**  The Kāśmīras, the Sindhus and Sauvīras, the Gāndhāras, and the Darśakas likewise, the Abhīsāras, the Kulūtas, the Śaivalas, and the Bāhlikas as well.

**6.10.53**  The Darvīkas with the Kacas, the Darvas, the Vātadhānas, the Amarathas, the Uragas, the Bahuvādyas too, O Kaurava, the Sudāmans, and the Sumallikas.

**6.10.54**  The Vadhras and the Karīṣakas too, and the Kulindas of the foothills likewise, the Vanāyus, the Daśāpārśvas, the Romans, and the Kuśabindus.

**6.10.55**  The Kacchas and the Gopālakacchas, the Lāṅgalas, the Paravallakas, the Kirātas, the Barbaras, the Siddhas, the Videhas, and the Tāmraliṅgakas.

**6.10.56**  The Oṣṭras, the Puṇḍras, the Sairandhras as well, and the mountain peoples, O honored one. Now the other peoples, those of the south, O bull of the Bharatas:

**6.10.57**  The Draviḍas, the Keralas, the Prācyas, the Bhūṣikas, the forest-dwellers, the Unnatyakas, the Māhiṣakas, the Vikalpas, and the Mūṣakas likewise.

**6.10.58**  The Karṇikas and the Kuntikas, the Saudbhidas, the Nalakālakas, the Kaukuṭṭakas as well, the Colas, the Koṅkaṇas, and the Mālavāṇakas.

**6.10.59**  The Samaṅgas and the Kopanas, the Kukuras, the Aṅgadas, O honored one, the Dhvajinīs, the Utsavasaṃketas, the Trigartas, and the Sarvasenas.

**6.10.60**  The Tryaṅgas, the Kekarakas, the Proṣṭhas, and the Parasaṃcarakas likewise, and the Vindhya tribes as well, the Pulindas together with the Kalkalas.

**6.10.61**  The Mālakas and the Mallakas, and the Aparavartakas as well, the Kulindas and the Kulakas, the Karaṇṭhas, and the Kurakas likewise.

**6.10.62**  The Mūṣakas and the Stanabālas, the Satiyas, the Pattipañjakas, the Ādidāyas, the Sirālas, the Stūbakas, and the Stanapas as well.

**6.10.63**  The Hṛṣīvidarbhas, the Kāntīkas, the Taṅgaṇas, the Parataṅgaṇas, and others, the barbarian peoples of the north, O best of the Bharatas:

**6.10.64**  The Yavanas together with the Kāmbojas, savage barbarian tribes, the Kṣaddruhas, the Kuntalas, and the Hūṇas together with the Paratakas.

**6.10.65**  And likewise the Maradhas and the Cīnas, and the Daśamālikas as well, settlements of kṣatriyas, and families of vaiśyas and śūdras.

**6.10.66**  The Śūdras and Ābhīras, then the Daradas, the Kāśmīras together with their herds, the Khaśikas and the Tukhāras, the Pallavas, and the dwellers of the mountain caverns.

**6.10.67**  The Ātreyas with the Bharadvājas, and likewise the Stanayoṣikas, the Aupakas and the Kaliṅgas, and the tribes of the Kirātas.

**6.10.68**  The Tāmaras and the Haṃsamārgas, and likewise the Karabhañjakas. These lands have I named, O lord, only by way of brief mention.

**6.10.69**  And rightly tended, the earth yields, in measure of its quality and strength, the great fruit of the threefold aim of life, like a cow that gives milk, a cow that grants every wish.

**6.10.70**  For her the kings are greedy, brave men skilled in dharma and gain; coveting her richness, those swift and strong ones lay down their very lives in battle.

**6.10.71**  The earth is, beyond question, the refuge of the bodies of gods and men; and for her men prey upon one another, as dogs snatch at a piece of meat.

**6.10.72**  Kings, O best of the Bharatas, long to enjoy the earth that bears all wealth; yet here no man's desires are ever satisfied.

**6.10.73**  Therefore, O king, the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas strive to seize the earth — by conciliation, by gift, by sowing discord, and by force.

**6.10.74**  Tended aright, the earth becomes father, mother, and son, sky and heaven to all living beings, O bull among men, looking upon them with a flawless eye.

**6.11.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Of this land of Bhārata, and of Haimavata as well, O sūta, tell me the measure of the span of life, and its fruit, both fair and foul.

**6.11.2**  What is to come, what has passed, and what now is, O Saṃjaya — tell me of these at length, and of Harivarṣa as well.

**6.11.3**  Saṃjaya said: In the land of Bhārata there are four ages, O bull of the Bharatas: the Kṛta, the Tretā, the Dvāpara, and the Puṣya, O increaser of the Kurus.

**6.11.4**  First comes the age called Kṛta, then the Tretā age, O lord; thereafter, with the shortening of the Dvāpara, the Puṣya sets in.

**6.11.5**  Four thousand years, O best of the Kurus, is the reckoned span of life in the Kṛta age, O best of kings.

**6.11.6**  Three thousand in the Tretā, O ruler of men, two thousand in the Dvāpara; but now it stands at a mere hundred.

**6.11.7**  In this Puṣya age there is no fixed measure of life at all, O bull of the Bharatas: here some die yet in the womb, and some die as soon as they are born.

**6.11.8**  In the Kṛta age, O king, men were born of great strength, great courage, and endowed with every excellence of progeny — sages rich in the wealth of austerity.

**6.11.9**  Of great zeal, great-souled, righteous, and truthful in speech — so were men born in the Kṛta age, O king, wealthy and fair to look upon.

**6.11.10**  Long-lived, of great valor, foremost among bowmen in battle — so are the kṣatriyas born in the Tretā: heroes and universal emperors.

**6.11.11**  In the Dvāpara, O great king, men of all the orders are born, of great zeal and great might, yet bent upon the slaughter of one another.

**6.11.12**  Endowed with but little luster, prone to wrath, greedy and given to falsehood — such are the men born in the Puṣya age, O Bhārata.

**6.11.13**  Envy, pride, and wrath, deceit and spite as well, passion and greed — these arise among mortals in the Puṣya age, O Bhārata.

**6.11.14**  In this Dvāpara, O king, there is a dwindling, O ruler of men. Higher in excellence is Haimavata, and Harivarṣa higher still beyond that.

**6.12.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: You have duly described to me the Jambū continent, O Saṃjaya. Now tell me of its breadth and its extent, truly and exactly.

**6.12.2**  And the measure of the ocean, O you of flawless sight; tell me too of the Śāka continent and the Kuśa continent, O Saṃjaya.

**6.12.3**  And truly of the Śālmala, and of the Krauñca continent as well; tell me all, O son of Gavalgaṇa, of Rāhu and of the Moon and the Sun.

**6.12.4**  Saṃjaya said: O king, many indeed are the continents by which this world is spread out; but I shall declare to you the seven, and the Moon and Sun and planets as well.

**6.12.5**  Eighteen thousand yojanas, O lord of peoples, and six hundred full besides — such is the breadth of the Jambu mountain region.

**6.12.6**  The breadth of the Salt Ocean is reckoned twice as great, strewn with diverse peoples and bright with gems and coral.

**6.12.7**  Adorned with mountains gleaming with manifold minerals, thronged by Siddhas and Cāraṇas, the ocean lies girdling all around.

**6.12.8**  I shall now describe the Śāka continent duly, O king. Hear me as I speak of it in proper order, O delight of the Kurus.

**6.12.9**  By the measure of the Jambū continent it is twice as great in breadth, O ruler of men, O great king; and so too is the Sea of Milk in due proportion, O best of the Bharatas, by which it is encircled all about.

**6.12.10**  There the lands are holy, and there no man dies; how then could there be famine? For its people are endowed with patience and luster.

**6.12.11**  This, O bull of the Bharatas, is a brief account of the Śāka continent, duly given, O great king. What else do you wish to hear?

**6.12.12**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: You have duly given me a brief account of the Śāka continent, O Saṃjaya, O greatly blessed one. Now tell me of it in full and exactly.

**6.12.13**  Saṃjaya said: There too, O king, are seven mountains adorned with gems, and rivers that are mines of jewels — hear from me their names. All there is supremely rich in excellence and holy, O lord of men.

**6.12.14**  Frequented by gods, sages, and Gandharvas, the supreme one is called Meru; and stretching to the east, O great king, is the mountain named Malaya, from which the clouds arise and spread forth on every side.

**6.12.15**  Beyond that, O Kaurava, is the great mountain Jaladhāra, where Vāsava ever draws up the choicest waters, whence rain springs forth in the season of rains, O lord of men.

**6.12.16**  Lofty is the mountain Raivataka, where the constellation Revatī is ever fixed in the sky — so was it ordained by the Grandsire.

**6.12.17**  And to the north, O king of kings, is the great mountain named Śyāma, from which the people have come to be dark of hue, O lord of the realms.

**6.12.18**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: A great doubt is mine this day at what you have said, O Saṃjaya. How, O son of the sūta, have the people there come to be dark of hue?

**6.12.19**  Saṃjaya said: In all the continents alike, O greatly wise one, O delight of the Kurus, there are two colors, fair and dark; and between the two, O king, lie all the other hues.

**6.12.20**  From what the darkness has arisen I shall tell you, O Bhārata: the blessed Kṛṣṇa abides there, and by his radiance the people have taken on a dusky hue.

**6.12.21**  Beyond that, O lord of the Kurus, is the mighty mountain Durgaśaila of great glory, the Kesarin clothed in fragrant blooms, from which the wind blows forth.

**6.12.22**  The breadth of these in yojanas is twice as great, region by region; and the lands within them, O Kaurava, have been declared by the wise.

**6.12.23**  Mahāmeru, Mahākāśa, Jalada, and Kumudottara, O king; and beyond Jaladhāra is the one remembered as Sukumāra.

**6.12.24**  For Raivata there is Kaumāra, for Śyāma there is Maṇīcaka, for Kesara there is Modākī, and beyond that the great Mahāpuruṣa.

**6.12.25**  Encircling it, O Kaurava, in its length and breadth, it is famed in proportion to Jambūdvīpa; and in its midst stands a great tree.

**6.12.26**  It is named Śāka, O great king, standing at the center of that island. There the lands are holy, and there Śaṃkara is worshipped.

**6.12.27**  There go the Siddhas, the Cāraṇas, and the gods; and righteous are the people there, O king, the four classes exceedingly so, O Bhārata.

**6.12.28**  The classes are devoted each to its own duty, and no thief is seen there; long-lived are they, O great king, free from old age and death.

**6.12.29**  The people there flourish like rivers flowing to the sea in the rains; the streams there are of holy water, and the Gaṅgā runs in many courses.

**6.12.30**  Sukumārī and Kumārī, Sītā and Kāverakā, the Mahānadī too, O Kaurava, and the river Maṇijalā, and Ikṣuvardhanikā as well, O best of the Bharatas.

**6.12.31**  From there flow forth holy-watered rivers, O upholder of the Kuru line, hundreds upon thousands of them, wherever Vāsava sends his rain.

**6.12.32**  Their names and likewise their number cannot be reckoned in full, for they are holy, those finest of rivers.

**6.12.33**  There are four holy lands, esteemed by the world: the Magas and the Maśakas, the Mānasas and the Mandagas.

**6.12.34**  The Magas are mostly brāhmaṇas, devoted to their own duty, O king; among the Maśakas dwell kṣatriyas, righteous and granting every desire.

**6.12.35**  Among the Mānasas, O great king, are vaiśyas who live by their work, endowed with every wish, valiant, resolute in dharma and wealth; and in Mandaga ever dwell śūdras, men of righteous conduct.

**6.12.36**  There is no king there, O lord of kings, no rod of punishment nor those who wield it; by their own dharma alone they guard one another's righteousness.

**6.12.37**  This much only can be told of that island, and this much only is to be heard concerning the mighty Śākadvīpa.

**6.13.1**  Saṃjaya said: Of the islands to the north, O Kaurava, a tale is heard; as I have heard it, O great king, hear it now from me as I tell it.

**6.13.2**  Here is a sea whose waters are clarified butter, another whose waters are curds and whey, an ocean of wine, and yet another, a sea of warm water.

**6.13.3**  Each is double the last, O ruler of men, all the islands; and on every side, O great king, they are girded round by mountains.

**6.13.4**  In the middle island stands a great mountain, white, of realgar; and to the west a dark mountain, resembling Nārāyaṇa, O king.

**6.13.5**  There Keśava himself guards the divine jewels, and, attending upon Prajāpati, ordains the welfare of the creatures.

**6.13.6**  In Kuśadvīpa a clump of kuśa-grass, at the center of the land, is held in worship; and the śalmali tree on the island Śālmalika, O king.

**6.13.7**  In Krauñcadvīpa the great mountain Krauñca, a mine heaped with jewels, is worshipped ever, O great king, by all four classes.

**6.13.8**  There is the great mountain Gomanda, O king, rich in every mineral, where the glorious lotus-eyed Lord dwells forever, ever praised by those who seek release, the lord Nārāyaṇa Hari.

**6.13.9**  In Kuśadvīpa, O lord of kings, is a mountain studded with coral, named Sudhāman, hard to assail; and a second, the golden mountain.

**6.13.10**  Named Dyutimat, O Kaurava; third, the mountain Kumuda; fourth, the one named Puṣpavat; and fifth, Kuśeśaya.

**6.13.11**  Sixth is the one named Harigiri; these six are the foremost of mountains, and the span between them, region by region, is double each preceding.

**6.13.12**  The first region is Audbhida, the second Veṇumaṇḍala, the third indeed Rathākāra, and the fourth is remembered as Pālana.

**6.13.13**  Dhṛtimat is the fifth region, Prabhākara the sixth, and Kāpila the seventh region; these are the seven clusters of regions.

**6.13.14**  In these the gods, the gandharvas, and the people, O lord of the earth, roam and take their delight, and no one there dies.

**6.13.15**  In them there are no robbers, nor any of barbarian birth, O king; all the folk are fair of hue and delicate, O Pārtha.

**6.13.16**  Concerning the remaining regions I shall speak, O lord of men, as I have heard it, O great king; hear it with an untroubled mind.

**6.13.17**  In Krauñcadvīpa, O great king, is the great mountain named Krauñca; beyond Krauñca is Vāmanaka, and beyond Vāmana, Andhakāraka.

**6.13.18**  Beyond Andhakāra, O king, is Maināka, foremost of mountains; and beyond Maināka, O king, the lofty mountain Govinda.

**6.13.19**  And beyond Govinda, O king, is a mountain named Nibiḍa; the span of each is double the last, O increaser of your line.

**6.13.20**  I shall tell you the countries there; hear me as I declare them. The country of Krauñca is Kuśala, that of Vāmana is Manonuga.

**6.13.21**  Beyond Manonuga is the country Uṣṇa, O upholder of the Kuru line; beyond Uṣṇa is Prāvaraka, and beyond Prāvara, Andhakāraka.

**6.13.22**  Beyond the country of Andhakāraka is remembered the country of Muni; and beyond the country of Muni is named Dundubhisvana.

**6.13.23**  Thronged with Siddhas and Cāraṇas, its people mostly fair of hue, O lord of men; these countries, O great king, are frequented by gods and gandharvas.

**6.13.24**  In Puṣkara is a mountain named Puṣkara, rich in gems and jewels; there the god Prajāpati himself dwells forever.

**6.13.25**  Upon him all the gods, with the great seers, ever wait in attendance, honoring him with words that gladden the heart, O lord of men.

**6.13.26**  From Jambūdvīpa proceed jewels of many kinds, indeed, to all those islands, for the people, O joy of the Kurus.

**6.13.27**  Through the brāhmaṇas' chastity, their truth, and their self-restraint, in measure of health and length of life it is double and ever doubling thereafter.

**6.13.28**  There is but one community, O king, in all these islands, O Bhārata, the lands of which I have spoken, where a single dharma is seen.

**6.13.29**  The Lord himself, Prajāpati, raising the rod of rule, O great king, stands ever guarding these islands.

**6.13.30**  He is king, he is the auspicious one, O king; he is father, he is grandfather; he shelters, O best of men, the people, both the dull and the wise.

**6.13.31**  And here, O Kaurava, the people partake of food that comes to them of itself, already prepared, O great king, ever there.

**6.13.32**  Beyond that is seen a region of the world named Samā, four-cornered, O great king, with a circuit of thirty-three.

**6.13.33**  There stand, O Kaurava, four elephants of the quarters, esteemed by the world, O best of the Bharatas, Vāmana, Airāvata, and the rest, and Supratīka too, O king, his cheeks and face streaming with rut.

**6.13.34**  Its measure I cannot venture to reckon here, for it is forever beyond counting, across, above, and below alike.

**6.13.35**  There indeed the winds blow from all the quarters, unobstructed, O great king, and those elephants take them in.

**6.13.36**  With their trunks, like lotuses, broad and of mighty splendor, they then slowly, yet swiftly again, release the winds without end.

**6.13.37**  Released by the breathing elephants of the quarters, the winds come hither, O great king, and thereby the creatures live on.

**6.13.38**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: A vast expanse indeed have you recounted to excess, O Saṃjaya; you have shown the disposition of the islands. Now tell me what is beyond, O Saṃjaya.

**6.13.39**  Saṃjaya said: The islands have been told, O great king; now hear of the planets truly from me: Svarbhānu, O best of the Kauravas, how great he is in his might.

**6.13.40**  Round in shape, O great king, the planet Svarbhānu is said to be; its diameter is full twelve thousand yojanas.

**6.13.41**  In girth six and thirty thousand, and in breadth, O sinless one, the learned and the masters of the old lore declare it six thousand.

**6.13.42**  The moon, O king, is remembered as eleven thousand in diameter, O best of the Kurus, and thirty-three thousand in circuit, fifty-nine thousand in breadth, the great cool-rayed one.

**6.13.43**  The sun is ten thousand—eight thousand and two more, O joy of the Kurus—in diameter; and thence, O king, its circuit is a full thirty thousand.

**6.13.44**  Fifty-eight thousand, O king, in breadth, O sinless one, that most exalted soaring one of light is said to be; such is the measure of the sun declared here, O Bhārata.

**6.13.45**  That Rāhu, by reason of his greatness, eclipses these two in their season, the moon and the sun, O great king; this is the account told in brief.

**6.13.46**  Thus, O great king, to you who asked, all has been told according to the truth, with the eye of the scriptures; therefore attain to peace.

**6.13.47**  This world, with its goings-forth, I have told as I have seen it; therefore take comfort, O Kaurava, concerning your son Duryodhana.

**6.13.48**  Having heard this Section of the Earth, O best of the Bharatas, pleasing to the mind, the prince becomes glorious, his purpose fulfilled, esteemed by the good; and his life, strength, valor, and splendor increase.

**6.13.49**  Whoever, O ruler of the earth, hears this on a parva-day, firm in his vows, his fathers are gladdened, and so too his forefathers.

**6.13.50**  But this region of Bhārata, wherein we dwell, holy from of old, now unfolds; all of it you have now heard.

**6.14.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then the wise Saṃjaya, son of Gavalgaṇa, having come from the battle, an eyewitness of all, knower of past, present, and to come,

**6.14.2**  approaching in haste, grieving, declared to the brooding Dhṛtarāṣṭra that Bhīṣma, the foremost among the Bharatas, had been slain.

**6.14.3**  "I am Saṃjaya, O great king; homage to you, O bull of the Bharatas. Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, the grandsire of the Bharatas, is slain.

**6.14.4**  The crest of all warriors, the abode of all who bear the bow, that grandsire of the Kurus lies today upon a bed of arrows.

**6.14.5**  He upon whose valor relying your son made bold to play at dice—he, Bhīṣma, lies slain, O king, in the fray by Śikhaṇḍin.

**6.14.6**  He who, in a great battle at the city of Kāśi, vanquished all the rulers of the earth gathered together, with a single chariot, that mighty car-warrior—

**6.14.7**  he, born of the Vasus, who fought Rāma the son of Jamadagni in combat and was not slain by the son of Jamadagni, is today slain by Śikhaṇḍin.

**6.14.8**  Like great Indra in prowess, like Himavat in steadfastness, like the ocean in depth, in forbearance the equal of the earth—

**6.14.9**  his fangs his arrows, his mouth the bow, his tongue the sword, unassailable, that man-lion, your father, has today been struck down by the Pāñcāla prince.

**6.14.10**  The great host of the Pāṇḍavas, beholding him rise up in the battle, trembled, stricken with fear, as a herd of cattle at the sight of a lion.

**6.14.11**  For ten nights, guarding your army, that destroyer of hosts, having wrought a deed most hard to do, went down like the setting sun.

**6.14.12**  He who, unshakable as Śakra, raining arrows by the thousand, slew in battle a hundred million of the warriors in ten days—

**6.14.13**  he lies groaning upon the ground like a tree felled by the wind, through your evil counsel, O king, though he deserved no such fate, O Bhārata."

**6.15.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How was Bhīṣma, the bull of the Kurus, slain by Śikhaṇḍin? How did he fall from his chariot, my father, the peer of Vāsava?

**6.15.2**  And how fared my sons, bereft of Bhīṣma, O Saṃjaya—of him so mighty, godlike, who for his elders' sake kept the vow of chastity?

**6.15.3**  When that great-souled one was slain — the mighty archer, vast in strength, the great chariot-warrior, the tiger among men — what then, tell me, was the temper of their minds?

**6.15.4**  A boundless anguish enters me, since you tell me he is slain — the bull of the Kurus, the unshakable hero, that bull among men.

**6.15.5**  Who followed after him as he advanced, and who went before him? Who held their ground, who turned back, and who pressed onward, Saṃjaya?

**6.15.6**  What heroes followed close behind that tiger among chariot-warriors, the unfaltering one, the bull among kṣatriyas, as he plunged headlong into the host of chariots?

**6.15.7**  He who, like the sun dispelling darkness, that slayer of foes scattered the enemy host — like one of a thousand rays — and struck terror into his adversaries, performing in battle a deed hard to do, at the command of the Kauravas.

**6.15.8**  As he was devouring whole armies, who hemmed him in close at hand — that accomplished and unassailable warrior, advancing straight upon them? How did the Pāṇḍavas hold back the son of Śaṃtanu in the fight?

**6.15.9**  He who hewed down the armies, swift, his fangs of arrows, his mouth gaping wide as a bow, terrible, his tongue a sword, unapproachable —

**6.15.10**  He who surpassed all other tigers among men, modest and unconquered — how did the son of Kuntī bring down that one who had never been vanquished in battle?

**6.15.11**  With his fierce bow and fierce shafts, standing upon his peerless chariot, harvesting the heads of his foes with whetted arrows —

**6.15.12**  He whom the great host of the Pāṇḍavas, beholding him rise up in the fray unassailable like the fire of doom, encircled again and again —

**6.15.13**  For ten nights that destroyer of armies harrowed my host, and then, like the sun, he set, having wrought a deed most hard to do.

**6.15.14**  He who, like Śakra, loosed an unfailing rain made of arrows, and in ten days slew a hundred million of the warriors in battle —

**6.15.15**  He now lies groaning upon the earth like a tree shattered by the wind — that descendant of Bharata, who deserved no such fate, brought low by my evil counsel.

**6.15.16**  How was the army of the Pāṇḍavas able, beholding the son of Śaṃtanu there, to strike at Bhīṣma of terrible prowess?

**6.15.17**  How did the sons of Pāṇḍu wage war against Bhīṣma, and how did Bhīṣma fail to conquer while Droṇa yet lived, Saṃjaya?

**6.15.18**  With Kṛpa close at hand there, and the son of Bharadvāja as well, how did Bhīṣma, the best of those who strike, come to his death?

**6.15.19**  And how was that supreme chariot-warrior Bhīṣma — hard for even the gods to withstand — slain in battle by the Pāñcāla, Śikhaṇḍin?

**6.15.20**  He who in battle ever rivaled the mighty son of Jamadagni, unvanquished by that son of Jamadagni, his valor the equal of Śakra's —

**6.15.21**  Tell me, Saṃjaya, of that hero Bhīṣma, slain in the fight, worthy of the strength of the greatest chariot-warriors — he without whom we know no refuge.

**6.15.22**  Which of my mighty archers did not abandon that unfaltering one, Saṃjaya? Which heroes, charged by Duryodhana, encircled him to guard him?

**6.15.23**  When all the Pāṇḍavas, with Śikhaṇḍin at their head, advanced upon Bhīṣma, did the Kurus not, in their fear, abandon that unfaltering one, Saṃjaya?

**6.15.24**  With the twang of his bowstring for thunder, his arrows for a dappling of raindrops, the great roar of his bow for its mighty voice — towering like a vast storm-cloud —

**6.15.25**  As he rained down upon the sons of Kuntī with the Pāñcālas and the Sṛñjayas, that hero smiting the enemy chariots like the wielder of the thunderbolt smiting the Dānavas —

**6.15.26**  A dreadful ocean of arrows and missiles, with shafts for its sea-monsters, unapproachable, its waves the bows, inexhaustible, in battle without an isle and without a raft, its whirlpools the maces and swords, its crocodiles the horses, its waters thronged with elephants —

**6.15.27**  He who swiftly drowned horses, elephants, foot-soldiers, and chariots in throngs upon the field, that carrier-off of the enemy's heroes —

**6.15.28**  That scorcher of foes, blazing with wrath and with splendor — what heroes encircled him as a shore holds back the home of sea-monsters?

**6.15.29**  When Bhīṣma, that slayer of foes, did his deed in battle for Duryodhana's sake, Saṃjaya — who were they that stood before him then?

**6.15.30**  Who guarded the right wheel of Bhīṣma of measureless splendor? What heroes, steadfast in their vows, held off the foe from behind?

**6.15.31**  Who took their station in front, guarding Bhīṣma close at hand? What heroes guarded the left wheel of the hero as he fought?

**6.15.32**  Stationed at the left wheel, who slew the Sṛñjayas, Saṃjaya? Who, gathered at the forefront of the ranks, guarded that unassailable one?

**6.15.33**  Who pressed forward at his flanks, following a path hard to tread? Amid the throng, who did battle against the heroes of the foe, Saṃjaya?

**6.15.34**  If he was guarded by heroes, and they in turn were sheltered by him, how did the armies of those hard to conquer not swiftly prevail in the fight?

**6.15.35**  As though against the lord of all worlds, the supreme Prajāpati — how were the Pāṇḍavas even able to strike at him, Saṃjaya?

**6.15.36**  The island upon which the Kurus relied as they fought their foes — that tiger among men, Bhīṣma, now sunk beneath the waters, you tell me is fallen, Saṃjaya.

**6.15.37**  In whose valor my son, mighty in strength, set his trust and reckoned the Pāṇḍavas as nothing — how was he slain by the foe?

**6.15.38**  He who long ago, frenzied for war, was sought as an ally by the gods with Indra at their head when they smote the Dānavas — my father, great in his vow —

**6.15.39**  At whose birth Śaṃtanu, in that son of mighty valor, blessing to the world and his father's pride, cast off sorrow, grief, and despair —

**6.15.40**  That refuge of wisdom, knower of it, devoted to the true law, pure, versed in the truth of the Vedas and their auxiliaries — how do you tell me he is slain?

**6.15.41**  Endowed with the discipline of every weapon, self-controlled, serene, high-minded — hearing that the son of Śaṃtanu is slain, I deem the rest of my army slain as well.

**6.15.42**  Unrighteousness has grown stronger than righteousness — such is my judgment — where the Pāṇḍavas, having slain their aged elder, still desire the kingdom.

**6.15.43**  Long ago Rāma, son of Jamadagni, knower of every weapon, unsurpassed, took up arms in the cause of Ambā and in the battle was vanquished by Bhīṣma.

**6.15.44**  That Bhīṣma, whose deeds were the equal of Indra's, the crest of all bowmen — you tell me he is slain. What grief can be greater than this?

**6.15.45**  He by whom hosts of kṣatriyas were again and again overcome in battle, and Rāma, son of Jamadagni, too — that slayer of the enemy's heroes —

**6.15.46**  Surely, then, in splendor, valor, and strength, Śikhaṇḍin the son of Drupada was greater than that mighty descendant of Bhṛgu, frenzied for war.

**6.15.47**  He who slew that hero — valiant, accomplished in war, skilled in every science, master of the supreme weapons, that bull of the Bharatas —
