**6.15.48**  What heroes followed that slayer of foes into the assembly of the enemy? Tell me how it came to pass — the battle of Bhīṣma with the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.15.49**  My son's army is like a woman whose hero is slain, Saṃjaya; that host of mine is like a herd of cattle scattered in panic, without a herdsman.

**6.15.50**  He whose manhood in the great battle surpassed that of all the world — when he was struck down, what then was the temper of your minds?

**6.15.51**  What strength is there in us even to live this day, Saṃjaya, having caused the death of that father of mighty valor, righteous before all the world?

**6.15.52**  Like men seeking the farther shore who see their boat foundering in fathomless water, my sons, I think, grieve sorely now that Bhīṣma is slain.

**6.15.53**  Surely my heart is made of the very substance of mountains, exceedingly hard, that, hearing the tiger among men, Bhīṣma, is slain, it does not burst asunder.

**6.15.54**  In whom — that bull of the Bharatas, unassailable — weaponry, wisdom, and statecraft were beyond all measure: how was he slain in battle?

**6.15.55**  Not by weapon, not by valor, not by austerity nor by wisdom, not by steadfastness, nor again by renunciation — by none of these is any creature delivered from death.

**6.15.56**  Surely Time is of mighty power, not to be overpassed by all the worlds, since you tell me that the son of Śaṃtanu, Bhīṣma, is slain, Saṃjaya.

**6.15.57**  Consumed by grief for my sons, brooding on this great sorrow — once I had hoped for protection from Bhīṣma, the joy of Śaṃtanu.

**6.15.58**  When Duryodhana saw the son of Śaṃtanu fallen to the earth like the sun, Saṃjaya — what did he do then?

**6.15.59**  Pondering with my mind, Saṃjaya, over my own and over the foe, I see nothing whatever remaining among the kings ranged against us.

**6.15.60**  Cruel is this duty of the kṣatriya, set forth by the seers, wherein the Pāṇḍavas, having slain the son of Śaṃtanu, still desire the kingdom.

**6.15.61**  Or it is we who desire the kingdom, having caused the grandsire's death; standing fast in the kṣatriya's duty, the sons of Pṛthā commit no wrong, my sons.

**6.15.62**  This is what a noble man must do in dire calamities, Saṃjaya: to strive valiantly with all his power — and in him that valor was firmly set.

**6.15.63**  As he was destroying the armies, modest and unconquered — how, dear one, did the sons of Pāṇḍu bring down the son of Śaṃtanu?

**6.15.64**  How were the armies arrayed, how did the great-souled ones do battle, and how was Bhīṣma, my father, slain by the foe, Saṃjaya?

**6.15.65**  And Duryodhana, and Karṇa, and Śakuni the son of Subala, and the cheat Duḥśāsana — when Bhīṣma was slain, what did they say?

**6.15.66**  That assembly strewn with the bodies of men, elephants, and horses, with arrows, spears, maces, swords, and javelins for its dice, dreadful to behold —

**6.15.67**  The foolish gamblers entered that hall of battle, hard to enter — in that fearful game played for life itself, which bulls among men cast the dice?

**6.15.68**  Who won there and who were defeated, their stakes lost, struck down — besides Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.15.69**  There is no peace for me here; hearing that Devavrata, my father of terrible deeds, has been slain in battle, sorrow has entered into me.

**6.15.70**  The great anguish rooted in my heart, brought on by my sons — you inflame it as one pouring clarified butter inflames a fire, Saṃjaya.

**6.15.71**  Beholding Bhīṣma slain — who had taken up that great burden, renowned throughout all the worlds — my sons, I think, now grieve.

**6.15.72**  I shall hear all those sorrows wrought by Duryodhana; therefore tell me everything that came to pass there, Saṃjaya.

**6.15.73**  In the battle of the lords of the earth, whatever sprang from the folly of that dullard, whether ill-counselled or well-counselled — tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.15.74**  What was done there by Bhīṣma in the battle as he sought victory — full of splendor, accomplished in arms — tell me that too, leaving nothing out.

**6.15.75**  How that battle of the armies of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas came to be — in what sequence, at what time, what happened and in what manner — tell it all.

**6.16.1**  Saṃjaya said: This question is worthy of you, great king, as befits you; yet you ought not to lay this blame upon Duryodhana.

**6.16.2**  The man who through his own ill conduct meets with misfortune ought not to lay the guilt of that sin upon another.

**6.16.3**  Great king, the man who commits among men every blameworthy deed deserves to be slain by all the world, doing as he does what is condemned.

**6.16.4**  Insult was endured by the Pāṇḍavas, deep in cunning, out of deference to you, together with their counsellors; and long indeed did they bear it patiently in the forest.

**6.16.5**  What I have seen with my own eyes of the horses, the elephants, and the heroes of measureless might, and what I have seen by the power of yoga —

**6.16.6**  Hear it, protector of the earth, and set not your mind on grief; this was surely ordained of old to come to pass thus, lord of men.

**6.16.7**  Having made obeisance to your father, the wise son of Parāśara, by whose grace I have obtained the divine and unsurpassed knowledge —

**6.16.8**  And by his grace, O king, I have gained sight that transcends the senses, hearing across great distances, knowledge of others' thoughts, and awareness of things past and yet to come.

**6.16.9**  Knowledge of births that have arisen out of due course, the power to move ever through the sky, and immunity from weapons in battle — all this from the boon of that great-souled one.

**6.16.10**  Hear from me, then, in full this wondrous tale, most marvelous and hair-raising — how the great war of the Bhāratas came to pass.

**6.16.11**  When those armies had been drawn up in array according to rule, Duryodhana, O great king, then spoke to Duḥśāsana.

**6.16.12**  "Duḥśāsana, let the chariots that guard Bhīṣma be yoked at once, and urge on all the divisions with all speed.

**6.16.13**  This clash of the Pāṇḍavas with their hosts and of the Kurus, brooded upon through many long years, has at last come upon me.

**6.16.14**  I deem no task in battle more urgent than the guarding of Bhīṣma; for, well protected, he will slay the Pārthas, the Somakas, and the Sṛñjayas with them.

**6.16.15**  And he of pure heart has said, 'I will not slay Śikhaṇḍin; for it is told that he was once a woman, and so he must be spared by me in battle.'

**6.16.16**  Therefore Bhīṣma must be guarded with especial care — such is my judgment; let all my men stand ready, bent on the slaying of Śikhaṇḍin.

**6.16.17**  Let the warriors of the East and the West, of the South and of the Northern road, skilled in every weapon and missile, guard the grandsire.

**6.16.18**  For unguarded, even a wolf may slay a lion of mighty strength; let us not have our lion slain by Śikhaṇḍin as though by a mere jackal.

**6.16.19**  Yudhāmanyu guards Phalguna's left wheel and Uttamaujas his right — these two are Phalguna's protectors, and Phalguna himself protects Śikhaṇḍin.

**6.16.20**  So act, Duḥśāsana, that Śikhaṇḍin — guarded by Pārtha and shunned by Bhīṣma — may not be able to slay the son of Gaṅgā."

**6.16.21**  Then, when the night had passed into dawn, a great clamor arose from the kings crying out, "Yoke them! Yoke them!"

**6.16.22**  With the blare of conches and kettledrums, with lion-roars, O Bhārata, with the neighing of horses and the rumble of chariot wheels,

**6.16.23**  with the trumpeting of elephants and the roaring of warriors, with shouts and the slapping of arms and battle cries — a tumult rose up on every side.

**6.16.24**  At sunrise, O great king, the whole vast host of the Kuru and Pāṇḍava armies arose, fully arrayed without exception — the forces of your sons, O lord of kings, and of the Pāṇḍavas alike.

**6.16.25**  There the elephants and chariots, adorned with gold, were seen gleaming like clouds shot through with lightning.

**6.16.26**  The masses of chariots looked like cities beyond number; and there your father shone exceedingly, like the full moon.

**6.16.27**  With bows, spears, and swords, with maces, lances, and javelins, with gleaming weapons of war, the warriors stood firm each in his own division.

**6.16.28**  Elephants, chariots, foot soldiers, and horses, O lord of peoples, stood ranged like an outspread net, in their hundreds and their thousands.

**6.16.29**  Banners of many fashions were seen raised aloft, radiant by the thousand, both of one's own side and of the foe.

**6.16.30**  Golden, their staffs bright with gems, blazing like fires, the flaming banners of the kings shone forth by the thousand.

**6.16.31**  Bright as the banners of great Indra raised in great Indra's halls; and among them those armored heroes, longing for battle, were beheld.

**6.16.32**  With weapons raised, splendid, their hand-guards bound, their quivers slung, those bull-eyed lords of men shone forth at the head of their hosts.

**6.16.33**  Śakuni the son of Subala, Śalya, the Sindhu king Jayadratha, Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, and Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja,

**6.16.34**  Śrutāyudha the Kaliṅga, the king Jayatsena, Bṛhadbala of Kosala, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas —

**6.16.35**  these ten tigers among men, heroes with arms like iron bars, commanders of full divisions, performers of sacrifices with abundant gifts,

**6.16.36**  these and many other kings and princes, wise in statecraft and of great might, followed in Duryodhana's command.

**6.16.37**  Armored, they were seen each stationed in his own division, all wearing black antelope skins and bearing banners, girt with garlands of muñja grass —

**6.16.38**  consecrated, as it were, for Duryodhana's cause and dedicated to the world of Brahmā; ten flourishing divisions stood drawn up and ready.

**6.16.39**  The eleventh, the great host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons, the mighty Kaurava army, stood before all the troops, with the son of Śaṃtanu at its head.

**6.16.40**  White-turbaned, with white horses and white armor, unshaken, we beheld Bhīṣma, O great king, like the risen moon.

**6.16.41**  The Kurus and Pāṇḍavas saw Bhīṣma, his banner a golden palm, standing in his silver chariot like the keen-rayed sun amid a white cloud.

**6.16.42**  Beholding Bhīṣma at the head of the host, the Pāṇḍavas trembled, and the mighty bowmen of the Sṛñjayas with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head.

**6.16.43**  As small beasts shrink before a great lion yawning wide, so all those led by Dhṛṣṭadyumna shuddered again and again.

**6.16.44**  These eleven divisions of yours, O Bhārata, were graced with splendor, while the Pāṇḍavas' seven were guarded by mighty men.

**6.16.45**  The two hosts looked like two oceans met together at the end of an age — their whirlpools maddened, teeming with great sea-monsters.

**6.16.46**  Never before, O king, had we seen or heard of such a gathering of assembled armies as this.

**6.17.1**  Saṃjaya said: Even as the blessed Vyāsa, Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana, had foretold, so did all the lords of earth come together, gathered as one.

**6.17.2**  On that day the moon stood in the mansion of Maghā, and the seven great planets, blazing, came together in the sky.

**6.17.3**  At its rising the sun seemed cleft in two, and the radiant orb, risen in the heavens, blazed again with a flaming crest.

**6.17.4**  And toward the blazing quarter jackals and crows cried out, hungering for the bodies, eaters of flesh and blood.

**6.17.5**  Day after day the aged Kuru grandsire and the son of Bharadvāja, rising at dawn with controlled minds, would say of the Pārthas —

**6.17.6**  "Let there be victory to the sons of Pāṇḍu" — thus those two tamers of foes spoke, yet fought for your cause, as the compact had been made.

**6.17.7**  Your father Devavrata, knowing all the niceties of dharma, gathered the kings together and spoke these words:

**6.17.8**  "O warriors, here is the great gate to heaven, flung open for you; pass through it to dwell in the world of Śakra and of Brahmā.

**6.17.9**  This is the eternal path trodden by your forefathers and those before them; honor yourselves in battle with minds undismayed.

**6.17.10**  For Nābhāga and Yayāti, Māndhātṛ, Nahuṣa, and Nṛga, made perfect by deeds such as these, attained the highest abode.

**6.17.11**  It is unrighteousness for a warrior to meet death by sickness in his home; but to fall in battle is his eternal dharma."

**6.17.12**  Thus addressed by Bhīṣma, O bull of the Bharatas, the kings marched forth, gracing their own divisions with their splendid chariots.

**6.17.13**  But that Karṇa, son of the Sun, with his counselors and kinsmen, was made by Bhīṣma to lay down his weapons in the battle, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.17.14**  Then your sons and the kings on your side, now without Karṇa, marched forth, making the ten quarters resound with their lion-roars.

**6.17.15**  With white parasols and pennons, with banners, elephants, and horses, with chariots and foot soldiers, those divisions shone in splendor.

**6.17.16**  With the sound of drums and tabors and the din of war-drums, and with the rumble of chariot wheels, the earth was set atremble.

**6.17.17**  With golden armlets and bracelets and with their bows, the great chariot-warriors were seen gleaming like moving mountains.

**6.17.18**  Bhīṣma, the lord of the Kuru host, bearing as his standard a great palm-tree with five stars, stood there like the spotless sun.

**6.17.19**  Those mighty bowmen, the kings of your side, O bull of the Bharatas, moved according to the command of the son of Śaṃtanu, O king.

**6.17.20**  And the Śaibya king Govāsana, with all his royal allies, advanced upon a kingly, pennoned lordly elephant; lotus-hued, he stood at the very front of all the divisions.

**6.17.21**  Aśvatthāman marched out, intent, his banner bearing a lion's tail; and Śrutāyu, Citrasena, Purumitra, and Viviṃśati,

**6.17.22**  Śalya, Bhūriśravas, and Vikarṇa the great chariot-warrior — these seven mighty bowmen, led by Droṇa's son, with their chariots of splendid hue, formed Bhīṣma's vanguard.

**6.17.23**  Their banners too, lofty and wrought of gold, gracing those splendid chariots, were seen shining bright.

**6.17.24**  A golden altar adorned with a water-pot, together with a bow, was the standard of Droṇa, foremost of teachers.

**6.17.25**  Drawing along a host of many hundreds of thousands, Duryodhana's great standard was an elephant fashioned of jewels.

**6.17.26**  Before him stood, in their chariots, Paurava and the Kaliṅga king, Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja, Kṣemadhanvan, and Sumitra.

**6.17.27**  And the king of Magadha marched forth in a costly chariot, his standard a bull, as though drawing the army's van after him.

**6.17.28**  Guarded by the lord of the Aṅgas and by the great-souled Kṛpa, the host of the easterners looked like a mass of autumn clouds.

**6.17.29**  Standing at the head of his division, the illustrious Jayadratha shone with his foremost standard, a silver boar.

**6.17.30**  A hundred thousand chariots obeyed his command, eight thousand elephants, and sixty thousand horsemen.

**6.17.31**  That vanguard of the army, guarded by the lord of the Sindhus, O king — a great force with chariots, elephants, and horses past counting — shone in splendor.

**6.17.32**  With sixty thousand chariots and ten thousand elephants, the lord of all the Kaliṅgas marched forth, together with Ketumat.

**6.17.33**  His great elephants, like mountains, shone forth, adorned with engines, lances, and quivers, and with pennons.

**6.17.34**  The Kaliṅga king shone with his foremost standard of a tree, with his white parasol, his golden ornament, and his yak-tail fan.

**6.17.35**  Ketumat too, mounted in battle upon an elephant with a wondrously fine goad, O king, was like the sun set within a cloud.

**6.17.36**  Blazing with splendor, mounted upon a noble elephant, King Bhagadatta marched forth like the wielder of the thunderbolt.

**6.17.37**  Riding upon the shoulders of elephants, the equals of Bhagadatta, Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti followed in Ketumat's train.

**6.17.38**  That formation, rich in chariot-troops, with elephant-bodies and a noble head, with cavalry for wings, descended fierce and struck out, facing every way.

**6.17.39**  It was ordered, O king, by Droṇa and by the royal son of Śaṃtanu, and likewise by the teacher's son, by Bāhlīka, and by Kṛpa.

**6.18.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then in a moment, O great king, a tumultuous, heart-shaking din was heard from the warriors eager to fight.

**6.18.2**  With the blare of conches and kettledrums, with the trumpeting of elephants, and with the rumble of chariot wheels, the earth itself seemed to split apart.

**6.18.3**  From the neighing of the horses and the roaring of the warriors there, in an instant the sky and all the quarters were filled with the din.

**6.18.4**  O unassailable king, the armies of your sons and of the Pāṇḍavas alike trembled as they came together against one another.

**6.18.5**  There the elephants and the chariots, adorned with gold, were seen gleaming like clouds shot through with lightning.

**6.18.6**  The banners of your men, O lord of men, fashioned in manifold shapes and ringed with golden armlets, shone like blazing fires.

**6.18.7**  Among your own and among the foe alike, O Bhārata, the great standards of Indra appeared, gleaming white as in the halls of mighty Indra himself.

**6.18.8**  Girt in golden corselets bright as fire and the sun, the heroes appeared like blazing planets.

**6.18.9**  With raised and gleaming weapons, leather-guards bound to their hands, bearing pennons, bull-eyed and mighty archers, they shone at the front of the host.

**6.18.10**  As guardians of Bhīṣma's rear, O lord of men, stood your sons—Duḥśāsana, Durviṣaha, Durmukha, and Duḥsaha as well,

**6.18.11**  Viviṃśati, Citrasena, and Vikarṇa the great chariot-warrior, Satyavrata, Purumitra, Jaya, Bhūriśravas, and Śala.

**6.18.12**  Twenty thousand chariots followed in their train—the Abhīṣāhas, the Śūrasenas, the Śibis, and the Vasātis,

**6.18.13**  the Śālvas, the Matsyas, the Ambaṣṭhas, the Trigartas and the Kekayas, the Sauvīras, the Kitavas, and the men of the East, the West, the North, and the Mālavas.

**6.18.14**  These twelve peoples, all heroes ready to lay down their lives, guarded the grandsire with a great array of chariots.

**6.18.15**  A division of ten thousand swift elephants, led by the king of Magadha, followed behind that array of chariots.

**6.18.16**  As wheel-guards for the chariots and foot-guards for the tuskers, there were in the midst of the host six myriads of hundreds.

**6.18.17**  In front marched the foot-soldiers, bearing bows, shields, and swords—many hundreds of thousands, fighting with claw-spears and javelins.

**6.18.18**  Eleven akṣauhiṇīs of your son, O Bhārata, were seen, great king, like the Gaṅgā within the bounds of the Yamunā.

**6.19.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Beholding the eleven akṣauhiṇīs arrayed, how did Yudhiṣṭhira the Pāṇḍava draw up his counter-array with so small a force?

**6.19.2**  He who knows the human battle-array and the divine, the gandharva and the demonic—how did that son of Kuntī, the Pāṇḍava, array his forces against Bhīṣma?

**6.19.3**  Saṃjaya said: Beholding the divisions of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son drawn up in array, the righteous-souled Pāṇḍava, the king of justice, spoke thus to Dhanaṃjaya:

**6.19.4**  By the word of the great seer Bṛhaspati, my dear, they teach this: let a small force fight close-massed, but let a large one spread out at will.

**6.19.5**  For the few against the many the array should be needle-pointed; and our army is far smaller than the enemy's.

**6.19.6**  "Heeding this word of the great seer, draw up the array, O Pāṇḍava." Hearing this from the king of justice, Phalguna replied:

**6.19.7**  "This array I shall set in order for you, O king—an array most hard to conquer, the unshakable one called Vajra, ordained by the wielder of the thunderbolt.

**6.19.8**  He who, like a rising wind, is unbearable to the foe in battle—Bhīma, best of strikers—shall fight in our van.

**6.19.9**  Crushing the splendor of the enemy's hosts, that best of men, skilled in the arts of war, shall go before us as our foremost leader.

**6.19.10**  At the sight of whom all the kings led by Duryodhana will turn back in panic, as small beasts before a lion.

**6.19.11**  Him we shall all take as our fearless rampart—Bhīma, best of strikers—as the immortals take refuge in the wielder of the thunderbolt.

**6.19.12**  For there is no man in the world who could bear even to look upon Vṛkodara enraged, that bull among men of most terrible deeds.

**6.19.13**  Bhīmasena, bearing his firm mace forged with the strength of the thunderbolt, ranging on with mighty speed, could dry up even the ocean."

**6.19.14**  "The Kekayas, Dhṛṣṭaketu, and the valiant Cekitāna—these stand by with their counselors as your onlookers, O lord of men,

**6.19.15**  as heirs against Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons," said Bībhatsu. And as Pārtha spoke thus, O honored one, all the troops honored him then in the battle with words of approval.

**6.19.16**  Having spoken thus, the mighty-armed Dhanaṃjaya did so; and having swiftly arrayed those forces, Phalguna then advanced.

**6.19.17**  Beholding the Kurus set in motion, the great host of the Pāṇḍavas appeared like the Gaṅgā in flood—still yet flowing on.

**6.19.18**  Their foremost were Bhīmasena and Dhṛṣṭadyumna son of Pṛṣata, Nakula and Sahadeva, and the valiant Dhṛṣṭaketu.

**6.19.19**  Then, marshaling them, the king too, encircled by an akṣauhiṇī, together with his brothers and sons, guarded the rear behind.

**6.19.20**  As wheel-guards of Bhīma stood the two sons of Mādrī of great luster, while the swift sons of Draupadī together with Subhadrā's son guarded his rear.

**6.19.21**  And Dhṛṣṭadyumna of Pāñcāla, that great chariot-warrior, was their protector, joined by the heroes of the host, the foremost charioteers, the Prabhadrakas.

**6.19.22**  Behind him came Śikhaṇḍin, guarded by Arjuna and bent upon the destruction of Bhīṣma, advancing on, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.19.23**  As Arjuna's rear-guard rode Yuyudhāna, the great chariot-warrior, and his wheel-guards were the two Pāñcālas, Yudhāmanyu and Uttamaujas.

**6.19.24**  The king Yudhiṣṭhira, son of Kuntī, stood in the central division, with huge rutting elephants that moved like moving mountains.

**6.19.25**  And the high-minded Yajñasena of Pāñcāla, with an akṣauhiṇī, valorous in the Pāṇḍavas' cause, followed Virāṭa from behind.

**6.19.26**  Their great banners, bright as sun and moon, adorned with finest gold and bearing diverse emblems, O king, stood upon the chariots.

**6.19.27**  Then, moving up from behind, Dhṛṣṭadyumna the great chariot-warrior, together with his brothers and sons, guarded Yudhiṣṭhira.

**6.19.28**  Surpassing the manifold banners upon the chariots of your men and of the foe, Arjuna's single standard, the great ape, stood high above all.

**6.19.29**  In front marched the foot-soldiers, bearing swords, spears, and lances—many hundreds of thousands, the protectors of Bhīmasena.

**6.19.30**  Ten thousand elephants, their cheeks and faces flowing with rut, gleaming in nets of gold, blazed like mountains.

**6.19.31**  Like raining clouds, wet with rut and fragrant as lotuses, they followed the king from behind like moving mountains.

**6.19.32**  Bhīmasena, brandishing his fearsome mace that resembled an iron bar, the high-minded and unassailable one, drew on that mighty host.

**6.19.33**  Him, hard to look upon as the burning, ray-wreathed sun, the warriors on every side could not bear to gaze upon at close quarters.

**6.19.34**  This was the array named Vajra, hard to break, facing every way—terrible, its banners flashing like lightning over the bow, guarded by the wielder of Gāṇḍīva.

**6.19.35**  Having arrayed it against your host, the Pāṇḍavas stood firm—an array unconquerable in the world of men, guarded by the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.19.36**  As the armies stood at the twilight hour, toward the rising of the sun a wind blew, scattering droplets, thundering though no cloud was there.

**6.19.37**  Fierce winds blew from every side, sweeping up grit low along the ground, and the dust that was raised veiled the world in darkness.

**6.19.38**  A great meteor fell, facing eastward, O bull of the Bharatas, and striking the rising sun, it burst asunder with a mighty roar.

**6.19.39**  Then, as the armies were being made ready, O bull of the Bharatas, the sun rose dim, and with a great noise the earth shook and split asunder with a roar, O best of the Bharatas.

**6.19.40**  Many thunderclaps broke out in all the quarters, O king, and a thick dust arose so that nothing at all could be discerned.

**6.19.41**  With the sudden tossing by the wind of the banners, hung with networks of little bells and wreathed in gold, and with their jangling,

**6.19.42**  those great pennoned standards, splendid as the sun, set everything ringing with their clang, as in groves of fan-palms.

**6.19.43**  Thus those tigers among men, the Pāṇḍavas, exulting in battle, stood arrayed against your son's host.

**6.19.44**  The very marrow of the warriors seemed to melt away, O bull of the Bharatas, at the sight of Bhīmasena stationed in front, mace in hand.

**6.20.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: At sunrise, Saṃjaya, which were the first, eager to fight and seeming to rejoice—my men led by Bhīṣma upon the field, or the Pāṇḍavas led by Bhīma at that hour?

**6.20.2**  To whose rear were moon, sun, and wind? At whose army did the beasts of prey howl? Whose young men's faces were serene? Tell me all this truly, as it was.

**6.20.3**  Saṃjaya said: Both armies advanced as if matched, O lord of men; both arrays wore an exultant aspect; both were splendid, gleaming like rows of forest, and both were filled with elephants, chariots, and horses.

**6.20.4**  Both armies were vast and fearsome in form, O Bhārata, and both alike unbearable; both were marshaled for the winning of heaven, and both were guarded by good and noble men.

**6.20.5**  The Kurus, the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, stood facing westward; the Pārthas, ready to fight, faced east. The host of the Kauravas was like the army of the lord of the Daityas, and the host of the Pāṇḍavas like the army of the lord of the gods.

**6.20.6**  A clear wind was at the back of the Pāṇḍavas; at the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra the beasts of prey howled. The pungent rut-scent of the lordly elephants your son's elephants could not endure.

**6.20.7**  Duryodhana, mounted on a lotus-hued elephant, girt with golden girths, of noble strength and flowing with rut, stood in the midst of the Kurus, extolled by bards and panegyrists.

**6.20.8**  His white parasol shone with the radiance of the moon, and a golden wreath gleamed upon his head; on every side Śakuni, king of Gāndhāra, guarded him with his hillmen and Gāndhāras.

**6.20.9**  At the head of the whole army stood the aged Bhīṣma, with white parasol, white bow, and conch, white-turbaned, with a pale banner and white horses, gleaming like a mountain of white.

**6.20.10**  His army comprised all the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, one portion of the Bāhlīkas and Śala, the Ambaṣṭha warriors and the Kṣatriyas dwelling by the Sindhu, and the valiant Sauvīras and men of the Five Rivers.

**6.20.11**  With his blood-red horses and golden chariot, the great-souled, mighty-armed Droṇa of undaunted spirit—the teacher, renowned among all kings—stood guarding the host from the rear like Indra.

**6.20.12**  In the midst of the whole army stood the son of Vṛddhakṣatra, with Bhūriśravas, Purumitra, and Jaya; and the Śālvas, Matsyas, and all the Kekaya brothers stood ready to fight with their elephant-divisions.

**6.20.13**  The great-souled son of Śaradvat, the mighty archer of Gautama's line, skilled in varied modes of war, held the northern flank and guarded the host from the north with Śakas, Kirātas, Yavanas, and Pahlavas.

**6.20.14**  With the great chariot-warriors of the Andhakas, Vṛṣṇis, and Bhojas, with the Saurāṣṭrakas and Nairṛtas, weapons in hand, Kṛtavarman of mighty strength, well-guarded, protected your force from the south.

**6.20.15**  A myriad chariots of the saṃśaptakas, sworn either to die or to conquer Arjuna, were sent forth; and wherever Arjuna was, there, O king, those weapon-skilled and valiant Trigartas advanced.

**6.20.16**  A hundred thousand elephants and more were yours, O Bhārata; for every elephant a hundred chariots, and for every chariot a hundred horses,

**6.20.17**  for every horse ten bowmen, and for every bowman ten shield-bearers: thus were the divisions arrayed by Bhīṣma for you, O Bhārata.

**6.20.18**  Day after day as it came, Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu and foremost of leaders, set up the array—now human, now divine, now gandharva, now demonic.

**6.20.19**  Vast with its flood of great chariot-warriors, like the sea at the turn of the moon, the array of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons, set up by Bhīṣma, faced westward in the battle.

**6.20.20**  Boundless in form was your banner-host, O lord of men, and fearsome—yet not so the Pāṇḍavas'. Still, theirs I deem the mighty and unassailable one, whose leaders are Keśava and Arjuna.

**6.21.1**  Saṃjaya said: Beholding the vast army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons drawn up for battle, King Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Kuntī, was overcome with dejection.

**6.21.2**  Seeing the impenetrable battle-array marshalled by Bhīṣma, and perceiving it to be as if unbreakable, the son of Pāṇḍu, downcast, spoke to Arjuna.

**6.21.3**  O Dhanaṃjaya, mighty-armed one, how shall we be able to fight in battle against the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, whose champion is the grandsire himself?

**6.21.4**  This array, unshakable and impenetrable, has been formed by Bhīṣma, scourge of foes, of abundant splendour, according to the method ordained in the science of war.

**6.21.5**  We, with our troops, are plunged into doubt, O scourge of foes. How shall there be escape for us from this mighty array?

**6.21.6**  Then Arjuna, slayer of foes, looking upon the army of yours, O king, addressed the dejected son of Pṛthā, Yudhiṣṭhira.

**6.21.7**  By that means whereby the few overcome even the many heroes, men superior in wisdom and endowed with virtues—understand it, O lord of the people.

**6.21.8**  I shall declare to you that cause, O king, since you bear no malice. The sage Nārada knows it, and so do Bhīṣma and Droṇa, O son of Pāṇḍu.

**6.21.9**  Resting upon this very truth, the Grandsire, long ago, in the war between the gods and the demons, spoke to the dwellers of heaven led by mighty Indra.

**6.21.10**  Those who seek to conquer do not win victory so much by strength and prowess as by truth and compassion, by righteousness and by exertion.

**6.21.11**  Casting off unrighteousness, greed, and delusion, and taking your stand upon exertion, fight without arrogance; for where there is righteousness, there is victory.

**6.21.12**  Know this, O king: certain is our victory in battle. For as Nārada has told me, where Kṛṣṇa is, there is victory.

**6.21.13**  Victory is an attribute inherent in Kṛṣṇa; it follows close behind Mādhava. And conquest is his besides, while humility is yet another of his virtues.

**6.21.14**  Govinda, of boundless splendour, is untroubled amid hosts of enemies; he is the most ancient Person. Where Kṛṣṇa is, there is victory.

**6.21.15**  For of old, becoming Hari, the dweller in Vaikuṇṭha, whose shafts are never blunted, thundering forth amid gods and demons, he asked: 'Who shall be victorious?'

**6.21.16**  By those who said, 'Following Kṛṣṇa may we conquer,' victory was won; for by his grace the three worlds were gained by Śakra and the other gods.

**6.21.17**  For you, O Bhārata, whose victory the all-consuming lord of the gods himself desires, I see no cause whatever for distress.

**6.22.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then King Yudhiṣṭhira urged on his own army, drawing up its divisions to counter those of Bhīṣma, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.22.2**  The Pāṇḍavas, those upholders of the Kuru line, arrayed their divisions as appointed, longing for the supreme heaven to be won through noble battle.

**6.22.3**  In the centre, Śikhaṇḍin's division was guarded by Savyasācin, and that of Dhṛṣṭadyumna was protected by Bhīma himself.

**6.22.4**  The right wing, O king, was guarded by Yuyudhāna, the glorious foremost of the Sātvatas, like Śakra wielding the bow.

**6.22.5**  Yudhiṣṭhira mounted his chariot, like the very car of great Indra, furnished with all its equipment, bright with gold and gems, its harness and trappings of gold, taking his place in the midst of the host of elephants.

**6.22.6**  Held high above him, his snow-white parasol with ribs of ivory shone exceedingly fair, and the great sages, circling that lord of men reverently, attended him with hymns of praise.

**6.22.7**  The household priests, aged great sages versed in sacred lore, pronouncing the doom of the foe, performed on every side the rites of benediction with muttered prayers, with sacred verses, and with healing herbs.

**6.22.8**  Then that high-souled foremost of the Kurus, bestowing upon the brāhmaṇas garments and cattle, fruits and flowers and pieces of gold, went forth like Śakra among the immortals.

**6.22.9**  Bright as a thousand suns, hung with a hundred little bells, adorned with the most precious river-born gold, the chariot of Arjuna, garlanded with flame like fire itself, blazed forth, drawn by white steeds and shod with goodly wheels.

**6.22.10**  Mounted upon it, ape-bannered, the reins held by Keśava, with Gāṇḍīva and shafts in hand, stood the bowman whose equal on earth there is not, nor shall ever be at any time.

**6.22.11**  About to overthrow the army of your son, he wore a form most terrible—he who, fair-armed and weaponless, could with his bare arms reduce men, horses, and elephants to ashes in battle.

**6.22.12**  That Bhīmasena, Vṛkodara, together with the twins, was the guardian of the heroes' chariot-host; beholding him sporting like a maddened bull or a lion, peerless in the world and the equal of great Indra—

**6.22.13**  —gazing upon Vṛkodara, unassailable, at the army's van, proud as a king of elephants, your warriors trembled, their spirits broken with fear, like camels mired in mud.

**6.22.14**  Then Janārdana spoke to the prince standing in the midst of the army, the unassailable, the best of the Bharatas, Guḍākeśa.

**6.22.15**  Vāsudeva said: This guardian, blazing in his strength, who eyes our army like a lion—this is Bhīṣma, the banner of the Kuru race, by whom thirty horse-sacrifices were performed.

**6.22.16**  These divisions hide the mighty one as clouds conceal the hot-rayed sun. Having destroyed these, O foremost of men, then seek your battle with the bull of the Bharatas.

**6.22.17**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: On which side, Saṃjaya, did the warriors at the front fight gladly? Who there were high of heart, and who were dispirited and bewildered?

**6.22.18**  Who struck first there in that heart-shaking battle—my men or the Pāṇḍavas? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.22.19**  In whose gathered army arose the fragrance of perfumes and garlands, and the auspicious words of the warriors as they roared aloud?

**6.22.20**  Saṃjaya said: In both armies there the warriors rejoiced with gladness, and on both sides arose the scents of garlands, incense, and drink.

**6.22.21**  As the close-massed, arrayed divisions, surging and swelling, crept forward, O bull of the Bharatas, the clash between them grew immense.

**6.22.22**  Tumultuous was the din of the instruments, mingled with conches and kettledrums, with the trumpeting of elephants and the exultant shouts of the troops.

**6.23.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: On the field of righteousness, on Kurukṣetra, gathered together and eager to fight, what did my men and the Pāṇḍavas do, O Saṃjaya?

**6.23.2**  Saṃjaya said: But King Duryodhana, beholding the Pāṇḍava army drawn up in array, then approached his teacher and spoke these words.

**6.23.3**  Behold, O teacher, this mighty host of the sons of Pāṇḍu, arrayed by the son of Drupada, your wise disciple.

**6.23.4**  Here are heroes, mighty archers, equal in battle to Bhīma and Arjuna: Yuyudhāna and Virāṭa, and Drupada the great chariot-warrior;

**6.23.5**  Dhṛṣṭaketu, Cekitāna, and the valiant king of Kāśi; Purujit, Kuntibhoja, and Śaibya, a bull among men;

**6.23.6**  Yudhāmanyu the bold, and the valiant Uttamaujas; the son of Subhadrā and the sons of Draupadī—all of them great chariot-warriors.

**6.23.7**  But of our own who are the most distinguished, mark them, O best of the twice-born: the leaders of my army; for your knowledge I name them to you.

**6.23.8**  Your honoured self, and Bhīṣma, and Karṇa, and Kṛpa, ever victorious in battle; Aśvatthāman and Vikarṇa, and likewise the son of Somadatta;

**6.23.9**  and many other heroes besides, who have laid down their lives for my sake, armed with manifold weapons, all skilled in war.

**6.23.10**  Boundless is this force of ours, guarded by Bhīṣma, whereas the force of theirs, guarded by Bhīma, is limited.

**6.23.11**  Therefore, stationed each in his proper place at all the points of access, let all of you, every one, guard Bhīṣma above all.

**6.23.12**  Then, kindling his joy, the aged Kuru grandsire, the glorious one, roaring loud like a lion, blew his conch.

**6.23.13**  Thereupon conches and kettledrums, tabors and drums and horns were sounded all at once, and the noise of it was tumultuous.

**6.23.14**  Then, standing in their great chariot yoked with white steeds, Mādhava and the son of Pāṇḍu blew their divine conches.

**6.23.15**  Hṛṣīkeśa blew Pāñcajanya, Dhanaṃjaya blew Devadatta, and Vṛkodara of dreadful deeds blew the great conch Pauṇḍra.

**6.23.16**  King Yudhiṣṭhira, son of Kuntī, blew Anantavijaya, and Nakula and Sahadeva blew Sughoṣa and Maṇipuṣpaka.

**6.23.17**  The king of Kāśi, that supreme archer, and Śikhaṇḍin the great chariot-warrior, Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Virāṭa, and the unvanquished Sātyaki;

**6.23.18**  Drupada and the sons of Draupadī, on every side, O lord of the earth, and the mighty-armed son of Subhadrā—each blew his several conch.

**6.23.19**  That tumultuous din, making sky and earth resound, rent the hearts of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons.

**6.23.20**  Then, beholding Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons drawn up in their ranks, as the clash of weapons was about to begin, the ape-bannered son of Pāṇḍu raised his bow

**6.23.21**  and then spoke this word to Hṛṣīkeśa, O lord of the earth: 'Set my chariot, O Acyuta, between the two armies,

**6.23.22**  that I may look upon these who stand here eager for battle, and see with whom I must do combat in this enterprise of war.

**6.23.23**  I would behold those who are gathered here ready to fight, seeking to do service in war to the evil-minded son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.'

**6.23.24**  Thus addressed by Guḍākeśa, O Bhārata, Hṛṣīkeśa, having stationed that finest of chariots between the two armies

**6.23.25**  before Bhīṣma and Droṇa and all the lords of the earth, said: 'O Pārtha, behold these Kurus gathered together.'

**6.23.26**  There the son of Pṛthā saw standing fathers and grandfathers, teachers, maternal uncles, brothers, sons, grandsons, and comrades too;

**6.23.27**  fathers-in-law and well-wishers as well, in both the armies. Beholding all these kinsmen arrayed there, the son of Kuntī

**6.23.28**  was filled with deep compassion, and in dejection spoke this: 'Seeing these my own people, O Kṛṣṇa, drawn up and longing to fight,

**6.23.29**  my limbs give way and my mouth grows parched, my body trembles and my hair stands on end.

**6.23.30**  Gāṇḍīva slips from my hand, and my very skin is aflame; I cannot stand firm, and my mind seems to reel.

**6.23.31**  And I behold omens of evil import, O Keśava; nor do I foresee any good in slaying my own kinsmen in battle.

**6.23.32**  I crave not victory, O Kṛṣṇa, nor kingship, nor pleasures. What is kingship to us, O Govinda, what enjoyments, or even life itself?

**6.23.33**  Those for whose sake we desire kingship, enjoyments, and pleasures—these very ones stand here in battle, having renounced their lives and their wealth:

**6.23.34**  teachers, fathers, sons, and likewise grandfathers, maternal uncles, fathers-in-law, grandsons, brothers-in-law, and kinsmen besides.

**6.23.35**  These I would not wish to slay, O slayer of Madhu, even though they slay me—no, not for the sovereignty of the three worlds, how much less for the earth!

**6.23.36**  By killing the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, what joy could be ours, O Janārdana? Only sin would cling to us were we to slay these, though they be aggressors.

**6.23.37**  Therefore we ought not to slay the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra together with their kin; for having killed our own people, how could we be happy, O Mādhava?

**6.23.38**  Even though these, their minds overcome by greed, see no fault in the ruin of the family, no crime in treachery to friends,

**6.23.39**  how should we not know to turn back from this sin, O Janārdana, we who clearly see the fault in the ruin of the family?

**6.23.40**  In the ruin of the family, the eternal laws of the family perish; and when the law has perished, lawlessness overwhelms the whole family.

**6.23.41**  From the prevalence of lawlessness, O Kṛṣṇa, the women of the family are corrupted; and when the women are corrupted, O scion of Vṛṣṇi, there arises confusion of castes.

**6.23.42**  Such confusion leads only to hell, both for the destroyers of the family and for the family itself; for their ancestors fall, deprived of the offerings of rice and water.

**6.23.43**  By these misdeeds of the destroyers of the family, which bring about the confusion of castes, the eternal laws of caste and the eternal laws of the family are uprooted.

**6.23.44**  And for those men whose family laws have been destroyed, O Janārdana, a dwelling in hell is ordained — so we have heard handed down.

**6.23.45**  Alas! We are resolved to commit a great sin, in that, out of greed for the pleasures of kingship, we stand ready to slay our own kin.

**6.23.46**  If the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, weapons in hand, were to slay me in battle, unresisting and unarmed, that would be the more blessed end for me.

**6.23.47**  Having spoken thus on the field of battle, Arjuna sank down upon the seat of the chariot, casting aside his bow and arrows, his mind overwhelmed with grief.

**6.24.1**  Saṃjaya said: To him thus overcome by pity, his troubled eyes brimming with tears, despondent, the Slayer of Madhu spoke these words.

**6.24.2**  The Blessed Lord said: Whence has this faintheartedness come upon you in this hour of peril, O Arjuna — a thing unworthy of the noble, barring the way to heaven, bringing dishonor?

**6.24.3**  Yield not to unmanliness, O Pārtha; it does not become you. Cast off this base weakness of heart and arise, scorcher of foes!

**6.24.4**  Arjuna said: How, O Slayer of Madhu, shall I fight back with arrows in battle against Bhīṣma and Droṇa, who are worthy of my worship, O Slayer of foes?

**6.24.5**  For it were better in this world to eat even the bread of beggary than to slay these noble-souled elders. If I slew my elders, though they covet gain, I should taste, here and now, only delights smeared with blood.

**6.24.6**  Nor do we know which is the better for us — that we should conquer them, or that they should conquer us. Those whom if we slew we should not wish to live — the very sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra — stand arrayed against us.

**6.24.7**  My very nature undone by the taint of pitiable weakness, my mind bewildered as to what is right, I ask you: tell me with certainty what is the better course. I am your disciple; instruct me, who have taken refuge in you.

**6.24.8**  For I do not see what could dispel this grief that withers my senses, even were I to win on earth an unrivaled and prosperous kingdom, or even lordship over the gods.

**6.24.9**  Saṃjaya said: Having spoken thus to Hṛṣīkeśa, Guḍākeśa, the scorcher of foes, said to Govinda, "I shall not fight," and fell silent.

**6.24.10**  To him, O descendant of Bharata, as he sat despondent between the two armies, Hṛṣīkeśa spoke these words, as if with a smile.

**6.24.11**  The Blessed Lord said: You have grieved for those who should not be grieved for, yet you speak words of wisdom. The wise grieve neither for the dead nor for the living.

**6.24.12**  Never was there a time when I was not, nor you, nor these lords of men; nor shall any of us ever cease to be hereafter.

**6.24.13**  As the embodied soul passes, within this body, through childhood, youth, and old age, so does it pass to another body. The steadfast are not bewildered by this.

**6.24.14**  The contacts of the senses with their objects, O son of Kuntī, give rise to cold and heat, pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent; endure them, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.24.15**  The man whom these do not disturb, O bull among men — the steadfast one, alike in pain and pleasure — he is fit for immortality.

**6.24.16**  Of the unreal there is no being; of the real there is no ceasing to be. The truth of both has been perceived by the seers of reality.

**6.24.17**  Know that to be indestructible by which all this is pervaded. None can bring about the destruction of this imperishable one.

**6.24.18**  These bodies of the eternal embodied soul are said to come to an end, while it is indestructible and immeasurable. Therefore fight, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.24.19**  He who thinks this one to be a slayer, and he who thinks it slain — neither of them understands. It neither slays nor is slain.

**6.24.20**  It is never born, nor does it ever die; having come to be, it shall not again cease to be. Unborn, eternal, everlasting, ancient, it is not slain when the body is slain.

**6.24.21**  He who knows this one to be indestructible, eternal, unborn, and undiminishing — how, O Pārtha, can such a man cause anyone to be slain, or slay anyone?

**6.24.22**  As a man casts off worn-out garments and takes on others that are new, so the embodied soul casts off worn-out bodies and enters into others that are new.

**6.24.23**  Weapons do not cut it, fire does not burn it, waters do not wet it, nor does the wind wither it.

**6.24.24**  It cannot be cut, it cannot be burned, it cannot be wetted, nor can it be dried. Eternal, all-pervading, fixed, unmoving, it is everlasting.

**6.24.25**  It is said to be unmanifest, unthinkable, and unchanging. Therefore, knowing it to be such, you ought not to grieve.

**6.24.26**  And even if you should think it to be ever born and ever dying, even then, O mighty-armed one, you ought not to grieve for it.

**6.24.27**  For of one that is born, death is certain; and of one that is dead, birth is certain. Therefore, over a thing that cannot be averted, you ought not to grieve.

**6.24.28**  Beings are unmanifest in their beginnings, manifest in their middle states, and unmanifest again at their end, O descendant of Bharata. What occasion, then, for lament?

**6.24.29**  One beholds it as a marvel; another likewise speaks of it as a marvel; and another hears of it as a marvel; yet even having heard, no one truly knows it.

**6.24.30**  This embodied soul, dwelling in the body of every being, is eternally inviolable, O descendant of Bharata. Therefore you ought not to grieve for any creature.

**6.24.31**  And looking to your own dharma, you ought not to waver; for there is no greater good for a warrior than a righteous battle.

**6.24.32**  Happy are the warriors, O Pārtha, to whom comes unsought such a battle, an open gate to heaven.

**6.24.33**  But if you will not wage this righteous war, then, forsaking your own dharma and your honor, you shall incur sin.

**6.24.34**  People, too, will tell of your undying disgrace; and for one held in honor, dishonor is worse than death.

**6.24.35**  The great warriors will think that you withdrew from the battle out of fear; and you who were once highly esteemed by them will fall into contempt.

**6.24.36**  And your enemies will speak many unspeakable words, scorning your prowess. What could be more painful than that?

**6.24.37**  Either slain, you shall attain heaven, or victorious, you shall enjoy the earth. Therefore arise, O son of Kuntī, resolved upon battle.

**6.24.38**  Holding pleasure and pain alike, gain and loss, victory and defeat, gird yourself then for battle; thus you shall incur no sin.

**6.24.39**  This wisdom has been declared to you according to Sāṃkhya; now hear it as it pertains to yoga, by which understanding, O Pārtha, once joined with it, you shall cast off the bondage of action.

**6.24.40**  In this path no effort is ever lost, and no obstacle arises; even a little of this dharma saves one from great fear.

**6.24.41**  Here the resolute understanding is one and single, O joy of the Kurus; but the thoughts of the irresolute are many-branched and endless.

**6.24.42**  The flowery speech that the unwise utter, O Pārtha — those who delight in the letter of the Veda, declaring that there is nothing more —

**6.24.43**  full of desire, intent on heaven, promising rebirth as the fruit of works, thronged with manifold rites directed toward enjoyment and power —

**6.24.44**  in those attached to enjoyment and power, whose minds are carried away by that speech, the resolute understanding fixed in contemplation is not established.

**6.24.45**  The Vedas have the three guṇas for their province; be free of the three guṇas, O Arjuna. Be free of the pairs of opposites, ever poised in purity, indifferent to acquisition and possession, master of yourself.

**6.24.46**  As much use as there is in a well when water floods on every side, so much is there in all the Vedas for the brāhmaṇa who truly knows.

**6.24.47**  Your right is to the work alone, never to its fruits. Let not the fruit of action be your motive, nor let your attachment be to inaction.

**6.24.48**  Established in yoga, perform your actions, abandoning attachment, O Dhanaṃjaya, alike in success and in failure; for this evenness of mind is called yoga.

**6.24.49**  For action is far inferior to the yoga of understanding, O Dhanaṃjaya. Seek refuge in understanding; pitiable are those who act for the sake of fruits.

**6.24.50**  He who is joined to understanding casts off, here in this life, both good deeds and evil deeds. Therefore devote yourself to yoga; yoga is skill in action.

**6.24.51**  For the wise, joined to understanding, abandoning the fruit born of action, are freed from the bondage of birth and reach the state beyond all ill.

**6.24.52**  When your understanding crosses beyond the thicket of delusion, then you shall grow indifferent to all that has been heard and all that is yet to be heard.

**6.24.53**  When your understanding, bewildered by scripture, shall stand unmoving and unwavering, fixed in contemplation, then you shall attain to yoga.

**6.24.54**  Arjuna said: What is the description of the man of steadfast wisdom, who abides in contemplation, O Keśava? How does the man of steady mind speak, how does he sit, how does he walk?

**6.24.55**  The Blessed Lord said: When a man casts off all the desires that come into his mind, O Pārtha, content in the Self by the Self alone, then is he called a man of steadfast wisdom.

**6.24.56**  He whose mind is untroubled amid sorrows, who has no craving for pleasures, and from whom longing, fear, and anger have departed — he is called a sage of steady mind.

**6.24.57**  He who is without attachment on every side, who, meeting with whatever is good or evil, neither rejoices nor recoils — his wisdom stands firm.

**6.24.58**  And when, like a tortoise drawing in its limbs from every side, he withdraws his senses from the objects of sense, his wisdom stands firm.

**6.24.59**  The objects of sense turn away from the embodied one who abstains from them, yet the relish for them remains; but even that relish departs once he has beheld the Supreme.

**6.24.60**  For even in the man of discernment who strives, O son of Kuntī, the turbulent senses carry away the mind by force.

**6.24.61**  Having restrained all these, let him sit, steadfast in yoga, intent on Me; for he whose senses are mastered — his wisdom stands firm.

**6.24.62**  In the man who dwells in thought upon the objects of sense, attachment to them is born; from attachment springs desire, and from desire arises anger.

**6.24.63**  From anger comes delusion, from delusion the wandering of memory; from the loss of memory comes the ruin of understanding, and from the ruin of understanding he perishes.

**6.24.64**  But the man of disciplined self, moving among the objects of sense with senses freed from longing and aversion and brought under his own control, attains to serenity.

**6.24.65**  In that serenity there arises for him the cessation of all his sorrows; for in the man of serene mind the understanding soon becomes firmly established.

**6.24.66**  There is no understanding for the undisciplined, nor for the undisciplined any power of contemplation; and for him without contemplation there is no peace — and for the peaceless, whence happiness?

**6.24.67**  For when the mind yields to the roving senses, it carries away his wisdom, as the wind carries away a ship upon the waters.

**6.24.68**  Therefore, O mighty-armed one, he whose senses are wholly restrained from the objects of sense — his wisdom stands firm.

**6.24.69**  In that which is night for all beings, the man of self-restraint is awake; and that in which all beings are awake is night for the sage who sees.

**6.24.70**  As the waters enter the ocean, which, ever filling, abides unmoved, so he into whom all desires enter attains peace — not he who lusts after desires.

**6.24.71**  The man who, abandoning all desires, moves about free from longing, without the sense of "mine" and without egoism — he attains to peace.

**6.24.72**  This is the state of Brahman, O Pārtha; attaining it, none is deluded. Abiding in it even at the hour of death, one reaches the nirvāṇa of Brahman.

**6.25.1**  Arjuna said: If you hold understanding to be better than action, O Janārdana, then why do you urge me to this terrible deed, O Keśava?

**6.25.2**  With words that seem confused, you bewilder, as it were, my understanding. Tell me, therefore, with certainty, the one path by which I may attain the higher good.

**6.25.3**  The Blessed Lord said: In this world a twofold path was taught of old by me, O blameless one — that of knowledge for the followers of Sāṃkhya, and that of action for those devoted to yoga.

**6.25.4**  Not by abstaining from actions does a man attain freedom from action, nor by renunciation alone does he reach perfection.

**6.25.5**  For no one ever, even for a moment, remains without performing action; everyone is driven to act, helplessly, by the qualities born of material nature.

**6.25.6**  He who, restraining the organs of action, sits dwelling in his mind upon the objects of sense — that deluded soul is called a hypocrite.

**6.25.7**  But he who, restraining the senses with the mind, O Arjuna, undertakes through the organs of action the yoga of action, unattached — he excels.

**6.25.8**  Perform your appointed action, for action is better than inaction; even the maintenance of your body could not be accomplished without action.

**6.25.9**  Save for action performed for the sake of sacrifice, this world is bound by action; therefore, O son of Kuntī, perform action for that purpose, free from attachment.

**6.25.10**  Having of old created mankind together with sacrifice, the Lord of Creatures said: By this shall you bring forth offspring; let this be the cow that yields all your desires.

**6.25.11**  By this nourish the gods, and let the gods nourish you; nourishing one another, you shall attain the highest good.

**6.25.12**  For the gods, nourished by sacrifice, will grant you the enjoyments you desire; but he who enjoys their gifts without offering anything in return to them is verily a thief.

**6.25.13**  The good, who eat the remnants of sacrifice, are freed from all sins; but the wicked, who cook only for their own sake, partake of evil.

**6.25.14**  From food creatures come into being; from rain food is produced; from sacrifice arises rain; and sacrifice is born of action.

**6.25.15**  Know that action springs from Brahman, and Brahman springs from the Imperishable; therefore the all-pervading Brahman is ever established in sacrifice.

**6.25.16**  He who in this world does not keep turning the wheel thus set in motion lives in vain, O Pārtha — a man of sinful life, delighting in the senses.

**6.25.17**  But the man whose delight is in the Self alone, who is content in the Self, and satisfied within the Self — for him there exists no duty to be done.

**6.25.18**  For him there is no purpose whatever in what is done, nor any in what is left undone; nor has he any need to depend upon any being among all creatures.

**6.25.19**  Therefore, ever unattached, perform the action that ought to be done; for performing action without attachment, a man attains the Supreme.

**6.25.20**  For by action alone did Janaka and others attain perfection; and with regard to the welfare of the world too, you ought to act.

**6.25.21**  Whatever a great man does, that very thing other people do; whatever standard he sets, the world follows it.

**6.25.22**  For me, O Pārtha, there is nothing whatever to be done in the three worlds, nothing unattained that need be attained; yet I remain engaged in action.

**6.25.23**  For if I did not remain ever tirelessly engaged in action, men would follow my path in every way, O Pārtha.

**6.25.24**  These worlds would fall to ruin if I did not perform action; I would be the author of confusion and would destroy these creatures.

**6.25.25**  As the ignorant act, attached to action, O Bhārata, so should the wise man act, but unattached, desiring the welfare of the world.

**6.25.26**  Let him not unsettle the understanding of the ignorant who are attached to action; the wise man, himself disciplined, should engage in all actions and so encourage them to do likewise.

**6.25.27**  Actions are in every way performed by the qualities of material nature; but he whose soul is deluded by egoism thinks, "I am the doer."

**6.25.28**  But he who knows the truth, O mighty-armed, about the divisions of the qualities and of action, recognizing that it is the qualities that act upon the qualities, does not become attached.

**6.25.29**  Those deluded by the qualities of material nature become attached to the actions of those qualities; but let not the man of complete knowledge unsettle those dull ones of incomplete knowledge.

**6.25.30**  Surrendering all actions to me, with your mind fixed on the Supreme Self, and becoming free from longing and from the sense of "mine," fight, your fever gone.

**6.25.31**  Those men who constantly follow this teaching of mine, full of faith and free from cavil, they too are released from the bondage of actions.

**6.25.32**  But those who, cavilling at it, do not follow this teaching of mine — know them to be deluded in all knowledge, mindless, and lost.

**6.25.33**  Even the man of knowledge acts in accordance with his own nature; beings follow their nature — what can restraint accomplish?

**6.25.34**  In the object of each sense, attraction and aversion are seated; let none come under their sway, for they are his two waylayers.

**6.25.35**  Better is one's own duty, though imperfectly done, than the duty of another well performed; better is death in one's own duty — the duty of another is fraught with danger.

**6.25.36**  Arjuna said: But impelled by what does a man commit sin, O Vārṣṇeya, even against his will, as if driven by force?

**6.25.37**  The Blessed Lord said: It is desire, it is wrath, born of the quality of passion, all-devouring and most sinful — know this to be the enemy here.

**6.25.38**  As fire is obscured by smoke, and a mirror by dust, as an embryo is enveloped by the womb's membrane, so is this enveloped by that.

**6.25.39**  Knowledge is enveloped by this constant enemy of the wise, O son of Kuntī — in the form of desire, an insatiable fire.

**6.25.40**  The senses, the mind, and the intellect are said to be its seat; through these it deludes the embodied one, veiling his knowledge.

**6.25.41**  Therefore, O bull among the Bharatas, first restraining the senses, cast off this sinful thing, the destroyer of knowledge and discrimination.

**6.25.42**  The senses, they say, are great; greater than the senses is the mind; greater than the mind is the intellect; but greater than the intellect is He.

**6.25.43**  Thus knowing Him who is beyond the intellect, and steadying the self by the Self, slay, O mighty-armed, the enemy in the form of desire, hard to overcome.

**6.26.1**  The Blessed Lord said: This imperishable yoga I declared to Vivasvat; Vivasvat told it to Manu; and Manu spoke it to Ikṣvāku.

**6.26.2**  Thus received in succession, the royal sages knew it; but through the long lapse of time that yoga was lost here, O scorcher of foes.

**6.26.3**  That very same ancient yoga has today been declared by me to you, for you are my devotee and my friend; and this is the supreme secret.

**6.26.4**  Arjuna said: Later was your birth, earlier the birth of Vivasvat; how then am I to understand this — that you declared it in the beginning?

**6.26.5**  The Blessed Lord said: Many are the births that have passed for me and for you, O Arjuna; all of them I know, but you do not know them, O scorcher of foes.

**6.26.6**  Though I am unborn and of imperishable Self, though I am the Lord of all beings, yet, governing my own material nature, I come into being through my own power of illusion.

**6.26.7**  For whenever there is a decline of righteousness, O Bhārata, and a rising up of unrighteousness, then I send forth myself.

**6.26.8**  For the protection of the good, for the destruction of the wicked, and for the firm establishment of righteousness, I come into being age after age.

**6.26.9**  He who thus knows in truth my divine birth and action, on leaving the body is not born again, but comes to me, O Arjuna.

**6.26.10**  Freed from passion, fear, and wrath, absorbed in me and taking refuge in me, many, purified by the austerity of knowledge, have come to my state of being.

**6.26.11**  In whatever way men approach me, in that same way do I favor them; in every way, O Pārtha, men follow my path.

**6.26.12**  Desiring the fruition of their actions, men here worship the gods; for swiftly in the world of men comes the success born of action.

**6.26.13**  The fourfold order was created by me according to the divisions of quality and action; though I am its author, know me to be the non-doer, the changeless.

**6.26.14**  Actions do not taint me, nor have I any longing for the fruit of action; he who knows me thus is not bound by actions.

**6.26.15**  Knowing this, the ancient seekers of liberation also performed action; therefore do you too perform action, as it was done by those of old in former times.

**6.26.16**  What is action, what is inaction? — even the wise are bewildered here. I shall declare to you that action, knowing which you shall be freed from evil.

**6.26.17**  For the nature of action must be understood, and the nature of wrong action must be understood, and the nature of inaction too must be understood; profound is the course of action.

**6.26.18**  He who sees inaction in action, and action in inaction, is wise among men; he is disciplined, a doer of all action.

**6.26.19**  He whose every undertaking is free from the prompting of desire, whose actions are burned up in the fire of knowledge — him the wise call a sage.

**6.26.20**  Having abandoned attachment to the fruit of action, ever content, depending on nothing, even when fully engaged in action he does nothing at all.

**6.26.21**  Free from longing, his mind and self restrained, having relinquished all possessions, performing action with the body alone, he incurs no sin.

**6.26.22**  Content with what comes unsought, beyond the pairs of opposites, free from envy, even-minded in success and in failure — though he acts, he is not bound.

**6.26.23**  For one who is free from attachment, liberated, whose mind is established in knowledge, who acts for the sake of sacrifice — his whole action dissolves away.

**6.26.24**  Brahman is the offering, Brahman the oblation, poured by Brahman into the fire of Brahman; Brahman alone is to be reached by him who meditates upon Brahman in his action.

**6.26.25**  Some yogins perform sacrifice to the gods alone, while others offer the sacrifice itself as oblation into the fire of Brahman.

**6.26.26**  Some offer the senses, hearing and the rest, into the fires of restraint; others offer sound and the other objects of sense into the fires of the senses.

**6.26.27**  And others offer all the activities of the senses and the activities of the vital breath into the fire of the yoga of self-restraint, kindled by knowledge.

**6.26.28**  Some make sacrifice with material things, some with austerity, some with yoga; and others, ascetics of strict vows, sacrifice with study of scripture and with knowledge.

**6.26.29**  Some offer the outgoing breath into the incoming breath, and likewise the incoming into the outgoing, restraining the courses of inhalation and exhalation, intent upon the control of breath.

**6.26.30**  Others, with regulated diet, offer the vital breaths into the vital breaths; all these indeed are knowers of sacrifice, their impurities consumed by sacrifice.

**6.26.31**  Those who feed on the nectar of the remnants of sacrifice go to the eternal Brahman; this world is not for him who offers no sacrifice — how then the other, O best of the Kurus?

**6.26.32**  Thus manifold sacrifices are spread out before the face of Brahman; know them all to be born of action, and knowing this, you shall be liberated.

**6.26.33**  Better than the sacrifice of material things is the sacrifice of knowledge, O scorcher of foes; all action in its entirety, O Pārtha, culminates in knowledge.

**6.26.34**  Know that by humble reverence, by inquiry, and by service; the wise, the seers of truth, will instruct you in that knowledge.

**6.26.35**  Knowing which you shall not again fall into such delusion, O son of Pāṇḍu, and through which you shall see all beings, without exception, within yourself and then in me.

**6.26.36**  Even if you were the most sinful of all sinners, by the raft of knowledge alone you shall cross over all wickedness.

**6.26.37**  As a kindled fire reduces firewood to ashes, O Arjuna, so does the fire of knowledge reduce all actions to ashes.

**6.26.38**  For there exists here no purifier equal to knowledge; he who is himself perfected in yoga finds it in time within his own self.

**6.26.39**  The man of faith, devoted to it and master of his senses, attains knowledge; and having attained knowledge, he quickly reaches the supreme peace.

**6.26.40**  The ignorant one, devoid of faith, whose very self is doubt, comes to ruin; for the doubting soul there is neither this world nor the world beyond, nor any happiness.

**6.26.41**  The one who has renounced action through yoga, whose doubt has been severed by knowledge, who is possessed of the self—such a one, O Dhanaṃjaya, actions do not bind.

**6.26.42**  Therefore, having cut away with the sword of knowledge this doubt born of ignorance that dwells in your heart, take your stand in yoga, and rise up, O Bhārata!

**6.27.1**  Arjuna said: You praise the renunciation of actions, O Kṛṣṇa, and again you praise yoga. Tell me with full certainty which one of these two is the better.

**6.27.2**  The Blessed Lord said: Both renunciation and the yoga of action lead to the highest good; yet of the two, the yoga of action is more excellent than the renunciation of action.

**6.27.3**  He is to be known as the perpetual renouncer who neither hates nor craves; for the one free from the pairs of opposites, O mighty-armed, is easily released from bondage.

**6.27.4**  It is children, not the wise, who speak of Sāṃkhya and yoga as distinct; one who is rightly established in even one of them gains the fruit of both.

**6.27.5**  The state that is attained by the followers of Sāṃkhya is reached by the yogins as well; he who sees Sāṃkhya and yoga as one, he truly sees.

**6.27.6**  But renunciation, O mighty-armed, is hard to attain without yoga; the sage yoked in yoga reaches Brahman before long.

**6.27.7**  Yoked in yoga, of purified self, his self mastered and his senses conquered, his self become the self of all beings—though he acts, he is not stained.

**6.27.8**  "I do nothing at all"—so should the yoked one, the knower of truth, think, even while seeing, hearing, touching, smelling, eating, walking, sleeping, breathing,

**6.27.9**  speaking, releasing, grasping, opening and closing his eyes—holding fast to the truth that it is the senses alone that move among the objects of the senses.

**6.27.10**  He who acts, surrendering his actions to Brahman and abandoning attachment, is untouched by evil, as a lotus leaf is untouched by water.

**6.27.11**  With the body, the mind, the intellect, and the senses alone, the yogins perform action, abandoning attachment, for the purification of the self.

**6.27.12**  The yoked one, having abandoned the fruit of action, attains lasting peace; the unyoked one, driven by desire and attached to the fruit, is bound.

**6.27.13**  Renouncing all actions with the mind, the master of himself, the embodied one rests happily in the city of nine gates, neither acting nor causing to act.

**6.27.14**  The Lord creates for the world neither agency nor actions, nor the union of action with its fruit; it is inherent nature that takes its course.

**6.27.15**  The all-pervading one takes on no one's evil deed, nor indeed any good deed; knowledge is veiled by ignorance, and by this creatures are deluded.

**6.27.16**  But for those whose ignorance of the self has been destroyed by knowledge, their knowledge, like the sun, illumines that Supreme.

**6.27.17**  With their understanding fixed on That, their self in That, established in That, holding That as their supreme goal, they go to that from which there is no return, their stains shaken off by knowledge.

**6.27.18**  In a brāhmaṇa endowed with learning and humility, in a cow, in an elephant, and in a dog, and even in one who eats the flesh of dogs, the wise see the same.

**6.27.19**  Even here the round of birth is conquered by those whose mind is established in equanimity; for Brahman is flawless and the same in all, and therefore they abide in Brahman.

**6.27.20**  He would not rejoice on gaining what is pleasant, nor shudder on gaining what is unpleasant—steady of understanding, undeluded, the knower of Brahman, established in Brahman.

**6.27.21**  With his self unattached to outward contacts, he finds the happiness that lies within the self; with his self yoked in union with Brahman, he enjoys imperishable happiness.

**6.27.22**  For the enjoyments born of contact are wombs of pain alone; they have a beginning and an end, O son of Kuntī, and the wise man does not delight in them.

**6.27.23**  He who is able, here in this very life, before he is freed from the body, to withstand the surge born of desire and anger—he is yoked, he is the happy man.

**6.27.24**  He whose happiness is within, whose delight is within, whose light too is within—that yogin, having become Brahman, attains the extinction that is Brahman.

**6.27.25**  The seers whose stains are worn away attain the extinction that is Brahman—their dualities cut away, their selves controlled, rejoicing in the welfare of all beings.

**6.27.26**  For the ascetics freed from desire and anger, their minds controlled, who have come to know the self, the extinction that is Brahman is present on every side.

**6.27.27**  Shutting out external contacts, fixing the gaze between the brows, making even the inward and outward breaths that move within the nostrils,

**6.27.28**  the sage whose senses, mind, and intellect are restrained, intent on liberation, from whom desire, fear, and anger have departed—he is forever truly free.

**6.27.29**  Knowing Me as the enjoyer of sacrifices and austerities, the great Lord of all the worlds, the friend of all beings, one attains peace.

**6.28.1**  The Blessed Lord said: He who performs the work that ought to be done without depending on its fruit—he is both a renouncer and a yogin, not the one who is without sacred fire and without rites.

**6.28.2**  That which they call renunciation, know that to be yoga, O Pāṇḍava; for no one becomes a yogin who has not renounced his selfish purposes.

**6.28.3**  For the sage who wishes to ascend to yoga, action is said to be the means; for that same one who has ascended to yoga, serenity is said to be the means.

**6.28.4**  For when one clings neither to the objects of the senses nor to actions, having renounced all selfish intentions, then he is said to have ascended to yoga.

**6.28.5**  Let a man lift up the self by the self, and not let the self sink down; for the self alone is the friend of the self, and the self alone is the enemy of the self.

**6.28.6**  For him who has conquered the self by the self, the self is the friend of the self; but for one without self-mastery, the self alone abides in enmity, like a foe.

**6.28.7**  For one of conquered self, who is tranquil, the supreme Self stays steadfast amid cold and heat, pleasure and pain, and likewise in honor and dishonor.

**6.28.8**  He whose self is content with knowledge and discernment, who stands unmoved on the summit, whose senses are conquered, to whom a clod, a stone, and gold are alike—such a yogin is called yoked.

**6.28.9**  He whose understanding is the same toward well-wisher, friend, and foe, toward the indifferent, the neutral, the hateful, and the kinsman, and toward the good and the wicked alike—he is the most excellent.

**6.28.10**  Let the yogin ever yoke himself, abiding in a secret place, alone, his mind and self controlled, free of expectation and free of possessions.

**6.28.11**  Setting up for himself in a clean place a firm seat, neither too high nor too low, covered with cloth, deerskin, and kuśa grass,

**6.28.12**  there, making the mind one-pointed, with the activity of thought and senses restrained, seated upon his seat, let him practice yoga for the purification of the self.

**6.28.13**  Holding body, head, and neck erect, motionless and steady, gazing at the tip of his own nose and not looking about in any direction,

**6.28.14**  his self tranquil, his fear gone, firm in the vow of chastity, restraining the mind, with his thought on Me, let him sit yoked, holding Me as the supreme.

**6.28.15**  Thus ever yoking the self, the yogin of disciplined mind attains the peace that culminates in extinction and abides in Me.

**6.28.16**  Yoga is not for one who eats too much, nor for one who does not eat at all, nor for one given to too much sleep, nor again for one who keeps awake, O Arjuna.

**6.28.17**  For one moderate in eating and recreation, moderate in his efforts at work, moderate in sleeping and waking, yoga becomes the destroyer of sorrow.

**6.28.18**  When the disciplined mind comes to rest in the self alone, free of longing for all desires, then one is said to be yoked.

**6.28.19**  As a lamp standing in a windless place does not flicker—that is the likeness remembered of the yogin of controlled mind, practicing the yoga of the self.

**6.28.20**  When the mind comes to rest, stilled by the practice of yoga, and when, beholding the self by the self, one is content within the self;

**6.28.21**  when one knows that boundless happiness which is grasped by the intellect and lies beyond the senses, and, established there, this man no longer wavers from the truth;

**6.28.22**  having gained which, he thinks no other gain greater than it, and, established in which, he is not shaken even by heavy sorrow—

**6.28.23**  let him know that disjunction from union with pain to be what is named yoga. That yoga is to be practiced with resolve and with an unwearied mind.

**6.28.24**  Abandoning without remainder all desires born of selfish intention, and restraining the whole host of the senses on every side by the mind alone,

**6.28.25**  little by little let him come to rest, by an intellect held firm in steadfastness; fixing the mind upon the self, let him not think of anything at all.

**6.28.26**  Whenever and wherever the restless, unsteady mind wanders away, from there let him rein it in and bring it under the sway of the self alone.

**6.28.27**  For supreme happiness comes to this yogin whose mind is tranquil, whose passion is stilled, who has become Brahman and is free of stain.

**6.28.28**  Thus ever yoking the self, the yogin freed from stain easily enjoys the boundless happiness of contact with Brahman.

**6.28.29**  His self yoked in yoga, seeing the same everywhere, he beholds the self abiding in all beings and all beings in the self.

**6.28.30**  He who sees Me everywhere and sees all things in Me—for him I am not lost, and he is not lost to Me.

**6.28.31**  The yogin who, established in oneness, worships Me as abiding in all beings—in whatever way he lives, that yogin lives in Me.

**6.28.32**  He who, by the likeness of himself, sees the same everywhere, O Arjuna, whether it be pleasure or pain—that yogin is held to be the highest.

**6.28.33**  Arjuna said: This yoga that you have declared to consist of equanimity, O slayer of Madhu—of it I do not see any stable foundation, because of the restlessness of the mind.

**6.28.34**  For the mind is restless, O Kṛṣṇa, turbulent, powerful, and obstinate; I think the restraining of it as hard to achieve as that of the wind.

**6.28.35**  The Blessed Lord said: Without doubt, O mighty-armed, the mind is hard to restrain and unsteady; yet by practice, O son of Kuntī, and by dispassion it is held in check.

**6.28.36**  By one whose self is unrestrained, yoga is hard to attain—such is My judgment; but by one whose self is mastered and who strives, it can be attained by the proper means.

**6.28.37**  Arjuna said: One who does not strive, though endowed with faith, his mind fallen away from yoga, having failed to reach perfection in yoga—what path, O Kṛṣṇa, does he go to?

**6.28.38**  Does he not, fallen from both, perish like a scattered cloud, O mighty-armed, without foundation and bewildered upon the path of Brahman?

**6.28.39**  This doubt of mine, O Kṛṣṇa, you ought to cut away without remainder; for no other than you can be found to dispel this doubt.

**6.28.40**  The Blessed Lord said: O Pārtha, neither here nor in the world beyond is there any ruin for him; for no one who does good, my dear, comes to a sorry end.

**6.28.41**  Having attained the worlds of those who do meritorious deeds, and having dwelt there for endless years, the one fallen from yoga is born again in the house of the pure and the prosperous.

**6.28.42**  Or else he comes to be born in the family of wise yogins; for such a birth as this is the more difficult to win in this world.

**6.28.43**  There he regains that union with the understanding gained in his former body, and from there he strives onward once more toward perfection, O joy of the Kurus.
