**6.41.72**  Then, saluting Śalya and walking around him in reverence, the king spoke to that unassailable one a word that sought his own highest good.

**6.41.73**  I ask your leave, O teacher; I shall fight, freed of all blame. With your sanction, O great king, may I conquer my foes.

**6.41.74**  Śalya said: Had you not come to me, resolved upon the battle, I would have cursed you, O great king, to defeat in the field.

**6.41.75**  I am pleased, and I am honored; let what you desire be yours. I grant you leave: fight, and win the victory.

**6.41.76**  Speak further, O hero: what do you need, what shall I give you? Things being as they are, O great king, what do you wish, other than battle?

**6.41.77**  A man is the slave of wealth, but wealth is no man's slave. That is the truth, O great king: I am bound by wealth to the Kauravas.

**6.41.78**  I shall fulfill your desire as you wish it, O sister's son. Therefore I speak to you like one unmanned: what do you wish, other than battle?

**6.41.79**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Counsel me always toward my highest good, O great king. Fight, if you must, for the enemy's sake—this is the boon I choose.

**6.41.80**  Śalya said: Tell me what help I may render you here, O best of kings. I shall fight, if I must, for the enemy's sake, for I have been bought by the Kauravas with wealth.

**6.41.81**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: That very promise you made me during the preparations is my boon, in truth: in battle you must break the fire and spirit of the charioteer's son.

**6.41.82**  Śalya said: This desire of yours shall be fulfilled as you wish it, O son of Kuntī. Go, fight without fear; I pledge you your victory.

**6.41.83**  Saṃjaya said: Then the son of Kuntī, having taken leave of his maternal uncle, the lord of the Madrakas, went forth from the great host, surrounded by his brothers.

**6.41.84**  But Vāsudeva approached the son of Rādhā upon the field, and then the elder brother of Gada spoke to him thus, for the Pāṇḍavas' sake:

**6.41.85**  I have heard, Karṇa, that out of enmity toward Bhīṣma you will not fight. Choose our side, O son of Rādhā, so long as Bhīṣma is not slain.

**6.41.86**  And when Bhīṣma is slain, O son of Rādhā, you may return again to the battle to aid the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, if you deem it fair and fitting.

**6.41.87**  Karṇa said: I shall do nothing displeasing to the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O Keśava. Know that I have laid down my very life as one who seeks Duryodhana's good.

**6.41.88**  Saṃjaya said: Hearing that word, Kṛṣṇa turned back, O descendant of Bharata, and rejoined the Pāṇḍavas, with Yudhiṣṭhira at their head.

**6.41.89**  Then in the midst of the host the eldest of the Pāṇḍavas proclaimed aloud: Whoever chooses us, him I in turn choose, for the sake of his aid.

**6.41.90**  Then, looking upon them, Yuyutsu, his heart gladdened, said this to the King of Righteousness, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Kuntī:

**6.41.91**  I shall fight, before his very eyes, the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra in the battle, for your cause, O great king, if you will choose me, O sinless one.

**6.41.92**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Come, come! We shall all fight your foolish brothers. Yuyutsu, both Vāsudeva and we say so with one accord.

**6.41.93**  I choose you, O mighty-armed one; fight for my sake. In you alone are seen the funeral offering and the continuing line of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**6.41.94**  Cleave to us, O prince of splendor, who cleave to you. The evil-minded, overbearing son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra shall not endure.

**6.41.95**  Saṃjaya said: Then Yuyutsu of the Kuru race, forsaking your sons, went over to the army of the sons of Pāṇḍu, sounding the war-drum as he went.

**6.41.96**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, greatly rejoicing along with his younger brothers, once more took up his radiant armor, bright with gold.

**6.41.97**  All those bulls among men mounted again their several chariots, and then they once more arrayed their battle-formation as it had been before.

**6.41.98**  They sounded their war-drums and kettledrums by the hundreds, and those bulls among men raised lion-roars of many kinds.

**6.41.99**  Beholding the Pāṇḍavas, those tigers among men, standing upon their chariots, the kings led by Dhṛṣṭadyumna all rejoiced again with gladness.

**6.41.100**  Seeing the dignity of the sons of Pāṇḍu, who honored those worthy of honor, the rulers of earth there praised them exceedingly.

**6.41.101**  And the kings spoke of the friendship and the compassion of those great souls, timely as it was, and of their supreme tenderness toward their kinsmen.

**6.41.102**  "Well done, well done!" rang out on every side—holy words of praise from men of renown, gladdening the mind and the heart.

**6.41.103**  The barbarians and the noble Āryas who there saw and heard of that conduct of the sons of Pāṇḍu wept then, their voices choked with feeling.

**6.41.104**  Then those high-spirited men, full of joy, beat their great war-drums and kettledrums by the hundreds, and blew their conches white as cow's milk.

**6.42.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When the armies were thus arrayed, both mine and the others', who struck the first blow there—the Kurus or the Pāṇḍavas?

**6.42.2**  Saṃjaya said: Together with his brothers, O king, your son Duryodhana, placing Bhīṣma at the head, advanced with his army.

**6.42.3**  Likewise all the Pāṇḍavas, with Bhīmasena at their head, eager for battle with Bhīṣma, advanced with gladdened hearts.

**6.42.4**  Roars and shrill clamoring cries, krakaca trumpets and cow-horn pipes, kettledrums, tabors, and drums, the neighing of horses and the trumpeting of elephants—

**6.42.5**  —rose from both armies, O king. Then they charged at us, and we roared back in answer; and at that there arose a vast tumult.

**6.42.6**  At that great upheaval, in the clash of the Pāṇḍavas and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the mighty hosts trembled at the blasts of conches and drums, like forests shaken by the wind.

**6.42.7**  As the armies, thronged with kings and elephants, horses and chariots, advanced in that inauspicious hour, there arose a tumultuous roar of the hosts, like that of oceans churned by the gale.

**6.42.8**  As that tumultuous, hair-raising din arose, the mighty-armed Bhīmasena bellowed like a bull.

**6.42.9**  The blare of conches and drums, the trumpeting of elephants, and the lion-roars of the troops—all these the roar of Bhīmasena overwhelmed.

**6.42.10**  Over the neighing of horses in their thousands throughout the ranks, the cry of Bhīmasena rose above every other sound.

**6.42.11**  Hearing that roar of his, loud as a thundercloud and ringing like Indra's bolt, your soldiers were stricken with terror.

**6.42.12**  And all the beasts of burden voided their dung and urine at that hero's cry, as lesser creatures do at the roar of a lion.

**6.42.13**  Showing himself in dreadful form, roaring like a great storm-cloud, terrifying your sons, he bore down upon your army.

**6.42.14**  As that great archer came on, his own brothers surrounded him, covering him with showers of arrows as clouds veil the sun.

**6.42.15**  There were Duryodhana your son, and Durmukha, Duḥsaha, Śala, Duḥśāsana the mighty car-warrior, and Durmarṣaṇa too, O king,

**6.42.16**  Viviṃśati, Citrasena, and Vikarṇa the great car-warrior, Purumitra, Jaya, Bhoja, and the valiant son of Somadatta.

**6.42.17**  Brandishing their great bows like storm-clouds flashing lightning, and taking up iron shafts like vipers darting forth from their holes,

**6.42.18**  Against them the sons of Draupadī, and the son of Subhadrā that great car-warrior, and Nakula and Sahadeva, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the grandson of Pṛṣata,

**6.42.19**  advanced against the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, harrowing them with sharp arrows as thunderbolts of great force shatter the peaks of mountains.

**6.42.20**  In that first onset, amid the dreadful twang of bowstrings against bracers, not one of yours nor of the foe turned his face from the fight.

**6.42.21**  I beheld the deftness of Droṇa's pupils, O bull of the Bharatas—marksmen who struck their targets unfailingly, loosing their arrows in dense streams, O king.

**6.42.22**  The din of the twanging bows did not cease, and blazing arrows shot forth like meteors falling from the vault of heaven.

**6.42.23**  But all the other rulers of earth, like mere spectators, O descendant of Bharata, looked on at that dreadful yet wondrous clash of kinsmen.

**6.42.24**  Then those great car-warriors, their fury kindled, each having wronged the other, strove against one another in rivalry, O king.

**6.42.25**  The hosts of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, thronged with elephants, horses, and chariots, shone exceedingly upon the field, like a painting wrought upon a canvas.

**6.42.26**  Then all those kings, with bows grasped firm, fell upon the foe together with their troops, at your son's command.

**6.42.27**  And the kings, by their thousands, commanded by Yudhiṣṭhira, rushed roaring upon your son's host.

**6.42.28**  Fierce was that meeting of the troops of both armies; and the sun was hidden away, the host shrouded in dust.

**6.42.29**  Among those who fought, those who broke and fled, and those who rallied again, no distinction could be made there between one's own men and the foe.

**6.42.30**  And as that tumultuous, terrible battle raged, your father shone forth surpassing all the hosts.

**6.43.1**  Saṃjaya said: In the forenoon of that fearful day, O lord of peoples, there began a most dreadful battle, the hewing-asunder of the bodies of kings.

**6.43.2**  From the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, longing for victory in the fray, there rose a roar like that of lions, making heaven and earth resound.

**6.43.3**  There were shrill clamoring cries mingled with the clap of palms and the blare of conches, and the lion-roars of heroes bellowing back at one another arose.

**6.43.4**  There was the twang of bowstrings struck by the leather guards, O bull of the Bharatas, the tread of foot-soldiers, and the loud neighing of horses,

**6.43.5**  the fall of goads and elephant-hooks, the clash of weapons, and the ringing of the bells of elephants as they charged against one another.

**6.43.6**  As that tumultuous, hair-raising din arose, the rumble of chariots became like the thunder of a rain-cloud.

**6.43.7**  Setting their minds upon cruelty and casting their lives utterly aside, they all, with banners raised on high, bore down upon the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.43.8**  The son of Śantanu himself, O king, charged upon Dhanaṃjaya in the battle, gripping his dreadful bow that was like the rod of Death.

**6.43.9**  Arjuna too, the radiant one, seizing his bow Gāṇḍīva renowned throughout the world, rushed against the son of Gaṅgā at the head of the battle.

**6.43.10**  Those two tigers among the Kurus each sought to slay the other; yet the mighty son of Gaṅgā, though he pierced Pārtha in the fight, could not make him waver, and likewise, O king, the son of Pāṇḍu could not make Bhīṣma waver in the battle.

**6.43.11**  And Sātyaki, the great archer, advanced upon Kṛtavarman; between those two there arose a tumultuous, hair-raising combat.

**6.43.12**  Sātyaki assailed Kṛtavarman and Kṛtavarman Sātyaki, the two of them mangling each other with terrible arrows.

**6.43.13**  Their every limb studded with arrows, those two mighty warriors shone like a pair of kiṃśuka trees in springtime, dappled and bright with blossoms in flower.

**6.43.14**  Abhimanyu, the great archer, gave battle to Bṛhadbala; thereupon, O lord of peoples, the king of Kosala cut down in the fight the banner of Subhadrā's son and felled his charioteer.

**6.43.15**  Then the son of Subhadrā, enraged at the slaying of his charioteer, pierced Bṛhadbala, O great king, with nine arrows.

**6.43.16**  Then with two more broad-headed shafts, golden-hued, that crusher of foes cut the banner with one and the rein-driver with the other; and the two of them, enraged, O king, mangled each other with sharp arrows.

**6.43.17**  Proud, insolent in battle, a great car-warrior who had earned his enmity—such was your son Duryodhana, whom Bhīmasena now engaged.

**6.43.18**  Those two tigers among men, foremost of the Kurus, mighty in strength, rained upon each other showers of arrows on the field of battle.

**6.43.19**  Beholding those two great-souled and accomplished warriors, masters of the wondrous arts of combat, amazement seized all beings, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.43.20**  Duḥśāsana, advancing against the great car-warrior Nakula, struck him with many whetted arrows that pierced to the vitals.

**6.43.21**  The son of Mādrī, as if laughing, cut apart with sharp arrows his standard and his bow together with its shaft, O descendant of Bharata; and then he tormented him with twenty-five small darts.

**6.43.22**  But your son, hard to assail, in that great battle cut with his arrows the yoke-pole of Nakula's car and brought down his banner as well.

**6.43.23**  Durmukha, advancing against the mighty Sahadeva, pierced him with a shower of arrows as he strove in the great battle.

**6.43.24**  Then the hero Sahadeva, in that great battle, with an exceedingly sharp arrow brought down Durmukha's charioteer.

**6.43.25**  Closing upon each other in the fight, those two, frenzied for combat and bent on stroke and counter-stroke, terrified one another with terrible arrows.

**6.43.26**  King Yudhiṣṭhira himself advanced against the king of the Madras; and the lord of the Madras cut his bow in two, O venerable one.

**6.43.27**  Casting aside that severed bow, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Kuntī took up another, swifter and stronger.

**6.43.28**  Then the king, enraged, covered the lord of the Madras with smooth-jointed arrows and cried, "Stand! Stand!"

**6.43.29**  Then Dhṛṣṭadyumna rushed upon Droṇa, O descendant of Bharata; but Droṇa, greatly enraged, cut into three pieces in the fight his stout bow—the very life-taker—as he strove with it.

**6.43.30**  And he loosed in the battle a most dreadful arrow, like a second rod of Death, which sank deep into his body.

**6.43.31**  Then taking up another bow and fourteen arrows, the son of Drupada pierced Droṇa in return in that engagement; and the two of them, greatly enraged, waged a most furious combat against each other.

**6.43.32**  In the fight Śaṅkha, the impetuous, advanced against the impetuous son of Somadatta, O great king, crying, "Stand! Stand!"

**6.43.33**  The hero pierced his right arm in the fight; and the son of Somadatta in turn struck Śaṅkha in the region of the collarbone.

**6.43.34**  Between those two, proud and swift in battle, O lord of peoples, there arose a combat of terrible form, like that between Vṛtra and Vāsava.

**6.43.35**  Against Bāhlīka, enraged in the fight, the immeasurable-souled Dhṛṣṭaketu, that great car-warrior, fierce of aspect, charged, O lord of peoples.

**6.43.36**  Then Bāhlīka, O king, assailed the indignant Dhṛṣṭaketu with many arrows and let forth a lion's roar.

**6.43.37**  But the king of the Cedis, greatly enraged, swiftly pierced Bāhlīka in the fight with nine arrows, as a rutting elephant assails another in rut.

**6.43.38**  There in the fight those two, enraged and roaring again and again, came together in fury like Mars and Mercury.

**6.43.39**  Against the rākṣasa Alambuṣa, cruel in his deeds, the cruel-deeded Ghaṭotkaca advanced in battle, as Śakra against Bala.

**6.43.40**  Then Ghaṭotkaca, greatly enraged, rent that mighty rākṣasa with ninety sharp arrows, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.43.41**  But Alambuṣa in the fight again and again held off the mighty son of Bhīmasena with smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.43.42**  Wounded by arrows, those two then shone in the engagement like the mighty Bala and Śakra in the war of gods and demons.

**6.43.43**  The mighty Śikhaṇḍin in the fight advanced against the son of Droṇa, O king; thereupon Aśvatthāman, enraged, struck Śikhaṇḍin as he stood firm.

**6.43.44**  Piercing him grievously with a keen iron shaft, he made him quake; yet Śikhaṇḍin too, O king, then smote the son of Droṇa.

**6.43.45**  With a fine, sharp, well-whetted arrow he struck him; and the two of them then smote each other in the fray with manifold arrows.

**6.43.46**  Against the valiant Bhagadatta in the fight, Virāṭa the commander of hosts advanced in haste, O king; and thereupon a battle arose between them.

**6.43.47**  Virāṭa, struck by Bhagadatta with a shower of arrows, greatly enraged, rained upon him in return, as a cloud pours its rain upon a mountain.

**6.43.48**  Then Bhagadatta swiftly covered Virāṭa, lord of the earth, in the fight, as a cloud covers the risen sun.

**6.43.49**  Against Bṛhatkṣatra the Kaikeya, Kṛpa the son of Śaradvat advanced; and Kṛpa covered him with a shower of arrows, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.43.50**  The Kekaya, enraged, overwhelmed the son of Gautama with a downpour of arrows; and the two, having slain each other's horses and cut apart each other's bows—

**6.43.51**  Bereft of their cars, the indignant pair came together for combat with swords; and between them there arose a battle of terrible form, most fierce.

**6.43.52**  Then King Drupada, full of gladness, advanced against the gladsome Jayadratha, lord of the Sindhus, O scorcher of foes.

**6.43.53**  Then the king of the Sindhus struck Drupada in the fight with three arrows, and Drupada pierced him in return.

**6.43.54**  Between them there arose a battle of terrible form, most fierce, delighting the beholders, like that between Venus and Mars.

**6.43.55**  Your son Vikarṇa advanced with swift horses against the mighty Sutasoma; and thereupon a battle arose.

**6.43.56**  Vikarṇa, though he pierced Sutasoma with arrows, could not make him waver; and neither could Sutasoma make Vikarṇa waver—and that was a wondrous thing.

**6.43.57**  Against Suśarman, that tiger among men, the great car-warrior Cekitāna, valorous and greatly enraged, charged for the sake of the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.43.58**  But Suśarman, O great king, held off the great car-warrior Cekitāna in the engagement with a vast shower of arrows.

**6.43.59**  Cekitāna too, in fury, covered Suśarman in that great battle with arrows, as a great cloud covers a mountain.

**6.43.60**  Śakuni, the valorous, charged against the valiant Prativindhya, O lord of kings, as a rutting elephant against another in rut.

**6.43.61**  But the son of Yudhiṣṭhira, greatly enraged, rent the son of Subala in the fight with sharp arrows, as Maghavat rends a dānava.

**6.43.62**  And Śakuni, the highly sagacious, rent Prativindhya, who was piercing him in return in the battle, with smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.43.63**  Against Sudakṣiṇa, O lord of kings, the valiant great car-warrior of the Kāmbojas, Śrutakarman charged in the engagement.

**6.43.64**  But Sudakṣiṇa in the fight, though he pierced the great car-warrior son of Sahadeva, could not make him waver, any more than the mountain Maināka.

**6.43.65**  Then Śrutakarman, enraged, assailed the great car-warrior of the Kāmbojas with many arrows, rending him, as it were, on every side.

**6.43.66**  Then Irāvān, greatly enraged and on his guard, advanced in the fight against the indignant Śrutāyus, who was yet more on his guard.

**6.43.67**  The son of Arjuna, that great car-warrior, having slain his horses in the fight, roared a mighty roar that filled the host.

**6.43.68**  But Śrutāyus, greatly enraged, slew the horses of Phalguna's son in the fight with the head of his mace; and thereupon a battle arose.

**6.43.69**  Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti closed in battle with the great car-warrior Kuntibhoja, that hero, together with his host and his sons.

**6.43.70**  There we beheld a wondrous feat of valor by the men of Avanti, in that, standing firm, they fought against a mighty host.

**6.43.71**  Anuvinda struck Kuntibhoja with his mace; and Kuntibhoja then swiftly overwhelmed him with volleys of arrows.

**6.43.72**  And the son of Kuntibhoja, too, pierced Vinda with arrows, and Vinda pierced him in return—and that was a wondrous thing.

**6.43.73**  The five Kekaya brothers, with their hosts, gave battle to five Gāndhāras with their hosts, O venerable one.

**6.43.74**  And your son Vīrabāhu gave battle to Uttara, son of Virāṭa, that best of car-warriors, and pierced him with sharp arrows; and Uttara too pierced that steadfast warrior with sharp arrows.

**6.43.75**  The king of the Cedis in the fight rushed upon Ulūka, O king; and Ulūka too assailed him with sharp, feathered arrows.

**6.43.76**  Between them there arose a combat of terrible form, O lord of peoples; greatly enraged and unvanquished, they rent each other.

**6.43.77**  Thus in thousands of single combats of car-warriors, elephants, horsemen, and foot-soldiers, your forces and theirs grew confounded in the fight.

**6.43.78**  For a moment that battle was fair to behold; but thereafter, O king, as in a frenzy, nothing whatever could be discerned.

**6.43.79**  Elephant met elephant in the fight, and car-warrior met car-warrior, horse closed with horse, and foot-soldier with foot-soldier.

**6.43.80**  Then a most unbearable and tumultuous battle came about there, as the heroes in the fight closed upon one another.

**6.43.81**  There the divine seers, the siddhas, and the cāraṇas, gathered together, gazed upon that terrible battle, like the war of gods and demons.

**6.43.82**  Then thousands of elephants and of chariots, floods of horses and floods of men, O venerable one, came together in confusion.

**6.43.83**  Here and there were seen car-warriors, elephants, and foot-soldiers, and horsemen too, O tiger among men, fighting again and again.

**6.44.1**  Saṃjaya said: O king, hundreds of thousands fought there, here and there, then and again, beyond all bounds; of this I shall tell you, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.44.2**  There son knew not father, nor father his own begotten son, nor brother brother, nor uncle his sister's son.

**6.44.3**  Nor sister's son his uncle, nor friend his friend; as if possessed, the Pāṇḍavas fought together with the Kurus.

**6.44.4**  Some of those tigers among men hurled themselves with their chariots upon the array of cars, and yoke shattered against yoke, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.44.5**  Chariot-poles clashed with chariot-poles and chariot-shafts with chariot-shafts; some warriors, their cars locked fast against locked cars, each strained to slay the other.

**6.44.6**  Some chariots, once they had collided with chariots, could not stir at all; and the great-bodied elephants in rut, charging together, crashed elephant upon elephant.

**6.44.7**  In their fury the war-elephants gored one another again and again with their tusks, and the foe's elephants, bearing lances and banners, tore into them.

**6.44.8**  O great king, when the swift and mighty elephants rushed upon them and struck them with their tusks, the wounded beasts shrieked there in utter agony.

**6.44.9**  Trained well in their lessons and goaded by spike and hook, the rut-maddened elephants advanced face to face against other rut-maddened elephants.

**6.44.10**  Some of the great elephants, locked in struggle even with those in rut, let loose a cry like the curlew's and fled this way and that.

**6.44.11**  Well-handled elephants, the ichor flowing from their cheeks and trunks, those noble war-beasts pierced through by sword, lance, and iron shaft—

**6.44.12**  their vital parts shattered, they screamed and fell down lifeless, while some, bellowing dreadful roars, stampeded toward every quarter.

**6.44.13**  The foot-guards of the elephants, broad-chested smiters, armed with swords and bows and gleaming battle-axes,

**6.44.14**  with maces and pestles, with slings and lances, with iron bludgeons and with bright, keen-edged blades—

**6.44.15**  these were seen, O great king, running here and there in fierce excitement, weapons raised, each eager to kill the other.

**6.44.16**  And the gleaming swords, drenched in the blood of men, shone forth as the heroes charged upon one another.

**6.44.17**  From the swords flung down and swept about by the arms of heroes there arose a tumultuous din as they fell upon the vital parts of the foe.

**6.44.18**  Of those crushed by mace and pestle, of those cleft by fine swords, of those split apart by elephants' tusks and trampled under elephants—

**6.44.19**  of those throngs of men crying out to one another here and there, terrible voices were heard, O Bhārata, like the voices of the departed dead.

**6.44.20**  And horsemen, on horses decked with chowries and crests, swift as wild geese, rushed against one another.

**6.44.21**  The great javelins they hurled, adorned with gold, swift and bright and keen, flew on like serpents.

**6.44.22**  Some horsemen, leaping upon the great chariots with their swiftest steeds, took off the heads of the heroic charioteers.

**6.44.23**  And many horsemen who came within his arrow-range a chariot-warrior, drawing near, struck down with broad-headed, smooth-jointed shafts.

**6.44.24**  Elephants like mountains and clouds, frenzied and decked in gold, dashed the horses aside and crushed them under their feet.

**6.44.25**  Their frontal globes split open and their flanks laid bare, some elephants, struck down by javelins, screamed in utmost anguish.

**6.44.26**  Some noble elephants, seizing the horses together with their riders and dashing them up, hurled them violently there amid the dreadful press of battle.

**6.44.27**  The elephants, tossing up horses with their riders upon the points of their tusks, ranged about, trampling whole hosts of chariots together with their banners.

**6.44.28**  Out of their masculine fury and their rutting frenzy, some of the great elephants there slew the horses and their riders with their trunks and their feet.

**6.44.29**  Some elephants, seizing with their trunks the chariots together with their horses and dragging them off, ranged toward every quarter, answering to every command.

**6.44.30**  Swift, bright, and keen, the arrows flew on like serpents, piercing through the bodies of men and horses and through their iron armor.

**6.44.31**  Bright spears, launched by the arms of heroes, fell here and there, O lord of the peoples, dreadful as great meteors.

**6.44.32**  With shields bound in panther-hide and tiger-skin, with bare and gleaming swords, the warriors smote down their foes in battle.

**6.44.33**  Charging in, full of wrath and gashed open along one flank, displaying their prowess, they rushed together with sword, shield, and battle-axe.

**6.44.34**  Some were torn open by spears and some hewn apart by battle-axes; some were crushed by elephants and others trampled down by horses.

**6.44.35**  Cut down by chariot-rims and hewn by sharpened arrows, the men cried out, O king, here and there, calling upon their kinsmen—

**6.44.36**  some upon their sons, some upon their fathers, some upon their brothers along with their kindred, upon uncles and nephews, and even upon their foes in the fray.

**6.44.37**  Very many, O Bhārata, their entrails strewn forth and their thighs broken, their fine arms severed and their flanks ripped open, were seen wailing, parched with thirst, still longing for life.

**6.44.38**  Some, overcome by thirst and weak of strength, O lord of the peoples, fallen upon the ground in battle, begged only for water.

**6.44.39**  Drenched in streams of blood and racked with pain, O Bhārata, they bitterly reproached themselves and your assembled sons.

**6.44.40**  Other valiant warriors, having made enmity with one another, would neither cast away their weapons nor cry out, O honored sir, but, exulting, threatened one another here and there.

**6.44.41**  Biting through their own lips with their teeth in their wrath, with faces drawn into knotted frowns, they glared at one another.

**6.44.42**  Others, sorely afflicted, tormented by their wounds and pierced by arrows, men of firm courage and great strength, fell utterly silent.

**6.44.43**  Other heroes, their chariots lost, seeking out another's car in the fray, fell and were crushed by the noble elephants, and they shone, O great king, like kiṃśuka trees in flower.

**6.44.44**  Many a fearful clamor arose throughout the ranks as that most terrible slaughter of the foremost heroes went on.

**6.44.45**  In that battle father slew son and son slew father; the sister's son slew the uncle, and the uncle slew the sister's son.

**6.44.46**  Friend slew friend, O king, and kinsman slew kinsman; thus did the Kurus there do battle with the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.44.47**  As that terror unfolded in that boundless, mighty war, the host of the Pārthas, coming up against Bhīṣma, trembled.

**6.44.48**  With his banner of five stars, a palmyra wrought in silver and raised high upon his great chariot, O bull of the Bharatas, the mighty-armed Bhīṣma then shone, O king, like the moon above Meru.

**6.45.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the greater part of the forenoon had passed on that dread day, as the immense and terrible slaughter of the foremost heroes went on,

**6.45.2**  Durmukha and Kṛtavarman, Kṛpa, Śalya, and Viviṃśati, urged on by your son, came up and guarded Bhīṣma.

**6.45.3**  Guarded by these five mighty car-warriors, O bull of the Bharatas, the great chariot-hero plunged into the ranks of the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.45.4**  Among the Cedis, the Kāśis, the Karūṣas, and the Pāñcālas, O Bhārata, Bhīṣma's palmyra banner was seen ranging about in many directions.

**6.45.5**  And then Bhīṣma struck off heads, and arms together with their weapons, with swift, broad-headed, smooth-jointed shafts.

**6.45.6**  As Bhīṣma seemed to dance along the paths of his chariot, O bull of the Bharatas, some of the elephants, struck in their vital parts, raised cries of agony.

**6.45.7**  Then Abhimanyu, in towering wrath, mounting his chariot yoked with excellent tawny steeds, drove straight toward Bhīṣma's car.

**6.45.8**  With his banner of the karṇikāra blossom, bright with gold, he rained his shafts upon Bhīṣma and upon those foremost of car-warriors.

**6.45.9**  Striking the banner of him whose ensign was the palmyra with a keen, feathered shaft, that hero did battle with Bhīṣma and with his followers.

**6.45.10**  Having pierced Kṛtavarman with one iron shaft and Śalya with five, he struck the great-grandsire with nine keen-pointed arrows.

**6.45.11**  With a single arrow, loosed from a fully drawn bow and well aimed, he pierced the gold-adorned banner.

**6.45.12**  And with a broad-headed, smooth-jointed shaft that cleaves through every guard, he struck the head of Durmukha's charioteer from his body.

**6.45.13**  With a keen-pointed broad-headed shaft he cut apart the gold-adorned bow of Kṛpa, and assailed those warriors with sharp-mouthed arrows.

**6.45.14**  In utmost fury that great car-warrior smote them, seeming to dance; and beholding his deftness, even the gods were delighted.

**6.45.15**  By the unerring aim of the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, all the car-warriors led by Bhīṣma deemed him a man of valor, Dhanaṃjaya himself made manifest.

**6.45.16**  His bow, kept ever in the path of deftness, glowing like a firebrand, ranged about all the quarters, loud-voiced like the Gāṇḍīva.

**6.45.17**  Coming up against him, Bhīṣma, slayer of hostile heroes, swiftly pierced the son of Arjuna in battle with nine fast-flying arrows.

**6.45.18**  And with three broad-headed shafts he cut down the banner of that one of surpassing might, and that man of steadfast vows struck his charioteer with three arrows.

**6.45.19**  In like manner Kṛtavarman, Kṛpa, and Śalya, O honored sir, though they pierced the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, could not make him waver, like the mountain Maināka.

**6.45.20**  Surrounded by those great car-warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's side, that hero, the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, poured showers of arrows upon the five chariot-fighters.

**6.45.21**  Then, warding off their great missiles with showers of arrows, the mighty son of Kṛṣṇa's sister roared aloud, loosing his shafts against Bhīṣma.

**6.45.22**  There, O king, the exceeding strength of his arms was made plain as he strove in battle and harried Bhīṣma with his arrows.

**6.45.23**  Against that very valiant one Bhīṣma too sent forth his shafts; but he cut to pieces in battle those arrows loosed from Bhīṣma's bow.

**6.45.24**  Then that hero of unfailing aim cut down Bhīṣma's banner in battle with nine arrows, and thereupon the people shouted aloud.

**6.45.25**  That silver palmyra of mighty stem, adorned with gold, cut down by the shafts of Subhadrā's son, fell to the earth, O Bhārata.

**6.45.26**  Seeing the banner fallen by the arrows of Subhadrā's son, O bull of the Bharatas, Bhīma roared in delight, gladdening the heart of Subhadrā's son.

**6.45.27**  Then Bhīṣma, of fearful aspect and great strength, brought forth in that moment many mighty and celestial missiles.

**6.45.28**  Then the great-grandsire of immeasurable spirit overwhelmed the son of Subhadrā with a hundred thousand smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.45.29**  Then ten great bowmen, mighty car-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, sped forward upon their chariots to protect the son of Subhadrā.

**6.45.30**  Virāṭa with his son, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the grandson of Pṛṣata, and Bhīma, and the Kekayas, and Sātyaki, O lord of the peoples—

**6.45.31**  As these swept down in haste, Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, in battle, struck the Pāñcāla prince with three arrows and Sātyaki with sharp ones.

**6.45.32**  And with a single keen, razor-edged, feathered shaft, loosed from a fully drawn bow, he cut down the banner of Bhīmasena.

**6.45.33**  The golden banner of Bhīmasena, bearing a lion, O best of men, struck down by Bhīṣma, fell from his chariot.

**6.45.34**  Bhīmasena, having pierced Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu with three arrows in battle, struck Kṛpa with one and Kṛtavarman with eight.

**6.45.35**  And Uttara, the son of Virāṭa, mounted upon an elephant whose trunk was raised on high, rushed against the king, the lord of the Madras.

**6.45.36**  As that royal elephant came rushing down in haste, the charioteer Śalya checked its matchless onset in the battle.

**6.45.37**  Enraged, that lordly elephant of Uttara—huge and well-trained—set its foot upon the yoke and crushed all four of Śalya's horses.

**6.45.38**  Standing in his car whose horses were slain, the lord of the Madras hurled an iron spear, like a serpent, that would be the death of Uttara.

**6.45.39**  His armor pierced through by it, entering a vast darkness, he fell from the elephant's shoulder, his goad and lance slipping from his grasp.

**6.45.40**  Then Śalya, taking up his sword and leaping down from his splendid chariot, advanced boldly and severed the great trunk of the lordly elephant.

**6.45.41**  Its vital parts torn by showers of shafts, its trunk cut off, that elephant gave a terrible cry of agony, then fell and died.

**6.45.42**  Having performed this feat, the king of the Madras, that mighty car-warrior, swiftly mounted the gleaming chariot of Kṛtavarman.

**6.45.43**  Seeing his noble brother Uttara slain, and seeing Śalya stationed there together with Kṛtavarman, Virāṭa's son Śaṅkha blazed up with wrath like fire fed with oblations.

**6.45.44**  Twanging his great bow adorned with gold, the mighty one rushed forward, eager to slay Śalya, the lord of the Madras.

**6.45.45**  Surrounded on every side by a great host of chariots, pouring forth a rain of arrows, he advanced toward Śalya's car.

**6.45.46**  Seeing him bearing down with the might of a rutting elephant, seven of your warriors' chariots hemmed him in on all sides, seeking to rescue the king of the Madras, who had passed between the very fangs of death.

**6.45.47**  Then the mighty-armed Bhīṣma, roaring like a thundercloud, seized a bow tall as a palm tree and charged upon Śaṅkha in the fray.

**6.45.48**  Beholding that mighty archer of vast strength rise up, the Pāṇḍava army trembled like a ship struck by the force of the wind.

**6.45.49**  Then Arjuna swiftly placed himself in front of Śaṅkha, thinking, "This man must be guarded today from Bhīṣma," and so the battle was joined.

**6.45.50**  A great cry of dismay arose among the warriors fighting in the field; "Splendor has met splendor," they said, and were filled with wonder.

**6.45.51**  Then Śalya, mace in hand, descending from his great chariot, slew the four steeds of Śaṅkha, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.45.52**  Quickly taking up his sword, Śaṅkha fled from the car whose horses were slain, and reaching the chariot of Bībhatsu he found safety there.

**6.45.53**  Then from Bhīṣma's chariot the winged shafts flew swiftly forth, and by them the sky and the earth were everywhere overspread.

**6.45.54**  The Pāñcālas, the Matsyas, the Kekayas, and the Prabhadrakas—Bhīṣma, the foremost of strikers, laid them low with his arrows.

**6.45.55**  Quickly leaving the Pāṇḍava Savyasācin on the field, he rushed against the Pāñcāla Drupada, who was surrounded by his army; and upon that dear kinsman, O king, he poured down a multitude of shafts.

**6.45.56**  Like forests scorched by fire at winter's end, the troops of Drupada appeared, burnt up by his arrows; and Bhīṣma stood in the battle like a smokeless flame.

**6.45.57**  As none can gaze upon the midday sun blazing with its splendor, so the warriors of the Pāṇḍava could not look upon Bhīṣma.

**6.45.58**  The Pāṇḍavas, oppressed by fear, looked about them on every side, but found no protector, like cattle tormented by the cold.

**6.45.59**  As the army, struck down and scattered, dispirited and crushed, gave way, a great cry of distress arose among the troops of the Pāṇḍus, O Bhārata.

**6.45.60**  Then Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, his bow ever drawn into a circle, loosed flaming-tipped shafts like venomous serpents.

**6.45.61**  Making all the quarters a single path with his arrows, that man of firm vows slew the chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, singling out each one by name, O Bhārata.

**6.45.62**  Then, as the armies broke and were churned to pieces on every side, and as the maker of day reached his setting, nothing whatever could be discerned.

**6.45.63**  And seeing Bhīṣma raging on in that great battle, the sons of Pṛthā sounded the recall of their troops, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.46.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the first day's withdrawal of the armies had been made, O bull of the Bharatas—Bhīṣma furious in the fight and Duryodhana exultant—

**6.46.2**  the Dharma-king, going swiftly to Janārdana, together with all his brothers and all the lords of men,

**6.46.3**  filled with the deepest grief, brooding upon defeat, spoke thus to the scion of the Vṛṣṇis, O king, having beheld Bhīṣma's prowess:

**6.46.4**  "Kṛṣṇa, behold the great archer Bhīṣma of terrible might, burning up my army with his shafts as fire burns dry grass in summer.

**6.46.5**  How shall we even be able to look upon this great soul, who licks up my army as fire licks at the oblation?

**6.46.6**  For at the sight of this tiger among men, this mighty bowman of vast strength, my army has scattered in flight, smitten by his arrows.

**6.46.7**  Yama in his wrath might be conquered in battle, and the wielder of the thunderbolt, and Varuṇa bearing his noose, and Kubera with his mace—

**6.46.8**  but Bhīṣma of vast splendor and mighty strength cannot be conquered. So matters standing, I am sunk in the fathomless waters of Bhīṣma, without a raft.

**6.46.9**  Through the weakness of my own judgment, Keśava, I have come up against Bhīṣma; I shall go to the forest, Govinda—better for me to live there.

**6.46.10**  I cannot give over these guardians of the earth to Bhīṣma, to their death; Bhīṣma, knower of great weapons, will surely destroy my army, Kṛṣṇa.

**6.46.11**  As moths rushing into a blazing fire go only to their destruction, so do the men of my army.

**6.46.12**  For the sake of a kingdom I, though valiant, have been brought to ruin, scion of the Vṛṣṇis; and my heroic brothers are worn thin, tormented by arrows.

**6.46.13**  For my sake, out of brotherly love, they have been cast out from kingdom and from ease. I now hold life itself a precious thing, for life today is hard to keep.

**6.46.14**  With what remains of my life I shall practice harsh austerities; I will not have these friends of mine slain in battle, Keśava.

**6.46.15**  Mighty Bhīṣma ceaselessly destroys my chariot-warriors by the many thousands, the foremost of strikers, with his celestial weapons.

**6.46.16**  What can I do that my purpose may be accomplished? Tell me, Mādhava, without delay. I see Savyasācin standing in the battle as though indifferent.

**6.46.17**  Bhīma alone, the mighty-armed, fights with utmost strength, remembering the warrior's duty, by sheer might of arm alone.

**6.46.18**  With his hero-slaying mace, that great-spirited one, to the utmost of his vigor, performs deeds hard to do among elephants, horses, chariots, and foot-soldiers.

**6.46.19**  Yet even he is not able, sir, to bring the enemy host to destruction by fair fighting alone, that hero, not in a hundred years.

**6.46.20**  This friend of yours alone knows the weapons, yet even he disregards us, while we are consumed by Bhīṣma and by the great-souled Droṇa.

**6.46.21**  The celestial weapons of Bhīṣma and of the great-souled Droṇa, loosed again and again, will burn up all the warriors.

**6.46.22**  Kṛṣṇa, Bhīṣma, fierce in his fury, joined with all the kings, will surely make an end of us—such is his prowess.

**6.46.23**  Therefore, O lord of yoga, look out for some great archer, some mighty car-warrior, who might quell Bhīṣma in the fight as a raincloud quells a forest fire.

**6.46.24**  By your grace, Govinda, the Pāṇḍavas, their foes slain, having regained their own kingdom, will rejoice together with their kinsmen."

**6.46.25**  Having spoken thus, the great-spirited son of Pṛthā sat sunk in thought, long turned inward, his mind overwhelmed by grief.

**6.46.26**  Knowing the Pāṇḍava stricken with grief, his mind broken by sorrow, Govinda then spoke, gladdening all the Pāṇḍavas:

**6.46.27**  "Grieve not, foremost of the Bharatas; you ought not to grieve, you whose brothers are heroes, archers above all the world.

**6.46.28**  And I am here to do you good, O king, and Sātyaki the great car-warrior, and the aged Virāṭa and Drupada, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna son of Pṛṣata.

**6.46.29**  And likewise all the kings with their forces, O best of kings, devoted to you, await your favor, O lord of peoples.

**6.46.30**  This son of Pṛṣata, Dhṛṣṭadyumna of mighty strength, ever wishing your good and devoted to your welfare, has been made the commander of your host; and Śikhaṇḍin too, O mighty-armed one, is to be the death of Bhīṣma.

**6.46.31**  Hearing this, the king then spoke to Dhṛṣṭadyumna, the great car-warrior, in that assembly, while Vāsudeva listened:

**6.46.32**  "Dhṛṣṭadyumna, mark well what I shall say to you, sir; this word of mine, once spoken, must not be transgressed.

**6.46.33**  You are my commander-in-chief, approved by Vāsudeva; as Kārttikeya was of old the eternal leader of the gods, so are you the general of the Pāṇḍus, O bull among men.

**6.46.34**  Therefore, O tiger among men, advance boldly and slay the Kauravas; and I will follow after you, and Bhīma, and Kṛṣṇa, sir,

**6.46.35**  and the two sons of Mādrī together, and the sons of Draupadī clad in armor, and those other foremost guardians of the earth, O bull among men."

**6.46.36**  Then Dhṛṣṭadyumna, rousing the spirits of all, said: "I, O Pārtha, was ordained of old by Śambhu to be the ender of Droṇa.

**6.46.37**  Bhīṣma in battle, and Droṇa, Kṛpa, Śalya, Jayadratha—all of them, arrogant in war, I shall this day meet in combat, O king."

**6.46.38**  Then a shout went up from the great archers, the Pāṇḍavas mad for battle, when that lord of kings, the son of Pṛṣata, the slayer of foes, rose up.

**6.46.39**  Then the son of Pṛthā said to the son of Pṛṣata, the commander of the host: "There is a formation called the Krauñca-aruṇa, the crusher of all foes—

**6.46.40**  the one which Bṛhaspati then declared to Indra in the war of gods and demons. Array it duly, the destroyer of the enemy's ranks; let the kings, together with the Kurus, behold what was never seen before."

**6.46.41**  Thus addressed by the god among men, as Viṣṇu was by the bearer of the thunderbolt, at daybreak he placed Dhanaṃjaya at the head of all the forces.

**6.46.42**  His banner, ranging the path of the sun, wondrous and ravishing to behold, had been fashioned by Viśvakarman at the command of Puruhūta.

**6.46.43**  Adorned with pennants of the hue of Indra's bow, moving through the sky like a thing of the air, resembling a city of the Gandharvas, it seemed to dance, sir, as the chariot wheeled through its courses.

**6.46.44**  With that jewel-bright banner and with the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva, the son of Pṛthā shone supremely furnished, like the Self-Existent with the sun.

**6.46.45**  King Drupada, surrounded by a great army, formed the head; and Kuntibhoja and the Caidya stood at the eyes, O lord of men.

**6.46.46**  The Dāśārṇakas and the Prayāgas, together with the bands of the Dāśerakas, the dwellers of the watered lowlands, and the Kirātas, formed the neck, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.46.47**  And with the Paṭaccaras and the Huṇḍas, O king, and the Pauravakas, and together with the Niṣādas, Yudhiṣṭhira held the back.

**6.46.48**  The two wings were Bhīmasena and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata, the sons of Draupadī and Abhimanyu, and Sātyaki the great car-warrior.

**6.46.49**  The Piśācas and the Daradas, the Puṇḍras together with the Kuṇḍīviṣas, the Maḍakas and the Laḍakas, the Taṅgaṇas and the Parataṅgaṇas—

**6.46.50**  the Bāhlikas and the Tittiras, the Colas and the Pāṇḍyas, O Bhārata—these peoples, O king, took their station on the right wing.

**6.46.51**  The Agniveṣyas, the Jagattuṇḍas, and the Paladāśas, O Bhārata, the Śabaras and the Tumbupas, and the Vatsas together with the Nākulas—and Nakula and Sahadeva took their station on the left flank.

**6.46.52**  The two wings held ten thousand chariots, and the head a hundred thousand; the back numbered a hundred million and twenty thousand besides; and at the neck were a hundred thousand and seventy thousand more.

**6.46.53**  At the wings, their tips, the outer wings, and the wings' ends, the elephants moved hemmed in on every side, O king, like mountains on the march.

**6.46.54**  Virāṭa, together with the Kekayas, guarded the rear, as did the king of Kāśi and Śaibya, with thirty thousand chariots.

**6.46.55**  Having marshalled this great battle-array, O Bhārata, the Pāṇḍavas, armoured and longing for the sunrise, stood ready for battle.

**6.46.56**  Sun-coloured, spotless, and vast, their white parasols shone resplendent upon the elephants and the chariots.

**6.47.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, beholding the great Krauñca formation—impenetrable and most terrible—drawn up by Pārtha of immeasurable splendour, your son,

**6.47.2**  approaching the preceptor, O venerable one, and Kṛpa and Śalya, and the son of Somadatta and Vikarṇa, and Aśvatthāman as well,

**6.47.3**  and Duḥśāsana and his other brothers, all of them, O Bhārata, and the many other heroes who had assembled for battle,

**6.47.4**  your son, gladdening them, spoke these timely words: "All of you wield manifold weapons and are versed in arms and missiles.

**6.47.5**  Each one of you, all great chariot-warriors, is alone able to slay the sons of Pāṇḍu with their host in battle—how much more so when united!

**6.47.6**  Unlimited is this our force, guarded by Bhīṣma, while limited is this force of theirs, O best of kings.

**6.47.7**  Let the Saṃsthānas and the Śūrasenas, the Veṇikas and the Kukuras, the Ārevakas and the Trigartas, the Madrakas and the Yavanas,

**6.47.8**  together with Śatruṃjaya and with Duḥśāsana, and with the heroic Vikarṇa, and with Nanda and Upananda,

**6.47.9**  joined with Citrasena and with the Pāṇibhadrakas, set their armies before them and guard Bhīṣma above all."

**6.47.10**  Then Droṇa and Bhīṣma and your son, O venerable one, drew up a great formation to repel the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.47.11**  Bhīṣma, surrounded on every side by a mighty host, advanced, leading on the great army like the king of the gods.

**6.47.12**  Behind him followed the mighty archer, the valorous son of Bharadvāja, with the Kuntalas and the Daśārṇas and the Māgadhas, O lord of peoples,

**6.47.13**  with the Vidarbhas and the Mekalas and the Karṇaprāvaraṇas as well, together with the whole army, behind Bhīṣma resplendent in battle.

**6.47.14**  The Gāndhāras, the Sindhus and Sauvīras, the Śibis, and then the Vasātis—and Śakuni with his own troops—guarded the son of Bharadvāja.

**6.47.15**  Then King Duryodhana, together with all his brothers, with the Aśvātakas and the Vikarṇas, and likewise with the Śarmilas and the Kosalas,

**6.47.16**  and with the Daradas and the Cūcupas, and likewise the Kṣudrakas and the Mālavas, joyfully guarded the host of Subala's son.

**6.47.17**  Bhūriśravas, Śala, Śalya, and Bhagadatta, O venerable one, and Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, guarded the left flank.

**6.47.18**  The son of Somadatta and Suśarman, and Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja, and Śatāyus and Śrutāyus, took their place on the right flank.

**6.47.19**  Aśvatthāman and Kṛpa, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvata line, with a great army stationed themselves at the rear of the host.

**6.47.20**  The guardians of its rear were kings from various lands—Ketumat and Vasudāna, and Abhibhū the son of the king of Kāśi.

**6.47.21**  Then all your warriors, rejoicing for battle, O Bhārata, filled with gladness blew their conches and let loose their lion-roars.

**6.47.22**  Hearing the lion-roar of those joyful men, the aged grandsire of the Kurus, the valorous one, roared aloud in answer and blew his conch.

**6.47.23**  Then conches and kettledrums and tabors of many kinds, and war-drums, were sounded by the foe, and the din became tumultuous.

**6.47.24**  Then, stationed upon a great chariot yoked with white horses, the two blew their excellent conches, adorned with gold and gems—

**6.47.25**  Hṛṣīkeśa blew the Pāñcajanya, Dhanaṃjaya the Devadatta, and Vṛkodara of terrible deeds blew the great conch Pauṇḍra.

**6.47.26**  King Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Kuntī, blew the Anantavijaya, while Nakula and Sahadeva blew the Sughoṣa and the Maṇipuṣpaka.

**6.47.27**  The king of Kāśi and Śaibya, and Śikhaṇḍin the great chariot-warrior, Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Virāṭa, and Sātyaki of great renown,

**6.47.28**  and the Pāñcāla prince the mighty archer, and the five sons of Draupadī—all blew their great conches and sounded their lion-roars.

**6.47.29**  That mighty din, raised there by those heroes, tumultuous, made both sky and earth resound.

**6.47.30**  Thus, O great king, the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, exultant, came together once more for battle, tormenting one another.

**6.48.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When the armies were thus drawn up, my own and the others, how did those foremost of warriors begin their onslaught?

**6.48.2**  Saṃjaya said: When the armies stood evenly arrayed, accoutred and bright with banners, beholding the host like a shoreless ocean,

**6.48.3**  in their midst stood King Duryodhana, your son, and said to all your warriors: "Fight, you who are armoured!"

**6.48.4**  Hardening their hearts to cruelty, casting away their lives, they all advanced upon the Pāṇḍavas with banners upraised.

**6.48.5**  Then there arose a tumultuous, hair-raising battle between your men and the foe, with chariots and elephants entangled together.

**6.48.6**  Arrows loosed by the chariot-warriors, gold-feathered and keen-edged, with unblunted points, fell upon the elephants and the horses.

**6.48.7**  As the battle thus raged, the mighty-armed Bhīṣma of terrible prowess, armoured, raised his bow and charged forward.

**6.48.8**  Upon the son of Subhadrā and upon Bhīmasena, upon Śini's grandson the great chariot-warrior, upon Kekaya and Virāṭa, and upon Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata—

**6.48.9**  upon these heroes among men, and upon the Cedis and Matsyas all around, the aged grandsire of the Kurus rained showers of arrows.

**6.48.10**  The great formation shook in that meeting of heroes, and there was a vast confusion among all the armies.

**6.48.11**  With their banners and elephants cast down, their finest steeds slain, and their ranks of chariots scattered, so the Pāṇḍavas became.

**6.48.12**  But Arjuna, the tiger among men, seeing Bhīṣma the great chariot-warrior, said in wrath to the Vṛṣṇi hero: "Drive to where the grandsire is.

**6.48.13**  This Bhīṣma, sorely enraged, O Vṛṣṇi hero, will surely destroy my host, intent upon Duryodhana's good.

**6.48.14**  This Droṇa, and Kṛpa, Śalya, and Vikarṇa, O Janārdana, and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra united, with Duryodhana at their head,

**6.48.15**  will slaughter the Pāñcālas, guarded by that firm-bowed one. Therefore I shall go against Bhīṣma for the sake of the army, O Janārdana."

**6.48.16**  To him Vāsudeva said: "Be on your guard, Dhanaṃjaya. I am bringing you, O hero, against the grandsire's chariot."

**6.48.17**  Having spoken thus, the descendant of Śūra brought that world-famed chariot up against Bhīṣma's chariot, O lord of men.

**6.48.18**  With its many fluttering pennons, with horses the colour of cranes, with its great and most terrible roaring ape-banner upraised, with the mighty thunder of his chariot bright as the sun,

**6.48.19**  the Pāṇḍava, smiting the Kaurava host and the Śūrasenas, came on swiftly, loosing arrows—he who dispels the grief of friends.

**6.48.20**  As he rushed on with violence like a rutting elephant, terrifying the heroes in battle and felling them with his shafts,

**6.48.21**  Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, guarded by the Saindhava chiefs and by the Easterners, Sauvīras, and Kekayas, suddenly came forth to meet Arjuna.

**6.48.22**  For what chariot-warrior, other than the Kuru grandsire, or Droṇa, or the son of the Sun, was fit to encounter the wielder of Gāṇḍīva?

**6.48.23**  Then Bhīṣma, O great king, the grandsire of the Kauravas, covered Arjuna with seventy-seven iron arrows.

**6.48.24**  Droṇa pierced him with twenty-five, Kṛpa with fifty arrows, Duryodhana with sixty-four, and Śalya with nine arrows,

**6.48.25**  and the lord of the Sindhus with nine, and Śakuni too with five, and Vikarṇa with ten broad-headed shafts, O king, pierced the Pāṇḍava.

**6.48.26**  Pierced on every side by those whetted arrows, the mighty archer of great arms did not waver, like a mountain being split.

**6.48.27**  That tiger among men, in turn, pierced Bhīṣma with twenty-five arrows and Kṛpa with nine, Droṇa with sixty and Vikarṇa with three arrows,

**6.48.28**  and the son of Ṛtāyana with three shafts and the king with five did the diademed one of immeasurable spirit strike back, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.48.29**  Sātyaki and Virāṭa, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata, and the sons of Draupadī, and Abhimanyu, surrounded Dhanaṃjaya.

**6.48.30**  Then the Pāñcāla prince, joined with the Somakas, rained arrows upon Droṇa the mighty archer, who was intent on the welfare of the son of Gaṅgā.

**6.48.31**  But Bhīṣma, foremost of chariot-warriors, swiftly pierced the Pāṇḍava with eighty whetted arrows, and at that your men cried out in joy.

**6.48.32**  Hearing the clamour of those exultant men, the valorous lion among chariot-warriors, himself exultant, then plunged into their midst.

**6.48.33**  Reaching the midst of those lions among chariot-warriors, Dhanaṃjaya sported with his bow, O king, making targets of those great chariot-warriors.

**6.48.34**  Then King Duryodhana, the lord of men, seeing his own army hard pressed by Pārtha in the fray, said to Bhīṣma:

**6.48.35**  "This mighty son of Pāṇḍu, dear sire, joined with Kṛṣṇa, cuts away the very root of our whole striving army, even while you, the son of Gaṅgā, and Droṇa, foremost of chariot-warriors, yet live.

**6.48.36**  For on your account even this Karṇa, the great chariot-warrior, has laid down his weapons and does not fight Pārtha in battle, though ever desiring my welfare.

**6.48.37**  Act, then, O son of Gaṅgā, so that Phalguna may be slain." Thus addressed, O king, your sire Devavrata said, "Shame upon the warrior's code!"—and drove against Pārtha's chariot.

**6.48.38**  Beholding the two white-horsed warriors locked in combat, O king, the kings raised loud lion-roars and conch-blasts, O Bhārata.

**6.48.39**  The son of Droṇa, and Duryodhana, and Vikarṇa your son, surrounding Bhīṣma in the battle, stood ready to fight, O venerable one.

**6.48.40**  In the same way all the Pāṇḍavas, surrounding Dhanaṃjaya, stood ready for the great battle; and then the fight began.

**6.48.41**  The son of Gaṅgā struck Pārtha in the battle with nine arrows, and Arjuna struck him back with ten that pierced the vitals.

**6.48.42**  Then, with a thousand well-aimed arrows, Arjuna the Pāṇḍava, glorious in battle, hemmed in all the quarters around Bhīṣma.

**6.48.43**  But that net of arrows, O Kaurava, Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu warded off in turn with his own net of arrows against Pārtha.

**6.48.44**  Both supremely exultant, both rejoicing in the battle, they fought as equals, each seeking to answer stroke with stroke.

**6.48.45**  The nets of arrows loosed from Bhīṣma's bow were seen breaking apart in masses, shattered by Arjuna's shafts.

**6.48.46**  And likewise the nets of arrows loosed by Arjuna, struck piecemeal by the son of Gaṅgā's shafts, fell down upon the earth.

**6.48.47**  Arjuna struck Bhīṣma with twenty-five sharp arrows, and Bhīṣma too, in the battle, pierced Pārtha with thirty arrows.

**6.48.48**  Piercing each other's horses and both their banners, the two mighty subduers of foes, those great-strengthed warriors, sported also with each other's chariot-poles and chariot-wheels.

**6.48.49**  Then, O great king, the wrathful Bhīṣma, foremost of those who strike, smote Vāsudeva with three arrows in the breast.

**6.48.50**  Pierced by the shafts loosed from Bhīṣma's bow, the slayer of Madhu shone in the battle, O king, like a kiṃśuka tree in full flower.

**6.48.51**  Then Arjuna, exceedingly enraged at seeing Mādhava wounded, pierced Gāṅgeya's charioteer in the encounter with three arrows.

**6.48.52**  Though both those heroes strove for each other's destruction, they could not, in that battle, gain any opening upon one another.

**6.48.53**  Through the skill and deftness of their charioteers they displayed in manifold ways their wondrous wheeling courses, advancing and retreating again.

**6.48.54**  Watching for an opening to strike, those two great car-warriors, O king, kept their stations again and again upon the intervening paths.

**6.48.55**  Both of them raised the blast of their conches mingled with lion-roars, and likewise those two great car-warriors made the twang of their bows resound.

**6.48.56**  By the blast of their conches and the din of their chariot-wheels the earth was suddenly rent, and it quaked and roared.

**6.48.57**  None, O bull of the Bharatas, could perceive any opening in either of them; both were mighty and brave in battle, and equal to one another.

**6.48.58**  By his ensign alone could the Kauravas there recognize Bhīṣma, and likewise the sons of Pāṇḍu knew Pārtha by his ensign alone.

**6.48.59**  Beholding such prowess in those two best of men, O king, all beings were struck with wonder in that battle, O Bhārata.

**6.48.60**  None could see a weak point in either of them in the fight, O Bhārata, even as in a man steadfast in righteousness none can find any fault anywhere.

**6.48.61**  Both of them became invisible behind their net of arrows, and both again quickly became visible in the battle.

**6.48.62**  There the gods together with the Gandharvas and the Cāraṇas, along with the seers, spoke to one another, beholding the prowess of those two.

**6.48.63**  These two great car-warriors, when roused in battle, cannot be vanquished in any wise, not even by all the worlds together with their gods, Asuras, and Gandharvas.

**6.48.64**  This battle, a marvel in the worlds, is exceedingly wondrous; battles such as this shall never come to pass in any way again.

**6.48.65**  Nor can Bhīṣma be conquered in the fight by the wise Pārtha, while bow in hand and stationed on his car he showers his shafts in battle.

**6.48.66**  And likewise the son of Pāṇḍu, that bowman hard to assail even by the gods in battle, Bhīṣma cannot venture to vanquish in the fray.

**6.48.67**  Such words, laden with praise of Gāṅgeya and Arjuna in their combat, were heard rising up here and there, O lord of peoples.

**6.48.68**  Then thy warriors and those of the Pāṇḍavas, O Bhārata, smote one another in the battle amid the prowess of those two.

**6.48.69**  With keen-edged swords and bright battle-axes, with arrows and with many other weapons of various kinds, the heroes of both armies cut one another down in the fight.

**6.48.70**  While that dreadful and most cruel battle was thus raging, O king, there came about a great encounter between Droṇa and the Pāñcāla prince.

**6.49.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How did the great bowman Droṇa and the Pāñcāla son of Pṛṣata, both intent, come together in battle? Recount that to me, Saṃjaya.

**6.49.2**  I deem destiny supreme, even above manly effort, Saṃjaya, since Bhīṣma the son of Śantanu could not overcome the son of Pāṇḍu in the fight.

**6.49.3**  For Bhīṣma, enraged in battle, could slay the worlds with all that moves and moves not; how then could he not, by his own might, overcome the son of Pāṇḍu in the fight, Saṃjaya?

**6.49.4**  Saṃjaya said: Hear, O king, with a steady mind, of this most dreadful battle. The son of Pāṇḍu cannot be vanquished even by the gods led by Vāsava.

**6.49.5**  Droṇa fought against Dhṛṣṭadyumna with sharp arrows, and with a broad-headed shaft struck down his charioteer from the chariot-box.

**6.49.6**  Then in his wrath, O venerable one, he afflicted the four steeds of Dhṛṣṭadyumna with four excellent shafts.

**6.49.7**  Thereupon the hero Dhṛṣṭadyumna, laughing, pierced Droṇa with ninety sharp arrows, and cried out, 'Stand! Stand!'

**6.49.8**  Then once more the immeasurable and valorous son of Bharadvāja covered with his shafts the wrathful Dhṛṣṭadyumna.

**6.49.9**  And he took up a dreadful arrow for the slaying of Pṛṣata's son, of touch like Śakra's thunderbolt, like a second rod of Death.

**6.49.10**  A great cry of dismay arose from all the host, O Bhārata, when they saw that shaft fitted to the string by the son of Bharadvāja in the encounter.

**6.49.11**  Therein we beheld a wondrous feat of manliness in Dhṛṣṭadyumna, that the hero alone in battle stood firm as an unmoving mountain.

**6.49.12**  And that blazing, dreadful arrow speeding toward him, his own death, he cut asunder, and let loose a shower of arrows upon the son of Bharadvāja.

**6.49.13**  Then all the Pāñcālas together with the Pāṇḍavas raised a shout, beholding that most difficult feat accomplished by Dhṛṣṭadyumna.

**6.49.14**  Then that valiant one, longing for Droṇa's death, hurled a swift lance adorned with gold and lapis.

**6.49.15**  That gold-decked lance rushing suddenly upon him, the son of Bharadvāja, as if laughing, cut into three pieces in the battle.

**6.49.16**  Seeing his lance struck down, the valorous Dhṛṣṭadyumna poured showers of arrows upon Droṇa, O lord of men.

**6.49.17**  Then the illustrious Droṇa, warding off that shower of arrows, cut asunder in the middle the bow of Drupada's son.

**6.49.18**  His bow being severed, that mighty and illustrious one hurled at Droṇa in the battle a heavy mace made of mountain-stuff.

**6.49.19**  That mace, loosed with force, sped on, eager to slay Droṇa; therein we beheld a wondrous feat of prowess in the son of Bharadvāja.

**6.49.20**  By his deftness he turned aside that gold-adorned mace, and having turned it aside, he sent forth arrows upon Pṛṣata's son.

**6.49.21**  Well-sharpened broad-headed shafts, dyed yellow, gold-feathered, and whetted on stone — they pierced his armor and drank his blood in the fight.

**6.49.22**  Then the high-souled Dhṛṣṭadyumna, taking up another bow and pressing forward against Droṇa in the fight, pierced him with five arrows.

**6.49.23**  Then those two bulls among men, smeared with blood, shone forth, O king, like two kiṃśuka trees in flower in the springtime.

**6.49.24**  Then, full of indignation, O king, and pressing forward at the head of the host, Droṇa again cut asunder the bow of Drupada's son.

**6.49.25**  Then the immeasurable one overwhelmed him, his bow severed, with smooth-jointed arrows, as a cloud drenches a mountain with rain.

**6.49.26**  And with a broad-headed shaft he struck down his charioteer from the chariot-box, and then his four steeds with four sharp arrows —

**6.49.27**  these he felled in the battle, and he uttered a lion-roar; then with another broad-headed shaft he cut the bow from his hand.

**6.49.28**  His bow severed, his car gone, his steeds slain, his charioteer killed, he descended mace in hand, making known his great manhood.

**6.49.29**  That mace, too, Droṇa swiftly struck down with his shafts, O Bhārata, before he had even descended from the car; that seemed a wonder.

**6.49.30**  Then that strong, fair-armed hero, seizing a broad shield marked with a hundred moons and gleaming bright, and a broad celestial sword,

**6.49.31**  rushed forward with speed, longing to slay Droṇa, as a lion seeking prey rushes upon a rutting elephant in the forest.

**6.49.32**  Therein we beheld a wondrous feat of manliness in the son of Bharadvāja — his deftness, his mastery of weapons, and the strength of his arms, O Bhārata —

**6.49.33**  in that he held off Pṛṣata's son with a shower of arrows, so that, mighty though he was, he could not advance in the battle.

**6.49.34**  There we beheld the great car-warrior Dhṛṣṭadyumna standing firm, warding off the floods of arrows with his shield, like one of practiced hand.

**6.49.35**  Then the mighty-armed and strong Bhīma suddenly rushed up, rendering aid in the battle to the high-souled son of Pṛṣata.

**6.49.36**  He pierced Droṇa, O king, with seven sharp arrows, and then quickly mounted Pṛṣata's son upon another car.

**6.49.37**  Then King Duryodhana urged on the Kaliṅga, attended by a great host, for the protection of the son of Bharadvāja.

**6.49.38**  Then that great host of the Kaliṅgas, O lord of men, advanced swiftly upon Bhīma at thy son's command.

**6.49.39**  And Droṇa too, that foremost of car-warriors, leaving the Pāñcāla prince, gave battle to the aged Virāṭa and Drupada, who had joined together; and Dhṛṣṭadyumna in the battle went forth to the King of Righteousness.

**6.49.40**  Then there arose a tumultuous, hair-raising battle between the Kaliṅgas and the high-souled Bhīma, world-destroying, of dreadful form, and terrifying.

**6.50.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Being thus commanded, how did the Kaliṅga, that leader of hosts, fight in battle, together with his army, against the mighty Bhīmasena of wondrous deeds —

**6.50.2**  that hero ranging with his mace like Death himself with rod in hand? How did the Kaliṅga, with his army, give him battle in the encounter?

**6.50.3**  Saṃjaya said: Thus addressed by thy son, O king of kings, that mighty one, guarded by a great host, set forth against Bhīma's car.

**6.50.4**  That great host of the Kaliṅgas rushing suddenly on, thick with chariots, elephants, and horses, its mighty weapons raised —

**6.50.5**  Bhīmasena assailed that army of the Kaliṅgas, O Bhārata, and Ketumat the Niṣāda prince advancing with the Cedis.

**6.50.6**  Then Śrutāyus, sorely enraged, together with King Ketumat, encountered Bhīma in the battle, while the Cedi ranks were arrayed.

**6.50.7**  With many thousands of cars, the lord of the Kaliṅgas, and Ketumat with ten thousand elephants and with the Niṣādas, beset Bhīmasena, O king, on every side in the battle.

**6.50.8**  And the Cedis, Matsyas, and Karūṣas, with Bhīmasena at their head, suddenly advanced upon the Niṣādas together with their kings.

**6.50.9**  Then there arose a battle of dreadful form and terrifying, the warriors in their lust to slay one another no longer knowing their own.

**6.50.10**  Then dreadful was Bhīma's sudden battle with the foe, O great king, like that of Indra with the great host of the Daityas.

**6.50.11**  From that army warring in the encounter, O Bhārata, there arose a mighty roar like that of the thundering sea.

**6.50.12**  The warriors then, cutting one another down, O lord of peoples, made the whole earth strewn over and red as the blood of a hare.

**6.50.13**  In their eagerness to slay, the warriors knew neither their own nor the foe; the heroes, hard to vanquish in battle, struck down even their own and their own kin.

**6.50.14**  A very great slaughter there was of the few against the many — of the Cedis against the Kaliṅgas and the Niṣādas, O lord of peoples.

**6.50.15**  Having put forth their manly might to the utmost, the strong Cedis abandoned Bhīmasena and turned back.

**6.50.16**  Though beset by all the Kaliṅgas when the Cedis had withdrawn, the son of Pāṇḍu, relying on the strength of his own arms, did not turn back.

**6.50.17**  The mighty Bhīmasena stirred not from his place upon the car, and overwhelmed the host of the Kaliṅgas with his keen arrows.

**6.50.18**  But the Kaliṅga king, a mighty archer, and his son, a great car-warrior renowned as Śakradeva, struck at the Pāṇḍava with their arrows.

**6.50.19**  Then mighty-armed Bhīma, shaking his gleaming bow, gave battle to the Kāliṅgas, relying on the strength of his own arms.

**6.50.20**  But Śakradeva, loosing many shafts in the fray, slew Bhīmasena's horses with his arrows, and rained down showers of arrows like a cloud at the end of the hot season.

**6.50.21**  But Bhīmasena of great might, standing upon his car whose horses were slain, hurled at Śakradeva a mace made wholly of refined iron.

**6.50.22**  Struck down by it, O king, the son of the Kaliṅga king fell from his car, together with his banner and his charioteer, to the surface of the earth.

**6.50.23**  Seeing his own son slain, the lord of the Kaliṅgas hemmed in Bhīma on every side with many thousands of cars.

**6.50.24**  Then mighty-armed Bhīma, casting aside his heavy great mace, drew forth his sword, eager to perform a terrible deed.

**6.50.25**  And he took up, O king, O bull among men, a peerless shield of bull's hide, adorned with stars and half-moons wrought of gold.

**6.50.26**  Then the Kaliṅga king, enraged, stroked his bowstring, and taking up one dreadful arrow like the venom of a serpent, the lord of men sent it forth at Bhīmasena, longing to slay him.

**6.50.27**  That sharp arrow speeding swiftly upon him, Bhīmasena cut in two, O king, with his broad sword, and shouted aloud in his joy, striking terror into the host.

**6.50.28**  Then the Kaliṅga king, enraged, swiftly hurled at Bhīmasena in the encounter fourteen lances whetted upon stone.

**6.50.29**  Before they reached him, while they were still in the air, the mighty-armed Pāṇḍava cut them apart at once, O king, unflustered, with his excellent sword.

**6.50.30**  Having shorn through those fourteen lances in the battle, Bhīma, that bull among men, fixed his gaze upon Bhānumat and rushed at him.

**6.50.31**  But Bhānumat then, covering Bhīma with a shower of arrows, raised a mighty roar, making the vault of heaven resound.

**6.50.32**  Bhīma did not endure that lion's roar in the great battle, and so he too bellowed forth a loud cry with a mighty voice.

**6.50.33**  Terrified by that sound, the army of the Kaliṅgas, O bull of the Bharatas, no longer reckoned Bhīma in the fight to be a man.

**6.50.34**  Then Bhīma, O great king, having roared a vast cry, sword in hand, leapt swiftly and, by its two tusks, seized that finest of elephants.

**6.50.35**  Then he mounted upon the middle of that king of elephants, O sire, and with his broad sword cut Bhānumat asunder through the middle.

**6.50.36**  Having slain the prince fighting close beside him, that subduer of foes brought down his sword upon the elephant's shoulder, which could bear a heavy load.

**6.50.37**  With its shoulder cleft, that lord of the elephant herd, crying out, fell, like a crag-crested mountain washed away by the rush of a river.

**6.50.38**  Then, O Bhārata, the descendant of Bharata, leaping down from that elephant, sword in hand, undaunted in spirit, stood mailed upon the ground.

**6.50.39**  He ranged along many paths, fearless, felling elephants, and appeared on every side like a whirling wheel of fire.

**6.50.40**  Overpowering them, smiting down troops of horses, elephants, ranks of cars, and bands of foot-soldiers, drenched in blood, Bhīma ranged like a hawk through the battle, terrible amid the host of his foes.

**6.50.41**  Swift as he was, he severed the bodies and the heads of those elephant-warriors in the encounter with his keen-edged sword.

**6.50.42**  A single foot-soldier, enraged, swelling the terror of his foes, he bewildered them then, like Yama, the ender, at the time of doom.

**6.50.43**  And those bewildered men, roaring, rushed upon him in the battle, as he ranged with utmost speed, sword in hand, through the great fray.

**6.50.44**  Cutting through the car-poles and yokes of the car-warriors in the battle, that mighty crusher of foes slew the car-warriors as well.

**6.50.45**  Bhīmasena, ranging along very many paths, was seen everywhere; the Pāṇḍava displayed the wheeling, the upward whirling, the brandishing, the leaping, the springing forth, the gliding, the onset and the upward thrust.

**6.50.46**  Some, cleft by the point of the sword of the high-souled Pāṇḍava, cried out, their vitals pierced, and fell down lifeless.

**6.50.47**  And some elephants, their tusk-tips and trunks lopped off, their frontal lobes shattered, riderless, smote their own ranks, O Bhārata, and fell upon the earth, bellowing great cries.

**6.50.48**  And severed lances and bows and the heads of elephant-drivers, gay-colored housings and girths gleaming with gold,

**6.50.49**  and neck-ornaments and spears, banners and barbed darts, quivers and engines and many-hued bows,

**6.50.50**  and bright fire-pots, and goads together with elephant-hooks, and bells of many kinds, O king, and gold-cored sword-hilts, falling and fallen, together with their riders — all these we saw.

**6.50.51**  With elephants slain, their limb-guards and trunks shorn away, the earth in that place was strewn over with the fallen, as though with mountains.

**6.50.52**  Having thus crushed those great elephants, that bull among men trampled the horses and struck down the finest of the horsemen, O Bhārata; and dreadful was that battle between him and them, O Bhārata.

**6.50.53**  And bits and harness-straps and whips gleaming with gold, and housings and javelins and costly swords,

**6.50.54**  and corselets and shields and gaily-colored saddle-cloths, scattered here and there, were seen in that great battle.

**6.50.55**  With manifold horse-trappings and with bright weapons he made the earth bestrewn, as though with many-colored flowers.

**6.50.56**  Leaping upon and seizing certain car-warriors, the mighty Pāṇḍava struck them down with his sword, banners and all.

**6.50.57**  As that glorious one again and again sprang up in all directions, and ran, and ranged along wondrous paths, the people in the battle marveled.

**6.50.58**  Some he slew with his foot; others he seized and dashed to the ground; others he cut down with his sword; and yet others he terrified with his roar.

**6.50.59**  Others again he felled to the ground by the force of his thighs; and some, merely beholding him, in their terror passed into death.

**6.50.60**  Thus that numerous army of the swift Kaliṅgas, having ringed Bhīṣma about in the battle, rushed upon Bhīmasena.

**6.50.61**  Then, O bull of the Bharatas, fixing his gaze upon Śrutāyus at the head of the Kaliṅga host, Bhīmasena advanced against him.

**6.50.62**  Seeing him coming on, the Kaliṅga king of immeasurable spirit pierced Bhīmasena in the breast with nine arrows.

**6.50.63**  Struck by the Kaliṅga's arrows, like an elephant tormented by the goad, Bhīmasena blazed up with wrath, as fire blazes with fuel.

**6.50.64**  Then Aśoka, bringing up a car adorned with gold, the charioteer furnished Bhīma with that car.

**6.50.65**  Swiftly mounting that car, the son of Kuntī, slayer of foes, rushed upon the Kaliṅga king, crying, 'Stand! Stand!'

**6.50.66**  Then mighty Śrutāyus, enraged, sent forth sharp arrows at Bhīma, displaying the lightness of his hand.

**6.50.67**  Sorely struck by nine sharp arrows loosed from that excellent bow by the Kaliṅga king, O king, the glorious Bhīma grew exceedingly wrathful, like a serpent beaten with a staff.

**6.50.68**  And enraged, that best of the mighty, the son of Pṛthā, Bhīma, drawing his bow with force, slew the Kaliṅga king with seven iron shafts.

**6.50.69**  And with two razor-edged arrows he dispatched to the abode of Yama the two mighty wheel-guards of the Kaliṅga king, Satyadeva and Satya.

**6.50.70**  Then again, Bhīma of immeasurable spirit, with three sharp iron arrows, sent Ketumat in the battle to the abode of Yama.

**6.50.71**  Then the Kaliṅgas, enraged, the warriors hemmed in the impatient Bhīmasena with many thousands of troops.

**6.50.72**  Then with spears and maces and swords, with lances and javelins and battle-axes, the Kaliṅgas, O king, overwhelmed Bhīmasena.

**6.50.73**  Warding off that terrible shower of arrows that had risen against him, the mighty Bhīma, taking up his mace and swiftly leaping forth, led seven hundred heroes to the abode of Yama.

**6.50.74**  And once more that crusher of foes sent two thousand Kaliṅgas to the world of death; and that was as though a marvel.

**6.50.75**  Thus again and again the hero shattered those ranks of the Kaliṅgas in the battle, with his gaze upon Bhīṣma of the great vow.

**6.50.76**  And the elephants, their riders slain by the high-souled Pāṇḍava, scattered through the ranks like clouds driven by the wind, trampling their own troops and bellowing, sore wounded by arrows.

**6.50.77**  Then the mighty-armed Bhīma, that strong one, blew his conch and made the hearts of all the Kāliṅga soldiery tremble.

**6.50.78**  And a stupor seized upon the Kaliṅgas, O scorcher of foes, and their soldiers and their mounts trembled in every part.

**6.50.79**  By Bhīma in the battle, O king, ranging on every side like a lordly elephant along many paths, running here and there, again and again springing up, a bewilderment was wrought among them.

**6.50.80**  And the host, panic-stricken with fear of Bhīmasena, quivered, churned and broken open, like a great lake stirred by a crocodile.

**6.50.81**  When the heroes had been terrified by Bhīma of wondrous deeds, some rallying back and some fleeing away in companies,

**6.50.82**  while all the Kāliṅga warriors were thus, the commander of the Pāṇḍava host, the son of Pṛṣata, said to his own ranks, 'Fight!'

**6.50.83**  Hearing the words of their commander, the troops led by Śikhaṇḍin advanced toward Bhīma with their smiting bands of cars.

**6.50.84**  And the Pāṇḍava, the King of Righteousness, supported them all from behind with a great cloud-colored division of elephants.

**6.50.85**  Having thus urged on all his own ranks, the son of Pṛṣata took up the guarding of Bhīmasena's rear, as befits a good man.
