**6.50.86**  For in the world there was none other for the king of the Pāñcālas, save Bhīma and Sātyaki, dearer than his own life's breath.

**6.50.87**  The son of Pṛṣata, slayer of hostile heroes, beheld that slayer of foes, the mighty-armed Bhīmasena, ranging among the Kaliṅgas.

**6.50.88**  He roared again and again, O king, and was filled with joy, that scorcher of foes; and in the battle he blew his conch and gave forth a lion's roar.

**6.50.89**  And Bhīmasena, beholding upon the gold-adorned car of him whose horses were dove-gray the banner of the kovidāra tree, took heart again.

**6.50.90**  But Dhṛṣṭadyumna of immeasurable spirit, seeing Bhīmasena beset by the Kaliṅgas, advanced in the battle to his rescue.

**6.50.91**  Sātyaki, seeing from afar those two, Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Vṛkodara, the high-minded heroes giving battle to the Kaliṅgas in the fray,

**6.50.92**  the grandson of Śini, that best of victors, having sped thither swiftly, that bull among men, took up the guarding of the rear of the son of Pṛthā and the son of Pṛṣata.

**6.50.93**  There, working havoc, his bow taken up, abiding in a terrible mood, he slew his foes in the battle.

**6.50.94**  And there Bhīma set flowing a river, sprung from the Kaliṅgas, miry with flesh and blood, streaming with gore.

**6.50.95**  Between the host of the Kaliṅgas and that of the Pāṇḍavas, the mighty Bhīmasena crossed over that river most hard to cross.

**6.50.96**  Seeing Bhīmasena thus, O king, your men cried out, 'This is Death himself, in the form of Bhīma, doing battle with the Kaliṅgas!'

**6.50.97**  Then Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, hearing that uproar in the battle, made haste against Bhīma, his ranks arrayed on every side.

**6.50.98**  At him rushed Sātyaki, Bhīmasena, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata, charging upon Bhīṣma's chariot adorned with gold.

**6.50.99**  Surrounding on every side the impetuous son of Gaṅgā in the fray, they all swiftly struck Bhīṣma, each with three terrible arrows.

**6.50.100**  Your father Devavrata pierced them all in return—those great bowmen straining at their utmost—with three straight-flying shafts apiece.

**6.50.101**  Then, holding off those mighty car-warriors with a thousand arrows, he slew with his shafts Bhīma's steeds caparisoned in golden trappings.

**6.50.102**  Standing in his chariot whose horses were slain, the valorous Bhīmasena hurled a lance with force against the chariot of the son of Gaṅgā.

**6.50.103**  That lance, before ever it reached him, your father Devavrata cut into three in the battle, and it fell shattered to the earth.

**6.50.104**  Then, seizing a heavy mace of tempered iron, the mighty Bhīmasena leaped swiftly down from his chariot, O bull among men.

**6.50.105**  And Sātyaki too, at once, wishing to please Bhīma, brought down with his shafts the charioteer of the aged Kuru.

**6.50.106**  But Bhīṣma, foremost of car-warriors, when his charioteer was slain, was carried off from the field of battle by those horses that ran like the wind.

**6.50.107**  Then, O king, when he of the great vows had been borne away, Bhīmasena blazed up like a fire grown high as it consumes dry brushwood.

**6.50.108**  Having slain all the Kāliṅgas, he stood in the midst of the host, and none of your warriors dared confront him, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.50.109**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna, foremost of car-warriors, taking the illustrious one up into his own chariot, bore him away in the sight of all the armies.

**6.50.110**  Honored by the Pāñcālas and the Matsyas, O bull of the Bharatas, having embraced Dhṛṣṭadyumna, he then came to Sātyaki.

**6.50.111**  Then Sātyaki of true valor, the tiger of the Yadus, gladdening him, spoke to Bhīmasena while Dhṛṣṭadyumna looked on:

**6.50.112**  By good fortune the king of the Kaliṅgas, and the prince Ketumān, and Śakradeva the Kāliṅga, and the Kaliṅgas themselves are slain in the fight.

**6.50.113**  By the strength and valor of your own arm, the great battle-array of the Kaliṅgas, thronged with elephants, horses, and chariots, has been crushed by you alone.

**6.50.114**  Having spoken thus, the long-armed grandson of Śini, the tamer of foes, springing from chariot to chariot, embraced the son of Pāṇḍu.

**6.50.115**  Then, mounting his own chariot once more, that great car-warrior, enraged, slew your men, lending strength to Bhīma's force.

**6.51.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the greater part of the afternoon of that day had passed, O Bhārata, amid the great destruction of chariots, elephants, horses, foot-soldiers, and cavalry,

**6.51.2**  the prince of Pāñcāla engaged with these three great car-warriors—the son of Droṇa, Śalya, and the high-souled Kṛpa.

**6.51.3**  That mighty heir of the Pāñcālas struck down the world-famed steeds of the son of Droṇa with ten sharp, swift-flying arrows.

**6.51.4**  Then the son of Droṇa, his horses slain, quickly mounted Śalya's chariot and rained arrows upon the heir of the Pāñcālas.

**6.51.5**  Seeing Dhṛṣṭadyumna locked in combat with the son of Droṇa, O Bhārata, the son of Subhadrā rushed up at once, scattering whetted shafts.

**6.51.6**  He pierced Śalya with twenty-five arrows, Kṛpa with nine, and Aśvatthāman with eight, O bull among men.

**6.51.7**  Then the son of Droṇa swiftly pierced the son of Arjuna with a feathered shaft, Śalya with twelve, and Kṛpa with three whetted arrows.

**6.51.8**  And Lakṣmaṇa, your grandson, advanced exultantly against your grandson who stood there, and thereupon a battle ensued.

**6.51.9**  Then Duryodhana's son, full of wrath, pierced the son of Subhadrā with nine arrows in the fight, O king, and it seemed a marvel.

**6.51.10**  But Abhimanyu, enraged, O bull of the Bharatas, quick of hand, pierced his cousin with fifty arrows, O king.

**6.51.11**  Then Lakṣmaṇa with a feathered shaft cut his bow at the grip, O great king, and at that the people raised a great shout.

**6.51.12**  Casting aside that severed bow, the son of Subhadrā, slayer of hostile heroes, took up another splendid bow of swifter force.

**6.51.13**  Those two bulls among men, eager in that battle, each seeking to repay the other blow for blow, struck one another with sharp, keen arrows.

**6.51.14**  Then King Duryodhana, lord of men, seeing his son, that great car-warrior, hard pressed by your grandson, went thither.

**6.51.15**  When your son had turned to the fight, all the lords of men hemmed in the son of Arjuna on every side with their array of chariots.

**6.51.16**  Surrounded by those heroes, hard to conquer in battle, that hero, whose prowess equaled Kṛṣṇa's, was not shaken at all, O king.

**6.51.17**  Then Dhanaṃjaya, seeing the son of Subhadrā locked in combat there, rushed forward in fury, longing to rescue his own son.

**6.51.18**  Then the kings, with their chariots, elephants, and horses, led by Bhīṣma and Droṇa, advanced together against Savyasācin.

**6.51.19**  The dust of the earth, suddenly thrown up by elephants, horses, chariots, and cavalry, rose dense to the path of the sun and was seen on high.

**6.51.20**  Those thousands of elephants and hundreds of kings, coming within the range of his arrows, could in no way press forward.

**6.51.21**  All creatures wailed, the quarters grew dark, and a fierce, dreadful calamity of the Kurus was plainly seen.

**6.51.22**  Neither the sky, nor the quarters, nor the earth, nor the sun could be made out, O best of the Bharatas, for the masses of arrows loosed by the diademed one.

**6.51.23**  Some leaders of chariot-bands were seen with their banners and elephants brought low, their steeds slain, the warriors sorely stricken, their chariots scattered in flight.

**6.51.24**  Other car-warriors, deprived of their chariots, were seen here and there running on every side, still bearing their weapons, with armlets upon their arms.

**6.51.25**  Riders abandoning their horses, and mahouts their tusked beasts, fled in every direction out of fear of Arjuna, O king.

**6.51.26**  Lords of men, struck by Arjuna, were seen fallen and falling from their chariots, their elephants, and their horses.

**6.51.27**  Arms raised on high, grasping maces and swords, O lord of the people, holding lances and quivers, arrows and bows,

**6.51.28**  and goads and standards—these of the warriors Arjuna hewed off here and there with his fierce arrows, wearing then a dreadful form.

**6.51.29**  Of massive iron clubs and of war-hammers, my lord, of lances and slings and swords in the battle,

**6.51.30**  of sharp battle-axes and javelins, O Bhārata, and of shattered mail-coats and cuirasses upon the ground,

**6.51.31**  of banners and shields and fans on every side, of parasols with golden staves and yak-tail whisks, O Bhārata,

**6.51.32**  and of goads and whips and harness-straps, my lord—heaps of these were seen strewn there upon the field of battle.

**6.51.33**  There was not a single man of your army, O Bhārata, who could in any wise advance against the heroic Arjuna in battle.

**6.51.34**  For whoever advanced against Pārtha in the fight, O lord of the people, he was sent to the other world by his sharp shafts.

**6.51.35**  As your warriors fled in every direction, Arjuna and Vāsudeva blew their excellent conches.

**6.51.36**  Seeing that host broken, your father Devavrata spoke in the battle to the hero, the son of Bharadvāja, as if with a smile:

**6.51.37**  This heroic son of Pāṇḍu, joined with Kṛṣṇa, the mighty one, deals with the armies just as Dhanaṃjaya would.

**6.51.38**  For he can in no way be conquered today in battle, since the form he wears appears like Yama the all-ending Death.

**6.51.39**  Nor can this great host be turned back: behold, this army flees, each man only watching the other run.

**6.51.40**  And the sun is now reaching the best of mountains, where it sets, as if withdrawing utterly the forms of all the world.

**6.51.41**  I think the hour for withdrawal has come, O bull among men; our warriors, weary and afraid, will not fight at all.

**6.51.42**  Having spoken thus to Droṇa, the best of teachers, Bhīṣma, that great car-warrior, then ordered the withdrawal of your forces.

**6.51.43**  Then came the withdrawal of the armies, both yours and theirs, O Bhārata, as the sun went down and the hour of twilight set in.

**6.52.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the night had brightened into dawn, Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu then gave orders for the marshalling of the troops, O Bhārata.

**6.52.2**  And the son of Śaṃtanu then formed the great battle-array of the Garuḍa, Bhīṣma, the grandsire of the Kurus, longing for the victory of your sons.

**6.52.3**  At Garuḍa's beak was your father Devavrata himself; as its two eyes were the son of Bharadvāja and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas.

**6.52.4**  Aśvatthāman and Kṛpa, the two illustrious ones, stood at the head, joined by the Trigartas, the Matsyas, the Kekayas, and the Vāṭadhānas.

**6.52.5**  Bhūriśravas, Śala, Śalya, and Bhagadatta, my lord, the Madrakas, the Sindhu-Sauvīras, and the men of the Five Rivers,

**6.52.6**  together with Jayadratha, were stationed at the neck; at the back was King Duryodhana, surrounded by his brothers and his followers.

**6.52.7**  Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, and the Kāmboja chief with the Śakas, formed the tail, O great king, and the Śūrasenas as well in their entirety.

**6.52.8**  The Māgadhas and the Kaliṅgas, with the hosts of the Dāśerakas, taking up the right wing of the array, stood there clad in armor.

**6.52.9**  The Kānanas and the Vikuñjas, the Muktas, and the Puṇḍrāviṣas, together with Bṛhadbala, took up the left wing.

**6.52.10**  Seeing that host marshalled, Savyasācin, the scorcher of foes, together with Dhṛṣṭadyumna, drew up his counter-array in the battle, opposing that most terrible formation with an array in the shape of a crescent moon.

**6.52.11**  Holding the right horn, Bhīmasena shone resplendent, surrounded by kings of many lands furnished with floods of diverse weapons.

**6.52.12**  Next to him came Virāṭa and Drupada, the great car-warrior; and immediately after them stood Nīla with his blue-armored troops.

**6.52.13**  Next after Nīla stood Dhṛṣṭaketu, the great car-warrior, encompassed by the Cedis, the Kāśis, the Karūṣas, and the Pauravas.

**6.52.14**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, with the Pāñcālas and the Prabhadrakas, stood in the center of the great host, ready for battle, O Bhārata.

**6.52.15**  Likewise the King of Righteousness, encompassed by his division of elephants; and then, O king, Sātyaki and the five sons of Draupadī.

**6.52.16**  Then Abhimanyu close at hand, and Irāvat beyond him, and then the son of Bhīmasena, O king, and the Kekaya princes, those great car-warriors.

**6.52.17**  Then the best of two-footed beings took the left flank, the guardian of all the world, whose own guardian was Janārdana.

**6.52.18**  Thus did the Pāṇḍavas draw up this great counter-array, for the destruction of your sons and of those who had joined their side.

**6.52.19**  Then the battle began, with chariots and elephants entangled together, as your men and the foe struck down one another.

**6.52.20**  Floods of horses and floods of chariots, O lord of peoples, were seen rushing together here and there, striking one another down.

**6.52.21**  From the streaming masses of chariots, racing onward and striking down each its own foe, there arose a tumultuous din mingled with the booming of war-drums.

**6.52.22**  In that exceedingly tumultuous clash, O Bhārata, as the heroic men slew one another—yours and theirs alike—the uproar touched the very heavens.

**6.53.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, when the armies were arrayed—both yours and the others'—Dhanaṃjaya, that surpassing chariot-warrior, smote your host of chariots, O Bhārata, felling the leaders of chariot-bands with his arrows in the battle.

**6.53.2**  Slaughtered by Pārtha as though by Time itself at the dissolution of an age, the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, straining hard in the fight, battled back against the Pāṇḍavas, seeking blazing glory and making death their only retreat.

**6.53.3**  With single-minded resolve they shattered the Pāṇḍava host again and again, O king, and were themselves shattered in turn in the combat.

**6.53.4**  With the Pāṇḍavas and the Kauravas now fleeing, now broken, now wheeling about to charge again, nothing whatever could be made out.

**6.53.5**  A dust rose up from the earth that veiled the sun, and there none could in any way distinguish the quarters or the intermediate quarters of the sky.

**6.53.6**  By guesswork, by watchwords, and by names and lineages the fighting was carried on then, here and there, O lord of peoples.

**6.53.7**  There the battle-array of the Kauravas could in no way be broken, guarded as it was by the wise Bhāradvāja, true to his word.

**6.53.8**  In like manner the great array of the Pāṇḍavas, guarded by Savyasācin and well protected by Bhīma, could not be pierced.

**6.53.9**  Sallying forth from the front of the host, the men fought there, O king, the chariots and elephants of both armies locked together in the fray.

**6.53.10**  In that great battle horsemen were brought down by horsemen with bright-pointed lances and with javelins in the fight.

**6.53.11**  Closing with a chariot-warrior, a chariot-warrior felled him with gold-adorned arrows in that exceedingly terrible battle.

**6.53.12**  Elephant-riders, locked in combat, brought down elephant-riders in companies—yours and theirs alike—with iron shafts, arrows, and lances.

**6.53.13**  Bands of foot-soldiers in the fray, exhilarated and bearing mutual wrongs, struck down foot-soldiers with slings and battle-axes.

**6.53.14**  In the encounter a foot-soldier felled a chariot-warrior, and a chariot-warrior too a foot-soldier, with sharpened weapons, on both sides alike.

**6.53.15**  Elephant-riders then brought down horsemen, and horsemen those mounted on elephants; and this seemed a marvel.

**6.53.16**  By foremost elephant-riders foot-soldiers were seen struck down here and there, and by those foot-soldiers in turn the elephant-warriors.

**6.53.17**  Bands of foot-soldiers being felled by horsemen, and bands of cavalry by foot-soldiers, were seen by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**6.53.18**  With banners cast down there, with bows and lances, with javelins and maces, with iron clubs and tremor-shafts,

**6.53.19**  with spears and gleaming variegated coats of mail, with barbed darts and goads, with bright swords, and with gold-feathered arrows,

**6.53.20**  with horse-cloths and elephant-housings and costly blankets, the earth shone, O best of the Bharatas, as though figured all over with wreaths and garlands.

**6.53.21**  With the fallen bodies of men and horses, and with elephants, in that great battle the earth became impassable, miry with flesh and blood.

**6.53.22**  The dust of the earth, drenched with the blood of battle, settled, and all the quarters became clear, O lord of men.

**6.53.23**  Headless trunks beyond counting rose up on every side, become as it were portents foretelling the destruction of the world, O Bhārata.

**6.53.24**  As that most fearsome and dreadful battle was raging, chariot-warriors were seen racing about on every side.

**6.53.25**  Then Droṇa and Bhīṣma, and Jayadratha lord of the Sindhus, Purumitra and Vikarṇa, and Śakuni son of Subala—

**6.53.26**  these, unassailable in battle and lion-like in prowess, shattered the ranks of the Pāṇḍavas again and again.

**6.53.27**  In like manner Bhīmasena too, and the rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca, Sātyaki and Cekitāna, and the sons of Draupadī, O Bhārata,

**6.53.28**  drove your men and your sons, together with all the kings, in flight upon the field, as the thirty gods rout the dānavas.

**6.53.29**  Thus those bulls among warriors, slaying one another in the fight, drenched with blood and dreadful of aspect, shone like dānavas.

**6.53.30**  Having vanquished their foes, the heroic high officers of both armies were seen like planets in the vault of heaven.

**6.53.31**  Then your son Duryodhana, with a thousand chariots, advanced upon the Pāṇḍavas in battle and upon the rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca.

**6.53.32**  In like manner all the Pāṇḍavas, with a mighty host, advanced to meet the valiant, foe-subduing Droṇa and Bhīṣma in the battle.

**6.53.33**  The Diademed One, enraged, advanced against the most capable of kings, while the son of Arjuna and Sātyaki advanced against the host of Subala's son.

**6.53.34**  Then once more there arose a hair-raising combat between your men and the foe, each striving for victory in the fight.

**6.54.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then those kings, enraged at the sight of Phalguna in the battle, surrounded him on every side with many thousands of chariots.

**6.54.2**  And having penned him in, O Bhārata, within a ring of chariots, they hemmed him about on every side with many thousands of arrows.

**6.54.3**  Bright sharp spears, maces and iron bludgeons, javelins and battle-axes, mallets and pestles too—enraged, they hurled them all in the battle against the chariot of Phalguna.

**6.54.4**  That downpour of weapons, sweeping on like a swarm of locusts, Pārtha checked on every side with his gold-adorned arrows.

**6.54.5**  Beholding there that more-than-human dexterity of Bībhatsu, the gods, dānavas, and gandharvas, the piśācas, serpents, and rākṣasas honored Phalguna, O lord of kings, crying 'Well done! Well done!'

**6.54.6**  And the valiant Gāndhāras, together with the sons of Subala, with a great host beset Sātyaki and Abhimanyu in the battle.

**6.54.7**  There the enraged followers of Subala's son, in their wrath, cut the splendid chariot of the Vārṣṇeya to bits in the fight with weapons of every kind.

**6.54.8**  But Sātyaki, scorcher of foes, abandoning his chariot amid that great peril, swiftly mounted the chariot of Abhimanyu.

**6.54.9**  The two of them, joined upon a single chariot, swiftly shattered the host of Subala's son with sharp, smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.54.10**  Droṇa and Bhīṣma, straining hard in the battle, laid waste the host of the King of Righteousness with keen, heron-feathered arrows.

**6.54.11**  Then the royal son of Dharma and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī, before the eyes of all the troops, charged the array of Droṇa.

**6.54.12**  There ensued a vast, tumultuous, hair-raising battle, as dreadful as was of old the war between the gods and the asuras.

**6.54.13**  As Bhīmasena and Ghaṭotkaca were performing mighty deeds, Duryodhana then came up and beset them both.

**6.54.14**  There we beheld the marvelous prowess of Hiḍimbā's son, in that he fought in the battle surpassing even his father, O Bhārata.

**6.54.15**  But Bhīmasena, greatly enraged, smote the unforgiving Duryodhana in the breast with a shaft, the Pāṇḍava as it were laughing the while.

**6.54.16**  Then King Duryodhana, stunned by that grievous blow, sank down upon the floor of his chariot and fell into a swoon.

**6.54.17**  Then his charioteer, perceiving him senseless, in haste bore him away from the battle, O king; and thereupon the army broke apart.

**6.54.18**  Then Bhīma, smiting with keen arrows that Kaurava host as it fled on every side, pursued it from behind.

**6.54.19**  And the son of Pṛṣata, foremost of chariot-warriors, and the Pāṇḍava son of Dharma, while Droṇa looked on and the son of Gaṅgā looked on, struck down the host with keen shafts that scatter the ranks of the foe.

**6.54.20**  That host of your son, fleeing in the battle, the great chariot-warriors Bhīṣma and Droṇa were not able to stay.

**6.54.21**  For though held back by Bhīṣma and by Droṇa, O lord of peoples, that host kept fleeing on, even as Droṇa and Bhīṣma looked on.

**6.54.22**  Then, as thousands of chariots fled this way and that, the son of Subhadrā and the bull of the Śini line, mounted upon a single chariot, hewed down the host of Subala's son on every side in the battle.

**6.54.23**  Those two then, the scion of the Śinis and the bull of the Kurus, shone forth like the moon and the sun met together in the sky on the night of the new moon.

**6.54.24**  Then Arjuna, enraged, rained upon your host, O lord of peoples, a shower of arrows, as a rain-cloud pours down its streams.

**6.54.25**  Slaughtered by Pārtha's arrows in the battle, the Kaurava host then fled, trembling with despair and terror.

**6.54.26**  Beholding them in flight, the great chariot-warriors Bhīṣma and Droṇa, wrought up and seeking Duryodhana's welfare, sought to stay them.

**6.54.27**  Then King Duryodhana, having recovered his senses, O lord of peoples, turned back that host which was fleeing on every side.

**6.54.28**  Wherever any man caught sight of your son, O Bhārata, there the great chariot-warriors among the warriors turned back.

**6.54.29**  Seeing those turn back, the other men too then took their stand—some out of rivalry with one another, O king, and others out of shame.

**6.54.30**  As they returned, their onrush was such, O lord of peoples, as is the swelling of the ocean at the rising of the moon.

**6.54.31**  Then King Suyodhana, seeing them rallied, went in haste to Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, and spoke these words.

**6.54.32**  Grandsire, mark what I shall say to you, O Bhārata. I deem it unbecoming, O Kaurava, while you yet live—

**6.54.33**  and while Droṇa, foremost of those skilled in arms, lives with his son and his friends, and Kṛpa the mighty archer—that this army of ours should flee.

**6.54.34**  The Pāṇḍavas are in no way a match for you, O king, nor in battle for Droṇa, nor for Droṇa's son, nor for Kṛpa.

**6.54.35**  Surely the sons of Pāṇḍu are objects of your favor, O grandsire, since you suffer, O hero, this army to be slaughtered.

**6.54.36**  You should have told me, O king, even before the encounter: 'I will not fight the Pāṇḍavas in battle, nor the son of Pṛṣata, nor Sātyaki.'

**6.54.37**  Hearing such a word from you, from the preceptor, and from Kṛpa, I should then and there, together with Karṇa, have pondered what was to be done.

**6.54.38**  If I am not to be forsaken by you two here in the battle, then fight, O bulls among men, with prowess befitting you.

**6.54.39**  Hearing these words, Bhīṣma, laughing again and again, his eyes rolling with anger, spoke to your son.

**6.54.40**  Many a time, O king, have I spoken to you the truthful and salutary word: the Pāṇḍavas are unconquerable in battle, even by the gods together with Vāsava.

**6.54.41**  Yet what I, an old man, am able to do this day, O best of kings, I shall do to the utmost of my power. Behold it now with your kinsmen.

**6.54.42**  This day, before the eyes of all, I shall hold back all the sons of Pāṇḍu, with their troops and their kinsmen, while the whole world looks on.

**6.54.43**  When Bhīṣma had thus spoken, O lord of men, your sons, filled with joy, blew their conches and beat their drums loudly.

**6.54.44**  And the Pāṇḍavas too, O king, on hearing that mighty din, blew their conches and sounded their kettledrums and tabours.

**6.55.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When Bhīṣma had taken his vow in that most terrible battle, having been provoked to wrath—especially by my grieving son—

**6.55.2**  what then did Bhīṣma do among the Pāṇḍavas, O Saṃjaya, and what did the Pāñcālas do against the grandsire? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.55.3**  Saṃjaya said: When the greater part of the forenoon of that day had passed, O Bhārata, and the great-souled Pāṇḍavas, having gained the advantage, were exultant,

**6.55.4**  your father Devavrata, knower of all the finer points of duty, advanced with swift horses against the host of the Pāṇḍavas, guarded by a great army and by your sons on every side.

**6.55.5**  Then there began a tumultuous, hair-raising battle between us and the Pāṇḍavas, born of your folly, O Bhārata.

**6.55.6**  From the twanging of bows and the clapping of bowstring-guards there arose a mighty sound there, as of mountains splitting apart.

**6.55.7**  "Stand! I am standing! Strike this man! Turn back! Hold firm! I am at my post! Smite away!"—such cries were heard on every side.

**6.55.8**  Upon the golden coats of mail, the diadems, and the banners there arose a clatter, like that of rocks falling upon mountains.

**6.55.9**  Heads cut off and ornamented arms, fallen by the hundreds and by the thousands, writhed upon the ground when they reached it.

**6.55.10**  Some of those foremost of men, though their heads had been struck off, stood yet with bows raised and weapons grasped firm.

**6.55.11**  A river of swift current began to flow, bearing blood, dreadful with the limbs of elephants for its rocks, its mire of flesh and gore.

**6.55.12**  Springing then from the bodies of fine horses, men, and elephants, it flowed toward the ocean of the other world, gladdening vultures and jackals.

**6.55.13**  Never was such a battle seen or heard of, O king, as that of your sons and of the Pāṇḍavas, O Bhārata.

**6.55.14**  No path remained there for chariots, so covered was it with warriors struck down in the fight and with fallen dark elephants, like mountain peaks.

**6.55.15**  With scattered, gleaming mail, with banners and parasols, O sire, that field of battle shone like the vault of heaven in autumn.

**6.55.16**  Some, pierced through by arrows and dragging their wounds in their last agony, yet fearless and mailed, rushed upon the foe in battle.

**6.55.17**  "Father! Brother! Friend! Kinsman! Comrade! My uncle! Do not abandon me!"—so cried others who had fallen in the fight.

**6.55.18**  "Charge! Come on! Do not flee! Why are you afraid? Where are you going? I stand here in the battle—fear not!"—thus others cried aloud.

**6.55.19**  There Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, his bow ever bent into a circle, loosed arrows blazing at the tips, like venomous serpents.

**6.55.20**  Making all the quarters one solid mass of arrows, that firm-vowed one slew the chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, marking out one after another, O Bhārata.

**6.55.21**  Dancing, as it were, upon his chariot-platform and displaying the swiftness of his hands, he appeared here and there, O king, like a whirling firebrand.

**6.55.22**  That single hero in battle the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas beheld, by reason of his swiftness, as if he were many hundreds of thousands.

**6.55.23**  They reckoned Bhīṣma there to have multiplied himself by magic; for having seen him in the east, men beheld him in the west.

**6.55.24**  Beholding him in the north, they saw him again in the south, O lord; thus did that hero, the son of Gaṅgā, show himself in the battle.

**6.55.25**  None among the Pāṇḍavas was able even to look upon him; they saw only the many shafts loosed from Bhīṣma's bow.

**6.55.26**  Beholding him working his feats in battle and destroying their host—your father ranging there in superhuman form—the heroes in that fight wailed aloud in many ways and grievously.

**6.55.27**  Like locusts, the kings fell, driven on by fate, by the thousands into the wrathful fire that was Bhīṣma, to their own destruction.

**6.55.28**  For no shaft of Bhīṣma's was ever in vain in that combat; piercing keenly, by reason of the multitude of bodies of men, elephants, and horses, none missed its mark.

**6.55.29**  With a single well-loosed feathered arrow he would pierce an elephant's mail and armour, as a thunderbolt cleaves the loftiest mountain.

**6.55.30**  Two and even three elephant-riders, packed close together and armoured though they were, your father slew with a single keen iron shaft.

**6.55.31**  Whoever came against Bhīṣma, that tiger among men, in the fight, was seen by me but for a moment, and then beheld struck down upon the earth.

**6.55.32**  Thus that great army of the Dharma-king, slaughtered by Bhīṣma of matchless valour, was shattered into a thousand pieces.

**6.55.33**  That mighty host, scorched by the rain of arrows, was scattered before the very eyes of Vāsudeva and of the great-souled Pārtha.

**6.55.34**  Though they strove, those heroes, tormented by Bhīṣma's arrows, could not check the great chariot-warriors who were fleeing.

**6.55.35**  Slaughtered by him whose valour equalled great Indra's, the mighty host broke, O great king, and not even two fled together.

**6.55.36**  With its men, elephants, and horses pierced through, its banners and chariot-poles fallen, the army of Pāṇḍu's sons grew senseless and broke into cries of woe.

**6.55.37**  There, compelled by the force of fate, father slew son and son slew father, and friend struck down his dear friend amid the wailing.

**6.55.38**  Others among the soldiers of Pāṇḍu's son were seen casting off their armour and fleeing with hair dishevelled, O Bhārata.

**6.55.39**  Like a herd of cattle in panic, its chariot-leaders thrown into confusion, the army of Pāṇḍu's son was then seen, raised in cries of anguish.

**6.55.40**  Seeing that army breaking apart, the son of Devakī, reining in the splendid chariot, spoke to Pārtha, the Bībhatsu:

**6.55.41**  "The hour has come, O Pārtha, that you have longed for. Strike at him, O tiger among men, unless you are bewildered by delusion!

**6.55.42**  What you declared of old, O hero, in the assembly of kings—that you would slay all the warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, headed by Bhīṣma and Droṇa,

**6.55.43**  with their followers—all who would do battle with me—that make true now, O son of Kuntī, that word of yours, O scorcher of foes!"

**6.55.44**  "O Bībhatsu, behold your own army breaking on every side, and all the lords of earth in Yudhiṣṭhira's host taking to flight.

**6.55.45**  For seeing Bhīṣma in battle, like Death with jaws agape, they perish in their terror, as little beasts before a lion."

**6.55.46**  Thus addressed, Dhanaṃjaya answered Vāsudeva: "Plunging into this ocean of troops, drive the horses to where Bhīṣma is."

**6.55.47**  Then Mādhava urged on the silver-hued steeds toward where Bhīṣma's chariot stood, O king, hard to gaze upon as the radiant sun.

**6.55.48**  Then that great host of Yudhiṣṭhira rallied once more, seeing the mighty-armed Pārtha rising up against Bhīṣma in the battle.

**6.55.49**  Then Bhīṣma, foremost of the Kurus, roaring again and again like a lion, swiftly covered the chariot of Dhanaṃjaya with showers of arrows.

**6.55.50**  In a moment that chariot of his, with its horses and its driver, veiled by the great rain of shafts, was no longer to be seen.

**6.55.51**  But Vāsudeva, unflinching, holding firm with steadfast resolve, urged on those horses, sorely wounded by Bhīṣma's arrows.

**6.55.52**  Then Pārtha, taking up his celestial bow that roared like a thundercloud, cut Bhīṣma's bow with three arrows and brought it down.

**6.55.53**  His bow thus severed, the Kaurava—your father—in the mere span between two blinks strung yet another great bow.

**6.55.54**  Then with both arms he drew that bow that thundered like a cloud; but Arjuna, wrathful, cut that bow of his too.

**6.55.55**  The son of Śaṃtanu applauded that swiftness of his: "Well done, mighty-armed Pārtha! Well done, O delight of Pāṇḍu!

**6.55.56**  In you alone, Dhanaṃjaya, does this great feat befit. I am exceedingly pleased, my son—do battle with me!"

**6.55.57**  Thus praising Pārtha, the hero took up another great bow and loosed his shafts in battle against Pārtha's chariot.

**6.55.58**  Vāsudeva displayed the highest skill in the driving of the steeds; rendering his shafts vain, he wheeled deftly in circling courses.

**6.55.59**  Even so, Bhīṣma pierced both Vāsudeva and Dhanaṃjaya hard with whetted arrows in every limb, O sire.

**6.55.60**  Those two tigers among men, gashed by Bhīṣma's shafts, shone like two bellowing bulls scored and marked by each other's horns.

**6.55.61**  And once again, greatly enraged and furious in the fight, Bhīṣma with smooth-jointed arrows shut off all the quarters around the two Kṛṣṇas.

**6.55.62**  Wrathful, he made the Vārṣṇeya tremble with keen shafts, while Bhīṣma, smiling again and again, laughed aloud the while.

**6.55.63**  Then Kṛṣṇa, the mighty-armed, beholding in battle the prowess of Bhīṣma, and marking the gentleness of Pārtha's fighting,

**6.55.64**  and seeing Bhīṣma pouring forth showers of arrows ceaselessly in the fight, blazing like the sun as he reached the midst between the two armies,

**6.55.65**  striking down the best and bravest of the soldiers of Pāṇḍu's son, working as it were the end of an age upon Yudhiṣṭhira's host—

**6.55.66**  the blessed Keśava, slayer of hostile heroes, no longer able to bear it, that one of measureless soul, thought: "Yudhiṣṭhira's army is as good as no more.

**6.55.67**  For in a single day Bhīṣma could destroy in battle the gods and dānavas—how much more the sons of Pāṇḍu, with their forces and their followers!

**6.55.68**  The great army of the high-souled Pāṇḍava is fleeing; and these Kauravas, seeing the Somakas swiftly broken, rush forward in the fight exultant, gladdening the grandsire.

**6.55.69**  I myself will slay Bhīṣma this day for the Pāṇḍavas' sake, girt in mail; this burden of the great-souled Pāṇḍavas I shall lift away."

**6.55.70**  Arjuna too, struck by the keen arrows in the combat, knew not what he should do in the fight, out of reverence for Bhīṣma.

**6.55.71**  As he was thinking thus, the grandsire, enraged, once more let fly his arrows against Pārtha's chariot.

**6.55.72**  By the very multitude of those arrows all the quarters were utterly veiled; neither sky nor quarters nor earth could be seen, nor the garland-rayed sun; tumultuous winds laden with smoke arose, and all the quarters were thrown into turmoil.

**6.55.73**  Droṇa and Vikarṇa, and Jayadratha, Bhūriśravas, Kṛtavarman, and Kṛpa, King Śrutāyus and the lord of the Ambaṣṭhas, and Vinda and Anuvinda, and Sudakṣiṇa,

**6.55.74**  and all the men of the East and the hosts of the Sauvīras, the Vasātis, and the Kṣudrakas and Mālavas—obedient to the command of King Śaṃtanu's son—hastened against the Diademed One.

**6.55.75**  The grandson of Śini beheld the Diademed One being hemmed in by floods and nets of horse, foot, and chariots, by many hundreds of thousands, and by leaders of elephant-herds.

**6.55.76**  Then, seeing Arjuna and Vāsudeva, those foremost of weapon-bearers, beset on every side by foot, elephants, horse, and chariots, the hero of the Śinis swiftly advanced.

**6.55.77**  That mighty bowman, the hero of the Śinis, rushing suddenly upon those hosts, came to Arjuna's aid, as Viṣṇu once aided the slayer of Vṛtra.

**6.55.78**  Beholding the army of Yudhiṣṭhira, its masses of elephants, horses, chariots, and banners shattered, all its warriors terrified by Bhīṣma, fleeing away, the hero of the Śinis spoke:

**6.55.79**  "Whither do you go, O kṣatriyas? This is no righteous course; it was proclaimed by the ancients before all good men. Do not forsake your vow, O heroes; uphold your own warrior's duty!"

**6.55.80**  When the younger brother of Vāsava beheld those foremost of kings fleeing on every side, and marked the slackness of Pārtha's fighting, and saw Bhīṣma rising up in the battle,

**6.55.81**  then that great-souled lord of all the Daśārhas, no longer brooking it, looked upon the Kurus rushing on from every quarter and, in praise, spoke to the illustrious grandson of Śini:

**6.55.82**  "Let those who flee flee on, O hero of Śini's line; and those who yet stand firm, O Sātvata, let them go too. Behold Bhīṣma flung down from his car by me this day, and Droṇa with his hosts brought low in the battle."

**6.55.83**  "Not one warrior of that host of the Kauravas, O Sātvata, shall escape me in my wrath upon the field this day. Therefore, taking up my terrible discus, I shall take the life of him of the mighty vow."

**6.55.84**  "Having slain Bhīṣma with his hosts in the battle, and Droṇa too, that foremost of warriors, O grandson of Śini, I shall do a pleasing thing for Dhanaṃjaya, and for the king, and for Bhīma, and for the twin sons of the Aśvins."

**6.55.85**  "Having slain all the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra and those foremost of kings who hold to their side, I shall this day, rejoicing, establish King Ajātaśatru in his kingdom."

**6.55.86**  Then the son of Vasudeva, leaping down from the chariot and loosing his steeds, raised in his arm the fair-naved discus, bright as the sun, of might equal to the thunderbolt, its edge keen as a razor.

**6.55.87**  Making the earth tremble with his steps, the great-souled Kṛṣṇa rushed with fury upon Bhīṣma, swollen with pride in the fray, as a lion bent on slaying springs upon a lordly elephant blind with rut.

**6.55.88**  He, the destroyer, the younger brother of mighty Indra, in his wrath bore down upon Bhīṣma amid the host; his trailing yellow-bordered garment streaming, he blazed like a cloud in the sky wreathed about with lightning's flash.

**6.55.89**  And the Sudarśana of that descendant of Śūra shone forth—that lotus-discus whose stalk was his strong and mighty arm; like the primeval lotus of the hue of the young sun, it shone, born from the navel of Nārāyaṇa.

**6.55.90**  That lotus, opened by the rising sun of Kṛṣṇa's wrath, its petals fair-formed with edges sharp as razors, grown upon the broad lake of his very body, shone forth, with the arm of Nārāyaṇa for its stalk.

**6.55.91**  Seeing the younger brother of mighty Indra wrathful, discus in hand and roaring aloud, all beings cried out in dread, thinking, "This is the doom of the Kurus."

**6.55.92**  That Vāsudeva, discus grasped in hand, as though about to dissolve the world of the living, springing forward, the teacher of the worlds shone like the fire of Time that would consume all creatures.

**6.55.93**  Beholding that god, the best of two-footed beings, rushing on with discus seized in hand, the son of Śaṃtanu, bow and arrow in hand, standing in his chariot, without dismay addressed him:

**6.55.94**  "Come, come, O Lord of the gods, dwelling-place of the worlds! Homage be to thee, O wielder of Śārṅga and the discus! By force cast me down, O Lord of the worlds, from this noblest of cars, O refuge of all beings, here in the battle."

**6.55.95**  "Slain by thee this day, O Kṛṣṇa, mine shall be blessedness here and in the world beyond. By thy onset, O lord of the Andhakas and Vṛṣṇis, I am honored, O hero, throughout the three worlds."

**6.55.96**  Then Pārtha too, leaping in haste from his chariot and running after that hero of the Yadus, seized Hari—him of stout, long, full-hanging arms—by the arms, himself broad-armed and powerful.

**6.55.97**  Though held back, that primeval god, the great yogin, sorely wrathful indeed, Viṣṇu, with violence dragged Jiṣṇu along with him, as a great wind drags a lone tree.

**6.55.98**  But Pārtha, planting his feet with might, the diademed and garlanded one, by force held back him who was swiftly rushing toward Bhīṣma—at the tenth step, O king, and that only with difficulty.

**6.55.99**  And when Kṛṣṇa stood still, Arjuna, garlanded with a wreath of wrought gold, bowed low and, well pleased, said: "Draw back thy wrath. Thou, O Keśava, art the refuge of the Pāṇḍavas."

**6.55.100**  "The deed shall not be left undone according to my vow—I swear it by my sons and by my brothers, O Keśava. I shall make an end of the Kurus, urged on by thee, O younger brother of Indra."

**6.55.101**  Then Janārdana, his mind made glad to hear that vow and pledge, abiding by the wish of the best of the Kauravas, mounted the chariot once more, his discus still in hand.

**6.55.102**  Taking up the reins again and seizing his conch, that slayer of foes, the descendant of Śūra, made the quarters resound with the blast of Pāñcajanya.

**6.55.103**  With necklace, armlets, and earrings tossed awry, his curved lashes and eyes besprent with dust, his teeth gleaming bright, the conch grasped in his hand—seeing him, the heroes of the Kurus cried aloud.

**6.55.104**  Then the din of tabors, kettledrums, and war-drums, the rumble of chariot-wheels and the boom of great drums, and fierce lion-roars arose throughout all the hosts of the Kurus.

**6.55.105**  The twang of Pārtha's Gāṇḍīva, like rolling thunder, went forth to the sky and to all the quarters; and the bright, clear shafts loosed from the Pāṇḍava's bow flew to every region.

**6.55.106**  Against him the lord of the Kauravas, with his force, together with Bhīṣma and Bhūriśravas, advanced, arrow uplifted in hand, like fire eager to consume dry grass.

**6.55.107**  Then Bhūriśravas hurled at Arjuna seven golden-feathered broad-arrows, Duryodhana a fierce-speeding javelin, Śalya a mace, and the son of Śaṃtanu a spear.

**6.55.108**  With seven shafts he warded off the seven choice arrows loosed by Bhūriśravas, and with a keen razor-edged shaft he cut down that javelin sped from Duryodhana's arm.

**6.55.109**  Then the hero, with two shafts, cut asunder the bright spear, gleaming like lightning, hurled by the son of Śaṃtanu, and the mace flung from the arm of the lord of the Madras.

**6.55.110**  Then, drawing back mightily with both arms his wondrous, immeasurable Gāṇḍīva bow, he brought forth, duly and according to rite, the terrible weapon of mighty Indra, marvelous, into the sky.

**6.55.111**  With that supreme weapon the great-souled bearer of the mighty bow, the diademed and garlanded one, held back all the hosts with floods and nets of arrows bright as the hue of flame.

**6.55.112**  The arrows loosed from Pārtha's bow, having cleft chariots, banner-tops, bows, and arms, pierced into the bodies of the foe—of lordly men, lordly elephants, and horses.

**6.55.113**  Then Pārtha, the diademed and garlanded one, covering the quarters and the intermediate quarters with keen, well-edged shafts, with the sound of the Gāṇḍīva made their hearts to quail.

**6.55.114**  As that most dreadful tumult was unleashed, the blare of conches, the boom of drums, and the fierce war-cries were swallowed up and silenced by the twang of the Gāṇḍīva.

**6.55.115**  Then, recognizing that sound of the Gāṇḍīva, the warrior-heroes led by King Virāṭa, and the king of the Pāñcālas, the valiant Drupada, came to that place, undaunted in spirit.

**6.55.116**  But all thy hosts, wherever rose the roar born of the Gāṇḍīva, there and thither only gave way; not one came up against him in opposition.

**6.55.117**  In that most terrible clash of kings, foremost warriors were slain together with their cars and charioteers, and elephants, scorched by the fall of iron shafts, bearing great banners and bright golden girths,

**6.55.118**  their strength spent, fell suddenly to the ground, their armor and bodies rent by the diademed one, struck hard by Pārtha's feathered shafts of fierce velocity, by keen broad-arrows with sharpened points.

**6.55.119**  Their frames cut through, their base-pegs shattered, the great banners at the forefront of the armies, and the bands of foot-soldiers and the chariots in the battle, and the horses and elephants—all by Dhanaṃjaya,

**6.55.120**  struck by arrows, their strength swiftly fled, their limbs grown rigid, fell upon the earth, their armor and bodies riven, O king, by that supreme weapon of Indra in the great battle.

**6.55.121**  Then by the diademed one, with floods of keen shafts, a most dreadful river was set flowing there upon the field of battle—its waters the blood streaming from the weapon-gashed bodies of men, its foam the bodies of men.

**6.55.122**  With a violent current, broad in its flow, it streamed forth in a shape of terrible roaring, its course dammed by the corpses of slain elephants and horses, its mire of flesh laden with the entrails and marrow of men.

**6.55.123**  Thronged by hosts of demons and goblins, its grassy banks strewn with skulls and tangled hair, bearing along thousands of heaped corpses, churning with a tumult of waves of shattered armor of every kind,

**6.55.124**  its gravel the cut bones of men, horses, and elephants, with the abyss of destruction for its depths, fearful to behold—that river, beset by rows of herons, by vultures and ospreys, by packs of carrion-eaters and hyenas,

**6.55.125**  its banks crowded, they beheld on every side—cruel, with the semblance of the great Vaitaraṇī, set flowing by the volleys of Arjuna's arrows, streaming with fat, marrow, and blood, most terrible.

**6.55.126**  Those Cedis, Pāñcālas, Kārūṣas, and Matsyas, and all the sons of Pṛthā together, roared aloud, scattering in terror the army of the host-commanders, as a lion the gathered herds of deer; and those two, filled with surpassing joy, sounded forth—the bearer of the Gāṇḍīva and Janārdana.

**6.55.127**  Then, seeing the sun with its web of rays withdrawn, and gazing up at that weapon of Indra spread wide and most dreadful, unbearable, like the end of an age, their limbs sorely gashed by weapons,

**6.55.128**  the Kurus, with Bhīṣma, and with Droṇa, Duryodhana, and Bāhlika, made their retreat, seeing the night drawing on, edged with the reddish streaks of the fading sun.

**6.55.129**  And Dhanaṃjaya too, having won glory and renown in the world and vanquished his foes, his task accomplished, went at nightfall to the camp together with the kings and his brothers. Then arose a tumultuous and most dreadful clamor among the Kurus at the onset of night.

**6.55.130**  "In the battle Arjuna has slain ten thousand chariots, and seven hundred elephants have been killed; and all the Easterners and the bands of Sauvīras, and the Kṣudrakas and Mālavas, have been struck down. A great deed has been done by Dhanaṃjaya, such as none other could do."

**6.55.131**  "Śrutāyus, the king and lord of the Ambaṣṭhas, and likewise Durmarṣaṇa and Citrasena, Droṇa, Kṛpa, the Saindhava and Bāhlika, Bhūriśravas, Śalya and Śala, O king—all of them, with Bhīṣma, were vanquished by the might of his own arm, by the diademed one, that great warrior of the world."

**6.55.132**  Speaking thus, all thy troops, O Bhārata, went to their tents; and with thousands of well-kindled torches and with shining lamps, the army of the Kurus, all its warriors terrified by the diademed one, made its encampment.

**6.56.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the night had passed, O Bhārata, the great-souled Bhīṣma, his wrath kindled against the foe, marched at the forefront of the Bhārata armies toward his adversaries, surrounded by the whole of his force.

**6.56.2**  Him Droṇa, Duryodhana, and Bāhlika, and likewise Durmarṣaṇa and Citrasena, and the exceedingly mighty Jayadratha, and other kings besides, followed on every side with their floods of troops.

**6.56.3**  Encircled by those great and mighty warriors, glorious and valorous, O king, by those foremost of monarchs, O best of kings, he shone like the Wielder of the Thunderbolt amid the gods.

**6.56.4**  At the forefront of that host, fixed aloft and tossing in the wind, the great banners—deep red, yellow, black, and white of hue—set upon the shoulders of mighty elephants, blazed forth.

**6.56.5**  That host, with the kingly son of Śaṃtanu, with its great warriors, elephants, and horses, shone like a sky filled with new-gathered clouds at the coming of the rains, charged with lightning and thunder.

**6.56.6**  Then the most fierce army of the Kurus, guarded by the son of Śaṃtanu, set forth facing the battle, marching against Arjuna with sudden onrush, like the terrible flood of a great river.

**6.56.7**  That array—its hidden strength made of beasts of many kinds, its wings the masses of elephants, horses, foot-soldiers, and chariots, like a vast cloud—the great-souled one whose banner bore the monkey-king beheld from afar.

**6.56.8**  That bull among men, the hero, the great-souled one, went forth at the head of his army in his bannered, white-horsed, fenced chariot, set upon the slaughter of all his youthful foes.

**6.56.9**  Beholding in the battle that monkey-bannered car, well-equipped, with fine seat and pole, made ready and driven by the bull of the Yadus, thy Kauravas, together with thy sons, sank into despair upon the field.

**6.56.10**  Drawn onward, guarded by the diademed one with weapon uplifted, that great warrior of the world—thy men beheld that royal array, thronged with four times four thousand war-elephants.

**6.56.11**  Even as on the former day the array had been formed by the King of Righteousness, the delight of the Kurus, so, each coming to his appointed place, did the foremost of the Cedis stand together with the foremost of the Pāñcālas.

**6.56.12**  Then thousands of kettledrums, struck with mighty force, resounded in the battle, and the blare of conches and the boom of war-drums, with lion-roars, rose throughout all the hosts.

**6.56.13**  Then the loud-twanging bows, with their arrows, stretched taut by the heroes, in an instant swallowed up the din of kettledrums and tabors, and the great blare of the conches too.

**6.56.14**  Beholding the sky veiled with the sound of conches, the web of earthen dust swiftly raised aloft, its radiance spread out like a vast canopy, the heroes suddenly rushed upon one another.

**6.56.15**  Car-warrior, struck by car-warrior, fell with his charioteer, with his horses, his chariot, and his banner; elephant, struck by elephant, fell; and foot-soldier, struck by foot-soldier, fell.

**6.56.16**  Wheeling and charging, troops of noble steeds, wondrous to behold, gashed by arrows and smitten by lances and swords, fell upon other troops of noble steeds.

**6.56.17**  Shields adorned with clusters of golden stars, flung by the heroes and cleft asunder by battle-axes and lances and swords, fell upon the earth.

**6.56.18**  Some car-warriors, crushed with their charioteers by elephants with their tusks—those mighty trunk-hands—fell down; and bull-elephants too, struck by arrows from the foremost of car-warriors, sank upon the ground.

**6.56.19**  Hearing the wail of anguish of the young horsemen and foot-soldiers, beaten down by the tusks and bodies of the beasts, hurled and laid low by the rushing flood of elephants, men sank to the earth.

**6.56.20**  As elephants, horses, and chariots reeled in confusion, and a great terror was loosed among the young horsemen and foot-soldiers, Bhīṣma beheld him of the monkey-king banner being hemmed about by the great car-warriors.

**6.56.21**  Against that diademed one—whose path blazed with great weapons, arrows, and thunderbolts—the son of Śaṃtanu, his palm-tree banner raised five palm-trees high, borne on by the spirited speed of his noble steeds, rushed forward.

**6.56.22**  And likewise against the son of Indra, the peer of Śakra himself, there rushed those led by Droṇa—Kṛpa and Śalya and Viviṃśati, Duryodhana and the son of Somadatta, O king.

**6.56.23**  Then, coming forth from the van of the chariot-host, the hero Abhimanyu, son of Arjuna, knower of all weapons, clad in armor wrought with gold, advanced with speed against them all.

**6.56.24**  Having struck down the great weapons of those great car-warriors, the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, untiring in his deeds, shone wreathed in flames fed by mighty incantations, like the lord Agni present at the sacrifice.

**6.56.25**  Then Bhīṣma, swiftly making a river whose waters were blood and whose foam was the slaughter of the foe, passed beyond the son of Subhadrā and, undaunted in spirit, advanced upon the great car-warrior Pārtha.

**6.56.26**  Then the diademed and garlanded one, laughing, with the great roar of the Gāṇḍīva, wondrous to behold, with a net of broad-headed shafts destroyed that net of mighty weapons.

**6.56.27**  Upon that Bhīṣma, the highest of all bearers of the bow, the great-souled one of the monkey-king banner, untiring in his deeds, swiftly rained down floods and nets of arrows and bright broad-shafts.

**6.56.28**  Such was that single combat between the two best of true men, fought without faintheartedness, terrible with the thunder of their bows: the whole world, and the Kurus and Sṛñjayas, beheld that duel of chariots waged by Bhīṣma and Dhanaṃjaya.

**6.57.1**  Saṃjaya said: Droṇa's son, Bhūriśravas, Śalya, and Citrasena, and the son of Saṃyamana too, O honored one, together gave battle to the son of Subhadrā.

**6.57.2**  The people watched him, alone and locked in fight with those five tigers among men of surpassing valor, as one might watch a lion's cub set upon by elephants.

**6.57.3**  In eluding aim, in heroism, in prowess, in mastery of weapons, and in nimbleness of hand, not one of them proved a match for the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister.

**6.57.4**  Beholding his son, that tamer of foes, displaying such prowess in the fight, Pārtha, intent in battle, then raised aloud a lion's roar.

**6.57.5**  Seeing your grandson harrying that host, O lord of peoples, your warriors, O king of kings, surrounded him on every side.

**6.57.6**  Undismayed, the bane of his enemies' spirit, the son of Subhadrā advanced to meet the bannered army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons, with fiery energy and with strength.

**6.57.7**  As he fought his foes in battle, his great bow, kept ever in the path of swift movement and bright as the sun, was seen by all.

**6.57.8**  Piercing Droṇa's son with a single shaft and Śalya with five, with eight more he struck down the banner of Saṃyamana's son.

**6.57.9**  That mighty, gold-shafted spear, like a serpent, hurled at him by the son of Somadatta, he struck aside with a sharp winged arrow.

**6.57.10**  Warding off the hundreds of dreadful arrows that Śalya loosed, the heir of Arjuna slew his horses in the battle.

**6.57.11**  Bhūriśravas and Śalya, Droṇa's son, the son of Saṃyamana, and Śala—though furious, they could make no headway against the strength of the arm of Kṛṣṇa's nephew.

**6.57.12**  Then, O king of kings, the Trigartas and the Madras together with the Kekayas, five-and-thirty thousand strong, urged on by your son—

**6.57.13**  foremost masters of the science of archery, unconquerable by foes in battle—closed in around the Diademed One, who, with his son, sought to destroy them.

**6.57.14**  Those two bulls among charioteers, father and son, hemmed in there, the Pāñcāla commander, conqueror of foes, beheld, O king.

**6.57.15**  Encircled by many thousands of floods of elephants and chariots, encircled too by horsemen and footmen by the hundred thousand,

**6.57.16**  twanging his bow in wrath and urging on his host, that scorcher of foes advanced against that division of the Madrakas and the Kekayas.

**6.57.17**  Guarded by that glorious warrior of the steadfast bow, the division, with its chariots, elephants, and horses arrayed and eager for the fray, shone resplendent.

**6.57.18**  That Pāñcāla, O delight of the Kurus, struck the son of Śaradvat, who was advancing at the front toward Arjuna, with three arrows in the region of the collarbone.

**6.57.19**  Then, slaying the Madrakas with shafts by tens upon tens, exulting and alone, he slew with a broad-headed arrow the horse of Kṛtavarman.

**6.57.20**  And Damana, heir of the great-souled Paurava, that scorcher of foes slew with a broad-pointed iron shaft.

**6.57.21**  Then the son of Saṃyamana pierced the Pāñcāla, frenzied in battle, with thirty arrows, and his charioteer with ten.

**6.57.22**  Deeply wounded, that mighty bowman, licking the corners of his mouth, cut his foe's bow asunder with an exceedingly sharp broad-headed arrow.

**6.57.23**  Then swiftly he struck him with five-and-twenty shafts, and slew his horses, O king, and both his flank-guarding charioteers.

**6.57.24**  Standing in his chariot whose horses were slain, O bull of the Bharatas, the son of Saṃyamana beheld the son of the great-souled Pāñcāla.

**6.57.25**  Seizing a most dreadful and excellent iron scimitar, on foot he swiftly rushed upon the son of Drupada who stood in his chariot.

**6.57.26**  As he came on like a great flood, like a serpent falling from the sky, whirling shield and scimitar, like Death loosed by Time itself,

**6.57.27**  blazing as it were with the flame of his weapon, with the stride of a maddened elephant—the Pāṇḍavas there beheld him, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata.

**6.57.28**  But as he ran headlong to the encounter, sharp scimitar in hand and bearing his shield, the son of the Pāñcāla king—

**6.57.29**  as he passed beyond the rush of arrows and drew near to the chariot, the commander in his wrath made haste and split his head with his mace.

**6.57.30**  As he was slain and fell, O king, his scimitar and his bright shield dropped swiftly from his hand to the ground.

**6.57.31**  Having slain him with the head of his mace, the great-souled son of the Pāñcāla king, of terrible prowess, won the highest glory.

**6.57.32**  When that mighty bowman, the prince and great car-warrior, was slain, a great cry of dismay arose from your army, O honored one.

**6.57.33**  Then the son of Saṃyamana, enraged at seeing his son slain, rushed with violence upon the Pāñcāla, that frenzied warrior.

**6.57.34**  Those two heroes, foremost of charioteers, met there in battle, and all the kings beheld them—the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas alike.

**6.57.35**  Then the wrathful son of Saṃyamana, slayer of enemy heroes, struck the son of Pṛṣata with three arrows, as one goads a great elephant with hooks.

**6.57.36**  Likewise Śalya, glorious in assemblies of battle, in his wrath struck the heroic son of Pṛṣata upon the breast; and thereupon the fight raged on.

**6.58.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Fate, I think, is higher than even human effort, Saṃjaya, in that my son's army is being slaughtered by the army of Pāṇḍu's sons.

**6.58.2**  For always, my dear, you report my own men as slain, while ever you tell of the Pāṇḍavas as untroubled and exultant.

**6.58.3**  My own men, bereft of manly valor today, O Saṃjaya, you report as fallen, as being struck down, and as slain outright.

**6.58.4**  Though they fight to the utmost of their power and strive for victory, still the Pāṇḍavas conquer, and my own are vanquished.

**6.58.5**  And so I have heard without ceasing, my dear, of the bitter sorrows wrought by Duryodhana, sorrows past bearing and beyond number.

**6.58.6**  I can see no means whereby the Pāṇḍavas might be defeated, or whereby my own might win victory in the war, O Saṃjaya.

**6.58.7**  Saṃjaya said: Hear, O king, with a steady heart, of the destruction of the bodies of men and the destruction of elephants, horses, and chariots—the great ruin that is the fruit of your own misrule.

**6.58.8**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna, harried by Śalya with nine arrows, in his fury harried the lord of the Madras in turn with iron shafts.

**6.58.9**  There we beheld a marvel, the prowess of the son of Pṛṣata, in that he swiftly checked Śalya, the glory of the battle-line.

**6.58.10**  No one could see any opening in those two, furious in the fray; for a while the combat between them seemed evenly matched.

**6.58.11**  Then Śalya, O great king, in the encounter cut asunder the bow of Dhṛṣṭadyumna with a sharp, yellow-feathered broad-headed arrow.

**6.58.12**  And he covered him with a rain of arrows, O Bhārata, as the water-bearing clouds at the coming of the rains cover a mountain.

**6.58.13**  But Abhimanyu, enraged when Dhṛṣṭadyumna was thus hard pressed, rushed with violence against the chariot of the Madra king.

**6.58.14**  Then the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, of immeasurable spirit, reaching in great wrath the chariot of the Madra lord, pierced the son of Ṛtāyana with three arrows.

**6.58.15**  Then your men, O king, eager to lay hold of Arjuna's son in the battle, swiftly surrounded the chariot of the Madra king and took their stand.

**6.58.16**  Duryodhana and Vikarṇa, Duḥśāsana and Viviṃśati, Durmarṣaṇa and Duḥsaha, Citrasena and Durmukha,

**6.58.17**  and Satyavrata—bless you—and Purumitra, O Bhārata: these stood in the battle, guarding the chariot of the Madra lord.

**6.58.18**  Against them came Bhīmasena in his wrath, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata, the sons of Draupadī and Abhimanyu, and the two sons of Mādrī among the Pāṇḍavas,

**6.58.19**  loosing weapons of every kind, O lord of peoples, advancing in high exultation and seeking each other's death; and they met in combat, O king, because of your evil counsel.

**6.58.20**  While that terrible chariot-battle was being waged, the car-warriors of your side and of the enemy alike became mere onlookers.

**6.58.21**  Loosing weapons of many forms, those great car-warriors, roaring at one another, joined in the fierce encounter.

**6.58.22**  Intent and seized with fury, all of them eager to slay one another, hurling great missiles and unable to brook the affront, they fell upon each other.

**6.58.23**  Duryodhana, enraged, in that great battle pierced Dhṛṣṭadyumna in haste and hard with four sharp arrows.

**6.58.24**  And Durmarṣaṇa pierced him with twenty arrows and Citrasena with five, Durmukha with nine and Duḥsaha with seven, Viviṃśati with five, and Duḥśāsana with three.

**6.58.25**  In return the son of Pṛṣata, that tormentor of foes, O king of kings, struck them back, each one with five-and-twenty arrows, displaying the nimbleness of his hand.

**6.58.26**  And in the battle, O Bhārata, Abhimanyu pierced Satyavrata and Purumitra, those two, with ten arrows each.

**6.58.27**  And the two sons of Mādrī, the delight of their mother, covered their maternal uncle in the battle with showers of arrows; and that was as it were a marvel.

**6.58.28**  Then Śalya, O great king, assailed his two sister's sons, those foremost of charioteers, with many arrows, each side bent on repaying blow for blow; yet though so covered, the two sons of Mādrī did not waver.

**6.58.29**  Then, catching sight of Duryodhana, the mighty Bhīmasena, that son of Pāṇḍu, wishing to make an end of the quarrel, took up his mace.

**6.58.30**  Seeing the mighty-armed Bhīmasena, mace raised on high like crested Kailāsa, your sons fled away in terror.

**6.58.31**  But Duryodhana, enraged, urged on the king of Magadha, with his division of ten thousand swift elephants; and placing the Magadha king in the van, he advanced upon Bhīmasena.

**6.58.32**  Seeing that host of elephants bearing down upon him, Vṛkodara, mace in hand, leapt down from his chariot, roaring aloud like a lion.

**6.58.33**  Grasping his great, heavy mace, hard as the core of a mountain, he ran upon the elephant-host like Death with gaping jaws.

**6.58.34**  Smiting the elephants with his mace, the mighty, great-armed Bhīmasena ranged over the field like Vāsava wielding the thunderbolt.

**6.58.35**  At the great roar of Bhīma as he bellowed, a roar that shook mind and heart, the elephants huddled together and writhed in confusion.

**6.58.36**  Then the sons of Draupadī, and the son of Subhadrā, that great car-warrior, and Nakula and Sahadeva, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata,

**6.58.37**  guarding Bhīma's rear, ran down upon the elephants with a rain of arrows, pouring it forth as clouds pour rain upon the mountains.

**6.58.38**  With razor-edged shafts and razor-tipped arrows, with broad-heads and yellow-feathered shafts and crescent-headed darts, the Pāṇḍavas struck down the heads of the elephant-riders.

**6.58.39**  With heads falling down, and with ornamented arms, and with hands still grasping their goads, it seemed as though a hail of stones were raining down.

**6.58.40**  The elephant-riders, their heads struck off, were seen upon the shoulders of the elephants like trees with broken crests upon mountain peaks.

**6.58.41**  And we saw other great elephants slain by Dhṛṣṭadyumna, fallen and being struck down by the great-souled son of Pṛṣata.

**6.58.42**  Then the king of Magadha, lord of the earth, sent an elephant like Airāvata in the battle against the chariot of the son of Subhadrā.

**6.58.43**  Beholding that splendid elephant of the Magadha king bearing down upon him, the heroic son of Subhadrā, slayer of enemy heroes, slew it with a single arrow.

**6.58.44**  As that king's elephant was brought low, the son of Kṛṣṇa, conqueror of hostile cities, struck off the king's head with a silver-feathered broad-arrow.

**6.58.45**  Bhīmasena the Pāṇḍava too, plunging into that host of elephants, ranged across the battle crushing the beasts as Indra shatters mountains.

**6.58.46**  In that battle we saw elephants felled by Bhīmasena with a single blow, like mountains smitten by the thunderbolt.

**6.58.47**  There lay the elephants slain, their tusks broken, their temples broken, their thighs broken, their backs broken, their frontal globes broken — each like a fallen mountain.

**6.58.48**  Other elephants we saw bellowing and sinking down, turning their backs upon the fight, voiding urine, shattered and panic-stricken, and yet others loosing their dung in terror.

**6.58.49**  Along Bhīmasena's paths we saw slain elephants huge as mountains, their life-breath gone, while still others lay groaning.

**6.58.50**  And other great elephants, their frontal globes split, vomiting blood, reeling, sank to the earth like crags toppling upon the ground.

**6.58.51**  His limbs smeared with fat and blood, drenched in marrow and grease, Bhīma ranged across the battle like Death himself, rod in hand.

**6.58.52**  Bearing that mace reddened with the blood of elephants, Vṛkodara was terrible and dreadful, like the wielder of the Pināka bearing his bow.

**6.58.53**  Churned by the wrathful Bhīmasena, the surviving tuskers fled headlong, trampling your own army as they went.

**6.58.54**  That hero, as he fought, the great bowmen and charioteers led by the son of Subhadrā guarded round about, as the immortals guard the wielder of the thunderbolt.

**6.58.55**  Bearing his blood-stained mace, drenched in the blood of elephants, Bhīmasena appeared, fierce of spirit, like the Ender of all things.

**6.58.56**  As he wielded his mace and strove in every quarter, O Bhārata, we saw him dancing as though it were Śaṃkara himself who danced.

**6.58.57**  We beheld, O great king, that fierce and slaughtering mace, heavy as the rod of Yama, ringing like the thunderbolt of Indra.

**6.58.58**  Clotted with hair and marrow and smeared with blood, it was like the Pināka of the wrathful Rudra as he strikes down living creatures.

**6.58.59**  As a herdsman drives a gathered flock before him with his staff, so did Bhīma drive the host of elephants before him with his mace.

**6.58.60**  Battered by his mace and by arrows on every side, your tuskers fled, trampling down your own divisions as they went.

**6.58.61**  Having scattered the elephants as a great wind scatters the clouds, Bhīma stood firm amid the tumult, like the trident-bearer in his burning-ground.

**6.59.1**  Saṃjaya said: When that host of elephants had been destroyed, your son Duryodhana urged on all his troops, crying, "Slay Bhīmasena!"

**6.59.2**  Then, at your son's command, all the divisions rushed upon Bhīmasena, who roared aloud with terrible cries.

**6.59.3**  That boundless flood of force, hard for even the gods to withstand, came rushing upon him, well-nigh impassable, like the sea swelling at the full moon.

**6.59.4**  Thronged with chariots, elephants, and horses, resounding with conch and kettledrum, endless and shoreless, with kings for its still deep pools —

**6.59.5**  that unshakable ocean of an army, like a second mighty deep, Bhīmasena held back in battle as the shore holds back the sea.

**6.59.6**  We beheld there a marvel, O king, credible yet wondrous — the more-than-human feat of Bhīmasena in battle.

**6.59.7**  The whole surging earth, with its horses, its chariots, and its elephants, Bhīmasena smote with his mace, undismayed.

**6.59.8**  Having stayed those floods of force with his mace, Bhīma, foremost of car-warriors, stood firm amid the tumult, unmoving as Mount Meru.

**6.59.9**  In that terrible and most dreadful hour of utmost tumult, his brothers and his sons, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata,

**6.59.10**  and the sons of Draupadī and Abhimanyu, and Śikhaṇḍin the great car-warrior — these did not forsake the mighty Bhīmasena, though terror had arisen.

**6.59.11**  Then, seizing his great heavy mace of wrought iron, he slew your warriors like Death rod in hand, the conqueror smashing the throngs of chariots and the throngs of horses.

**6.59.12**  Bhīma ranged across the battle like the fire at the end of an age, striking down all before him, mighty as Time at the dissolution of the world.

**6.59.13**  Dragging tangles of chariots along by the rush of his thighs, the Pāṇḍava crushed all the elephants as a tusker crushes beds of reeds.

**6.59.14**  He smote the car-warriors from their chariots, the elephant-fighters from their elephants, the horsemen from the backs of their steeds, and the foot-soldiers down upon the ground.

**6.59.15**  With men and elephants and horses slain here and there, that field of battle became like the very slaughter-house of Death.

**6.59.16**  Like the Pināka of the wrathful Rudra as he strikes down living creatures — fierce, like the rod of Yama, ringing like the thunderbolt of Indra — men beheld the dreadful, slaughtering mace of Bhīmasena.

**6.59.17**  As the great-souled son of Kuntī whirled his mace, his form blazed forth most terrible, like that of Time at the ending of an age.

**6.59.18**  Seeing him thus routing that mighty host again and again, coming on like Death itself, all grew downcast in spirit.

**6.59.19**  Wherever the Pāṇḍava looked as he raised his mace, in that very quarter all the troops were rent asunder, O Bhārata.

**6.59.20**  He clove the armies apart, unconquered amid that flood of force, devouring the divisions like the gaping-mouthed Ender.

**6.59.21**  Seeing Vṛkodara thus terrible in his deeds, his great mace upraised, Bhīṣma straightway rushed against him.

**6.59.22**  With his chariot loud as a great thundercloud and bright as the sun, he covered him with a rain of arrows, like the rain-laden cloud-god pouring down his showers.

**6.59.23**  Seeing him come on thus, like Death with gaping jaws, the mighty-armed Bhīma, brooking no rival, went forth to meet Bhīṣma.

**6.59.24**  In that moment Sātyaki, true to his pledge, the hero of Śini's line, fell upon the grandsire, smiting down his foes with his stout bow and making your son's army tremble.

**6.59.25**  As he came on with steeds gleaming like silver, hurling arrows from his stout bow, none of your own troops, O Bhārata, could turn him back in that hour.

**6.59.26**  Alambusa, son of Ṛṣyaśṛṅga, pierced him with the points of sharp arrows; but the hero, the grandson of Śini, struck back at him with four and bore down upon him with his chariot.

**6.59.27**  Marking the best of the Vṛṣṇis come on, wheeling about in the midst of his foes, putting to flight the bulls of the Kurus and roaring again and again in the fray —

**6.59.28**  none could check that peerless warrior, blazing like the noonday sun; and there was no one there unappalled, O king, save the son of Somadatta.

**6.59.29**  For Bhūriśravas, son of Somadatta, O Bhārata, taking up his bow of fierce force and seeing his own chariots borne away, went forth to meet Sātyaki, eager to fight.

**6.60.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then Bhūriśravas, O king, in furious wrath pierced Sātyaki with nine arrows, as one goads a great elephant with hooks.

**6.60.2**  And Sātyaki of measureless spirit, in the sight of all the host, covered that Kaurava in turn with smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.60.3**  Then King Duryodhana, surrounded by his brothers, intent in battle, ringed the son of Somadatta about on every side.

**6.60.4**  In the same way all the Pāṇḍavas of mighty vigor stood encircling the impetuous Sātyaki on every side in the fray.

**6.60.5**  But Bhīmasena, kindled with wrath, raising his mace, O Bhārata, beset all your sons with Duryodhana at their head.

**6.60.6**  With many thousands of chariots, filled with wrath and indignation, your son Nandaka pierced the mighty Bhīmasena with six sharp heron-feathered shafts whetted on stone.

**6.60.7**  And Duryodhana in battle, in his wrath, struck the mighty Bhīmasena upon the breast with three sharp arrows.

**6.60.8**  Then the mighty-armed Bhīma of great strength mounted his own excellent chariot and spoke thus to Viśoka his charioteer:

**6.60.9**  "These mighty car-warriors, the valiant and powerful sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, in their great wrath have risen up to slay me in the fight.

**6.60.10**  This day I shall slay them all before your eyes, of that there is no doubt; therefore, O charioteer, hold my steeds well in hand and stay watchful in the battle."

**6.60.11**  So saying, the son of Pṛthā pierced your son Duryodhana with ten sharp gold-adorned arrows, and struck Nandaka in turn with three shafts between the breasts.

**6.60.12**  But Duryodhana, having pierced the mighty Bhīma with sixty arrows, struck Viśoka in turn with three others keenly whetted.

**6.60.13**  And in the battle, O king, the king cut Bhīma's shining bow asunder at the grip with three sharp arrows, as though in mockery.

**6.60.14**  But Bhīma, seeing his charioteer Viśoka there in the fight sorely beset by the sharp shafts of your bow-wielding son,

**6.60.15**  brooking it not and burning with wrath, laid hold of a celestial bow, O great king, O bull of the Bharatas, to slay your son.

**6.60.16**  In his fury he took up a feathered razor-edged shaft, and with it Bhīma cut asunder the king's excellent bow.

**6.60.17**  Casting away his severed bow, blazing as it were with wrath, Duryodhana swiftly took up another bow of greater force.

**6.60.18**  He fitted to it a dreadful arrow, bright as Time and Death, and with it in his wrath struck Bhīmasena between the breasts.

**6.60.19**  Pierced to the quick and racked with pain, he sank back into the body of his chariot, and there, seated in the chariot's seat, he fell into a swoon.

**6.60.20**  Seeing Bhīma stricken thus, the great bowmen and mighty car-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, with Abhimanyu at their head, could not endure it.

**6.60.21**  Then, calm and unflinching, they let fall a tumultuous rain of fierce-blazing weapons upon the head of your son.

**6.60.22**  Then the mighty Bhīmasena, recovering his senses, pierced Duryodhana with three arrows and again struck him with five.

**6.60.23**  And the great bowman of a Pāṇḍava pierced Śalya with twenty-five gold-feathered arrows; pierced thus, Śalya withdrew from the fight.

**6.60.24**  Then fourteen of your sons came forth to meet Bhīma — Senāpati and Suṣeṇa and Jalasaṃdha and Sulocana,

**6.60.25**  Ugra and Bhīmaratha and Bhīma and Bhīmabāhu and Alolupa, Durmukha and Duṣpradharṣa and Vivitsu and Vikaṭa and Sama.

**6.60.26**  Loosing many arrows, their eyes red with rage, they rushed upon Bhīmasena together and pierced him sorely.

**6.60.27**  But the mighty Bhīmasena, beholding your sons, licking the corners of his mouth like a wolf amid the flock, that hero, the Pāṇḍava, cut off the head of Senāpati with a razor-edged shaft.

**6.60.28**  Piercing through Jalasaṃdha, he sent him to the abode of Yama; then slaying Suṣeṇa, he dispatched him to Death.

**6.60.29**  With a broad-arrow he struck to the ground the head of Ugra, helmet and all, fair as the moon and adorned with two earrings.

**6.60.30**  And with seventy arrows Bhīma led Bhīmabāhu, together with his steeds, his banner, and his charioteer, to the other world in that battle, O sire.

**6.60.31**  The two impetuous brothers, Bhīma and Bhīmaratha both, Bhīmasena, as though in mockery, O king, sent to the abode of Yama.

**6.60.32**  Then in that great battle, before the eyes of all the troops, Bhīma sent Sulocana too to the abode of Yama with a razor-edged shaft.

**6.60.33**  But your sons, the others who remained there, seeing that prowess of Bhīmasena, were tormented with dread of Bhīma; and being slaughtered by that great soul, O king, they fled away in all directions.

**6.60.34**  Then the son of Śaṃtanu spoke to all the great chariot-warriors: "Here is Bhīma, enraged in battle, striking down the great car-warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons—

**6.60.35**  "the foremost, the eldest, and the bravest gathered together—this fierce-bowed one lays them low. Crush him, O kings!"

**6.60.36**  Thus addressed, all the soldiers of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son rushed in fury upon mighty Bhīmasena.

**6.60.37**  Bhagadatta, mounted on an elephant in rut, O lord of peoples, fell suddenly upon the place where Bhīma stood firm.

**6.60.38**  Even as he charged, with arrows whetted on stone he made Bhīmasena invisible in the fray, as a thundercloud hides the sun.

**6.60.39**  The great car-warriors led by Abhimanyu, trusting in the strength of their own arms, could not endure this veiling of Bhīma in the battle.

**6.60.40**  With a downpour of arrows they hemmed him in on every side, and with a rain of shafts they pierced that elephant from all directions.

**6.60.41**  Struck by that storm of weapons, the elephant of Prāgjyotiṣa, O king, riddled with many keen and various-marked shafts, charged forward at redoubled pace.

**6.60.42**  Its blood gushing forth, it became a sight to behold in the fray, like a great cloud shot through with the sun's rays.

**6.60.43**  Goaded on by Bhagadatta, the elephant streaming with rut rushed upon them all like Death loosed by Time, and, gathering twofold speed, made the earth tremble beneath its feet.

**6.60.44**  Beholding that enormous form, all the great car-warriors, deeming it irresistible, lost much of their cheer.

**6.60.45**  Then the king, enraged, O tiger among men, struck Bhīmasena upon the breast with a smooth-jointed arrow.

**6.60.46**  Deeply pierced by that king, the great archer and mighty car-warrior, his limbs overcome by a swoon, leaned upon his banner-staff.

**6.60.47**  Seeing them frightened and Bhīmasena fallen into a swoon, the valiant Bhagadatta let forth a mighty roar.
