**6.60.48**  Then Ghaṭotkaca, O king, seeing Bhīma in such a plight, that dreadful Rākṣasa, filled with wrath, vanished on the spot.

**6.60.49**  Weaving a terrible illusion that swelled the fear of the timid, in half the blink of an eye he reappeared, clad in a fearsome form.

**6.60.50**  He himself mounted Airāvata, conjured by his magic, and other elephants of the quarters became his followers as well.

**6.60.51**  Añjana, Vāmana, and resplendent Mahāpadma—these three great elephants were ridden by Rākṣasas.

**6.60.52**  Of vast bulk, O king, streaming rut in three currents, endowed with splendor, vigor, and strength, mighty in might and prowess.

**6.60.53**  Then Ghaṭotkaca, that scorcher of foes, urged on his own elephant, eager to slay Bhagadatta together with his mount.

**6.60.54**  And those other elephants, driven on by the mighty Rākṣasas, four-tusked and furious, fell upon Bhagadatta's elephant from the four quarters and gored it with their tusks.

**6.60.55**  Crushed by those elephants, anguished with pain and tormented by arrows, it bellowed a tremendous cry, loud as the thunderbolt of Indra.

**6.60.56**  Hearing that frightful, terrible-sounding cry of the bellowing beast, Bhīṣma spoke to Droṇa and to King Suyodhana:

**6.60.57**  "Yonder Bhagadatta, the great archer, fights in battle with the wicked son of Hiḍimbā, and contends only with great difficulty."

**6.60.58**  "The Rākṣasa is a master of illusion, and the king is exceedingly wrathful—the two have met, both of great valor, both like Time and Death itself."

**6.60.59**  "Here is heard the great clamor of the exultant Pāṇḍavas, and the mighty cry of the elephant trumpeting in its terror."

**6.60.60**  "Let us go there—good fortune to you—to guard the king; unguarded in the fray, he will swiftly yield up his life."

**6.60.61**  "Make haste, O heroes of great valor! Why should we tarry to set out? For a great and dreadful battle rages on, one to make the hair stand on end."

**6.60.62**  "Devoted, nobly born, valiant, a leader of armies is he—it is fitting, O unfaltering ones, that we should make his rescue."

**6.60.63**  Hearing those words of Bhīṣma, all the kings together, led by the son of Bharadvāja, desiring to save Bhagadatta, set out at top speed to where he was.

**6.60.64**  Marking them depart, the Pāñcālas, led by Yudhiṣṭhira and joined by the Pāṇḍavas, followed the foe from behind.

**6.60.65**  Then, beholding those armies, the valiant lord of the Rākṣasas roared a mighty roar, like the bursting crash of a thunderbolt.

**6.60.66**  Hearing that din of his and seeing the elephants doing battle, Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, spoke once more to the son of Bharadvāja:

**6.60.67**  "This battle with the wicked son of Hiḍimbā does not please me; for now he is filled with strength and valor, and has allies besides."

**6.60.68**  "He cannot be vanquished in fight, not even by the wielder of the thunderbolt himself; he is a sure-aiming smiter, while our mounts are weary and we are gashed and torn all day long by the Pāñcālas and the Pāṇḍavas."

**6.60.69**  "Therefore this battle with the triumphant Pāṇḍavas does not please me. Let the withdrawal be proclaimed for today; tomorrow we shall fight with the foe."

**6.60.70**  Hearing the words of the grandsire, the Kauravas did accordingly; tormented by fear of Ghaṭotkaca, they contrived their retreat by stratagem.

**6.60.71**  When the Kauravas had turned back, the victorious Pāṇḍavas raised a lion's roar, mingled with the blare of conches and the sound of flutes.

**6.60.72**  Thus, O bull of the Bharatas, did the day's battle between the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus unfold, with Ghaṭotkaca set in the forefront.

**6.60.73**  Then the Kauravas, O king, set out for their own camp, abashed at nightfall, defeated by the sons of Pāṇḍu.

**6.60.74**  The sons of Pāṇḍu too, those great car-warriors, their bodies torn by arrows, glad of heart from the battle, made their way to their camp.

**6.60.75**  Setting Bhīmasena and Ghaṭotkaca at their head, O great king, they honored one another, filled with the highest joy.

**6.60.76**  Sounding manifold cries mingled with the blare of trumpets, and raising lion-roars blended with the notes of conches,

**6.60.77**  those great-souled ones, roaring aloud and making the earth tremble, striking at the very heart of your son, O sire—those scorchers of foes set out for their camp at nightfall.

**6.60.78**  But King Duryodhana, dejected at the slaying of his brothers, brooded for a while, overcome by tears and grief.

**6.60.79**  Then, having made all due arrangements for the camp according to custom, he sat sunk in thought, consumed by sorrow and worn by the calamity that had befallen his brothers.

**6.61.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: A great fear has come upon me, Saṃjaya, and amazement too, on hearing of the deeds of the sons of Pāṇḍu, deeds hard even for the gods to perform.

**6.61.2**  And hearing, Saṃjaya, of the utter defeat of my sons, great is my anxiety, O charioteer: how, in the end, shall it be?

**6.61.3**  Surely the words of Vidura shall sear my heart, for all unfolds, it seems, by the working of fate, O Saṃjaya.

**6.61.4**  —seeing that the smiting hosts of the Pāṇḍavas do battle against heroes led by Bhīṣma, masters of weapons, the very best of warriors.

**6.61.5**  By whom are the great-souled, mighty sons of Pāṇḍu made invincible? By whom were they granted boons, my son, or what knowledge do they possess, that they perish not, like the hosts of stars in heaven?

**6.61.6**  Again and again I cannot bear it—my army slaughtered by the Pāṇḍavas. It is upon me alone that the rod of fate falls, most terrible of all.

**6.61.7**  How it is that the sons of Pāṇḍu cannot be slain, and how my sons are doomed to die—all this declare to me truly, Saṃjaya.

**6.61.8**  For I can see no farther shore of this sorrow by any means, like a man striving to swim across the mighty ocean with his arms.

**6.61.9**  I deem that a most dreadful calamity has surely come upon my sons; Bhīma will cause them all to be slain—of this there is no doubt.

**6.61.10**  For I see no hero who might guard my sons in battle; certain is the destruction of my sons in the fray, Saṃjaya.

**6.61.11**  Therefore, O charioteer, as I ask you now, you must tell me truly everything—the cause and, above all, the reason of it.

**6.61.12**  And what Duryodhana did on seeing his own men turn their backs in battle—Bhīṣma and Droṇa, and Kṛpa, and the son of Subala, and Jayadratha, or the great archer Aśvatthāman, or the mighty Vikarṇa—

**6.61.13**  —and what resolve was then taken by those great-souled ones when my sons turned away, O most wise Saṃjaya?

**6.61.14**  Saṃjaya said: Listen attentively, O king, and having heard, take it to heart. No spell-craft of any kind, no such illusion, and no terrifying trickery, O king, do the Pāṇḍavas employ.

**6.61.15**  They fight as is just, and they are mighty in the field. Holding that all their deeds are renowned through righteousness, O Bhārata, the sons of Pṛthā ever undertake them, seeking great glory.

**6.61.16**  They turn not back from battle, those mighty ones endowed with righteousness and joined to surpassing fortune; where there is righteousness, there is victory. For this the sons of Pṛthā are unslayable in war and ever victorious, O king.

**6.61.17**  Your sons are evil-souled, ever delighting in sins, cruel, and base in their deeds; for this they come to grief in battle.

**6.61.18**  Very many cruel wrongs were done by your sons here against the sons of Pāṇḍu, O lord of men, as if by men of the lowest sort.

**6.61.19**  Yet disregarding all those offenses of your sons, the Pāṇḍavas were ever forbearing, O elder of the Pāṇḍu line; but your sons hold them in no esteem, O lord of peoples.

**6.61.20**  Of that evil deed done ceaselessly there has now come a great and terrible fruit, like the fruit of the kiṃpāka. Devour it, O great king, together with your sons and your friends.

**6.61.21**  You would not heed, O king, though restrained by your well-wishers—by Vidura, and Bhīṣma, and the great-souled Droṇa—

**6.61.22**  —and by me as well, restrained more than once. You took not the counsel both salutary and wholesome, as a dying man refuses the wholesome medicine; cleaving to your sons' opinion, you deemed the Pāṇḍavas already conquered.

**6.61.23**  Hear now once more, truly, that of which you ask me—the cause of the Pāṇḍavas' victory, O best of the Bharatas. That will I tell you as I have heard it, O subduer of foes.

**6.61.24**  Questioned by Duryodhana on this matter, the grandsire—having seen all his brothers, mighty car-warriors, vanquished in battle—

**6.61.25**  —that scion of Kuru, his heart bewildered with grief, approaching the most wise grandsire with humility at nightfall—what your son then said, hear it from me, O lord of men.

**6.61.26**  Duryodhana said: You, and Droṇa, and Śalya, and Kṛpa, and likewise the son of Droṇa, and Kṛtavarman the son of Hṛdika, and Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja,

**6.61.27**  Bhūriśravas, and Vikarṇa, and the valiant Bhagadatta—you are all famed as great car-warriors, nobly born, ready to lay down your lives.

**6.61.28**  To my mind you are a match even for the three worlds; yet against the prowess of the Pāṇḍavas you all cannot stand.

**6.61.29**  Herein a doubt has arisen in me; declare it to me as I ask—on whom do the sons of Kuntī rely, that they conquer us at every step?

**6.61.30**  Bhīṣma said: Hear my word, O king, which I shall speak to you, O scion of Kuru. Often have I told you this, yet you have not acted upon it.

**6.61.31**  Let peace be made with the Pāṇḍavas, O best of the Bharatas; this I deem fitting, for the earth's sake and for your own, O lord.

**6.61.32**  Enjoy this earth, O king, in happiness together with your brothers, scorching all your enemies and gladdening your kinsmen too.

**6.61.33**  You would not listen to me of old, my son, though I cried out; and now this has come upon you, because you scorned the sons of Pāṇḍu.

**6.61.34**  And what the cause is of the invincibility of those whose deeds are untiring—hear it, O great king, as I declare it, O lord.

**6.61.35**  There is no being in all the worlds, none now nor any yet to come, who could conquer in battle the Pāṇḍavas while they are guarded by the bearer of the Śārṅga bow.

**6.61.36**  But hear faithfully, O knower of dharma, that which was sung of old and told to me, my son, by sages of disciplined souls.

**6.61.37**  In ancient times, it is said, all the gods and the seers, gathered together, waited upon the Grandsire upon the mountain Gandhamādana.

**6.61.38**  Seated in their midst, Prajāpati beheld a celestial chariot, blazing with radiance, most excellent, poised in the sky.

**6.61.39**  Discerning him through meditation, Brahmā, with self-controlled and gladdened heart, joined his palms and bowed before the supreme Lord of lords.

**6.61.40**  Then the seers and the gods, seeing Brahmā risen, all stood with palms joined, gazing upon the great marvel.

**6.61.41**  Having worshipped him duly, Brahmā—best of the knowers of Brahman, creator of the world, supreme knower of the highest dharma—spoke to that Supreme One.

**6.61.42**  You are the wealth of all, the form of all, the lord of all; Viṣvaksena, maker of all, the self-controlled, the sovereign of the universe, Vāsudeva. Therefore I take refuge in you, the divine, whose essence is yoga.

**6.61.43**  Victory to you, O Universe, great God! Victory, you who delight in the welfare of the worlds! Victory, O sovereign master of yoga, all-pervading One! Victory, you who are both the near and the far of yoga!

**6.61.44**  O lotus-wombed, wide-eyed One, victory to you, Lord of the lords of the worlds! O master of what has been, what shall be, and what is, victory to you, son of a gentle-souled father's son!

**6.61.45**  O One of countless virtues, unconquerable, victory to you, final refuge of all! O Nārāyaṇa, hard to fathom, victory to you, bearer of the Śārṅga bow!

**6.61.46**  O One endowed with every secret excellence, O form of the universe, the flawless, O lord of all, mighty-armed One, victory to you, intent upon the good of the worlds!

**6.61.47**  O great serpent, primeval Boar, tawny-haired, all-pervading One, victory to you, dwelling-place of Hari, lord of the peoples, abode of the universe, boundless and undecaying!

**6.61.48**  O One whose station is the manifest, the unmanifest, and the immeasurable, governing the senses yet possessed of senses, knower of the countless states of the soul—victory to you, profound One, granter of desires!

**6.61.49**  O endless One of perfect wisdom, ever the illuminator of beings, you whose work is accomplished, whose wisdom is fulfilled, knower of dharma, the victor and the unvanquished!

**6.61.50**  O hidden Self, Self of all beings, source from which manifest creation springs forth, truth of the real meaning of things, lord of the worlds—victory to you, illuminator of beings!

**6.61.51**  O self-born, greatly blessed One, intent upon the dissolution of the aeon, the bringer-forth, born of the mind—victory to you, beloved of the people of Brahman!

**6.61.52**  O One who delights in the outpouring of creation, lord of desire, supreme Lord, source of the nectar of immortality, the truly existent, fire at the ages' end—giver of victory!

**6.61.53**  O lord of the lords of creatures, divine One, lotus-naveled, of great might, self-existent, mighty being, whose very essence is action—victory to you, granter of the fruits of action!

**6.61.54**  The goddess Earth is your two feet, the quarters your arms, heaven your head; I am your form, the gods your body, the moon and sun your two eyes.

**6.61.55**  Strength, austerity, truth, and dharma are yours, O Lord, and Desire your offspring; fire is your splendour, the wind your breath, and the waters are sprung from your sweat.

**6.61.56**  The Aśvins are forever your two ears, the goddess Sarasvatī your tongue, the Vedas grounded in your sacraments—for upon you this whole world rests.

**6.61.57**  Neither your number nor your measure, neither your splendour nor your prowess, nor your strength do we know, O lord of yoga and of yogins, nor your origin.

**6.61.58**  Devoted to your worship, O God, composed through our observances, we ever adore you, O Viṣṇu, the supreme Lord, the great Lord.

**6.61.59**  The seers, gods, and gandharvas, the yakṣas, rākṣasas, and serpents, the piśācas and the humans, beasts, birds, and creeping things—

**6.61.60**  all this and more I have created upon the earth, born of your grace, O lotus-naveled, wide-eyed Kṛṣṇa, destroyer of evil dreams.

**6.61.61**  You are the goal of all beings, you the guide, you the face of the world; by your grace, O lord of the gods, the wise are forever happy.

**6.61.62**  By your grace, O God, the earth has ever been free from fear; therefore become, O wide-eyed One, the increaser of the lineage of the Yadus.

**6.61.63**  For the firm establishment of dharma, for the slaying of the sons of Diti, and for the upholding of the world—heed my prayer, O Lord.

**6.61.64**  This supreme mystery, made wholly of your grace, O all-pervading Vāsudeva, has been faithfully sung by me to you.

**6.61.65**  Having brought forth the god Saṃkarṣaṇa, your own self by your self, O Kṛṣṇa, you by yourself created Pradyumna, sprung from yourself.

**6.61.66**  And from Pradyumna you created Aniruddha, whom they know as the imperishable Viṣṇu; and Aniruddha created me, Brahmā, the upholder of the worlds.

**6.61.67**  Thus I, made of Vāsudeva, have been fashioned by you alone. Dividing your self into portions, take birth in the estate of man, O all-pervading One.

**6.61.68**  There, slaying the asuras for the happiness of all the worlds, establishing dharma and winning renown, you shall attain yoga in its very truth.

**6.61.69**  For the brahmin-seers in the world, and the gods too, O One of measureless might, devoted, sing of you by those many names, of you whose essence is the Supreme Self.

**6.61.70**  All the hosts of beings abide in you, taking refuge in you, the granter of boons, O fair-armed One; the wise declare you to be without beginning, middle, or end, of boundless yoga, the bridge of the world.

**6.62.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then that blessed God, supreme Lord of the worlds, answered Brahmā thus, in a voice tender and deep.

**6.62.2**  "Through yoga, my son, all that you desire is known to me; so shall it be"—and saying this, he vanished then and there.

**6.62.3**  Then the gods, seers, and gandharvas, filled with the utmost wonder and consumed by curiosity, all spoke to the Grandsire.

**6.62.4**  "Who is this, O all-pervading Lord, to whom you have bowed with humility and praised with the most excellent of words? We desire to hear of him."

**6.62.5**  Thus addressed, the blessed Grandsire answered all the gods, the brahmin-seers, and the gandharvas in a gentle voice.

**6.62.6**  "That which is the supreme, that which is to come and that which must be, who is the Self of beings and the Lord, and who is the Brahman, the highest abode—

**6.62.7**  with him, gracious, did I hold this converse, O bulls among gods; for the blessing of the world I implored the Lord of the universe.

**6.62.8**  'Take your stand in the world of men, known as Vāsudeva; for the slaying of the asuras, be born upon the earth.'

**6.62.9**  Those daityas, dānavas, and rākṣasas who were slain in battle have now been born among men, terrible in form and of great strength.

**6.62.10**  For their slaying, the self-controlled Lord, together with Nara, taking on a human birth, shall walk upon the earth.

**6.62.11**  Nara and Nārāyaṇa, those two most ancient and excellent of seers, of measureless splendour, have together been born into the world of men.

**6.62.12**  Unconquerable in battle when they strive together, even by the immortals—yet the foolish do not recognize these two seers, Nara and Nārāyaṇa.

**6.62.13**  I, Brahmā, lord of all the world, am his son; Vāsudeva, the great Lord of all the worlds, is the One worthy of your worship.

**6.62.14**  Never, O best of gods, is the One of mighty valour, bearer of conch, discus, and mace, to be slighted with the thought, 'This is but a man.'

**6.62.15**  This is the supreme mystery, this the supreme abode, this the supreme Brahman, this the supreme glory.

**6.62.16**  This is the imperishable, the unmanifest, this the eternal and the great; this, named Puruṣa, is sung of yet not known.

**6.62.17**  This is the supreme splendour, this the supreme bliss, this the supreme truth, proclaimed by the Maker of all.

**6.62.18**  Therefore the Lord of measureless might, Vāsudeva, must never be slighted by any of the gods with Indra, nor by the worlds, with the thought, 'This is but a man.'

**6.62.19**  And whoever, dull of wit, should say of Hṛṣīkeśa in contempt, 'This is no more than a man,' him they call the basest of men.

**6.62.20**  Whoever should despise that great-souled yogin, Vāsudeva, entered into a human body, him people call a man steeped in darkness.

**6.62.21**  Whoever does not recognize the divine Self of all that moves and is still, marked with the Śrīvatsa, radiant, the lotus-naveled One—him people call steeped in darkness.

**6.62.22**  Whoever scorns that great-souled One, who wears the diadem and the Kaustubha gem and grants safety to his friends, sinks into terrible darkness.

**6.62.23**  Knowing thus the truth of the matter, Vāsudeva, Lord of the lords of the worlds, is to be revered by all the worlds, O best of gods."

**6.62.24**  Having spoken thus to all the hosts of gods long ago, that blessed One, the Self of all the worlds, dismissed them and went to his own abode.

**6.62.25**  Then the gods with the gandharvas, the sages and the apsarases, having heard that discourse sung by Brahmā, went gladdened to heaven.

**6.62.26**  This I heard, my son, in an assembly of seers of disciplined souls as they told the ancient tale of Vāsudeva.

**6.62.27**  From Rāma the son of Jamadagni, from the wise Mārkaṇḍeya, and from Vyāsa and Nārada too I have heard it, O master of sacred lore.

**6.62.28**  Having understood this truth and heard of the imperishable Lord, the great-souled Vāsudeva, Lord of the lords of the worlds—

**6.62.29**  he whose son is Brahmā, the father of all the world—how then should this Vāsudeva not be worshipped and adored by men?

**6.62.30**  Of old, my son, you were warned by sages versed to the depths of the Vedas: 'Go not to war against that wise Vāsudeva, nor against the Pāṇḍavas'—yet in your delusion you do not heed it.

**6.62.31**  I deem you a cruel rākṣasa, and so you are, shrouded in darkness, since you hate Govinda and the Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya—the divine Nara and Nārāyaṇa, whom no other man would hate.

**6.62.32**  Therefore I tell you, O king: he is indeed the eternal and imperishable One, made of all the worlds, the everlasting, the ruler, the sustainer, the upholder, the unshaken.

**6.62.33**  He who upholds the three worlds, the Lord and teacher of all that moves and is still, the warrior, the victory, and the victor, the source of all nature, the sovereign—

**6.62.34**  O king, he is made of pure goodness, free of darkness and of passion; where Kṛṣṇa is, there is dharma, and where dharma is, there is victory.

**6.62.35**  By the power of his greatness and by the yoga of his very self, the sons of Pāṇḍu are sustained, O king, and victory shall be theirs.

**6.62.36**  He who ever bestows upon the Pāṇḍavas a mind set on what is good, and gives them strength in battle always, and guards them from all dangers—

**6.62.37**  this is that eternal God, made of all mysteries, the auspicious One, to be known as Vāsudeva, of whom you ask me, O Bhārata.

**6.62.38**  By brāhmaṇas, kṣatriyas, vaiśyas, and śūdras, each bearing his own marks, ever devoted to their proper duties, he is served and worshipped.

**6.62.39**  At the end of the Dvāpara age and the beginning of the Kali age, he was hymned by Saṃkarṣaṇa according to the Sātvata rite.

**6.62.40**  It is he, Vāsudeva, who age after age brings forth anew the whole world of asuras and of mortals, and the cities girt within the ocean's bounds, and his own dwelling among men, again and again.

**6.63.1**  Duryodhana said: Vāsudeva is spoken of in all the worlds as a great being. I wish to know his origin and his abiding ground, O Grandsire.

**6.63.2**  Bhīṣma said: Vāsudeva is a great being, born together with the gods. Nothing higher than the lotus-eyed One is to be seen, O bull of the Bharatas; and Mārkaṇḍeya too tells of Govinda the wondrous and the mighty.

**6.63.3**  The Self of beings, the great-souled Supreme Person, fashioned all beings, and these three—the waters, the wind, and fire.

**6.63.4**  Having created the earth, that divine Lord of all the worlds, the great-souled Supreme Person, made his couch upon the waters; and the God, made wholly of the waters, slept there through the power of yoga.

**6.63.5**  From his mouth he brought forth Agni, and from his breath the Wind as well; and from his mind the Imperishable One created Sarasvatī and the Vedas.

**6.63.6**  He it was who in the beginning created the worlds, the gods together with the hosts of seers, and death and destruction too—the imperishable source of all creatures.

**6.63.7**  He is the Law and the knower of the Law, the granter of boons, the fulfiller of every desire; he is the doer and the deed, the primeval god, self-luminous.

**6.63.8**  What has been, what is, and what is yet to come—all this Janārdana ordained of old, and the two twilights, the directions, the sky, and the ruling order.

**6.63.9**  Govinda likewise fashioned the seers and austerity in their turn, and the great-souled, imperishable lord shaped the Creator of the world as well.

**6.63.10**  He fashioned Saṃkarṣaṇa, firstborn of all beings, and he fashioned too the divine Śeṣa, whom men know as the Endless One.

**6.63.11**  He who upholds all beings and this earth with its mountains—him the brāhmaṇas, through the yoga of meditation, declare to be of mighty splendor.

**6.63.12**  And the great asura named Madhu, sprung from the channel of his ear—fierce, fierce in deeds, set upon a fierce design—him the Supreme Person slew, doing honor to Brahmā.

**6.63.13**  From that slaying, my son, gods, dānavas, and men, and the seers, call Janārdana the Slayer of Madhu; and the lord is also the Boar, the Lion, and he whose stride spans the three worlds.

**6.63.14**  Hari is the mother and the father of all living things; for higher than the lotus-eyed one nothing has ever been, nor ever shall be.

**6.63.15**  From his mouth he brought forth the brāhmaṇas, from his arms the kṣatriyas, from his thighs, O king, the vaiśyas, and from his feet the śūdras—the god, bound to austerity, the resting-place of all embodied beings.

**6.63.16**  He who, at the new moon and the full moon alike, attends in worship upon Keśava, become one with brahman and become one with yoga, shall attain the great goal.

**6.63.17**  Keśava is the supreme radiance, the grandsire of all the worlds; thus, O lord of men, do the sages speak of Hṛṣīkeśa.

**6.63.18**  Know him, then, to be teacher, father, and guru; he with whom Kṛṣṇa is pleased has thereby won the imperishable worlds.

**6.63.19**  And the man who in a place of fear takes refuge in this Keśava, and who ever recites this hymn, shall be safe and shall be happy.

**6.63.20**  Those mortals who take refuge in Kṛṣṇa do not fall into bewilderment; those sunk in great peril Janārdana ever protects.

**6.63.21**  Knowing all this in its very truth, O Bhārata, Yudhiṣṭhira with his whole being took refuge in the great-souled Keśava, lord of the world, O king—the lord and master of all yogas.

**6.64.1**  Bhīṣma said: Hear now from me too, O great king, this Hymn to the One Become Brahman, which of old upon the earth was uttered by the brahmā-seers and by the gods.

**6.64.2**  "Lord and god of the gods, even of the Sādhya gods, knower of the being and becoming of the worlds"—so Nārada addressed you; and Mārkaṇḍeya called you what has been, what is, and what shall be.

**6.64.3**  The sacrifice of sacrifices, the austerity of austerities, the god even of the gods—so the blessed Bhṛgu named you, O Viṣṇu, of awful form in days of yore, lord of all beings.

**6.64.4**  "You are Vāsudeva among the Vasus, and the establisher of Śakra; you are the god of the gods"—so Dvaipāyana declared.

**6.64.5**  "In the first creations of creatures they call Dakṣa the Lord of Beings, the maker of all that lives"—so then Aṅgiras spoke of you.

**6.64.6**  "The unmanifest arises from your body, the manifest abides in your mind, and the gods are born of your speech"—so Asita Devala spoke.

**6.64.7**  By your head the heaven is filled, by your arms the earth is upheld; your belly is the three worlds—you are the eternal Person.

**6.64.8**  Thus do men matured by austerity come to know you; and you are the best even of those seers who are content in the vision of the Self.

**6.64.9**  Of the noble royal seers, who never turn back in battle, who hold all duty foremost—of these you are the goal, O Slayer of Madhu.

**6.64.10**  This, my son, is the full account and the brief account alike, proclaimed of Keśava as it truly is; be well pleased with Keśava.

**6.64.11**  Saṃjaya said: Having heard this holy tale, O great king, your son held Keśava in high regard, and the Pāṇḍavas too, those mighty warriors.

**6.64.12**  To him, O great king, Bhīṣma the son of Śāntanu spoke again: You have heard, O king, the greatness of the great-souled Keśava.

**6.64.13**  And you have heard the truth of Nara as well, of which you asked me—wherefore the two, Nara and Nārāyaṇa, were born among men.

**6.64.14**  And how those two heroes are not to be slain, unconquered in their encounters, and how the Pāṇḍavas, O king, are not to be reached in battle by any man.

**6.64.15**  For Kṛṣṇa is firmly devoted to the glorious Pāṇḍavas; therefore I say to you, O lord of kings: let there be peace with the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.64.16**  Self-mastered, enjoy the earth together with your strong brothers; but if you slight the divine Nara and Nārāyaṇa, you shall perish.

**6.64.17**  Having spoken thus, your father fell silent, O lord of peoples, and dismissed the king and went in to his couch.

**6.64.18**  The king too, having bowed to the great-souled one, went off to his camp and lay that night upon his bright couch, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.65.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the night had passed and the day-maker had risen, the two armies, O great king, came together once more for battle.

**6.65.2**  Enraged, eager each to vanquish the other, they all charged together into the fray, eyeing one another.

**6.65.3**  The Pāṇḍavas and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O king—through your ill counsel—each drew up its array, and the smiters, full of fury, joined in combat.

**6.65.4**  Bhīṣma, O king, guarded the crocodile-array on every side, and the Pāṇḍavas likewise, O king, guarded their own array.

**6.65.5**  Your father Devavrata, best of chariot-warriors, rode forth at the head of the chariot-host, encircled by a great line of cars.

**6.65.6**  Each followed close upon the other, drawn up in his appointed place—the charioteers, the foot-soldiers, the elephant-riders, and the horsemen.

**6.65.7**  Seeing them ranged up for the encounter, the glorious Pāṇḍavas too stood ready with that royal hawk-shaped array, invincible in battle.

**6.65.8**  At its beak shone the mighty Bhīmasena; its two eyes were the unassailable Śikhaṇḍin and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata.

**6.65.9**  Its head was the hero Sātyaki, true in valor, and Pārtha, brandishing the Gāṇḍīva, was then at its neck.

**6.65.10**  With a full akṣauhiṇī the great-souled, illustrious Drupada, together with his son, formed the left wing then in the battle.

**6.65.11**  The right wing was the Kaikeya, lord of an akṣauhiṇī; and at the rear were the sons of Draupadī and the valiant son of Subhadrā.

**6.65.12**  At the rear stood the illustrious king Yudhiṣṭhira himself, the wise one of fair prowess, together with his two brothers, the twins.

**6.65.13**  Then Bhīma, plunging into the battle at the mouth of the crocodile-array, closed with Bhīṣma in the fight and covered him with arrows.

**6.65.14**  Thereupon Bhīṣma loosed his great weapons, O Bhārata, bewildering the marshaled host of Pāṇḍu's sons in that mighty fray.

**6.65.15**  As the host fell into confusion, Dhanaṃjaya in haste pierced Bhīṣma with a thousand arrows at the head of the battle.

**6.65.16**  And having warded off the weapons loosed by Bhīṣma in the fight, he stood ready for battle with his own exultant host.

**6.65.17**  Then king Duryodhana, best of the strong, that great warrior, having seen before the dread slaughter of his host and recalling the slaying of his brothers in the fight, spoke to the son of Bharadvāja.

**6.65.18**  Teacher, you who are without fault have ever wished my good; for it is upon you and upon the grandsire Bhīṣma that we have relied.

**6.65.19**  We aspire, beyond doubt, to conquer even the gods in battle—how much more the sons of Pāṇḍu, lacking in might and valor in war.

**6.65.20**  Thus addressed by your son, O honored one, Droṇa then broke through the Pāṇḍava host before the very eyes of Sātyaki.

**6.65.21**  But Sātyaki then held Droṇa back, O Bhārata, and thereupon a tumultuous, hair-raising combat broke out.

**6.65.22**  The valorous son of Bharadvāja, enraged in the fight, pierced the grandson of Śini with sharp arrows in the region of the collarbone, as though laughing the while.

**6.65.23**  Then Bhīmasena in his wrath pierced the son of Bharadvāja, shielding Sātyaki, O king, from Droṇa, best of all who bear arms.

**6.65.24**  Then Droṇa and Bhīṣma and Śalya too, O honored one, in their fury covered Bhīmasena with arrows in the fight.

**6.65.25**  Thereupon the enraged Abhimanyu and the sons of Draupadī, O honored one, pierced with sharp arrows all those warriors whose weapons were raised.

**6.65.26**  And as the mighty Bhīṣma and Droṇa, full of wrath, came rushing on, Śikhaṇḍin the great archer went out to meet them in that great battle.

**6.65.27**  Grasping with might his bow that roared like a thunder-cloud, the hero swiftly rained down arrows, veiling the very sun.

**6.65.28**  But the grandsire of the Bharatas, on encountering Śikhaṇḍin, turned aside from him in the battle, remembering his womanhood.

**6.65.29**  Then Droṇa, O great king, charged against him in the fight, guarding Bhīṣma, urged on by your son.

**6.65.30**  But Śikhaṇḍin, on encountering Droṇa, best of the bearers of arms, turned aside from him in the battle, terrible as the fire at the end of an age.

**6.65.31**  Then with a great force your son, O lord of peoples, came up to Bhīṣma and guarded him, seeking great renown.

**6.65.32**  In like manner the Pāṇḍavas, O king, putting Dhanaṃjaya at their head, advanced upon Bhīṣma alone, having set their minds firmly upon victory.

**6.65.33**  Dreadful was that battle, like the war of the gods with the dānavas, between those who ever longed for victory and for surpassing, wondrous glory.

**6.66.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then Bhīṣma the son of Śāntanu waged a tumultuous fight, wishing, for fear of Bhīmasena, to carry your sons to safety.

**6.66.2**  In the forenoon that most terrible battle of the kings raged on, the destroyer of the foremost heroes of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas alike.

**6.66.3**  As that confused and most fearful battle went on, there arose a tumultuous din, mightily touching the very sky.

**6.66.4**  With the trumpeting of great elephants, the neighing of horses, and the blare of kettledrums and conches, the uproar arose.

**6.66.5**  Eager to fight, valiant, mighty in strength, longing for victory, they bellowed at one another like great bulls within their pens.

**6.66.6**  Of the heads struck off in the battle by sharp arrows there came, as it were, a rain of stones in the sky, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.66.7**  Heads still wearing their earrings and turbans, gleaming bright with gold, were seen to lie fallen, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.66.8**  With limbs mangled by arrows, with arms that still grasped their bows, and with others adorned with bracelets upon the hand, the earth was strewn over.

**6.66.9**  With bodies sheathed in armor, with hands still richly adorned, and with fair faces like the moon, their eyes red at the corners,—

**6.66.10**  —and with all the limbs of elephants, horses, and men, O lord of the earth, the whole earth in a moment lay covered over.

**6.66.11**  With tumultuous clouds of dust, lit up by the lightning-flash of weapons, the clash of arms became like the rumbling of thunder.

**6.66.12**  That clash, tumultuous, bitter, with blood for its waters, rolled on between the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas, O Bhārata.

**6.66.13**  In that dread and fearful tumult, that hair-raising turmoil, the warriors, drunk with the frenzy of battle, poured down their showers of arrows.

**6.66.14**  There the elephants, scorched by the rain of arrows, trumpeted in agony—both yours and the enemy's, O best of the Bharatas, in that clash—and the horses, their riders slain, fled wildly across the ten directions.

**6.66.15**  Others, tormented by the blows of arrows, leaped up and fell again—warriors of yours and of the foe alike, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.66.16**  Heaped-up masses of horses, of elephants, and of careering chariots could be seen here and there, O lord of peoples.

**6.66.17**  With maces and swords, with javelins and with smooth-jointed shafts, the warriors, driven on by Death, struck one another there.

**6.66.18**  Other heroes, skilled in unarmed combat, grappled in the fray with their bare arms in many a way, as though with bars of iron.

**6.66.19**  With fists and knees and open palms, O lord of peoples, the heroes smote each other—your men together with the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.66.20**  And there chariot-warriors stripped of their cars, wielding their excellent swords, rushed upon one another, each bent on the other's slaughter.

**6.66.21**  Then King Duryodhana, surrounded by a host of Kaliṅgas, placing Bhīṣma at the head of the battle, advanced against the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.66.22**  And so too all the Pāṇḍavas, ranging themselves about Vṛkodara, charged in fury upon Bhīṣma in battle, their steeds borne on with headlong speed.

**6.67.1**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing his brothers and the other princes locked in combat with Bhīṣma, Dhanaṃjaya, his weapon upraised, rushed upon the son of Gaṅgā.

**6.67.2**  At the blare of the Pāñcajanya and of the Gāṇḍīva bow, and at the sight of Pārtha's banner, terror entered into us all.

**6.67.3**  Catching upon no tree, risen like a blazing comet, many-hued and wondrous, divine and bearing the mark of the ape—such, O great king, did we behold the banner of the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva.

**6.67.4**  Like lightning set amid the clouds, gleaming in the sky, the warriors beheld the gold-backed Gāṇḍīva in the hands of that mighty car-warrior.

**6.67.5**  And we heard, loud as the thunder of Śakra, the dread clap of his palms as he smote down your host.

**6.67.6**  As a storm-cloud driven by a fierce wind, charged with lightning and thunder, floods all the quarters with rain, so on every side he deluged them with showers of arrows.

**6.67.7**  Dhanaṃjaya, wielding his terrible weapons, rushed upon the son of Gaṅgā; bewildered by his missiles, we no longer knew the east from the west.

**6.67.8**  Lost as to which way to turn, their plumes drooping, their weapons spent and their wits undone, those warriors clung one to another, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.67.9**  They sought refuge with Bhīṣma alone, together with all your sons; in that battle Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, became the haven of the afflicted.

**6.67.10**  Then the car-warriors in terror leaped from their chariots, the horsemen from the backs of their steeds, and the foot-soldiers sank to the ground.

**6.67.11**  Hearing the roar of the Gāṇḍīva, like the crash of a thunderbolt, all the armies in their fear melted away, O Bhārata.

**6.67.12**  Then the lord of the cowherds, Govāsana, came surrounded by the foremost Kāmbojas—tall and swift of foot—and by the many thousands of herdsmen's forces.

**6.67.13**  And the lord of the Kaliṅgas was ringed about by the Madras, Sauvīras, and Gāndhāras, by the Trigartas, O lord of peoples, and by all the chief warriors of the Kaliṅgas.

**6.67.14**  Elephants and great hosts of men, with Duḥśāsana at their head, and King Jayadratha together with all the kings—

**6.67.15**  and excellent horsemen, fourteen thousand strong, urged on by your son, encircled the son of Subala.

**6.67.16**  Then all your men together, with their chariots and mounts marshalled apart, advanced upon the Pāṇḍavas in battle, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.67.17**  Stirred up by car-warriors, elephants, horses, and foot-soldiers, the dread dust of battle arose, resembling a great mass of clouds.

**6.67.18**  With a mighty host of warriors fighting with lances, javelins, and iron shafts, mounted on elephants, horses, and chariots, Bhīṣma closed with the Diademed one.

**6.67.19**  The lord of Avanti closed with the king of Kāśi, the lord of Sindhu with Bhīmasena; and Ajātaśatru, with his sons and counsellors, engaged the glorious bull of the Madras, Śalya.

**6.67.20**  Vikarṇa engaged Sahadeva, Citrasena engaged Śikhaṇḍin, and the Matsyas advanced against Duryodhana and against Śakuni, O lord of peoples.

**6.67.21**  Drupada, Cekitāna, and the great car-warrior Sātyaki closed with the high-souled Droṇa and his son; while Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman rushed together upon Dhṛṣṭaketu.

**6.67.22**  Thus the armies, their horses galloping and their elephants and chariots wheeling about, closed and grappled in furious combat on every side.

**6.67.23**  Though the sky was cloudless, fierce lightnings flashed and the quarters were veiled in dust; great meteors appeared, attended by crashing thunderclaps, O lord of peoples.

**6.67.24**  A great wind blew, a rain of dust came down, and in the heavens the sun was hidden away, mantled by the dust raised by the host.

**6.67.25**  An utter bewilderment came upon all living things, overwhelmed as they were by the dust and tormented by the nets of weapons.

**6.67.26**  A tumultuous massing of webs of arrows arose—shafts loosed from the arms of heroes, piercing through every kind of armour.

**6.67.27**  Weapons upraised by mighty arms, bright as the stars, lit up the firmament, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.67.28**  Shields of bull's hide, of varied hue and overlaid with networks of gold, flew through all the quarters, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.67.29**  Struck down on every side by swords bright as the sun, bodies and heads were seen falling throughout all the quarters.

**6.67.30**  With wheels, axles, and chariot-boxes shattered, their tall banners cast down and their horses slain, the great car-warriors sank to the earth here and there.

**6.67.31**  And here some horses, wounded by weapons, ran wildly about, dragging the chariots after them, their car-warriors slain.

**6.67.32**  Struck by arrows, their bodies torn, yet still bound in their harness, the finest of steeds dragged the yokes about here and there, O Bhārata.

**6.67.33**  Chariots could be seen, with their drivers, their horses, and their fighting men, all slain together by a single mighty elephant, O king.

**6.67.34**  Catching in the battle the scent of the rut flowing from the lordly tuskers, many an elephant, at the clash of those floods of troops, took to flight.

**6.67.35**  The field of battle was covered over with elephants struck down by iron shafts, falling lifeless together with their lances and their mighty riders.

**6.67.36**  At the clash of those floods of troops, chariots—shattered by the lordly elephants hurled against them—fell in battle together with their warriors and their banners.

**6.67.37**  Seized in battle by the trunks of elephants huge as the king of serpents, chariot-poles could be seen snapped asunder, O great king.

**6.67.38**  Their lattice-work of chariots smashed, the car-warriors were seized by the hair by the tuskers, whirled about like the branches of trees, and ground to pieces in the fray.

**6.67.39**  Dragging chariot upon chariot, locked together in the fight, the lordly elephants rushed in every direction, plunging through every clamour.

**6.67.40**  As they dragged their burdens so, those elephants had the look of beasts hauling a tangle of lotus-stalks caught fast in the pools.

**6.67.41**  Thus was that vast field of battle covered over with horsemen and foot-soldiers and with great car-warriors and their banners.

**6.68.1**  Saṃjaya said: Śikhaṇḍin, together with Virāṭa of the Matsyas, O lord of peoples, swiftly fell upon Bhīṣma, that great archer most hard to conquer.

**6.68.2**  Dhanaṃjaya assailed Droṇa, Kṛpa, and Vikarṇa—great archers of mighty strength—and many other heroic kings in the battle.

**6.68.3**  And the lord of Sindhu, that great archer, with his counsellors and kinsmen, and the kings of the East and of the South, O bull among lords of the earth—

**6.68.4**  and against your son, the great archer, the unforgiving Duryodhana, and against Duḥsaha as well, did Bhīmasena advance in battle.

**6.68.5**  But Sahadeva advanced upon Śakuni and the great car-warrior Ulūka—father and son, both great archers hard to vanquish.

**6.68.6**  Yudhiṣṭhira, O great king, that mighty car-warrior, met in battle the host of elephants—he whom your son had so wronged.

**6.68.7**  And the son of Mādrī, the valiant Nakula, raising his war-cry in the fray, that Pāṇḍava engaged the noble chariot-warriors of the Trigartas.

**6.68.8**  The unassailable Sātyaki, Cekitāna, and the great car-warrior, son of Subhadrā, advanced in battle against the Śālvas and Kekayas.

**6.68.9**  And Dhṛṣṭaketu and the rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca, hard indeed to overcome, advanced in battle against the chariot-host of your sons.

**6.68.10**  The commander of the army, the immeasurable-souled and mighty Dhṛṣṭadyumna, closed in battle, O king, with Droṇa, whose deeds were like Indra's.

**6.68.11**  Thus did these great archers, your heroes, meeting the Pāṇḍavas in battle, set to and join the fight.

**6.68.12**  When the sun had reached the midst of the sky and the heavens had fallen into turmoil, the Kurus and the sons of Pāṇḍu slew one another.

**6.68.13**  Bearing their standards, their frames bright with gold, ranging over the field of battle, the chariots with their pennons and their coverings of tiger-skin shone resplendent.

**6.68.14**  From those who had met in battle, each longing to overcome the other, there arose a tumultuous din, like that of roaring lions.

**6.68.15**  There we beheld a wondrous and most terrible encounter, which the heroic Sṛñjayas waged in battle against the Kurus.

**6.68.16**  Neither the sky nor the quarters, O king, nor the sun, O scorcher of foes, nor even the intermediate points could we see, for the arrows loosed on every side.

**6.68.17**  From the spears with their gleaming points, from the lances hurled, and from the burnished swords, came radiances like the hue of blue lotuses.

**6.68.18**  The splendours of the many-coloured cuirasses and of the ornaments lit up with their brilliance the sky, the quarters, and the intermediate quarters; and there, O king, the field of battle shone resplendent in every part.

**6.68.19**  Tigers among men upon their lion-thrones of chariots, advancing into the fray, shone forth in battle, O king, like the planets in the vault of heaven.

**6.68.20**  But Bhīṣma, the best of car-warriors, in his wrath held back the mighty Bhīmasena before the eyes of the whole army.

**6.68.21**  Then shafts loosed by Bhīṣma, gold-feathered and whetted on stone, polished with oil and keen-edged, smote Bhīma in the battle.

**6.68.22**  Against him the mighty Bhīmasena hurled a swift-flying spear, like an enraged venomous serpent, O Bhārata.

**6.68.23**  As it came rushing on, that gold-shafted and irresistible spear, Bhīṣma cut asunder in the battle with his smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.68.24**  Then with another broad-headed arrow, burnished and keen, he cut the bow of Bhīmasena in two, O Bhārata.

**6.68.25**  But Sātyaki then swiftly closed with Bhīṣma in the fray and assailed your sire with a multitude of arrows, O lord of men.

**6.68.26**  Then, fitting to his bow a sharp and most dreadful arrow, Bhīṣma struck down the charioteer of the Vṛṣṇi hero from his car.

**6.68.27**  His charioteer slain, his horses bolted, O king, and ran this way and that with the speed of thought and of the wind.

**6.68.28**  Then from the whole army arose a tumultuous uproar, and a cry of dismay broke forth among the high-souled Pāṇḍavas.

**6.68.29**  "Rush in! Seize them! Hold the horses! Run!"—such was the tumultuous clamour that arose about the chariot of Yuyudhāna.

**6.68.30**  At that very moment Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, once more smote the army of the Pāṇḍavas, as the slayer of Vṛtra smites the host of the Asuras.

**6.68.31**  Cut down by Bhīṣma, the Pāñcālas together with the Somakas, fixing their noble resolve upon the battle, rushed straight at Bhīṣma himself.

**6.68.32**  And the sons of Pṛthā too, led by Dhṛṣṭadyumna, charged at the son of Śaṃtanu in the fray, eager to overcome the host of your son.

**6.68.33**  In the same way your warriors, O king, led by Bhīṣma and Droṇa, rushed against the foe with great speed, and thereupon the battle was joined.

**6.69.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then Virāṭa struck Bhīṣma, that mighty car-warrior, with three arrows, and the great car-warrior pierced his horses with three arrows more.

**6.69.2**  In return Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, that mighty bowman of skilled hand and great strength, pierced him with ten gold-feathered shafts.

**6.69.3**  Droṇa's son, a great car-warrior of the dreadful bow and firm hand, struck the wielder of Gāṇḍīva with six arrows between the breasts.

**6.69.4**  Phalguna, slayer of hostile heroes, cut his bow asunder, and that tormentor of foes pierced him sorely with sharp, feathered shafts.

**6.69.5**  Unable to bear the cutting of his bow by Pārtha in the battle, and overpowered by wrath, he took up another swift-shooting bow.

**6.69.6**  He pierced Phalguna, O king, with ninety whetted arrows, and struck Vāsudeva with seventy excellent shafts.

**6.69.7**  Then Phalguna, his eyes turned coppery with rage, together with Kṛṣṇa, drawing a long and burning breath and pondering again and again,

**6.69.8**  That tormentor of enemies, the wrathful wielder of Gāṇḍīva, gripping his bow firm with his left hand, took up keen, well-jointed, dreadful arrows that bring life to its end.

**6.69.9**  With these he swiftly struck Droṇa's son, foremost of the strong, in battle; and piercing his armor, those shafts drank his blood in the fray.

**6.69.10**  Yet though pierced by the wielder of Gāṇḍīva, Droṇa's son did not waver; unshaken, hurling back showers of arrows, he stood firm in the fight, O king, wishing to protect Bhīṣma of the great vow.

**6.69.11**  The bulls among men praised that mighty deed of his, that he was not abashed in combat before the two Kṛṣṇas joined together.

**6.69.12**  For ever in the ranks he fought, established in fearlessness, having obtained from Droṇa the whole array of weapons with their withdrawal, a thing most hard to gain.

**6.69.13**  "This man is the son of my teacher, the dearly beloved son of Droṇa, and a brāhmaṇa besides, and so to be especially honored by me" —

**6.69.14**  forming this resolve, the heroic Bībhatsu, scorcher of foes, foremost of car-warriors, showed mercy toward the son of Bharadvāja's line.

**6.69.15**  Leaving Droṇa's son alone, then, the valiant son of Kuntī, scorcher of foes, fought on, swiftly slaying your warriors.

**6.69.16**  But Duryodhana assailed the mighty bowman Bhīmasena with ten vulture-feathered, gold-nocked arrows whetted on stone.

**6.69.17**  Then Bhīmasena, filled with wrath, took up a stout and gaily-wrought bow, a destroyer of foes, and ten whetted arrows.

**6.69.18**  With sharp, swift, fiercely glowing shafts drawn back to the ear, the unfaltering one quickly struck the king of the Kurus on his broad chest.

**6.69.19**  The jewel on its golden chain, ringed about by those arrows, shone upon his breast like the sun encircled by the planets.

**6.69.20**  But your splendid son, struck by Bhīmasena, brooked it no more than an elephant brooks the sound of a clapped palm raised against it.

**6.69.21**  Then, O great king, full of wrath and striking terror into the host, he pierced Bhīma with gold-nocked arrows whetted on stone.

**6.69.22**  As they fought in battle, sorely wounding each other, those two mighty sons of yours, godlike, shone resplendent.

**6.69.23**  Subhadrā's son, that slayer of hostile heroes, pierced Citrasena, the tiger among men, with ten arrows, and Purumitra with seven.

**6.69.24**  And piercing Satyavrata with seventy, that hero, equal to Śakra in war, as though dancing in the fray, begot anguish in us.

**6.69.25**  In return Citrasena struck him with ten shafts, Satyavrata with nine, and Purumitra with seven.

**6.69.26**  Pierced and streaming blood, the son of Arjuna cut Citrasena's great gaily-wrought bow, that warder-off of foes, and breaking through his armor, struck him on the breast with a shaft.

**6.69.27**  Then your heroic warriors, the princes, great car-warriors, joining together in fury, pierced him with whetted arrows; but he, knowing the highest weapons, smote them all with sharp shafts.

**6.69.28**  Beholding that deed of his, your sons surrounded him, as he burned your army in battle like a raging fire consuming dry grass.

**6.69.29**  Like a blazing fire when the cold season is past, the son of Subhadrā shone surpassingly bright as he cut down your troops.

**6.69.30**  Beholding that exploit of his, O lord of peoples, your grandson Lakṣmaṇa swiftly fell upon the son of the Sātvata princess in battle.

**6.69.31**  But Abhimanyu, filled with wrath, pierced Lakṣmaṇa of auspicious marks with six shafts, and his charioteer with three arrows.

**6.69.32**  In the same way Lakṣmaṇa, O king, struck the son of Subhadrā with whetted arrows; and that, O great king, seemed a marvel.

**6.69.33**  Having slain his four horses and his charioteer, the mighty son of Subhadrā rushed upon Lakṣmaṇa with whetted arrows.

**6.69.34**  Standing upon his car whose horses were slain, Lakṣmaṇa, slayer of hostile heroes, in his wrath hurled a spear at the chariot of Subhadrā's son.

**6.69.35**  As it sped suddenly upon him, dreadful of form, hard to withstand, and like a serpent, Abhimanyu cut it apart with sharp arrows.

**6.69.36**  Then Gautama, taking Lakṣmaṇa up onto his own car, bore him away from the battle in his chariot before the eyes of all the host.

**6.69.37**  Then, in that great and terrible tumult that prevailed, they rushed at one another, eager to kill, each seeking the other's death.

**6.69.38**  Your mighty bowmen and the great car-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, offering up their very lives in the battle, slew one another.

**6.69.39**  Their hair loosened, their armor gone, bereft of chariots and their bows cut to pieces, the Sṛñjayas fought with the Kurus with their bare arms.

**6.69.40**  Then mighty-armed Bhīṣma of great strength, full of wrath, smote the army of the high-souled Pāṇḍavas with celestial weapons.

**6.69.41**  There the earth was strewn over with elephants whose riders were slain, with men and fallen horses, with car-warriors and horsemen alike.

**6.70.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, O king, mighty-armed Sātyaki, frenzied in war, drawing in the battle his bow, that excellent accomplisher of heavy tasks,

**6.70.2**  loosed well-feathered shafts like venomous serpents, displaying clear, swift, and dazzling lightness of hand with his weapons.

**6.70.3**  As he flexed his bow and loosed yet other arrows, and again took up more and set still others to the string,

**6.70.4**  and hurled his shafts and slew the foe in the fray, his form appeared exceedingly like a cloud pouring down rain.

**6.70.5**  Beholding him thus rising in fury, king Duryodhana then sent against him ten thousand chariots, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.70.6**  But Sātyaki of true valor, the supreme bowman of great might, slew all those mighty archers with a celestial weapon.

**6.70.7**  Having wrought that dreadful deed, the hero, his bow grasped firmly, then closed with Bhūriśravas in the battle.

**6.70.8**  For he, that increaser of the Kurus' fame, beholding that host laid low by Yuyudhāna, charged at him in wrath.

**6.70.9**  Twanging that great bow colored like Indra's bow, the rainbow, he loosed shafts resembling the thunderbolt and like venomous serpents by the thousand, O great king, displaying the lightness of his hand.

**6.70.10**  Those arrows whose touch was death the followers of Sātyaki could not endure, O king; they fled in every direction, abandoning in the battle, O king, Sātyaki frenzied in war.

**6.70.11**  Seeing this, the ten mighty sons of Yuyudhāna, renowned great car-warriors with brilliant armor, weapons, and banners,

**6.70.12**  closing in battle with the mighty bowman Bhūriśravas, all in great fury addressed him whose banner bore the sacrificial post, in that great fray:

**6.70.13**  "Ho, ho, heir of the Kauravas, O mighty one! Come, fight with us in battle, with all of us together or with each one apart.

**6.70.14**  Either vanquish us and win glory in combat, or we, vanquishing you, shall give joy to our father."

**6.70.15**  Thus addressed by those heroes, the mighty one, foremost of men, who gloried in valor, beholding them drawn up before him, said to them:

**6.70.16**  "Well is this spoken, O heroes, that such is your resolve this day. Fight all together, intent upon it; I shall slay you in the battle."

**6.70.17**  Thus addressed, those heroic, swift-handed bowmen rained upon that tamer of foes a mighty shower of arrows.

**6.70.18**  In the afternoon, O great king, a tumultuous combat arose on the field of battle between the one and the many assembled together.

**6.70.19**  That one, foremost of car-warriors, they covered over with showers of arrows, as in the rains the clouds drench a great mountain, O king.

**6.70.20**  But the floods of arrows loosed by them, gleaming like the rod of Yama and the thunderbolt, that great car-warrior swiftly cut to pieces ere they reached him, again and again before they came.

**6.70.21**  There we beheld a marvel, the prowess of Somadatta's son, that he alone closed in battle with the many, undaunted.

**6.70.22**  Having poured forth that rain of arrows, the ten great car-warriors, O king, surrounded the mighty-armed one and set about to slay him.

**6.70.23**  Then the son of Somadatta, in wrath, that great car-warrior, O descendant of Bharata, cut their bows asunder with ten arrows in the twinkling of an eye.

**6.70.24**  Then, their bows broken, he cut off their heads in the battle, O king, with whetted, well-jointed broad-headed shafts; slain, they fell to the earth like trees shattered by the thunderbolt.

**6.70.25**  Seeing those heroes, his mighty sons, slain in the battle, the Vārṣṇeya, roaring aloud, O king, rushed at Bhūriśravas.

**6.70.26**  Pressing car against car in the battle, those two mighty ones, having each slain the other's chariot-horses in the fray, met together, those great car-warriors, leaping forward, both bereft of their cars.

**6.70.27**  Grasping their great swords and bearing their excellent shields, those two tigers among men shone resplendent as they took their stand for combat.

**6.70.28**  Then Bhīmasena, hastening up to Sātyaki who bore his excellent sword, mounted him upon his car at that time, O king.

**6.70.29**  And your son too, O king, swiftly mounted Bhūriśravas upon a chariot in the battle, before the eyes of all the bowmen.

**6.70.30**  While that combat thus went on, the Pāṇḍavas in their fury did battle with Bhīṣma, the great car-warrior, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.70.31**  As the sun grew red, Dhanaṃjaya, making haste, slew twenty-five thousand great car-warriors.

**6.70.32**  For these, commanded by Duryodhana then to the destruction of Pārtha, went to their ruin as soon as they reached him, like moths into a flame.

**6.70.33**  Then the Matsyas and the Kekayas, skilled in the science of the bow, surrounded Pārtha, that great car-warrior, together with his son.

**6.70.34**  At that very time, as the sun was going down to its setting, a bewilderment came over all the troops alike.

**6.70.35**  Then your father Devavrata, O great king, at the hour of twilight, called off the troops, their mounts grown weary.

**6.70.36**  At the clash of the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus, each against the other, both armies, sorely shaken, withdrew to their own encampments.

**6.70.37**  Then, having gone each to its own camp, they settled there, O Bhārata—the Pāṇḍavas together with the Sṛñjayas, and the Kurus likewise, in due order.

**6.71.1**  Saṃjaya said: Having rested through the night, O king, the Kurus and Pāṇḍavas together, when the dark watch had passed, marched forth once more to battle.

**6.71.2**  There arose a mighty din, O Bhārata, among your forces and among theirs, as the foremost chariots were yoked and the tusked elephants made ready.

**6.71.3**  As the foot soldiers armed themselves and the horses too, O Bhārata, a tumultuous blare of conches and kettledrums rose up on every side.

**6.71.4**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira addressed Dhṛṣṭadyumna: O mighty-armed one, scorcher of foes, array the host in the form of the Makara, the sea-monster.

**6.71.5**  Thus addressed by Pārtha, the great chariot-warrior Dhṛṣṭadyumna, O great king, the best of chariot-fighters, gave his commands to the warriors.

**6.71.6**  Drupada, with the Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya, formed its head; Sahadeva and the great chariot-warrior Nakula were its two eyes; and the mighty Bhīmasena, O great king, was its snout.

**6.71.7**  The son of Subhadrā, the sons of Draupadī, the Rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca, Sātyaki, and the King of Righteousness took their stand at the neck of the formation.

**6.71.8**  At the back, O great king, stood Virāṭa, lord of armies, together with Dhṛṣṭadyumna, surrounded by a great host.

**6.71.9**  The five Kekaya brothers held the left flank; while Dhṛṣṭaketu, tiger among men, and the valiant Karakarṣa, taking the right wing, stood to guard the formation.

**6.71.10**  At the two feet, O great king, stood the illustrious great chariot-warriors Kuntibhoja and Śatānīka, encircled by a mighty host.

**6.71.11**  And the mighty bowman Śikhaṇḍī, ringed about by the Somakas, together with Irāvat, took his post at the tail of the Makara.

**6.71.12**  Having arrayed this great formation thus, O Bhārata, the Pāṇḍavas, mailed and armored at sunrise, stood ready once more for battle, O great king.

**6.71.13**  Swiftly they advanced upon the Kauravas with elephants, horses, chariots, and foot, with banners raised aloft and varied, and with weapons gleaming and keen.

**6.71.14**  Beholding that arrayed host, your father Devavrata, O king, drew up his own army in counter-formation as a great Krauñca, the heron.

**6.71.15**  At its beak shone the mighty bowman Bhāradvāja; and Aśvatthāman and Kṛpa were its two eyes, O lord of men.

**6.71.16**  And Kṛtavarman, best among men and foremost of all bowmen, joined by the Kāmbojas, the Araṭṭas, and the Bāhlikas, stood at the head.

**6.71.17**  At the neck stood Śūrasena and your son Duryodhana, O honored one, O great king, surrounded by many kings.

**6.71.18**  The lord of Prāgjyotiṣa, together with the Madras, Sauvīras, and Kekayas, held the breast, O best of men, encircled by a great host.

**6.71.19**  Suśarman, lord of Prasthala, with his own troops, took the left wing and stood there mailed and ready.

**6.71.20**  The Tuṣāras, the Yavanas, and the Śakas, together with the Cūcupas, holding the right wing, stood guard over the formation, O Bhārata.

**6.71.21**  Śrutāyus, Śatāyus, and the son of Somadatta, O honored one, took their stand at the rear of the array, guarding one another.

**6.71.22**  Then the Pāṇḍavas closed with the Kauravas for battle at sunrise, O great king, and thereupon a mighty combat began.

**6.71.23**  Chariot-warriors charged the elephants, and elephants advanced upon the chariots; horsemen rushed at horsemen, and chariot-fighters at riders.

**6.71.24**  And the chariot-warrior fell upon the charioteer and upon the elephants in that great battle, O king; the elephant-riders fell upon the chariot-riders, and the chariot-fighters upon the horsemen.

**6.71.25**  Chariot-fighters with foot soldiers, and horsemen too with foot soldiers, charged at one another in the fray, O king, beside themselves with fury.

**6.71.26**  Guarded by Bhīmasena, Arjuna, the twins, and other great chariot-warriors, the army of the Pāṇḍavas shone like the night adorned with stars.

**6.71.27**  And likewise your army, with Bhīṣma, Kṛpa, Droṇa, Śalya, Duryodhana, and the rest, blazed forth like the sky thick-set with planets.

**6.71.28**  But the valiant son of Kuntī, Bhīmasena, seeing Droṇa, drove with swift horses against the host of Bhāradvāja.

**6.71.29**  But Droṇa, enraged in the fight, the valorous one, pierced Bhīma in battle with nine iron shafts, aiming at his vital parts, O king.

**6.71.30**  Then Bhīma, struck hard in the combat, sent the charioteer of Bhāradvāja off to the abode of Yama.

**6.71.31**  Then the mighty Bhāradvāja, taking the reins of his horses himself, scattered the Pāṇḍava army as fire consumes a heap of cotton.

**6.71.32**  Cut down by Droṇa and by Bhīṣma, O best of men, the Sṛñjayas together with the Kekayas turned and fled in defeat.

**6.71.33**  In the same way your army, mangled by Bhīma and Arjuna, swooned here and there, like a lovely woman overcome with wine.

**6.71.34**  Then both formations were broken apart in that destruction of the foremost heroes, and a terrible confusion arose between your men and theirs, O Bhārata.

**6.71.35**  We beheld this marvel of your warriors locked with the foe, O Bhārata: how all of them, brought to a single narrow path, fought on.

**6.71.36**  Warding off one another's weapons, O lord of peoples, the great chariot-warriors of both Pāṇḍavas and Kauravas fought hand to hand.

**6.72.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: An army so rich in excellences, so manifold and supreme, arrayed thus according to the science of war, and unfailing too, O Saṃjaya—

**6.72.2**  Exceedingly well-provisioned, ever devoted to our cause, obedient, free of vice, its valor proven in former days—

**6.72.3**  Neither too old nor too young, neither lean nor gross, for the most part lithe and tall of limb, sinewy of body and free of disease—

**6.72.4**  Equipped with armor and weapons, furnished with weapons in abundance, skilled in sword-combat, in wrestling, and in fighting with the mace—

**6.72.5**  In battle skilled with the lance, the spear, and the javelin, with iron bludgeons, with slings, with darts, and with pestle-clubs, in every kind—

**6.72.6**  With the kampana, with the bow, and with the barbed kaṇapa in every form, with the wondrous catapult-slings, and skilled in fist-fighting too—

**6.72.7**  Versed firsthand in the sciences, trained hard in martial exercises, and accomplished in every art of wielding weapons—

**6.72.8**  Skilled in mounting, in leaping aside, in advancing, in springing across the intervening space, in striking aright, in charging, and in withdrawing—

**6.72.9**  Many times well-tested in the handling of elephants, horses, and chariots, and, after due examination, provided with its wages in proper measure—

**6.72.10**  Not through mere fellowship, nor through favor, nor on account of kinship, nor through the force of friendship, nor by the recruiting of low-born men—

**6.72.11**  Composed of prosperous, noble men, whose kinsmen were contented and well-honored, men richly bound by past favors, renowned and high of spirit—

**6.72.12**  Guarded by victorious, foremost men of many a foremost deed, like unto the guardians of the world, my son, and famed throughout the earth—

**6.72.13**  Protected by many Kṣatriyas honored across the world, who had come to us of their own free will with their troops and their retainers—

**6.72.14**  Like the great ocean filled on every side by rivers, encompassed by chariots and elephants that, though wingless, seemed to bear wings—

**6.72.15**  That dreadful sea whose waters were its varied warriors, whose surging waves were its mounts, thronged with slings, swords, maces, spears, arrows, and lances—

**6.72.16**  Close-packed with banners and ornaments, heaped with jeweled cloth, set astir by its coursing mounts as by the rush of the wind—

**6.72.17**  Roaring as though shoreless, vast and like unto the sea, well-shielded by Droṇa and Bhīṣma, and guarded by Kṛtavarman—

**6.72.18**  And by Kṛpa and Duḥśāsana, and by the warriors led by Jayadratha, by Bhagadatta and Vikarṇa, by the son of Droṇa, by the son of Subala, and by Bāhlika—

**6.72.19**  Guarded by the world's foremost heroes, steadfast and great of soul—that such an army should be cut down in battle, this is the working of an ancient fate.

**6.72.20**  Never had men beheld a martial array such as this, nor even the blessed sages of old upon the earth, O Saṃjaya.

**6.72.21**  When so vast a flood of force, furnished with the wealth of weapons and missiles, is slain in battle, what can this be but the work of destiny?

**6.72.22**  All this appears to me turned upside down, O Saṃjaya, in that so terrible a host could not overcome the Pāṇḍavas in the fight.

**6.72.23**  Or else the gods have gathered there for the Pāṇḍavas' sake and do battle against my army—and so it is being slain, O Saṃjaya.

**6.72.24**  For Vidura spoke counsel here that was salutary and wholesome, O Saṃjaya; yet my dull-witted son Duryodhana would not take it to heart.

**6.72.25**  I think that the mind of that all-knowing, great-souled one was set, even beforehand, upon what was foreordained, my son—for he had foreseen all this long ago.

**6.72.26**  Or else, O Saṃjaya, all this was bound by every means to come to pass: as the Ordainer fashioned it of old, so it is, and not otherwise.

**6.73.1**  Saṃjaya said: Through your own fault, O king, has such a calamity come upon you; for Duryodhana does not see, O bull of the Bharatas, those things which you saw, O king, when the confounding of righteousness was wrought.

**6.73.2**  Through your fault that game of dice took place long ago, O lord of peoples; through your fault this war with the Pāṇḍavas began. Now reap the fruit yourself, having wrought the evil by your own hand.

**6.73.3**  For the deed that one does oneself is by oneself enjoyed, whether here or hereafter, O king; and this has come upon you exactly as it was earned.

**6.73.4**  Therefore, O king, having met with this great calamity, make yourself steadfast and hear the battle as it befell, while I recount it, O honored one.

**6.73.5**  Bhīmasena, having cleft the great host with his whetted arrows, then, that hero, fell upon all the younger brothers of Duryodhana.

**6.73.6**  Duḥśāsana, Durviṣaha, Duḥsaha, Durmada, Jaya, Jayatsena, Vikarṇa, Citrasena, Sudarśana—

**6.73.7**  Cārucitra, Suvarman, Duṣkarṇa, and Karṇa as well—these and many other great chariot-warriors standing near at hand.

**6.73.8**  Seeing the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra in furious wrath, the mighty Bhīma plunged into that great host shielded in battle by Bhīṣma.

**6.73.9**  Then they called to one another: Here has Vṛkodara come! Let us seize him alive, we kings, and take him captive!

**6.73.10**  Pārtha, ringed about by those resolute brothers, was like the Sun at the dissolution of all creatures, hemmed in by the cruel great planets.

**6.73.11**  Having reached the very midst of the formation, no fear entered the Pāṇḍava, even as it entered not the great Indra when he met the Dānavas in the war of gods and demons.

**6.73.12**  Then hundreds of thousands of chariot-warriors on every side, O lord, hemmed in that single man, covering him with their dreadful shafts.

**6.73.13**  That hero, heedless of the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, slew in battle their foremost fighters—the elephant-riders, the horsemen, and the chariot-warriors.

**6.73.14**  Knowing the resolve of those who sought to take him captive, the great-minded Bhīmasena set his mind, O king, upon the slaughter of them all.

**6.73.15**  Then, forsaking his chariot and taking up his mace, the Pāṇḍava smote that vast ocean which was the flood of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's host.

**6.73.16**  When Bhīmasena had plunged in, Dhṛṣṭadyumna too, the son of Pṛṣata, leaving Droṇa, set out in haste for where the son of Subala was.

**6.73.17**  Having cloven the great host of your warriors, that bull among men came upon Bhīmasena's empty chariot in the fray.

**6.73.18**  Beholding Viśoka, Bhīmasena's charioteer, alone amid the battle, Dhṛṣṭadyumna, O great king, grew sick at heart and well-nigh swooned away.

**6.73.19**  Choked with tears, uttering a faltering voice, he asked in grief: "Where is Bhīma, dearer to me than my own breath?"

**6.73.20**  Viśoka, with joined palms, spoke thus to Dhṛṣṭadyumna: "Having stationed me here, the mighty and valiant son of Pāṇḍu

**6.73.21**  has plunged into this great ocean that is the host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons, O tiger among men, after speaking to me these affectionate words:

**6.73.22**  'Wait for me, charioteer, holding back the horses but a moment, while I swiftly slay these who are bent upon my destruction.'

**6.73.23**  Then, beholding that mighty one rushing forward with his mace in hand, a fierce clash arose among all the troops alike.

**6.73.24**  And while that tumultuous and terrible battle was raging, O king, your friend, cleaving the great array, entered into it."

**6.73.25**  Having heard the words of Viśoka, the mighty Dhṛṣṭadyumna, son of Pṛṣata, answered the charioteer there in the midst of the battle:

**6.73.26**  "There is no purpose for me, charioteer, in living this day, if I forsake Bhīmasena in battle and cast off my love for the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.73.27**  If I go without Bhīma, what will the warrior-caste say of me—that, when Bhīma had passed onto a single perilous path, I stood idle in the fight?

**6.73.28**  The gods, with Agni at their head, work the ruin of that man who, abandoning his comrades, returns safe and sound to his home.

**6.73.29**  Mighty Bhīma is my friend and my kinsman; he is devoted to us, and I too am devoted to him, that slayer of foes.

**6.73.30**  I shall therefore go to that place where Vṛkodara has gone. Behold me slaying the enemy, as Vāsava slew the Dānavas."

**6.73.31**  Having spoken thus, the hero advanced through the midst of the Bhārata host, along Bhīmasena's tracks marked by elephants crushed by his mace.

**6.73.32**  Then he beheld Bhīma scorching the enemy's army, shattering the kings in battle by main force, as the wind shatters trees.

**6.73.33**  Smitten in the battle, the chariot-warriors, the horsemen, the foot-soldiers, and the elephants alike raised a great cry of anguish.

**6.73.34**  And a wail of woe arose, O venerable one, from your army as it was being slaughtered by the skilled Bhīmasena, that fighter of wondrous craft.

**6.73.35**  Then all those masters of weapons, surrounding Vṛkodara, fearlessly closed upon him with a shower of missiles from every side.

**6.73.36**  Seeing the foremost of weapon-bearers, the son of Pāṇḍu, beset on every side by the heroes of the world, by that dreadful and well-marshalled host, the mighty son of Pṛṣata came toward Bhīmasena.

**6.73.37**  Then the son of Pṛṣata drew near and comforted Bhīmasena—his body torn by arrows, on foot, spewing the venom of his wrath, mace in hand like Death himself at the hour of doom.

**6.73.38**  Swiftly the great-souled one drew the darts from his body, mounted him upon his own chariot, and, embracing Bhīmasena warmly, consoled him in the very midst of the foe.

**6.73.39**  Then, as that great struggle raged, your son went up to his brothers: "This evil-souled son of Drupada has joined with Bhīmasena. Go, all of you together, to slay him; let not the foe make havoc of your ranks."

**6.73.40**  Hearing those words and unable to brook them, the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, urged by their eldest brother's command, rushed forth with uplifted weapons to the kill, terrible as baleful comets at the dissolution of an age.

**6.73.41**  Seizing their painted bows, those heroes, making the earth tremble with the twang of their bowstrings, rained arrows upon the son of Drupada, as rain-clouds drench a mountain with their torrents of water; yet, striking them down with keen shafts, that fighter of wondrous craft was nowise daunted in the battle.

**6.73.42**  Beholding your sons risen up and standing about him in battle, the young son of Drupada, that mighty car-warrior, eager to slay those fierce heroes, fitted the bewildering weapon, the pramohana, sorely wroth against your sons, O king, as great Indra against the Daityas in battle.

**6.73.43**  Then the heroic warriors were bewildered in the battle, their minds and senses smitten by the pramohana weapon; and all the Kurus, with their horses, elephants, and chariots, fled in every direction—seeing your sons, swooning and stripped of their wits, as though seized by their last hour.

**6.73.44**  At that very moment, Droṇa, the foremost of weapon-bearers, fell upon Drupada and pierced him with three cruel arrows.

**6.73.45**  Sorely wounded then in the battle by Droṇa, O king, Drupada the lord of earth withdrew, remembering their ancient feud.

**6.73.46**  Having vanquished Drupada, the valiant Droṇa blew his conch; and hearing the blast of his conch, all the Somakas trembled.

**6.73.47**  Then the resplendent Droṇa, foremost of weapon-bearers, learned that your sons had been bewildered in the battle by the pramohana weapon.

**6.73.48**  Then Droṇa, eager for the king's welfare, hastened thither from the field; and there the valiant Bhāradvāja, that mighty archer, beheld Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Bhīma ranging through the great battle.

**6.73.49**  And that mighty car-warrior beheld your sons sunk in stupor; then, taking up the prajñā weapon, he dispelled the bewildering weapon.

**6.73.50**  Then your sons, those great car-warriors, their life-breath restored, advanced once more in the battle to fight against Bhīma and the son of Pṛṣata.

**6.73.51**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, summoning his own troops, said: "Let them go with all their might to follow the path of Bhīma and the son of Pṛṣata in the fight.

**6.73.52**  Let the twelve mailed heroes on their chariots, with the son of Subhadrā at their head, go and learn the tidings, for my heart is ill at ease."

**6.73.53**  Thus commanded, those brave and dauntless warriors, all of them proud of their manhood, answered "So be it," and, when the sun had reached the meridian, every one of them set forth.

**6.73.54**  The Kekayas, the sons of Draupadī, and the valiant Dhṛṣṭaketu, placing Abhimanyu at their head, set forth surrounded by a great host.

**6.73.55**  Those tamers of foes, forming in the battle the needle-mouthed array, broke through that chariot-division of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons in the fray.

**6.73.56**  As those mighty archers advanced with Abhimanyu at their head, your army—gripped by dread of Bhīmasena and confounded by Dhṛṣṭadyumna—

**6.73.57**  was unable to hold its ground, O lord of men; it stood like a woman swooning with drink and bewildered upon the road.

**6.73.58**  Those mighty archers, their banners wrought of gold, advanced and rushed onward, eager to come to the aid of Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Vṛkodara.

**6.73.59**  And the two, beholding those mighty archers led by Abhimanyu, were filled with joy as they smote your host.

**6.73.60**  But seeing his own teacher suddenly bearing down upon him, the heroic Pāñcāla prince, the son of Pṛṣata, no longer counted on the slaughter of your sons.

**6.73.61**  Then, having set Vṛkodara upon the chariot of the Kekaya prince, in great wrath he charged against Droṇa, that master of the arts of the bow.

**6.73.62**  As he swiftly bore down, the valiant Bhāradvāja, slayer of foes, in wrath sundered his bow with a broad-headed shaft.

**6.73.63**  And he sent forth hundreds of other arrows at the son of Pṛṣata, for the welfare of Duryodhana, mindful of the bread of his lord.

**6.73.64**  Then the son of Pṛṣata, slayer of hostile heroes, taking up another bow, pierced Droṇa with seventy gold-feathered, stone-whetted shafts.

**6.73.65**  Droṇa, tormentor of foes, again clove his bow, and swiftly slew his four horses with four excellent arrows.

**6.73.66**  That valiant one sent forth a dread shaft of Yama's destroying power, and with a broad-headed arrow dispatched his charioteer to death.

**6.73.67**  Swiftly leaping down from that car whose horses were slain, the mighty-armed great car-warrior mounted upon the great chariot of Abhimanyu.

**6.73.68**  Then the host, with its chariots, elephants, and horses, was shaken, while Bhīmasena looked on and the son of Pṛṣata looked on.

**6.73.69**  Seeing that army broken by Droṇa of boundless splendour, all those great car-warriors together were unable to stem its rout.

**6.73.70**  And that army, slaughtered by Droṇa with keen arrows, wandered hither and thither, like the ocean churned into turmoil.
