**6.73.71**  Beholding that army thus, your host was filled with joy; and seeing the teacher in his wrath scorching the enemy's array, the warriors cried out on every side, "Well done! Well done!" O Bhārata.

**6.74.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then King Duryodhana, recovered from his swoon, once more turned back the unwavering Bhīma with showers of arrows.

**6.74.2**  And your sons, those great car-warriors, banding together once more and closing upon Bhīma in battle, fought him with high resolve.

**6.74.3**  Bhīmasena too, regaining his own chariot in the battle and mounting it again, the mighty-armed one advanced to where your son was.

**6.74.4**  And taking up his swift, sturdy, gaily-painted bow, the bringer of death, he pierced your sons with arrows in the fight.

**6.74.5**  Then King Duryodhana struck the mighty Bhīmasena sorely in a vital part with a sharp-pointed iron shaft.

**6.74.6**  Sorely wounded by your bow-wielding son, that mighty archer, his eyes red with wrath, swiftly raised his bow

**6.74.7**  and planted three arrows in Duryodhana's two arms and his breast; yet the king, though so struck, stirred not, like the king of mountains.

**6.74.8**  Seeing the two, wroth in battle, smiting each other, all the younger brothers of Duryodhana, those heroes, ready to lay down their lives,

**6.74.9**  recalling their former counsel for the seizing of him of dread deeds, and resolving it firmly in their minds, set about to take him captive.

**6.74.10**  As they bore down upon him in the fray, the mighty Bhīmasena went forth to meet them, O great king, as an elephant meets rival elephants.

**6.74.11**  And in great wrath that splendid and far-famed one smote with an iron shaft Citrasena, your son, O great king.

**6.74.12**  And likewise he struck your other sons, O Bhārata, in the fight with manifold gold-feathered, swift-speeding arrows.

**6.74.13**  Then, having marshalled their own ranks on every side in the battle, those twelve great car-warriors led by Abhimanyu,

**6.74.14**  sent by the King of Righteousness and following in Bhīmasena's track, advanced, O great king, to meet your mighty sons.

**6.74.15**  Beholding those heroes stationed on their chariots, splendid as the sun and fire, all mighty archers, radiant and clothed in glory,

**6.74.16**  blazing in the great battle and gleaming in their golden mail, your mighty sons let Bhīma be in the fray.

**6.74.17**  But the son of Kuntī could not brook that they should escape with their lives; pursuing them, he once again harried all your sons.

**6.74.18**  Then, seeing Abhimanyu in the battle joined with Bhīmasena and the son of Pṛṣata, the great car-warriors in your army—

**6.74.19**  Duryodhana and the rest, their bows grasped firm, with their horses urged to great speed, advanced to where those chariots were.

**6.74.20**  Then in the afternoon, O king, a great battle broke forth between your mighty warriors and the foe, O Bhārata.

**6.74.21**  Abhimanyu, having slain Vikarṇa's swift horses, then covered him with twenty-five small arrows.

**6.74.22**  Abandoning that car whose horses were slain, Vikarṇa, the great car-warrior, mounted, O king, the shining chariot of Citrasena.

**6.74.23**  As the two brothers, those increasers of the Kuru line, stood upon one chariot, the son of Arjuna covered them with a net of arrows, O Bhārata.

**6.74.24**  Then Durjaya and Vikarṇa pierced the son of Kṛṣṇa with five iron shafts; yet the son of Kṛṣṇa stirred not, immovable as Meru.

**6.74.25**  And Duḥśāsana in the battle, O venerable one, fought against the five Kekayas, O lord of kings; and that was as it were a wondrous thing.

**6.74.26**  The sons of Draupadī, enraged in battle, held Duryodhana at bay; each one of them, O lord of the people, struck your son with three arrows.

**6.74.27**  And your unassailable son too, O king, smote the sons of Draupadī in battle, each one separately, with sharp shafts.

**6.74.28**  Pierced by them in turn and drenched with blood, he shone like a mountain streaked with mineral-laden torrents pouring down its slopes.

**6.74.29**  Bhīṣma too, mighty in the fray, O king, drove back the host of the Pāṇḍavas as a herdsman drives the cattle of his flock.

**6.74.30**  Then, O lord of the people, the twang of the Gāṇḍīva arose on the right of the army, as Pārtha struck down his foes.

**6.74.31**  On every side in that battle headless trunks rose up, O Bhārata, amid the armies of both the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.74.32**  With chariots for boats those tigers among men crossed that sea of armies, whose water was blood, whose whirlpools were chariots, whose islands were elephants, and whose waves were horses.

**6.74.33**  With hands lopped off, stripped of armor, robbed of their bodies, the foremost of men were seen lying fallen there by hundreds and by thousands.

**6.74.34**  With slain rutting elephants, awash in floods of blood, the earth shone, O best of the Bharatas, as though strewn with mountains.

**6.74.35**  There we beheld a wondrous thing, O Bhārata, among both your men and theirs: there was not a single man there who did not yearn to fight.

**6.74.36**  Thus the heroes fought, your warriors together with the Pāṇḍavas, seeking great renown and longing for victory in the battle.

**6.75.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, as the sun reddened toward setting, King Duryodhana, frenzied with the battle and eager to slay Bhīma, charged upon him.

**6.75.2**  Beholding that hero among men, his inveterate foe, coming on, Bhīmasena, sorely enraged, spoke these words:

**6.75.3**  This is the hour I have awaited through many a long year. Today I shall slay you, unless you forsake the field of battle.

**6.75.4**  Today, with your death, I shall wipe out the affliction of Kuntī, the whole of our exile in the forest, and the affliction of Draupadī.

**6.75.5**  You who, turning gambler, held the Pāṇḍavas in contempt—behold now, O son of Gāndhārī, the calamity come upon you for that sin.

**6.75.6**  Following of old the counsel of Karṇa and of the son of Subala, you scorned the Pāṇḍavas and out of willfulness wrought your pleasure upon them.

**6.75.7**  And in your folly you spurned the Dāśārha when he came pleading, and gladly you gave Ulūka your insolent message.

**6.75.8**  Today I shall slay you with your followers and your kinsmen, and so wipe clean the sin you wrought of old.

**6.75.9**  Having spoken thus, he drew his dreadful bow and brandished it again and again, and took up terrible shafts that gleamed like the great thunderbolt.

**6.75.10**  Swiftly, in fury, he loosed at once at Suyodhana twenty-six arrows, shaped like blazing tongues of fire, like thunderbolts, flying straight.

**6.75.11**  With two he split his bow, with two he pierced his charioteer, and with four he sent his swift horses to the abode of Yama.

**6.75.12**  And with two well-drawn shafts that crusher of foes severed in the battle the royal parasol from the king's splendid chariot.

**6.75.13**  And with three he clove asunder his blazing, lofty standard; and having cut it down, he roared aloud before the very eyes of your son.

**6.75.14**  And that splendid standard, adorned with many gems, fell suddenly from the chariot to the earth, like lightning from a storm-cloud.

**6.75.15**  All the kings saw the standard of the Kuru lord—the gleaming, jewel-wrought, splendid serpent emblem, bright as the sun—cut down.

**6.75.16**  Then Bhīma, that great car-warrior, smiling as it were, struck him in the fight with ten arrows, as one goads a great elephant with hooks.

**6.75.17**  Then Jayadratha, king of the Sindhus, foremost of car-warriors, guarded Duryodhana's rear as befits a man of worth.

**6.75.18**  And Kṛpa, foremost of car-warriors, mounted the wrathful Duryodhana of immeasurable might, that scion of the Kurus, upon his own chariot, O king.

**6.75.19**  Sorely wounded and stricken by Bhīmasena in the encounter, King Duryodhana then sank down upon the floor of the chariot.

**6.75.20**  Then Jayadratha, eager to slay Bhīma, surrounded him and, with many thousands of chariots, barred every quarter against him.

**6.75.21**  Then, O king, Dhṛṣṭaketu and the valiant Abhimanyu, the Kekayas and the sons of Draupadī, did battle against your sons.

**6.75.22**  Citrasena and Sucitra, Citrāśva and Citradarśana, Cārucitra and Sucāru, and likewise Nanda and Upananda—

**6.75.23**  these eight great archers, tender of years and full of glory, hemmed in the chariot of Abhimanyu on every side, O king.

**6.75.24**  Then high-souled Abhimanyu swiftly smote them, piercing each one with five smooth-jointed arrows, shafts like the thunderbolt and like death itself, sped from his wondrous weapon.

**6.75.25**  Brooking it not, they all rained sharp arrows upon the son of Subhadrā, that best of car-warriors, as rain-clouds pour upon Mount Meru.

**6.75.26**  Though hard pressed in the battle, Abhimanyu, master of weapons and frenzied for the fight, made your warriors tremble, O great king, as the Wielder of the Thunderbolt made the great Asuras tremble in the war of gods and demons.

**6.75.27**  Then, O Bhārata, that foremost of car-warriors sent against Vikarṇa fourteen terrible broad-headed shafts, like venomous serpents, cutting down his standard, his charioteer, and his horses, as though dancing in the battle.

**6.75.28**  And again the mighty son of Subhadrā sent against Vikarṇa other arrows, yellow, with unblunted points, whetted on stone.

**6.75.29**  Reaching Vikarṇa, those shafts fledged with heron and peacock feathers pierced his body and entered the earth, like blazing serpents.

**6.75.30**  Those arrows, gold-feathered and gold-pointed, drenched in Vikarṇa's blood, were seen upon the earth as though vomiting gore.

**6.75.31**  Seeing Vikarṇa thus pierced through, his other brothers rushed in the battle upon the car-warriors led by the son of Subhadrā.

**6.75.32**  Charging swiftly likewise, those warriors splendid as the sun, mounted on their chariots, furious and frenzied for the fight, pierced one another in the battle.

**6.75.33**  Durmukha, having pierced Śrutakarman with seven swift arrows, severed his standard with one and his charioteer with seven.

**6.75.34**  His horses, swift as the wind, caparisoned in nets of gold, he reached and slew with six shafts, and struck down his charioteer.

**6.75.35**  Standing in his car with its horses slain, the great car-warrior Śrutakarman, sorely enraged, hurled a lance like a great blazing meteor.

**6.75.36**  That keen, flaming lance, having cleft through the broad armor of the glorious Durmukha, tore on and entered the earth.

**6.75.37**  Seeing him there bereft of his car, the mighty Sutasoma, before the eyes of all the armies, took him up onto his own chariot.

**6.75.38**  The hero Śrutakīrti likewise advanced in the battle, O king, against your glorious son Jayatsena, eager to slay him.

**6.75.39**  As high-souled Śrutakīrti drew his bow, your son Jayatsena, O king, cut it asunder in the battle with a keen razor-headed shaft, smiling as it were, O Bhārata.

**6.75.40**  Seeing his brother with bow cut through, the fiery Śatānīka came to his aid, roaring again and again like a lion.

**6.75.41**  And Śatānīka, twanging his bow hard in the battle, swiftly pierced Jayatsena with ten stone-whetted shafts.

**6.75.42**  Then with another keen shaft that cleaves through every armor, Śatānīka pierced Jayatsena deep in the breast.

**6.75.43**  While this was passing, Duṣkarṇa, beside his brother and mad with rage, severed in the battle the bow of Nakula's son.

**6.75.44**  Then, taking up another bow, excellent and equal to its burden, the mighty Śatānīka laid hold of sharpened arrows.

**6.75.45**  And crying "Stand! Stand!" he challenged Duṣkarṇa before his brother and loosed sharp arrows like blazing serpents.

**6.75.46**  Then, O sir, with one shaft he cut his bow and with two his charioteer, swiftly in the battle, and pierced him himself with seven.

**6.75.47**  And that stainless warrior swiftly slew his dappled horses, swift as thought, all of them, with twelve sharp arrows.

**6.75.48**  Then with another broad-headed shaft, well-loosed and unerring, the enraged warrior pierced Duṣkarṇa deep in the breast in the battle.

**6.75.49**  Seeing Duṣkarṇa slain, O king, five great car-warriors, seeking to kill Śatānīka, surrounded him on every side.

**6.75.50**  As the glorious Śatānīka was being covered with showers of arrows, the five Kekaya brothers, in fury, rushed to his aid.

**6.75.51**  Seeing them charging on, your sons, great car-warriors, advanced against them, O great king, like elephants against mighty elephants.

**6.75.52**  Durmukha and Durjaya, the youth Durmarṣaṇa, Śatruṃjaya and Śatrusaha—all those glorious brothers, in fury, advanced together against the Kekayas, O great king.

**6.75.53**  With chariots vast as cities, yoked with steeds swift as thought, adorned with banners of varied and brilliant hues,

**6.75.54**  those heroes, bearing splendid bows, with gleaming armor and standards, entered the enemy host as lions pass from forest to forest.

**6.75.55**  Then between them, as they smote one another, there raged a tumultuous and most terrible battle, with chariots and elephants locked together—a battle of men who had wronged each other, O king, that swelled the realm of Yama.

**6.75.56**  After the sun had set a brief while, they waged a most fearful fight; car-warriors and horsemen were strewn about by the thousands.

**6.75.57**  Then the son of Śaṃtanu, Bhīṣma, in fury, with smooth-jointed arrows destroyed the host of those high-souled ones, and with his shafts sent the armies of the Pāñcālas to the abode of Yama.

**6.75.58**  Having thus broken the host of the Pāṇḍavas, that great archer sounded the recall of the armies and withdrew to his own camp, O king.

**6.75.59**  And the King of Righteousness too, beholding Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Vṛkodara, kissed them both upon the head and, well pleased, went to his camp.

**6.76.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, O great king, the heroes, who had wronged one another, drenched in blood, went each to their own camps.

**6.76.2**  And having rested as was meet and paid one another due honor, they appeared again in their armor, longing to do battle once more.

**6.76.3**  Then your son, O king, overwhelmed with care, his limbs smeared with flowing blood, questioned his grandsire thus:

**6.76.4**  "The fierce and terrible armies of the Pāṇḍavas, well arrayed and thick with banners—their swift, heroic floods of chariots have torn through, slain, and crushed our hosts.

**6.76.5**  Bewildering all in the fight, those glorious warriors penetrated even that crocodile array, firm as the thunderbolt, and by Bhīma I have been struck down with dreadful arrows that shone like the rod of Death.

**6.76.6**  Looking up at him in his wrath, I swooned with terror, O king, and found no peace today. I desire, O you of true vows, by your grace to win victory and to slay the sons of Pāṇḍu."

**6.76.7**  Thus addressed, the high-souled son of Gaṅgā, foremost of those who bear arms, perceiving that Duryodhana was stirred to wrath, smiled, and the wise one, untroubled in mind, answered him:

**6.76.8**  "Plunging into their host with utmost effort, with my whole soul, O prince, I desire to grant you victory and well-being, nor do I shield myself for your sake.

**6.76.9**  But these many fierce car-warriors, glorious, most valiant, masters of weapons, who are the Pāṇḍavas' allies in battle—untiring, they spew forth the venom of their wrath.

**6.76.10**  They cannot be conquered here in haste, swollen with valor and made your foes by you. Yet I shall fight against them, O king, with my whole soul, casting away my very life, O hero."

**6.76.11**  In battle, for your sake, O mighty one, my life is no longer the thing I will most guard this day. For your sake I would burn up all the worlds, gods and demons alike — how much more, then, your enemies here.

**6.76.12**  Therefore I will do battle with the Pāṇḍavas, O king, and I will do all that is dear to you." Hearing this very thing, Duryodhana grew supremely confident and glad of heart.

**6.76.13**  Then, full of joy, he commanded all the armies and all the kings: "March forth!" At his order they swiftly issued out — myriads of chariots, horses, foot-soldiers, and elephants.

**6.76.14**  Filled with high spirit, O king, vast, bristling with weapons of every kind, arrayed with elephants and horses and foot-soldiers, your forces shone resplendent upon the field of battle, O king.

**6.76.15**  Drawn up in well-marshalled bands, the herds of tusked elephants blazed forth on every side; and your troops, O lord of men, were commanded by warriors skilled in weapons and missiles.

**6.76.16**  By the chariots and the massed foot-soldiers, elephants, and horses, advancing into battle in proper order as they were urged on, dust of the hue of the newly risen sun was raised up, and it shone as it veiled the very rays of the sun.

**6.76.17**  The banners set upon chariots and elephants, stirred by the wind and whirling on every side, shone in that battle, O king — of many colors, like flashes of lightning ranged with clouds in the sky.

**6.76.18**  As the kings twanged their bows, there arose a tumultuous and terrible din, like that of the ocean churned by the hosts of gods and great demons in the first age of the world.

**6.76.19**  Roaring fiercely, of many forms and colors, the army of your sons surged up in this manner — destroyer of the enemy's host, resembling the massed clouds at the ending of an age.

**6.77.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then to your son, who had sunk again into brooding thought, the son of Gaṅgā, foremost of the Bharatas, spoke words meant to gladden his heart.

**6.77.2**  "I, and Droṇa, and Śalya, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas, and Aśvatthāman, and Vikarṇa, and Somadatta, and the lord of the Sindhu,

**6.77.3**  Vinda and Anuvinda of Avantī, Bāhlika together with the Bāhlikas, the mighty king of the Trigartas, and the king of Magadha hard to conquer,

**6.77.4**  and Bṛhadbala of Kosala, Citrasena, Viviṃśati, and chariot-warriors by the many thousands, splendid under their great banners —

**6.77.5**  and horses of the finest breeds, O king, well mounted by their riders, and lordly elephants frenzied with rut, their cheeks and faces streaming with ichor,

**6.77.6**  and valiant foot-soldiers too, bearing weapons of every sort, sprung from many lands, are ready to fight for your sake.

**6.77.7**  These and many others, who for your sake have given up their lives, are able even to conquer the gods in battle — such is my judgment.

**6.77.8**  Yet I must always speak to you, O king, what is for your good: the Pāṇḍavas cannot be conquered even by the gods with Vāsava at their head, for they have Vāsudeva as their ally and possess valor equal to great Indra's.

**6.77.9**  Nevertheless, O lord of kings, in every way I shall carry out your word: either I will conquer the Pāṇḍavas in battle, or the Pāṇḍavas will conquer me."

**6.77.10**  Having spoken thus, he gave him an auspicious herb endowed with potency, one that draws out embedded darts; and at once his wounds were freed of their shafts.

**6.77.11**  Then, when the dawn broke clear, the valiant Bhīṣma, skilled in battle-arrays, himself marshalled a formation with his own army.

**6.77.12**  It was the Circle array, O best of men, thick with weapons of every kind, filled with chief warriors and with elephants and foot-soldiers.

**6.77.13**  It was encircled on every side by chariots in their many thousands and by great troops of horsemen bearing swords and lances.

**6.77.14**  To each elephant were assigned seven chariots, to each chariot seven horsemen; behind each horseman ten archers, and to each archer seven shield-bearers.

**6.77.15**  Arrayed in this manner, O great king, with its mighty chariot-warriors, your army stood ready for the great battle, guarded in the fight by Bhīṣma.

**6.77.16**  Ten thousand horses, and likewise as many elephants, and ten thousand chariots besides — and your sons, clad in mail, the valiant ones led by Citrasena, guarded their grandsire on every side.

**6.77.17**  Guarded by those heroes, and themselves shielding him in turn, the mighty kings, fully armed, were seen ranged about him.

**6.77.18**  Duryodhana, clad in armor and mounted upon his chariot in the battle, blazed forth, graced with splendor, like Śakra in the third heaven.

**6.77.19**  Then there arose a mighty clamor from your sons, O Bhārata, and the tumultuous rumble of chariots and the blare of musical instruments.

**6.77.20**  Marshalled by Bhīṣma for the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, facing westward in the fight, the Circle — a vast and mighty array, impenetrable to the slayers of foes — shone on every side, O king, hard for the enemy to approach in battle.

**6.77.21**  But beholding the Circle, that most terrible array, King Yudhiṣṭhira himself then formed the Thunderbolt array in answer.

**6.77.22**  When the troops were thus drawn up, each stationed in his appointed place, the chariot-warriors and the horsemen raised a lion-roar.

**6.77.23**  Eager to break the array, longing for battle, the heroes sallied forth against one another with their troops, ready to strike.

**6.77.24**  The son of Bharadvāja advanced upon the Matsya king, and the son of Droṇa upon Śikhaṇḍin; King Duryodhana himself rushed against the grandson of Pṛṣata.

**6.77.25**  Nakula and Sahadeva, O king, went against the lord of the Madras; Vinda and Anuvinda of Avantī fell upon Irāvat.

**6.77.26**  All the kings together did battle with Dhanaṃjaya in the fight, while Bhīmasena, intent in the fray, held back the son of Hṛdika.

**6.77.27**  Citrasena and Vikarṇa, and Durmarṣaṇa too, O lord — the son of Arjuna, O king, fought with these sons of yours in the battle.

**6.77.28**  The son of Hiḍimbā, foremost of the Rākṣasas, rushed with fury upon the great archer of Prāgjyotiṣa, as one frenzied elephant charges another in rut.

**6.77.29**  Then, O king, the Rākṣasa Alambusa, full of wrath, charged in battle against Sātyaki, hard to overcome in war, together with his troops.

**6.77.30**  Bhūriśravas, intent in the fight, did battle with Dhṛṣṭaketu, while Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Dharma engaged King Śrutāyus.

**6.77.31**  Cekitāna in the battle fought against Kṛpa; the rest, intent upon it, advanced against Bhīma, that mighty chariot-warrior.

**6.77.32**  Then kings by the thousands surrounded Dhanaṃjaya, their hands grasping spears, lances, iron arrows, maces, and bludgeons.

**6.77.33**  Then Arjuna, exceedingly wrathful, spoke thus to the descendant of Vṛṣṇi: "Behold, O Mādhava, the armies of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son drawn up for battle by the great-souled son of Gaṅgā, who knows the art of arrays.

**6.77.34**  Behold the heroes eager for battle, clad in mail, O Mādhava; behold the king of the Trigartas together with his brothers, O Keśava.

**6.77.35**  This day I shall lay them low before your very eyes, O Janārdana — these who, O best of the Yadus, are eager to fight with me upon the field of battle."

**6.77.36**  Having spoken thus, the son of Kuntī, stroking the string of his bow, rained down showers of arrows upon the throngs of kings.

**6.77.37**  Those great archers in their turn filled him with showers of arrows, as the rain-clouds fill a pool with their downpour in the season of the rains.

**6.77.38**  A great cry of woe arose within your army, O lord of peoples, when they saw the two Kṛṣṇas heavily overspread with arrows in that great battle.

**6.77.39**  The gods, the divine seers, the Gandharvas, and the great serpents were struck with the utmost wonder to behold the two Kṛṣṇas brought to such a pass.

**6.77.40**  Then, in his wrath, O king, Arjuna sent forth the weapon of Indra; and there we beheld the marvellous prowess of the Victor.

**6.77.41**  For with floods of arrows he warded off the rain of weapons loosed by his foes; and yet there was not one there, O lord of peoples, who was left unpierced.

**6.77.42**  Among those thousands of kings, and their horses and elephants likewise, the son of Pṛthā pierced one with two shafts and another with three, O honored one.

**6.77.43**  Being struck down by the son of Pṛthā, they fled to Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu; and for those sinking in the fathomless deep, Bhīṣma then became their savior.

**6.77.44**  As they came rushing back there in rout, your broken army was thrown into turmoil, O great king, like the mighty ocean lashed by the winds.

**6.78.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the battle had thus broken out, and Suśarman had withdrawn, and the heroes had been routed by the great-souled Pāṇḍava,

**6.78.2**  while your army, the like of the ocean, was being swiftly thrown into tumult, and the son of Gaṅgā had hastened forth to meet the Victor,

**6.78.3**  King Duryodhana, beholding the prowess of the son of Pṛthā in the battle, came up in haste and spoke to all those kings,

**6.78.4**  and at their head, in the midst of all the army, to the mighty hero Suśarman especially, with words that greatly kindled their spirit:

**6.78.5**  "Here is Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, foremost of the Kurus, eager with his whole soul to fight Dhanaṃjaya, having cast away his very life.

**6.78.6**  As that Bhārata advances upon the enemy's host with the whole army, do you all, intent in the battle, guard the grandsire."

**6.78.7**  "So be it," they said, and with that those troops of the kings, O great king, gathered together from every side about the grandsire.

**6.78.8**  Then Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, advancing suddenly, fell upon the mighty Bhārata Arjuna as he came on in the battle.

**6.78.9**  Borne upon his great chariot — yoked with pure-white horses, bearing the standard of the dread ape, thundering like a cloud — he shone exceedingly.

**6.78.10**  As Dhanaṃjaya advanced in the battle, a tumultuous outcry arose from all the troops out of fear at the sight of the Diademed One.

**6.78.11**  And seeing Kṛṣṇa, the reins in his hands, like a second sun risen to its noonday height upon the field, they were not able to gaze upon him.

**6.78.12**  Even so the Pāṇḍavas were not able to look upon Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, with his white horses and his white bow, like the white planet risen on high.

**6.78.13**  He was surrounded on every side by the high-souled Trigartas, by his brothers, by your sons, and by other mighty chariot-warriors.

**6.78.14**  The son of Bharadvāja in the battle pierced the Matsya king with a feathered shaft, and with arrows cut down his banner and his bow, each with a single one, on the field.

**6.78.15**  Casting aside that severed bow, Virāṭa the commander of the host swiftly took up another, firm and able to bear the strain, and arrows shaped like venomous snakes, blazing like serpents.

**6.78.16**  He pierced Droṇa with three shafts and his horses with four, struck his banner with one and his charioteer with five, and his bow with a single arrow; thereat the bull among the twice-born was enraged.

**6.78.17**  Droṇa slew his horses with eight arrows with smooth-bending joints, O best of the Bharatas, and his charioteer with one feathered shaft.

**6.78.18**  His horses slain and his charioteer dead, that best of chariot-warriors leapt down from his car and swiftly mounted the chariot of Śaṅkha.

**6.78.19**  Then those two, father and son, standing upon the one chariot, held off the son of Bharadvāja by main force with a great shower of arrows.

**6.78.20**  Then the son of Bharadvāja, in his wrath, swiftly hurled in the battle an arrow like a venomous serpent against Śaṅkha, O lord of men.

**6.78.21**  Cleaving his heart and drinking his blood in the fray, that arrow passed on into the earth, its surface drenched and reddened with gore.

**6.78.22**  Struck by the arrow of the son of Bharadvāja, he fell swiftly from the chariot, letting go his bow and arrows, close beside his own father.

**6.78.23**  Seeing his own son slain, Virāṭa fled in terror, abandoning in the battle Droṇa, who was like Death with gaping jaws.

**6.78.24**  Then the son of Bharadvāja swiftly rent asunder the great host of the Pāṇḍavas in the battle, by hundreds and by thousands.

**6.78.25**  Śikhaṇḍin too, O great king, closing with the son of Droṇa in the encounter, struck him between the brows with three swift iron arrows.

**6.78.26**  With those three set upon his forehead, that tiger among men shone like Meru with three golden peaks reared aloft.

**6.78.27**  Then Aśvatthāman, enraged, in half the twinkling of an eye, O king, took aim with many arrows at Śikhaṇḍin's charioteer, his banner, his horses, and his weapon, and brought them all down in the fight.

**6.78.28**  Leaping down from his car whose horses were slain, that foremost of chariot-warriors, Śikhaṇḍī, tormentor of foes, took up a keen-edged sword and a bright shield, and in his wrath ranged over the field like a hawk.

**6.78.29**  As he moved through the battle, sword in hand, O great king, the son of Droṇa could descry no opening in him; and that seemed a marvel indeed.

**6.78.30**  Then, O bull of the Bharatas, the son of Droṇa, kindled to utmost fury, loosed many thousands of arrows in that fray.

**6.78.31**  But that most terrible shower of arrows, descending upon him in battle, the foremost of the mighty cut apart with his sharp-edged sword.

**6.78.32**  Then the son of Droṇa shattered his bright and lovely shield, blazoned with a hundred moons, and broke his sword in the encounter; and with his keen shafts, O king, he pierced him again and again.

**6.78.33**  Thereupon Śikhaṇḍī, brandishing the sword that had been broken by those arrows, swiftly hurled it like a blazing serpent.

**6.78.34**  As it flew at him suddenly, bright as the fire of doom, the son of Droṇa cut it in the air, displaying his deftness of hand, and pierced Śikhaṇḍī with many shafts of iron.

**6.78.35**  Then Śikhaṇḍī, O king, sorely smitten by those keen shafts, swiftly mounted the chariot of the high-souled Mādhava.

**6.78.36**  Then Sātyaki, in wrath, that mighty one, pierced with dreadful arrows in the fray the cruel and powerful Rākṣasa Alambusa.

**6.78.37**  Then that lord of the Rākṣasas, O Bhārata, severed his bow with a crescent-headed shaft, pierced him with arrows in the battle, and, weaving a Rākṣasa illusion, overwhelmed him with showers of darts.

**6.78.38**  Therein we beheld a marvel of valor in the grandson of Śini: even while struck down by keen shafts in the battle, he was not dismayed.

**6.78.39**  Then the scion of the Vṛṣṇis fitted to his bow the weapon of Indra, O Bhārata, which the illustrious Mādhava had obtained from Vijaya.

**6.78.40**  That weapon reduced the Rākṣasa illusion to ashes; and then he covered Alambusa on every side with dreadful shafts, as a rain-cloud in the wet season drenches a mountain with its streams of water.

**6.78.41**  Thus afflicted by the high-souled Mādhava, the demon fled in terror, abandoning Sātyaki in the fight.

**6.78.42**  Having vanquished that lord of the Rākṣasas, whom even Maghavat could not conquer in battle, the grandson of Śini roared aloud, while your warriors looked on.

**6.78.43**  And Sātyaki, of true prowess, slew your men with many keen arrows; afflicted with terror, they fled.

**6.78.44**  At that very moment, O great king, the mighty son of Drupada, Dhṛṣṭadyumna, covered your son, that lord of men, with straight-jointed arrows in the battle.

**6.78.45**  Though shrouded by the shafts of Dhṛṣṭadyumna, O Bhārata, your son, that lord of men, O king of kings, was not shaken.

**6.78.46**  Swiftly he pierced Dhṛṣṭadyumna in battle with sixty and then with thirty arrows; and that seemed a marvel.

**6.78.47**  Then his commander, that mighty car-warrior, in wrath severed his bow, O sire, and swiftly slew his four horses, and quickly pierced him with seven keen-edged shafts.

**6.78.48**  The mighty-armed one, his horses slain, leapt down from his chariot, and on foot, raising his sword, rushed at the son of Pṛṣata.

**6.78.49**  Then Śakuni, of great might, eager for the king's safety, came up and lifted that lord of all the world onto his own car.

**6.78.50**  Then the son of Pṛṣata, slayer of hostile heroes, having defeated the king, fell upon your host as the wielder of the thunderbolt fell upon the Asuras.

**6.78.51**  In the battle Kṛtavarman assailed the great car-warrior Bhīma with arrows, and shrouded him as a vast cloud shrouds the sun.

**6.78.52**  Then, smiling in the fray, Bhīmasena, scorcher of foes, in great wrath loosed his arrows at Kṛtavarman.

**6.78.53**  Though tormented by them, that surpassing car-warrior of the Sātvatas, skilled in arms, did not falter, O great king, but struck Bhīma in turn with keen shafts.

**6.78.54**  Then Bhīmasena of great might slew his four horses, brought down his charioteer, and felled his richly adorned banner.

**6.78.55**  And that slayer of hostile heroes covered him with arrows of many kinds, so that, his whole body bristling, he looked like a porcupine.

**6.78.56**  Then, leaving his car whose steeds were slain, he swiftly went over to the chariot of Vṛṣaka, your brother-in-law, O great king, while your son looked on.

**6.78.57**  And Bhīmasena too, exceedingly wrathful, charged upon your host, and in his fury smote it down like Death himself, staff in hand.

**6.79.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Many and various single combats between the Pāṇḍavas and my own men have I heard of from you, Saṃjaya, as you tell your tale.

**6.79.2**  Yet you tell me of not one of my own as elated, O Saṃjaya; ever you describe the sons of Pāṇḍu as joyous and unbroken.

**6.79.3**  My own you describe as worsted, dispirited, and bereft of vigor in the battle, O sūta; this is surely the work of fate, beyond doubt.

**6.79.4**  Saṃjaya said: According to their strength and according to their zeal your men strive in the fight, showing their manhood to the utmost of their power, O bull among men.

**6.79.5**  As the sweet water of Gaṅgā, the river of the gods, by long contact with the nature of the great ocean comes to take on saltiness,

**6.79.6**  even so, O king, does the prowess of your high-souled warriors, on meeting the heroic sons of Pāṇḍu, come to nothing in the battle.

**6.79.7**  They labor to the limit of their power and perform deeds hard to achieve; you should not, O best of the Kurus, lay blame upon the Kauravas.

**6.79.8**  It is through your fault, and your son's, O lord of peoples, that this dread and mighty destruction of the earth, that swells the realm of Yama, has come to pass.

**6.79.9**  It has sprung from your own fault, and you ought not to grieve, O king; for kings can guard neither all their interests nor even their lives.

**6.79.10**  Desiring the worlds reserved for the doers of good deeds, the lords of earth plunge into the host and fight, ever intent upon heaven.

**6.79.11**  In the forenoon, O great king, the slaughter of men began; with single mind hear of it from me, for it was like the war of gods and demons.

**6.79.12**  The two princes of Avanti, mighty bowmen, high-souled and of great strength, fierce in war, beholding Irāvat, advanced upon him; and there began among them a tumultuous battle that made the hair stand on end.

**6.79.13**  Irāvat, sorely enraged, swiftly pierced the two godlike brothers with keen, straight-jointed shafts; and those two, fighters of varied skill, pierced him in turn in the fray.

**6.79.14**  As they fought thus, O king, no difference could be seen between them, as they strove for the destruction of the foe, each seeking to repay stroke for stroke.

**6.79.15**  Then Irāvat, O king, with four arrows sent the four steeds of Anuvinda to the abode of Yama.

**6.79.16**  And with two exceedingly sharp broad-headed shafts he severed his bow and his banner in the battle, O sire; and that, O king, seemed a marvel.

**6.79.17**  Then Anuvinda, abandoning his own car, mounted the chariot of Vinda, and took up a new bow, excellent and able to bear a heavy strain.

**6.79.18**  Then the two heroes of Avanti, foremost of car-warriors, standing together upon one car, swiftly loosed their arrows at the high-souled Irāvat.

**6.79.19**  The arrows loosed by them, gold-adorned and of great speed, reaching the path of the sun, covered over the sky.

**6.79.20**  Then Irāvat in wrath rained a shower of arrows upon those two great car-warriors, the brothers, and struck down their charioteer as well.

**6.79.21**  And when the charioteer had fallen lifeless to the ground, the chariot, its horses now maddened and bolting, rushed off in every direction.

**6.79.22**  Having vanquished those two, O great king, the son of the daughter of the serpent-king, proclaiming his manhood, swiftly scattered your host.

**6.79.23**  That great host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, smitten in the battle, reeled in many ways, like a man who has drunk poison.

**6.79.24**  Then the Rākṣasa lord, son of Hiḍimbā, of great might, rushed upon Bhagadatta in his sun-colored car with its banner.

**6.79.25**  Then the king of Prāgjyotiṣa, mounted upon his lordly elephant, came on as did the wielder of the thunderbolt of old in the war over Tārakā.

**6.79.26**  There the gods with the Gandharvas and the seers, gathered together, could find no difference between the son of Hiḍimbā and Bhagadatta.

**6.79.27**  As Śakra, lord of the gods, struck terror into the Dānavas, even so in the battle, O king, did he strike terror into the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.79.28**  Routed by him on every side, those Pāṇḍava warriors, O Bhārata, could find no protector among their own ranks.

**6.79.29**  There we saw only the son of Bhīmasena standing upon his car, O Bhārata; the rest of the great car-warriors, despairing, fled away.

**6.79.30**  But when the troops of the Pāṇḍavas had rallied once more, O Bhārata, a terrible din arose among your troops in the battle.

**6.79.31**  Then Ghaṭotkaca, O king, in that great fight covered Bhagadatta with arrows, as a rain-cloud covers Mount Meru.

**6.79.32**  Striking down those shafts of the Rākṣasa loosed from the bow, the king swiftly smote the son of Bhīmasena in all his vital parts.

**6.79.33**  Though smitten by many straight-jointed shafts, that lord of the Rākṣasas was not shaken, like a mountain being cleft.

**6.79.34**  At him the king of Prāgjyotiṣa in wrath hurled fourteen lances in the battle; and the Rākṣasa cut them all asunder.

**6.79.35**  Having cut down those lances with his keen shafts, the mighty-armed one pierced Bhagadatta with seventy heron-feathered arrows.

**6.79.36**  Then the king of Prāgjyotiṣa, as if smiling, O Bhārata, struck down his four horses in the battle with his arrows.

**6.79.37**  Standing upon his car whose steeds were slain, that valiant lord of the Rākṣasas hurled a dart with force at the elephant of the Prāgjyotiṣa king.

**6.79.38**  As it flew suddenly at him, that gold-shafted dart of great speed, the king cut it in three, and it fell scattered upon the earth.

**6.79.39**  Seeing his dart struck down, the son of Hiḍimbā fled in fear, as Namuci, best of the Daityas, fled of old from the battle with Indra.

**6.79.40**  Having vanquished in battle that hero, valiant and of famed manhood, unconquerable in war, O king, even by Yama and by Varuṇa,

**6.79.41**  with his elephant he trampled down the Pāṇḍava host in the battle, as a wild elephant, O king, ranges crushing a bed of lotuses.

**6.79.42**  Then the lord of the Madras, joining battle with the twins, covered with floods of arrows those two sons of Pāṇḍu, his sister's sons.

**6.79.43**  But Sahadeva in the battle, beholding his maternal uncle come to grips with him, warded him off with a flood of arrows, as a cloud screens the sun.

**6.79.44**  Covered by that flood of arrows, he grew all the more joyful in his bearing; and between those two there arose, for their mother's sake, a love beyond compare.

**6.79.45**  Then, smiling in the fray, that great car-warrior, O king, sent the four horses of Nakula with four excellent arrows to the abode of Yama.

**6.79.46**  Then that great car-warrior, swiftly leaping down from his car whose steeds were slain, mounted the vehicle of his own illustrious brother.

**6.79.47**  Then the two heroes, standing together upon one car, drawing their stout bows in wrath, in a moment covered over the chariot of the king of the Madras.

**6.79.48**  Though covered by many straight-jointed shafts from his sister's sons, that tiger among men did not waver, like a mountain; and, as if smiling, he beat back that very shower of arrows.

**6.79.49**  Then Sahadeva, the valiant, in wrath, raising a shaft and fixing his gaze on the king of the Madras, sped it forth, O Bhārata.

**6.79.50**  That arrow, sped by him, swift as Garutmat, pierced through the king of the Madras and fell upon the surface of the earth.

**6.79.51**  Pierced deep and shaken, that great warrior sank down upon the floor of his chariot, O great king, and fell into a swoon.

**6.79.52**  Seeing him fallen senseless in the fight, hard pressed by the twin sons of Mādrī, his charioteer bore him away from the battle in the chariot.

**6.79.53**  When they saw the chariot of the lord of the Madras turned away in flight, all the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra grew downcast and thought, 'All is lost.'

**6.79.54**  Having vanquished their maternal uncle in battle, the two mighty sons of Mādrī, exultant, blew their conches and let forth a lion's roar.

**6.79.55**  Rejoicing, the two rushed upon your army, O lord of peoples, as the immortal gods Indra and Upendra would charge against the host of the Daityas.

**6.80.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then king Yudhiṣṭhira, when the sun had reached its zenith, fixed his gaze upon Śrutāyus and urged on his horses.

**6.80.2**  Then the king charged at Śrutāyus, that tamer of foes, striking him with nine sharp, smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.80.3**  That king, a great archer, having warded off in battle the shafts sent by the son of Dharma, dispatched seven arrows at the son of Kuntī in return.

**6.80.4**  Piercing his armor in the fray, they drank his blood, as if searching out the very life-breaths within the body of that great-souled one.

**6.80.5**  But the son of Pāṇḍu, sorely wounded by that great-souled king, struck the king in the heart in battle with a boar-eared arrow.

**6.80.6**  Then with another broad-headed shaft Pārtha, foremost of chariot-warriors, swiftly cast down to the earth the banner of that great-souled one from his chariot.

**6.80.7**  Seeing his banner fallen, that king Śrutāyus, O monarch, pierced the son of Pāṇḍu with seven sharp, barbless arrows.

**6.80.8**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Dharma, blazed up with wrath, like the fire that would consume all beings at the end of an age.

**6.80.9**  Beholding the son of Pāṇḍu enraged, the gods, gandharvas, and rākṣasas trembled, O great king, and the whole world grew distraught.

**6.80.10**  And this thought arose in the minds of all beings: 'This king, in his fury, will surely burn up the three worlds this day.'

**6.80.11**  The seers and the gods then performed a great rite of blessing, O king, to bring peace to the worlds when the son of Pāṇḍu was thus enraged.

**6.80.12**  And he, possessed by wrath, licking the corners of his mouth, bore a terrible form like the sun at the ending of an age.

**6.80.13**  Then all your troops, O lord of peoples, lost there all hope of life, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.80.14**  But that king of great renown, restraining his anger by his firmness, cut asunder the great bow of Śrutāyus at the grip.

**6.80.15**  Then, his bow severed, the king pierced him between the breasts with an iron shaft in battle, while all the army looked on.

**6.80.16**  Swiftly, O king, that mighty one slew with his arrows the horses of that great-souled foe, and quickly his charioteer as well.

**6.80.17**  Abandoning his chariot whose steeds were slain, and seeing the prowess of the king, Śrutāyus then fled swiftly from the battle.

**6.80.18**  When that great archer was vanquished in battle by the son of Dharma, the whole army of Duryodhana, O king, turned its back in flight.

**6.80.19**  Having done this, O great king, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Dharma, his mouth gaping like Death itself, smote your army.

**6.80.20**  Cekitāna of the Vṛṣṇi line covered Gautama, foremost of chariot-warriors, with his arrows, while all the armies looked on.

**6.80.21**  But Kṛpa, the son of Śaradvat, having warded off those arrows in the fight, pierced with his feathered shafts the watchful Cekitāna in battle, O king.

**6.80.22**  Then with another broad-headed arrow, O sire, the swift-handed one cut his bow and struck down his charioteer in battle, and he slew his horses, O king, and both his flank-drivers as well.

**6.80.23**  Leaping swiftly down from his chariot, the Sātvata seized his mace; and with that hero-slaying mace, foremost of mace-wielders, he killed the horses of Gautama and struck down his charioteer.

**6.80.24**  Gautama, standing upon the ground, hurled at him sixteen arrows; those shafts, piercing through the Sātvata, sank into the surface of the earth.

**6.80.25**  Then Cekitāna, enraged, hurled that mace once more, longing to slay Gautama, as Indra the sacker of cities longed to slay Vṛtra.

**6.80.26**  That great mace, bright and stone-cored, as it came flying down, Gautama warded off with many thousands of arrows.

**6.80.27**  Then Cekitāna, drawing his sword from its sheath, O descendant of Bharata, and assuming the utmost nimbleness, rushed upon Gautama.

**6.80.28**  Gautama too, casting aside his bow and seizing a well-whetted sword, charged with great speed upon Cekitāna, O king.

**6.80.29**  Both of them, endowed with strength and bearing excellent swords, hewed at each other with their keen-edged blades.

**6.80.30**  Then those two bulls among men, struck by the force of each other's swords, sank to the earth that bears all creatures, their limbs overcome by a swoon and dazed by exertion.

**6.80.31**  Then Karakarṣa, a friend, ran swiftly forward; seeing Cekitāna, that warrior fierce in battle, in such a plight, he lifted him onto his chariot while all the army looked on.

**6.80.32**  In the same way the valiant Śakuni, your brother-in-law, O lord of peoples, swiftly lifted Gautama, foremost of chariot-warriors, onto his chariot.

**6.80.33**  And the mighty Dhṛṣṭaketu, enraged, swiftly pierced the son of Somadatta in the breast with ninety arrows, O king.

**6.80.34**  The son of Somadatta, with those arrows lodged thickly in his chest, shone forth, O great king, like the sun at midday with its rays.

**6.80.35**  But Bhūriśravas in battle, with his excellent arrows, deprived the great chariot-warrior Dhṛṣṭaketu of his chariot, slaying his driver and his horses.

**6.80.36**  And seeing him bereft of his chariot, his horses and charioteer slain, he covered him with a mighty shower of arrows in the battle.

**6.80.37**  And the high-minded Dhṛṣṭaketu, abandoning that chariot, then mounted the car of Śatānīka, O sire.

**6.80.38**  Citrasena and Vikarṇa, O king, and Durmarṣaṇa likewise, chariot-warriors clad in golden mail, rushed upon the son of Subhadrā.

**6.80.39**  Then a terrible battle ensued between Abhimanyu and those three, O king, as of the body assailed by the three humors, wind, bile, and phlegm.

**6.80.40**  Though he stripped your sons of their chariots in that great fight, O king, that tiger among men did not slay them, remembering then the words of Bhīma.

**6.80.41**  Then, surrounded by many hundreds of kings mounted on elephants, horses, and chariots, Bhīṣma, hard for even the gods to approach in battle,

**6.80.42**  Seeing him advancing swiftly to rescue your sons, and marking Abhimanyu, that lone youthful great chariot-warrior, the son of Kuntī of the white steeds spoke thus to Vāsudeva:

**6.80.43**  'Urge on the horses, Hṛṣīkeśa, to where these chariots throng; for these many heroes, skilled in arms and frenzied for battle, must not destroy our army; drive there, O Mādhava.'

**6.80.44**  Thus addressed by the son of Kuntī of measureless might, that scion of the Vṛṣṇis sent the chariot yoked with white horses into the battle.

**6.80.45**  A great uproar arose among your troops, O sire, when Arjuna, enraged in battle, advanced against your men.

**6.80.46**  Coming upon those kings who guarded Bhīṣma, the son of Kuntī then spoke this word to Suśarman, O king:

**6.80.47**  'I know you to be a foremost warrior and my bitter enemy of old; behold now the dreadful fruit of your turn that has come due. This day I shall show you your forefathers long departed.'

**6.80.48**  Though he heard the harsh speech of Bībhatsu, the slayer of foes, as he spoke thus, Suśarman, that leader of chariot-bands, answered him nothing at all, neither fair nor foul.

**6.80.49**  Surrounded by many kings, he advanced upon the hero Arjuna from the front, from the rear, from the flanks, from every side.

**6.80.50**  Encircling Arjuna in battle together with your sons, O sinless one, he covered him with arrows as the clouds cover the sun.

**6.80.51**  Then there began a most mighty battle, with blood for its waters, between your men and the Pāṇḍavas in the fray, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.81.1**  Saṃjaya said: But Dhanaṃjaya, being smitten by their arrows, hissing like a strong serpent struck by a foot, with arrow after arrow forcibly cut asunder in battle the bows of those great chariot-warriors.

**6.81.2**  Having shattered in an instant the bows of those valiant kings in battle, the great-souled one pierced them all at once with his arrows, resolving then to leave none of them remaining.

**6.81.3**  Smeared with blood, they fell in the fight, struck down by the son of Indra, O king, their limbs riven, their heads fallen, their lives departed, their armored bodies cut through.

**6.81.4**  Overpowered by Pārtha's might, they fell to the earth and, of varied form, perished all together; seeing those princes slain in battle, the king of the Trigartas rushed forward in an instant.

**6.81.5**  Then the thirty-two others who guarded the rear of those chariots fell upon Pārtha; surrounding Pārtha and drawing their loud-twanging bows, they rained upon him a great flood of arrows, as the rain-clouds drench a mountain with their torrents of water.

**6.81.6**  Hard pressed by that downpour of arrow-floods, Dhanaṃjaya, his wrath kindled in the fight, with sixty oil-washed arrows in the combat slew those rear-guards as well.

**6.81.7**  Having vanquished those sixty chariots in battle, the glorious Dhanaṃjaya, glad at heart, the ever-victorious one, then hastened to slay Bhīṣma, having struck down the forces of the kings in the fray.

**6.81.8**  The king of the Trigartas, seeing those great chariot-warriors of his kinsmen slain, set those lords of men before him in battle and rushed swiftly to slay Pārtha.

**6.81.9**  Seeing Dhanaṃjaya, foremost of wielders of weapons, thus assailed, the warriors led by Śikhaṇḍin advanced, keen weapons in hand, eager to guard the chariot of Arjuna.

**6.81.10**  And Pārtha, the great bowman, seeing those heroes among men charging together with the king of the Trigartas, scattered them in battle with sharp shafts loosed from the Gāṇḍīva; and as he sought to reach Bhīṣma in the fight, he beheld Duryodhana and the king of the Sindhus and other kings.

**6.81.11**  Advancing against those who sought to bar his way, the hero of boundless valor fought them awhile with his strength; then, leaving aside the king and Jayadratha and the other lords, that mighty-armed, high-spirited one, of fearsome strength, went on, bow and arrows in hand, toward the son of Gaṅgā in the fray.

**6.81.12**  And Yudhiṣṭhira of terrible might, the great-souled one of endless fame, his anger kindled, came swiftly forward; leaving aside in battle the lord of the Madras, his own appointed share, he went together with the sons of Mādrī and Bhīmasena against Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, for combat.

**6.81.13**  Engaged by those foremost of great chariot-warriors in battle, the son of Gaṅgā, that wondrous fighter, the great-souled son of Śaṃtanu, was not shaken by all the assembled sons of Pāṇḍu come upon him.

**6.81.14**  Then king Jayadratha, true to his word, of most fierce might and high spirit, coming up in the fight, with his excellent bow forcibly cut asunder the bows of those great chariot-warriors.

**6.81.15**  Yudhiṣṭhira, Bhīmasena, the twins, and Pārtha likewise, the great-souled Duryodhana, his wrath roused in the fight and his fury like venom, struck with arrows bright as fire.

**6.81.16**  By Kṛpa, by Śalya, and by Śala, and likewise, O lord, by Citrasena in the battle, they were pierced with arrows by those whose wrath was greatly swollen, as the gods are by the assembled hosts of the Daityas.

**6.81.17**  Seeing Śikhaṇḍin, his weapon cut away by the son of Śaṃtanu, the king Ajātaśatru, the great-souled one, his anger kindled in battle, spoke wrathfully these words to Śikhaṇḍin:

**6.81.18**  'Having spoken thus before your father to me, that you would slay that Bhīṣma of the great vow with floods of arrows bright as the spotless sun, you made the vow, saying, "I speak the truth."

**6.81.19**  'Yet you do not make that vow bear fruit, in that you do not slay Devavrata in the fight; do not become false to your pledge, O hero among men; guard your duty, your lineage, and your fame.'

**6.81.20**  'Behold Bhīṣma in the fight, of terrible swiftness, scorching all the hosts of my army with nets of arrow-floods of exceeding fierce splendor, like Death wrought by Time in an instant.'

**6.81.21**  'Your bow cut away, heedless of the battle, vanquished by the son of Śaṃtanu, the kingly one, abandoning your kinsmen and your brothers, whither will you go? This is not worthy of you.'

**6.81.22**  'For seeing that Bhīṣma of boundless valor, and our army broken and fleeing thus, you are surely afraid, O son of Drupada; for the color of your face is downcast.'

**6.81.23**  'Even while Dhanaṃjaya joins this great battle, O hero among men, how is it, O renowned upon the earth, that you this day show fear of Bhīṣma, O hero?'

**6.81.24**  Hearing the harsh-syllabled speech of the King of Righteousness, bound up with rebuke, that great-souled one, taking it as a command of censure, hastened to the slaying of Bhīṣma, O king.

**6.81.25**  As Śikhaṇḍin rushed with great speed against Bhīṣma, charging upon him, Śalya checked him with a terrible and well-nigh invincible weapon.

**6.81.26**  And though he saw that weapon hurled at him, blazing with a power like the fire at the end of an age, the son of Drupada was not bewildered, O king, for his splendor was the equal of great Indra's.

**6.81.27**  That mighty bowman stood his ground there, beating back the weapon with his arrows; then Śikhaṇḍin took up another fierce weapon, the Vāruṇa, to counter it. The gods stationed in the sky and the kings below beheld that weapon shattered by his weapon.

**6.81.28**  But great-souled Bhīṣma, that hero, having in the battle cut off the splendidly wrought bow and the banner of Pāṇḍu's son, King Yudhiṣṭhira of Ajamīḍha's line, raised a war cry, O king.

**6.81.29**  Then, casting aside his bow with its arrows and seeing Yudhiṣṭhira overcome with fear, Bhīmasena seized his mace and on foot fell upon Jayadratha in the fray.

**6.81.30**  As Bhīmasena rushed upon him at great speed, mace in hand, Jayadratha pierced him on every side with five hundred dreadful, whetted arrows that were like the rod of Yama.

**6.81.31**  Heeding not those arrows, the impetuous Vṛkodara, his mind seized by wrath, slew in the battle all the Āraṭṭa-born steeds of the king of the Sindhus.

**6.81.32**  Then your son of matchless power, beholding this, came hurrying on his chariot to slay Bhīmasena, his weapon upraised, like the king of the gods himself.

**6.81.33**  And Bhīma too, roaring aloud all at once, advanced against him, brandishing his mace in menace; and seeing that mace upraised like the rod of Yama, your Kurus on every side...

**6.81.34**  ...all of them abandoned your fierce son, wishing to avoid the descent of that mace, and fell back amid the tumultuous, most dreadful and bewildering crush of battle, O Bhārata.

**6.81.35**  But Citrasena, his mind undismayed, watching the great mace come hurtling on, abandoned his chariot and on foot in the battle, seizing his bright sword and shield, leapt down like a lion from a mountain peak and made his way to another spot upon the ground.

**6.81.36**  And that mace, reaching the splendid chariot, smashed it utterly in the fray together with its horses and driver, and fell blazing to the earth, like a great meteor falling from the fallen sky to the ground.

**6.81.37**  Seeing that exceedingly great marvel, your men, O Bhārata, were filled with delight, and all together raised a shout on every side, and with their troops honored your son.

**6.82.1**  Saṃjaya said: Coming up to that high-spirited Citrasena, now bereft of his chariot, your son Vikarṇa took him up onto his own car.

**6.82.2**  While that turmoil thus raged, exceedingly confused, Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu swiftly rushed upon Yudhiṣṭhira.

**6.82.3**  Then the Sṛñjayas trembled, with their chariots, elephants, and horses, and they thought Yudhiṣṭhira had passed into the very jaws of death.

**6.82.4**  But Yudhiṣṭhira too, the Kaurava lord, joined by the twins, advanced against the great bowman, that tiger among men, Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu.

**6.82.5**  Then the son of Pāṇḍu, loosing thousands of arrows in the battle, covered Bhīṣma over as a cloud covers the sun.

**6.82.6**  Those nets of arrows, well aimed by him, O Bhārata, the son of Gaṅgā received by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**6.82.7**  And likewise the nets of arrows shot by Bhīṣma, O sire, appeared in the sky like flights of moving birds.

**6.82.8**  And in half the blink of an eye Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu made the son of Kuntī invisible in the battle, piece by piece, with his net of arrows.

**6.82.9**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira in his wrath sent against the great-souled Kaurava an iron shaft like a venomous serpent.

**6.82.10**  But before it could reach him, that mighty car-warrior Bhīṣma, O king, cut that shaft, sped from his bow, with a razor-edged arrow in the battle.

**6.82.11**  And having cut that iron shaft, fit to measure with death itself, Bhīṣma in the battle slew the gold-caparisoned horses of the Kaurava lord.

**6.82.12**  Then, abandoning the chariot whose horses were slain, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Dharma swiftly mounted the chariot of great-souled Nakula.

**6.82.13**  Then, much enraged, Bhīṣma the conqueror of hostile cities closed in upon the twins as well in the battle and covered them over with his arrows.

**6.82.14**  Seeing those two, O great king, sorely afflicted by Bhīṣma's arrows, Yudhiṣṭhira fell into the deepest anxiety, longing for Bhīṣma's death.

**6.82.15**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira urged on those obedient kings, his band of allies: "Let all of you together slay Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu!"

**6.82.16**  Then all those kings, hearing the word of Pārtha, surrounded the grandsire with a great host of chariots.

**6.82.17**  Surrounded on every side, your father Devavrata sported with his bow, O king, bringing down mighty car-warriors.

**6.82.18**  The sons of Pṛthā watched that Kaurava ranging through the battle as one might watch a lion's cub in the forest entering amid a herd of deer.

**6.82.19**  Seeing him menace the brave in battle and terrify them with his arrows, they trembled, O great king, as herds of deer tremble before a lion.

**6.82.20**  The warriors beheld the course of that lion of the Bharatas in the battle, like the course of fire aided by the wind, eager to burn the dry brushwood.

**6.82.21**  Bhīṣma struck down the heads of the chariot-warriors in the battle, as a skilled man brings down ripe fruits from the palm trees.

**6.82.22**  And with the heads falling to the surface of the earth, O great king, there arose a tumultuous din, like that of falling stones.

**6.82.23**  As that tumultuous and most dreadful battle raged on, there came a great confounding together of all the armies.

**6.82.24**  When those battle-arrays were broken, the warriors, calling out one to another, man against man, took their stand for combat.

**6.82.25**  But Śikhaṇḍin, coming up to the grandsire of the Bharatas, rushed at him with speed and cried, "Stand, stand!"

**6.82.26**  But Bhīṣma, disregarding that Śikhaṇḍin in the battle, mindful of Śikhaṇḍin's womanhood, advanced in wrath against the Sṛñjayas.

**6.82.27**  Then the Sṛñjayas, glad at seeing the mighty car-warrior Bhīṣma, raised lion-roars of many kinds, mingled with the blare of conches.

**6.82.28**  Then the battle raged on, chariots and elephants entangled together, as the sun, O lord, took its place declining toward the western quarter.

**6.82.29**  Then Dhṛṣṭadyumna of the Pāñcālas and the mighty car-warrior Sātyaki, sorely pressing your army with showers of spears and lances and with many weapons, O king, slew your men in the battle.

**6.82.30**  Though slaughtered in the battle, O bull among men, your men, setting their minds on the noble course of war, did not forsake the field; and those mighty car-warriors slew the host in the battle to the full of their valor.

**6.82.31**  There arose a great cry from your high-souled men, O king, as they were slain in the battle by the great-souled son of Pṛṣata.

**6.82.32**  Hearing that dreadful clamor of your men, the two mighty car-warriors Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti turned to face the son of Pṛṣata.

**6.82.33**  Those two mighty car-warriors, slaying his horses in haste, both covered the son of Pṛṣata over with a downpour of arrows.

**6.82.34**  Then the mighty Pāñcāla, leaping swiftly down from his chariot, quickly mounted the chariot of the most great-souled Sātyaki.

**6.82.35**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, surrounded by a great army, advanced in wrath against the two scorchers of foes, the princes of Avanti, in the battle.

**6.82.36**  Likewise your son too, O sire, with every effort, surrounding Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, took his stand to support them.

**6.82.37**  And Arjuna too, much enraged, O bull among warriors, fought the warriors in the battle as the wielder of the thunderbolt fights the Asuras.

**6.82.38**  And Droṇa, enraged in the battle and doing what was dear to your son, scattered all the Pāñcālas as fire scatters a heap of cotton.

**6.82.39**  And your sons, with Duryodhana at their head, O lord of the people, surrounding Bhīṣma in the battle, fought against the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.82.40**  Then King Duryodhana, as the sun was turning red, said to all your men, "Make haste!" O Bhārata.

**6.82.41**  While they thus fought and wrought deeds hard to do, the sun mounted the western mountain and shone no more.

**6.82.42**  In a moment, at the onset of night, there flowed a dreadful river surging with streams of blood, thronged with packs of jackals.

**6.82.43**  With she-jackals, inauspicious, howling out their fearful cries, the battlefield became dreadful, crowded with hosts of spirits.

**6.82.44**  And Rākṣasas and Piśācas and other eaters of flesh were seen on every side, by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**6.82.45**  Then Arjuna, having vanquished Suśarman and the other kings together with their followers in the midst of the host, returned toward his own camp.

**6.82.46**  And Yudhiṣṭhira too, the Kaurava king, then went with his brothers to his own camp by night, surrounded by his army.

**6.82.47**  And Bhīmasena too, O lord of kings, having conquered in the battle the car-warriors led by Duryodhana, then went toward his own camp.

**6.82.48**  And King Duryodhana too, having in the great battle surrounded Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, swiftly set out toward the camp.

**6.82.49**  Droṇa, the son of Droṇa, Kṛpa, Śalya, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas, surrounding the whole host, went toward the camp.

**6.82.50**  Likewise Sātyaki, O king, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata, surrounding their warriors in the battle, went toward the camp.

**6.82.51**  Thus, O great king, your men, those scorchers of foes, together with the Pāṇḍavas, withdrew at the hour of night.

**6.82.52**  Then, having gone to their own camps, the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus alike settled down, O great king, honoring one another.

**6.82.53**  Having made provision for their own safety and posted their pickets in due order, and having drawn out their barbed shafts and bathed in waters of many kinds, those heroes...

**6.82.54**  ...all having had the rites of blessing performed and being praised by the bards, those illustrious ones diverted themselves with the sound of song and instruments.

**6.82.55**  For a while all that was like a glimpse of heaven; for the mighty car-warriors held no talk of battle there at all.

**6.82.56**  And there, O king, while the armies slept and the men were spent with weariness, those two camps, abounding in elephants and horses, became a sight worth beholding.

**6.83.1**  Saṃjaya said: When that night had passed and the lords of men had slept in comfort, the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas went forth once more to battle.

**6.83.2**  Then there was a great din from both the armies as they marched out to battle, vast as the ocean.

**6.83.3**  Then King Duryodhana, Citrasena, Viviṃśati, and Bhīṣma the foremost of car-warriors, and the brahmin son of Bharadvāja...

**6.83.4**  ...united and well prepared, arrayed the great host of the Kauravas in battle-order and, mailed for war, set it against the Pāṇḍavas, O king.

**6.83.5**  Bhīṣma, your father, O lord of the people, formed a great battle-array, dreadful and like the ocean, with its mounts surging like waves.

**6.83.6**  At the head of all the troops marched Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, attended by the Mālavas, the Southerners, and the men of Avanti.

**6.83.7**  Next after him came the valiant son of Bharadvāja, with the Pulindas, the Pāradas, and the Kṣudrakas and Mālavas.

**6.83.8**  Next after Droṇa, ready for battle, came the valiant Bhagadatta, with the Māgadhas, the Kaliṅgas, and the Piśācas, O lord of the people.

**6.83.9**  After the lord of Prāgjyotiṣa came King Bṛhadbala of Kosala, attended by the Mekalas, the Traipuras, and the Cicchilas.

**6.83.10**  After Bṛhadbala came the brave Trigarta, lord of Prasthala, together with many Kāmbojas and Yavanas by the thousands.

**6.83.11**  And the impetuous and brave son of Droṇa, after the Trigarta, O Bhārata, marched forth, making the face of the earth resound with his lion-roar.

**6.83.12**  Then King Duryodhana, with his whole host, advanced just behind the son of Droṇa, ringed about by his own brothers.

**6.83.13**  After Duryodhana came Kṛpa, the son of Śaradvat; and thus this great battle-array set forth, vast as the ocean.

**6.83.14**  There, O lord, shone the banners and the white parasols, the bright armlets, and the precious, beautifully wrought bows.

**6.83.15**  Beholding that great array of your men, the mighty chariot-warrior Yudhiṣṭhira spoke at once to the son of Pṛṣata, the commander of his host:

**6.83.16**  "Behold, O great archer, the array they have fashioned, vast as the sea. Do you too, O son of Pṛṣata, form a counter-array without delay."

**6.83.17**  Then the brave son of Pṛṣata, O great king, formed a most fearsome array, the Śṛṅgāṭaka, which could shatter the enemy's formation.

**6.83.18**  At its horns stood Bhīmasena and the great chariot-warrior Sātyaki, with chariots in their many thousands and with horse and foot.

**6.83.19**  At its center stood that best of men, white-horsed, ape-bannered Arjuna; and in the midst were King Yudhiṣṭhira and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī.

**6.83.20**  And the other great archers, the lords of men with their forces, skilled in the science of battle-arrays, filled out that formation.

**6.83.21**  Behind them came Abhimanyu, and the great chariot-warrior Virāṭa, and the exultant sons of Draupadī, and the Rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca.

**6.83.22**  Having drawn up this great array, O Bhārata, the valiant Pāṇḍavas stood ready for battle, eager to fight and longing for victory.

**6.83.23**  The tumultuous beat of drums, mingled with the blare of conch-shells and the roars, the slapping of arms, and the war-cries, grew terrible on every side.

**6.83.24**  Then the warriors, closing upon one another in the fray, O king, glared at each other with unblinking eyes, kindled with fury.

**6.83.25**  First in their minds, O lord of men, those warriors set themselves against one another for battle, challenging each man his fellow.

**6.83.26**  Then there broke out a battle dreadful to look upon and full of terror, as your men and the foe struck down one another.

**6.83.27**  Sharp iron shafts fell in the fray, O Bhārata, terror-bringing with their gaping mouths, like serpents in swarms.

**6.83.28**  Bright spears flew forth, oil-washed and keenly whetted, blazing like lightning-flashes leaping from the clouds, O king.

**6.83.29**  And maces wrapped in bright cloth and adorned with gold were seen falling there, fair as mountain-peaks; and the swords gleamed, resembling the clear sky.

**6.83.30**  And shields of bull's hide, marked with a hundred moons, O Bhārata, shone splendidly in the battle, O king, as they fell on every side.

**6.83.31**  The two armies, contending with one another in the fray, O lord of men, shone like the hosts of the Daityas and the gods arisen for war; and they rushed upon each other on every side in the battle.

**6.83.32**  Chariots, swiftly driven by their warriors in that fierce encounter, locked yoke against yoke; and those bulls among kings fought on.

**6.83.33**  From the clashing of the battling tuskers, O best of the Bharatas, fire arose upon their tusks, with smoke, on every side.

**6.83.34**  Some of the elephant-warriors, struck through by lances on all sides, were seen falling like trees tumbling from a mountain-peak.

**6.83.35**  And foot-soldiers too were seen striking one another down, brave men of varied aspect, fighting with claw-blades and lances.

**6.83.36**  Closing upon one another, the soldiers of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas sent each other, with dreadful weapons of every kind, to the abode of Yama.

**6.83.37**  Then Bhīṣma, son of Śāntanu, making all resound with the rumble of his chariot, came upon the Pāṇḍavas in the fray, bewildering them with the twang of his bow.

**6.83.38**  And the chariots of the Pāṇḍavas too, roaring with a fearsome din, rushed forward girt for battle, with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head.

**6.83.39**  Then the battle broke out between your men and theirs, O Bhārata, with men, horses, chariots, and elephants entangled one with another.

**6.84.1**  Saṃjaya said: Bhīṣma, wrathful in the battle and scorching all around, the Pāṇḍavas could not bear to look upon, as upon the blazing sun.

**6.84.2**  Then, at the command of the son of Dharma, all the troops rushed upon the son of Gaṅgā, who was crushing them with his sharp arrows.

**6.84.3**  But that Bhīṣma, glorying in battle, struck down with his shafts the Somakas together with the Sṛñjayas and the great-archer Pāñcālas.

**6.84.4**  Cut down by Bhīṣma, the Pāñcālas together with the Somakas charged swiftly at Bhīṣma himself, casting aside the fear that death inspires.

**6.84.5**  Then the hero Bhīṣma, son of Śāntanu, severed in an instant, O king, the arms and heads of those chariot-warriors in the fight.

**6.84.6**  Your father Devavrata stripped the chariot-warriors of their cars; and the heads of the horsemen fell from their steeds.

**6.84.7**  And we saw elephants, emptied of their riders and lying like hills, O great king, stupefied by Bhīṣma's weapon.

**6.84.8**  There was not one man among the Pāṇḍavas there, O lord of peoples, save the foremost of chariot-warriors, the mighty Bhīmasena.

**6.84.9**  For he, closing with Bhīṣma, smote him in the fray; and then a dreadful crash arose at the meeting of Bhīṣma and Bhīma.

**6.84.10**  Terrible and fearsome it was to all the troops; and so the Pāṇḍavas, rejoicing, raised then a lion's roar.

**6.84.11**  Then King Duryodhana, ringed about by his brothers, guarded Bhīṣma in the battle while the slaughter of men went on.

**6.84.12**  But Bhīma, that best of chariot-warriors, having slain Bhīṣma's charioteer, while the horses of the car bolted and it careered on every side, that slayer of foes swiftly severed with a shaft the head of Sunābha.

**6.84.13**  By that keen razor-edged arrow he was slain and fell to the earth; and when that son of yours, the great chariot-warrior, was slain, O great king, his seven valiant brothers could not endure it in the fight.

**6.84.14**  Ādityaketu, Bahvāśin, Kuṇḍadhāra, Mahodara, Aparājita, Paṇḍitaka, Viśālākṣa, and Sudurjaya —

**6.84.15**  in gleaming harness, with their varied armor and standards, those crushers of foes, eager to fight, rushed upon the Pāṇḍava in the battle.

**6.84.16**  Mahodara pierced Bhīma in the fray with nine feathered shafts like thunderbolts, as the slayer of Vṛtra pierced Namuci.

**6.84.17**  Ādityaketu struck him with seventy, Bahvāśin with five, Kuṇḍadhāra with ninety, and Viśālākṣa with seven.

**6.84.18**  Aparājita, O great king, the ever-victorious great chariot-warrior, assailed the mighty Bhīmasena with many arrows.

**6.84.19**  And Paṇḍitaka in the fray struck at him with three arrows. But Bhīma could not endure this assault from his foes in the battle.

**6.84.20**  Gripping his bow with his left hand, that tormentor of foes severed in the fray, with a smooth-jointed arrow, the head

**6.84.21**  of your son Aparājita, with its handsome nose; and overcome by Bhīma in the fight, his head fell to the earth.

**6.84.22**  Then with another broad-headed shaft he dispatched the great chariot-warrior Kuṇḍadhāra to the world of the dead, before the eyes of all.

**6.84.23**  Then once more the warrior of measureless soul, fitting an arrow to the string, sent it in the battle against Paṇḍita, O Bhārata.

**6.84.24**  That shaft, having slain Paṇḍita, entered the surface of the earth, as a serpent, driven by fate, swiftly enters it after striking a man dead.

**6.84.25**  Cleaving off the head of Viśālākṣa with three arrows, the undaunted hero felled it to the ground, remembering an old grievance.

**6.84.26**  He pierced the great archer Mahodara through the breast with an iron shaft in the fray, O king; and slain, he fell to the earth.

**6.84.27**  And having shorn away the standard of Ādityaketu with a shaft in the fight, that slayer of foes severed his head with a most keen broad-headed arrow.

**6.84.28**  Then Bhīma, in great wrath, sent Bahvāśin with a smooth-jointed arrow to the abode of Yama.

**6.84.29**  Then the others, your remaining sons, fled away, O lord of peoples, deeming true that which he had spoken in the assembly-hall.

**6.84.30**  Then King Duryodhana, stricken by the calamity of his brothers, said to your warriors: "Let this Bhīma be slain in the battle!"

**6.84.31**  Thus those great-archer sons of yours, O lord of peoples, seeing their brothers slain, called to mind that vow of his.

**6.84.32**  What the deeply wise Vidura had spoken, salutary and unerring — that word of the seer with divine vision had now come to pass.

**6.84.33**  Possessed by greed and delusion, through love of your sons, O lord of men, you did not heed in time that great and truthful word.

**6.84.34**  Surely it is for the destruction of your sons that the mighty, strong-armed Pāṇḍava was born, even as he now slaughters the Kauravas.

**6.84.35**  Then King Duryodhana, O honored sir, approaching Bhīṣma, overcome by great grief and utterly worn, lamented thus:

**6.84.36**  "My valiant brothers have been slain by Bhīmasena in the battle, and the rest, though they strive their utmost, are being slain along with all my soldiers."

**6.84.37**  "And you, in your neutrality, ever disregard us. I have set my foot upon an evil road — behold this fate of mine!"

**6.84.38**  Hearing this cruel speech, your father Devavrata spoke this word to Duryodhana, whose eyes were filled with tears:

**6.84.39**  "This was told you before by me, and by Droṇa and by Vidura, and by the illustrious Gāndhārī — the truth, my child, which you did not understand."

**6.84.40**  "And an agreement was made by me with you before, O scourge of foes: that neither I nor the preceptor are ever to be relieved of duty in battle."

**6.84.41**  "For whichever of the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra Bhīma shall set eyes upon in the fray, him will he slay in the battle — this I tell you truly."

**6.84.42**  "Therefore, O king, grow firm, and fixing your resolve steadfastly upon the fight, do battle with the Pārthas, making heaven your final goal."

**6.84.43**  "The Pāṇḍavas cannot be conquered, not even by the gods and demons with Indra at their head. Therefore, fixing your mind firmly upon the battle, fight on, O Bhārata."

**6.85.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When they saw my many sons slain by one man, O Saṃjaya, what did Bhīṣma, Droṇa, and Kṛpa do in the battle?

**6.85.2**  Day after day my sons go to their destruction, O Saṃjaya. I think, O charioteer, that they are altogether and grievously smitten by fate.

**6.85.3**  Since all my sons are vanquished and do not conquer — and that too in the presence of Bhīṣma, of Droṇa, and of the great-souled Kṛpa,

**6.85.4**  and of the heroic son of Somadatta, and of both Bhagadatta and Aśvatthāman, my child — those most great-souled heroes —

**6.85.5**  and of the other heroes — that my sons, standing in their very midst, should be slain in the battle, what can this be but the working of destiny?

**6.85.6**  For the dull-witted Duryodhana would not understand what was foretold of old, though he was restrained by me, my child, and by Bhīṣma and Vidura,

**6.85.7**  and by Gāndhārī too — that witless one — ever out of desire for his good. He did not heed it of old, through delusion; and this is the fruit that has come to him.

**6.85.8**  For Bhīmasena, enraged, day after day in the battle leads my senseless sons to the abode of Yama.

**6.85.9**  Saṃjaya said: This is the coming-to-pass of that excellent word of Vidura, which you did not understand, O lord, when it was spoken for your good in that hour.

**6.85.10**  "Restrain your sons from the dice-game, and do not wrong the Pāṇḍavas"—so spoke your well-wishers, who sought your good, again and again.

**6.85.11**  That counsel, well spoken, which you would not heed—as a sick man will not take the medicine that would heal him—has now overtaken you, fulfilled.

**6.85.12**  Having failed to follow the wholesome words of Vidura, Droṇa, and Bhīṣma, and of the other well-wishers, the Kauravas now go to their ruin.

**6.85.13**  All this came to pass long ago, O lord of peoples; therefore hear from me, in its truth, how the battle unfolded.

**6.85.14**  At midday a most terrible combat arose, one that wrought the destruction of the world, O king; hear of it as I recount it.

**6.85.15**  Then all the armies, at the command of the son of Dharma, rushed forward in fury, eager to slay Bhīṣma himself.

**6.85.16**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin and Sātyaki, that great chariot-warrior, with their ranks marshalled, O great king, advanced against Bhīṣma alone.

**6.85.17**  Arjuna and the sons of Draupadī and Cekitāna, in the battle, fell upon all the kings whom Duryodhana had appointed.

**6.85.18**  The hero Abhimanyu, and the son of Hiḍimbā, that great chariot-warrior, and Bhīmasena, full of wrath—these rushed against the Kauravas.
