**6.85.19**  Divided into three, the Pāṇḍavas struck down the Kauravas in the fight; and likewise, O king, on the Kaurava side the foes were struck down in the battle.

**6.85.20**  But Droṇa, foremost of chariot-warriors, full of wrath, charged the Somakas together with the Sṛñjayas, meaning to send them to the realm of Yama.

**6.85.21**  There arose a great wailing among the high-souled Sṛñjayas, O king, as they were slain in the battle by the archer, the son of Bharadvāja.

**6.85.22**  Many warriors, smitten there by Droṇa in the fight, were seen writhing upon the ground like men tormented by disease.

**6.85.23**  Of those moaning and wailing and groaning in the battle, an unceasing din was heard, like that of men wasted by hunger.

**6.85.24**  In the same way the mighty Bhīmasena wrought a terrible slaughter among the Kauravas, raging like a second Death.

**6.85.25**  As the armies slew one another in that great combat, a dreadful river began to flow, streaming with floods of blood.

**6.85.26**  That battle, O great king, of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas grew vast and dreadful in form, swelling the kingdom of Yama.

**6.85.27**  Then Bhīma, wrathful in the fight and especially fierce, fell upon the host of elephants and dispatched them to their death.

**6.85.28**  There, O descendant of Bharata, the elephants struck by Bhīma's iron shafts fell, and sank down, and trumpeted, and wandered through the quarters.

**6.85.29**  The great elephants, their trunks lopped off and their feet severed, O venerable one, cried out in terror like curlews and lay down upon the earth.

**6.85.30**  Nakula and Sahadeva charged the host of cavalry; and those horses, decked with golden frontlets and trappings of gold, were seen being slain by the hundreds and the thousands.

**6.85.31**  With the falling horses, O king, the earth was strewn over; and with steeds tongueless, panting, neighing, and lifeless—horses of every form, O best of men—the ground was made resplendent.

**6.85.32**  With the horses slain by Arjuna in the encounter, O descendant of Bharata, the earth shone dreadful here and there, O lord of peoples.

**6.85.33**  With chariots shattered, banners cut down, and parasols of dazzling splendour, with necklaces and golden chains and armlets, and with heads still bearing their earrings—

**6.85.34**  With turbans flung down, and pennons on every side, with shining axle-poles, O king, and with yoke-straps and reins now lifeless—the earth, so covered, shone as in springtime strewn with blossoms.

**6.85.35**  Thus too did this destruction befall the Pāṇḍavas, O descendant of Bharata, when the son of Śaṃtanu, Bhīṣma, was wrathful, and Droṇa, that best of chariot-warriors.

**6.85.36**  And when Aśvatthāman and Kṛpa, and likewise Kṛtavarman, and the others too were wrathful, destruction came upon your men as well.

**6.86.1**  Saṃjaya said: While that fierce slaughter of the best of heroes was going on, O king, the illustrious Śakuni, son of Subala, rushed upon the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.86.2**  And likewise the Sātvata, the son of Hṛdika, slayer of hostile heroes, O king, charged in battle against the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.86.3**  Then with the foremost steeds of the Kāmbojas and those born of the rivers, the horses of the Āraṭṭas, those bred on the great land, and those of the Sindhu in every kind—

**6.86.4**  with the gleaming horses born in Vanāyu and those that dwell in the mountains, and others, the swift partridge-coloured ones, fleet as the wind—

**6.86.5**  with these chief horses, decked in gold, armoured and well caparisoned, swift as the wind, the mighty son of the Pāṇḍava, that scorcher of foes, his form glad with eagerness, advanced upon that army.

**6.86.6**  Now Arjuna's heir, the valiant one named Irāvān, was begotten by the wise son of Pṛthā upon the daughter of the king of the Nāgas.

**6.86.7**  She, childless, her husband slain by Suparṇa, piteous and forlorn of mind, had been given by the high-souled Airāvata.

**6.86.8**  And the son of Pṛthā took her to wife, for she yielded to her desire; thus was this son of Arjuna born in a field not his own.

**6.86.9**  He grew up in the world of the Nāgas, sheltered by his mother, but cast off by his evil-minded uncle out of hatred for the son of Pṛthā.

**6.86.10**  Handsome, endowed with valour, virtuous, and true in his prowess, he went swiftly to the world of Indra on hearing that Arjuna had gone there.

**6.86.11**  Approaching his high-souled father of true prowess, calm and with palms joined in reverence, he saluted him: "I am Irāvān; blessings on you—I am your son, O lord."

**6.86.12**  And he made known all that union with his mother had been; and the Pāṇḍava recalled the whole of it just as it had come to pass.

**6.86.13**  And embracing his son, who in qualities was the very likeness of himself, the son of Pṛthā was filled with joy in the abode of the king of the gods.

**6.86.14**  Then in the world of the gods, O king, the mighty-armed one was charged by Arjuna with affection concerning his own affairs, O descendant of Bharata: "In the time of battle you must lend us your aid, O lord."

**6.86.15**  "So be it," he said, and when the time of battle had come, he arrived, O king, surrounded by many horses of various hues and at-will swiftness.

**6.86.16**  Those horses, with golden frontlets, of many colours and swift as thought, sprang up of a sudden, O king, like wild-geese upon the great ocean.

**6.86.17**  Closing with your swift bands of horses, striking chest against chest and muzzle against muzzle upon one another, they fell suddenly to the ground, O king, smitten by the force of their charge.

**6.86.18**  And from those bands of horses falling thus upon one another, a fearsome sound was heard, as at the swooping of the great birds.

**6.86.19**  And likewise, O great king, the horsemen, meeting one another in the fray, wrought a dreadful mutual slaughter.

**6.86.20**  As that turmoil raged on, exceedingly furious and confused, the bands of horses on both sides were brought to ruin on every side.

**6.86.21**  The heroes, their arrows spent, their horses slain, worn out with toil, came to destruction, hewing one another down.

**6.86.22**  Then, when the cavalry was depleted and but little of it remained, O descendant of Bharata, the heroic sons of Subala came forth to the forefront of the fight.

**6.86.23**  Mounting the finest horses, whose touch was swift as the wind's rush and who in speed matched the wind, well-trained and in their prime—

**6.86.24**  Gaja, Gavākṣa, Vṛṣaka, Carmavān, Ārjava, and Śuka—these six, endowed with strength, sallied forth from the great host.

**6.86.25**  Though held back by Śakuni and by their own mighty warriors, these—armoured, skilled in war, terrible of aspect, and exceedingly strong—

**6.86.26**  breaking through that most unconquerable host, O mighty-armed one, joined with great force and seeking victory for the sake of heaven, those Gāndhāras, frenzied for battle, entered it then in their gladness.

**6.86.27**  Seeing them so entered, the valiant Irāvān then spoke in the battle to his warriors, who bore varied ornaments and weapons:

**6.86.28**  "Let such a course be ordained that these warriors of the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, with their followers and mounts, may all be slain in the battle."

**6.86.29**  "So be it," said all those warriors of Irāvān, and they smote that hostile host, hard for foes to conquer in battle.

**6.86.30**  Seeing that host struck down by the host in the battle, the sons of Subala, all unable to brook it, rushed upon Irāvān and surrounded him on every side in the fight.

**6.86.31**  Striking with sharp lances and urging one another on, those heroes ran about, working great confusion.

**6.86.32**  Then Irāvān, pierced by the sharp lances of those high-souled ones, was smeared with streaming blood, like an elephant goaded with the driver's hooks.

**6.86.33**  Sorely struck upon the breast and the back and both flanks—one against many—yet, O king, through his steadfastness he did not waver at all.

**6.86.34**  Then Irāvān, that conqueror of hostile cities, full of wrath, pierced them all with whetted arrows and bewildered them in the fight.

**6.86.35**  And that subduer of foes, plucking out all the lances from his own body, smote the sons of Subala in the battle with those very weapons.

**6.86.36**  Drawing his whetted sword and taking up his shield, on foot he came swiftly forward, eager to slay the sons of Subala in the fight.

**6.86.37**  Then all those sons of Subala, their spirits returned to them, once more filled with rage, rushed upon Irāvān.

**6.86.38**  And Irāvān too, with his sword displaying the deftness of his hand, advanced against all those sons of Subala, proud in his strength.

**6.86.39**  As he moved with such nimbleness, all those sons of Subala, ranging about swift-footed, could find no opening in him.

**6.86.40**  Then, seeing him standing upon the ground in the encounter, they all once more closed about him tightly and set about to seize him.

**6.86.41**  Then, of those who had drawn near, that tormentor of foes, with his sword, cut away their limbs with strokes both upward and downward.

**6.86.42**  And the weapons of them all, and their arms too, though decked with ornaments, fell; their bodies cut to pieces, they sank lifeless to the ground.

**6.86.43**  But Vṛṣaka, O great king, wounded in many places, escaped from that most terrible hewing-down of heroes.

**6.86.44**  Seeing them all fallen, Duryodhana then, alarmed and full of wrath, addressed a Rākṣasa of dreadful aspect—

**6.86.45**  the son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga, a great archer, a master of illusion and a subduer of foes, enemy to Bhīmasena ever since the slaying of Baka of old.

**6.86.46**  "Behold, O hero, how this mighty son of Phalguna, a master of illusion, has done me a dire and grievous harm, the destruction of my forces."

**6.86.47**  "And you, dear friend, move at will and are skilled in the weapons of illusion, and you bear enmity against the son of Pṛthā; therefore slay him in the battle."

**6.86.48**  "So be it," said the Rākṣasa of dreadful aspect, and with a lion's roar he set forth to where the young son of Arjuna was.

**6.86.49**  Attended by warriors well-mounted and skilled in war, who fought with gleaming lances, by valiant smiters, and surrounded by his own ranks, he came eager to slay the mighty Irāvān in the battle.

**6.86.50**  But Irāvān too, full of wrath, swift and valiant, that slayer of foes, held off the Rākṣasa who sought to kill him.

**6.86.51**  Beholding him rushing on, the Rākṣasa of exceeding might, in haste, then set about to employ his illusion.

**6.86.52**  By him were fashioned, all by illusion, horses just as many, mounted by dreadful Rākṣasas bearing pikes and spears in their hands.

**6.86.53**  Those two thousand smiters, meeting in fury, ere long sent one another to the world of the dead.

**6.86.54**  When that host had been slain, those two, both maddened with the lust of battle, stood facing each other in the fray, even as Vṛtra and Vāsava of old.

**6.86.55**  Beholding the rākṣasa, frenzied for combat, rushing upon him, the mighty Irāvān, inflamed with wrath, charged against him in turn.

**6.86.56**  As the evil-minded one drew near in the battle, with his sword he cut asunder his blazing bow and his five quivers as well.

**6.86.57**  Seeing his bow cut through, he sprang swiftly into the sky, as though to bewilder the enraged Irāvān by his magic.

**6.86.58**  Then Irāvān too, leaping into the firmament, baffled the rākṣasa with his own illusions; and that one, knower of every vital point, able to take any form at will and hard to assail, cut the other's limbs to pieces with his shafts.

**6.86.59**  Yet that foremost of rākṣasas, though hewn by the arrows again and again, knit himself whole once more, O great king, and recovered his youth.

**6.86.60**  For illusion is inborn in their kind, and their age and form are shaped by will; thus the rākṣasa's body, severed and severed again, grew back each time.

**6.86.61**  Irāvān too, exceedingly wroth, hewed that mighty rākṣasa again and again with a keen battle-axe.

**6.86.62**  Cleft by that strong hero like a tree under the axe, the rākṣasa roared horribly, and that cry grew into a tumult.

**6.86.63**  Gashed by the battle-axe, the rākṣasa streamed with abundant blood; thereupon the strong one fell into a fury and put forth his utmost speed in the encounter.

**6.86.64**  Then the son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga, beholding his foe grown mighty in the fight, assumed a huge and dreadful shape and set about seizing him, there in the very midst of the battle-front, while all looked on.

**6.86.65**  Beholding that illusion of the great-souled rākṣasa, Irāvān too, sore enraged, set about conjuring an illusion of his own.

**6.86.66**  As he, overmastered by wrath and never turning back in battles, stood there, the kindred of his mother's line came to his aid.

**6.86.67**  Encompassed on every side in the fray by a host of serpents, O king, he took on a vast form, like Ananta wreathed in his coils, and then with snakes of many kinds he covered over the rākṣasa.

**6.86.68**  But that bull among rākṣasas, while being mantled by the serpents, bethought himself, took the form of Suparṇa, and devoured the snakes.

**6.86.69**  When by that magic the kindred of his mother's line had been consumed, the rākṣasa, finding Irāvān bewildered, slew him with his sword.

**6.86.70**  The head of Irāvān, with its earrings and diadem, gleaming like the lotus and the moon, the rākṣasa struck down to the earth.

**6.86.71**  When that hero, Arjuna's son, had been slain by the rākṣasa, the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, together with their king, were freed of their grief.

**6.86.72**  In that great and terrible battle, once again a fearful and grievous confusion arose between the two armies.

**6.86.73**  Horses, elephants, and foot-soldiers, all confounded together, were slain by the tuskers; and chariots and elephants too were destroyed there by the infantry.

**6.86.74**  And in the press of battle great masses of foot and chariots and many horses, O king, were slain by the chariot-warriors of thine and theirs alike.

**6.86.75**  Arjuna too, not knowing that his own begotten son was slain, struck down in the battle those heroic kings who guarded Bhīṣma.

**6.86.76**  In like manner thy warriors, O king, and the mighty Sṛñjayas, offering up their very lives in the fight, slew one another.

**6.86.77**  With their hair loosened, stripped of their armour, robbed of their chariots, their bows cut asunder, they grappled hand to hand, closing with one another.

**6.86.78**  And Bhīṣma, with shafts that pierced the vital parts, struck down great chariot-warriors, the mighty one making the army of the Pāṇḍavas tremble in the fray.

**6.86.79**  By him in Yudhiṣṭhira's host were many men slain — elephants and horsemen and chariot-fighters and horses as well.

**6.86.80**  There, O Bhārata, beholding the prowess of Bhīṣma in the battle, we saw it most marvellous, like the prowess of Śakra himself.

**6.86.81**  In like manner, O Bhārata, fierce was then the battle of Bhīmasena and of the son of Pṛṣata and of the bowman of the Sātvata line.

**6.86.82**  Beholding the valour of Droṇa, fear entered the Pāṇḍavas: 'He alone in battle is able to slay us together with our hosts.'

**6.86.83**  'How much more, then, when he is ringed about by the heroes of the earth and bands of warriors?' — thus they spoke, O great king, hard-pressed by Droṇa in the fight.

**6.86.84**  As that fearful battle thus raged on, O bull of the Bharatas, the heroes of both armies brooked no quarter from one another.

**6.86.85**  As though possessed, the mighty bowmen fought, become very rākṣasas — thy men and the sons of Pāṇḍu alike, O sire, all in a frenzy.

**6.86.86**  We saw not a single warrior, O lord of men, who sought to guard his own life in that battle that resembled a war of the Daityas.

**6.87.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When they saw Irāvān slain, what did the sons of Pṛthā, those great chariot-warriors, do in the battle? Tell me that, O Saṃjaya.

**6.87.2**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing Irāvān slain in the battle, the rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca, son of Bhīmasena, sent forth a mighty roar.

**6.87.3**  At the sound of his roaring, the earth, robed in her oceans and clad in her mountains and forests, shook violently then, O king, and so too the firmament, all the quarters, and the intermediate points.

**6.87.4**  Hearing that mighty roar, O Bhārata, in thy army there arose a stiffening of the thighs, a trembling, and a breaking out of sweat.

**6.87.5**  And all thy men, O lord of kings, downcast in spirit, coiled in upon themselves like snakes, like elephants in dread of the lion.

**6.87.6**  Roaring his mighty roar like a thunderclap, the rākṣasa, lifting on high his blazing spear, assumed a terrifying shape.

**6.87.7**  Surrounded by bulls among rākṣasas armed with dreadful weapons of every kind, he came on in fury, like Yama the all-ending Death.

**6.87.8**  Beholding him bearing down, wrathful and terrible to look upon, and seeing his own host for the most part turned away in fear of him —

**6.87.9**  Then king Duryodhana rushed upon Ghaṭotkaca, grasping his great bow and roaring again and again like a lion.

**6.87.10**  And behind him there followed the lord of the Vaṅgas himself, with ten thousand elephants, mountain-like and streaming with rut.

**6.87.11**  Beholding thy son bearing down upon him, hedged about by that array of elephants, O great king, the night-ranger flew into a rage.

**6.87.12**  Then began a battle, tumultuous and making the hair stand on end, O lord of kings, between the rākṣasas and the host of Duryodhana.

**6.87.13**  And beholding the elephant-array rising up like a bank of clouds, the rākṣasas, weapons in hand, charged at it in fury.

**6.87.14**  Sounding forth divers cries, like thunderclouds charged with lightning, they smote the elephant-riders with arrows, darts, lances, and iron shafts.

**6.87.15**  With bhiṇḍipālas and spears, with maces and battle-axes, with mountain-peaks and trees, they struck down the great elephants.

**6.87.16**  Elephants with their frontal globes split, bathed in blood, their bodies torn open, we beheld, O great king, being slaughtered by the night-rangers.

**6.87.17**  As those elephant-warriors were being destroyed and broken, Duryodhana, O great king, rushed upon the rākṣasas.

**6.87.18**  Yielding himself to wrath and setting his own life at naught, the mighty one loosed sharpened arrows upon the rākṣasas.

**6.87.19**  And that great bowman, thy son Duryodhana, O foremost of the Bharatas, in his fury slew the chief rākṣasas there.

**6.87.20**  That great chariot-warrior slew with four arrows four of them — the swift, the most fearsome, Vidyujjihva, and Pramāthin.

**6.87.21**  Then again that one of immeasurable spirit, O foremost of the Bharatas, loosed an irresistible torrent of arrows against the host of the night-rangers.

**6.87.22**  Beholding that great deed of thy son, O honoured one, the mighty Ghaṭotkaca, son of Bhīmasena, blazed up with wrath.

**6.87.23**  And twanging his great bow, whose sound was like the thunderbolt of Indra, he rushed with speed upon Duryodhana, that crusher of foes.

**6.87.24**  Beholding him bearing down like Death let loose by Time, thy son Duryodhana, O great king, faltered not at all.

**6.87.25**  Then the cruel one, enraged, his eyes red with anger, spoke to him: 'Those whom thou, most ruthless, didst banish into exile for a long while — those Pāṇḍavas, O king, whom thou didst vanquish by fraud at the game of dice —'

**6.87.26**  '— and when Draupadī Kṛṣṇā, clad in a single garment, in her courses, was dragged into the assembly hall, O evil-minded one, and by thee tormented in many ways —'

**6.87.27**  '— and how, to do thee a pleasure, the vile lord of the Sindhus, while she dwelt in the hermitage, harassed her, setting at naught my elders —'

**6.87.28**  '— of these affronts, O basest of thy line, and of the others too, this day I shall make an end, unless thou quit the battle.'

**6.87.29**  Having spoken thus, the son of Hiḍimbā, twanging his great bow, biting his lip with his teeth and licking the corners of his mouth —

**6.87.30**  — overwhelmed Duryodhana with a mighty rain of arrows, as a storm-cloud in the rains drenches a mountain with its torrents of water.

**6.88.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then that shower of arrows, unbearable even to the Dānavas, the lord of kings sustained in the battle, as a great elephant withstands the downpour of rain.

**6.88.2**  Then, filled with wrath, hissing like a serpent, thy son, O bull of the Bharatas, came into the utmost peril.

**6.88.3**  He loosed five-and-twenty whetted, keen iron shafts; and they fell all at once, O king, upon that bull among rākṣasas, like wrathful venom-fanged serpents upon the mountain Gandhamādana.

**6.88.4**  Pierced by them and streaming with blood like an elephant in rut, that eater of flesh set his mind upon the king's destruction and grasped a great dart, able to rend even the mountains.

**6.88.5**  Flaming like a great meteor, that dart the mighty-armed one raised on high, as Maghavān his thunderbolt, eager to slay thy son.

**6.88.6**  Seeing it lifted up, the lord of the Vaṅgas, in haste, urged on against the rākṣasa his elephant, huge as a hill.

**6.88.7**  With that strong and swift-footed prince of elephants, in the battle, he made for the path where Duryodhana's chariot stood, and with the tusker barred the way of thy son's car.

**6.88.8**  Seeing his path blocked by the wise king of the Vaṅgas, Ghaṭotkaca, O great king, his eyes red with wrath, hurled that uplifted great dart at the elephant.

**6.88.9**  Struck by it, O king, as it sped from his arm, the elephant, with a gush of welling blood, fell down and died.

**6.88.10**  And as the elephant fell, the strong lord of the Vaṅgas, springing down with speed, came to the surface of the earth.

**6.88.11**  Duryodhana too, beholding that noble elephant struck down, and seeing his host broken, fell into the deepest distress.

**6.88.12**  Yet setting before him the law of the warrior and his own self-respect, the king, though the hour to retreat had come, stood firm as an unshaken mountain.

**6.88.13**  And fitting to his bow a sharpened arrow, blazing like the fire of Time, in utmost wrath he loosed it at that dreadful night-ranger.

**6.88.14**  Beholding that arrow speeding on, bright as Indra's thunderbolt, the huge-bodied Ghaṭotkaca by his nimbleness eluded it.

**6.88.15**  Once more the fierce one roared, his eyes red with wrath, affrighting all beings, like a thunder-cloud at the ending of an age.

**6.88.16**  Hearing that dreadful roar of the rākṣasa, Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, drew near to the preceptor and spoke:

**6.88.17**  'By the dreadful roar that is heard, raised by the rākṣasa, surely the son of Hiḍimbā is even now doing battle with king Duryodhana.'

**6.88.18**  "This one cannot be conquered in battle by any living creature. Go there, good fortune to you, and guard the king well.

**6.88.19**  The illustrious king is hard pressed by that wicked-souled rākṣasa. This, O scorchers of foes, is now the foremost duty of us all."

**6.88.20**  Hearing the grandsire's words, the great chariot-warriors made haste and, summoning their utmost speed, rushed to where the Kaurava stood.

**6.88.21**  Droṇa and Somadatta and Bāhlika and Jayadratha, Kṛpa, Bhūriśravas, Śalya, Citrasena, Viviṃśati—

**6.88.22**  Aśvatthāman and Vikarṇa, the prince of Avanti and Bṛhadbala, and the many thousands of chariots that followed them—all seeking to rescue your hard-pressed son Duryodhana.

**6.88.23**  Beholding that unassailable host, guarded by the foremost men of the world, advancing upon him with weapons drawn, the best of rākṣasas, mighty-armed, did not waver—like the mountain Maināka.

**6.88.24**  Grasping his vast bow, surrounded by his kinsmen, who bore spears and maces in their hands and weapons of every kind,

**6.88.25**  there arose a tumultuous, hair-raising battle between the chief of the rākṣasas and the army of Duryodhana.

**6.88.26**  The twanging clamor of the bows rose tumultuous on every side; it was heard, O great king, like the crackling of bamboos burning in a fire.

**6.88.27**  From the weapons falling upon the armor of embodied warriors there arose a sound, O king, like that of mountains splitting asunder.

**6.88.28**  The javelins hurled by the arms of heroes, as they coursed through the sky, O lord of peoples, had the look of serpents gliding aloft.

**6.88.29**  Then, supremely enraged, the mighty-armed lord of rākṣasas drew his enormous bow taut and let loose a terrible roar.

**6.88.30**  In his wrath he cut through the teacher's bow with a crescent-headed arrow, and with a broad shaft he struck down Somadatta's banner and roared aloud.

**6.88.31**  He pierced Bāhlika between the breasts with three arrows, struck Kṛpa with one, and Citrasena with three shafts.

**6.88.32**  With a shaft drawn to the full and well aimed, reaching Vikarṇa at the collarbone, he smote him; and Vikarṇa sank down upon the floor of his chariot, drenched in blood.

**6.88.33**  Then again the immeasurable-souled one, enraged, loosed fifteen iron shafts at Bhūriśravas, O bull of the Bharatas; and they, piercing his armor, swiftly passed on and entered the surface of the earth.

**6.88.34**  He struck the charioteers of Viviṃśati and of Droṇa's son, and both fell upon the floors of their chariots, letting go the reins of their horses.

**6.88.35**  With a crescent-headed arrow he tore down the gold-adorned boar-emblem of the king of the Sindhus, O great king, and with a second he severed his bow.

**6.88.36**  Then with four iron shafts, his eyes red with wrath, he slew the four horses of the high-souled prince of Avanti.

**6.88.37**  With a sharp, yellow shaft drawn to the full, he pierced through the prince Bṛhadbala, O great king; and he, sorely wounded and shaken, sank down within his chariot.

**6.88.38**  Then, deeply possessed by wrath, the lord of rākṣasas, mounted on his chariot, hurled sharp, keen arrows like venomous serpents; and these, O great king, pierced Śalya, skilled in war.

**6.89.1**  Saṃjaya said: Having turned back all your warriors in the fight, the rākṣasa, eager to slay, rushed upon Duryodhana, O best of the Bharatas.

**6.89.2**  Seeing him charging swiftly upon the king, your warriors, frenzied for battle, rushed forward eager to kill him.

**6.89.3**  Drawing their bows tall as palm trees, those men of great might rushed upon him alone, roaring like a troop of lions.

**6.89.4**  Then they hemmed him in on every side with a torrent of arrows, as autumn clouds enshroud a mountain with their downpour of rain.

**6.89.5**  Sorely wounded and pained, like an elephant goaded by the driver's hook, he sprang up into the sky on every side like the son of Vinatā.

**6.89.6**  He let forth a mighty roar like an autumn thundercloud, his terrible voice making the quarters, the sky, and the intermediate regions resound.

**6.89.7**  Hearing that roar of the rākṣasa, king Yudhiṣṭhira, the best of the Bharatas, spoke these words to Bhīmasena:

**6.89.8**  "Surely the rākṣasa is fighting against the great chariot-warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, as one hears his cry roaring with a terrible voice. I perceive, dear one, that a grievous burden has fallen upon him there.

**6.89.9**  And the grandsire, enraged, is bent upon destroying the Pāñcālas; and Phalguna, to protect them, fights against the foes.

**6.89.10**  Hearing of this, O mighty-armed one, two tasks press upon us. Go and protect the son of Hiḍimbā, who has come into the gravest peril."

**6.89.11**  Heeding his brother's word, Vṛkodara hastened forth, terrifying all the kings with his lion's roar, and with great speed, O king, like the ocean at the time of the new moon.

**6.89.12**  Satyadhṛti followed him, and the son of Sucitti, mad for battle, and Śreṇimān and Vasudāna, and the son of the king of Kāśi, Abhibhū.

**6.89.13**  And the sons of Draupadī, great chariot-warriors led by Abhimanyu, and the valiant Kṣatradeva, and Kṣatradharman likewise,

**6.89.14**  and the lord of Anūpa, Nīla, stationed amid his own forces—with a great array of chariots they surrounded the son of Hiḍimbā.

**6.89.15**  And with six thousand ever-rutting, war-striking elephants, joined together, they guarded the lord of rākṣasas, Ghaṭotkaca.

**6.89.16**  With a great lion-roar, with the rumble of chariot-wheel rims, and with the thunder of hoofbeats, they made the earth tremble.

**6.89.17**  Hearing the noise of their onset, your host, agitated by fear of Bhīmasena and pale of face, turned and fled, O great king, abandoning Ghaṭotkaca.

**6.89.18**  Then the battle broke out here and there among those high-souled men, your warriors and the foes alike, who never turned back in combat.

**6.89.19**  Loosing weapons of every kind, the great chariot-warriors rushed upon one another and joined in a clash—a tangled, most terrible fight, fraught with terror for the faint of heart.

**6.89.20**  Horses closed with elephants, foot-soldiers with charioteers, each seeking great glory against the other in the fight, O king.

**6.89.21**  And of a sudden, from that collision, there arose a thick, dense dust, churned up by the feet and wheel-rims of chariots, horses, elephants, and infantry.

**6.89.22**  The dense dust, smoke-gray and red, covered over the field of battle; and neither one's own men nor the foe, O king, could recognize one another.

**6.89.23**  Father knew not son, nor son his father, in that hair-raising carnage where all bounds were broken.

**6.89.24**  Of the weapons and of the men crying aloud there rose a mighty din, O best of the Bharatas, like that of bamboos burning in a fire.

**6.89.25**  A river streamed there with waves of the blood and entrails of elephants, horses, and men, its weeds and grasses made of human hair.

**6.89.26**  As the heads of men fell from their bodies in the battle, a very great sound was heard, like that of falling stones.

**6.89.27**  With headless men, with elephants whose limbs were severed, and with horses whose bodies were torn apart, the earth was strewn over.

**6.89.28**  Loosing weapons of many kinds, the great chariot-warriors rushed upon one another and joined in the clash of arms.

**6.89.29**  Horses, urged on by their riders, met other horses and, striking against one another in the fight, fell down with their lives fled.

**6.89.30**  Men met men, their eyes deeply red with wrath, and pressing breast against breast they grappled and slew one another.

**6.89.31**  Driven on by their mahouts, elephants struck the enemy's elephants, smiting those elephants in the fray with the tips of their tusks.

**6.89.32**  Streaming with welling blood and adorned with banners, locked together they looked like clouds shot through with lightning.

**6.89.33**  Some, pierced by tusk-tips and their frontal globes broken by javelins, charged about trumpeting, roaring like rain-clouds.

**6.89.34**  Some, their trunks cut in two, and others with their limbs severed, fell in that tumult like mountains shorn of their wings.

**6.89.35**  Other splendid elephants, their flanks rent open by the foe's elephants, poured forth blood in floods, like mountains streaming with their ores.

**6.89.36**  Others, struck by iron shafts and pierced by javelins, their riders slain, appeared like mountains shorn of their peaks.

**6.89.37**  Some, possessed by rage, blind with rut and unrestrained, crushed chariots, horses, and footmen by the hundred in the battle.

**6.89.38**  And horses, struck by their riders with lances and javelins, wheeled this way and that, throwing the quarters into confusion.

**6.89.39**  Chariot-warriors against chariot-warriors, men of noble birth ready to give up their bodies, putting forth their utmost strength, performed deeds without fear.

**6.89.40**  In that carnage they struck at one another as at a bridegroom's choosing, those skilled in war seeking glory, O king, or else heaven.

**6.89.41**  As that hair-raising battle raged on, the great army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son was for the most part turned to flight.

**6.90.1**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing his own army slaughtered, king Duryodhana himself, enraged, rushed against Bhīmasena, the tamer of foes.

**6.90.2**  Grasping his enormous bow, whose twang was like the thunderbolt of Indra, he covered the Pāṇḍava with a great torrent of arrows.

**6.90.3**  And fitting to his bow a very sharp, feathered crescent-headed shaft, filled with wrath, he severed Bhīmasena's bow.

**6.90.4**  Seeing his opening, the great chariot-warrior in haste fitted a keen arrow that could cleave even mountains, and with it the mighty-armed one struck Bhīmasena upon the chest.

**6.90.5**  Sorely wounded and pained, the splendid warrior, licking the corners of his mouth, clung to his gold-wrought banner-staff.

**6.90.6**  Seeing Bhīmasena thus distressed, Ghaṭotkaca blazed up with wrath, like a fire about to consume all.

**6.90.7**  And the great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, led by Abhimanyu, charged forward shouting, agitated, upon the king.

**6.90.8**  Seeing them rushing on, enraged and agitated, the son of Bharadvāja spoke these words to your great chariot-warriors:

**6.90.9**  "Go swiftly, good fortune to you, and protect the king, who has come into the gravest peril and is sinking in an ocean of distress.

**6.90.10**  These enraged, mighty bowmen, the great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, with Bhīmasena at their head, have fallen upon Duryodhana,

**6.90.11**  loosing weapons of every kind, intent on victory, roaring terrible roars and making this earth tremble."

**6.90.12**  Hearing those words of the teacher, your warriors, with Somadatta at their head, turned upon the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.90.13**  Kṛpa, Bhūriśravas, Śalya, Droṇa's son, Viviṃśati, Citrasena and Vikarṇa, then the lord of the Sindhus and Bṛhadbala, and the two mighty bowmen of Avanti—these surrounded the Kaurava king.

**6.90.14**  Having advanced twenty paces, the Pāṇḍavas and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra joined in the clash, each seeking to slay the other.

**6.90.15**  Having spoken thus, the mighty-armed son of Bharadvāja drew his great bow taut and then struck Bhīma with twenty-six arrows.

**6.90.16**  And again the mighty-armed one swiftly covered him with arrows, as an autumn cloud enshrouds a mountain with its downpour of rain.

**6.90.17**  Bhīmasena struck him back with ten arrows—that mighty bowman of great strength, in haste, upon the left side.

**6.90.18**  Sorely wounded and pained, and aged withal, O Bhārata, his senses suddenly lost, he sank down upon the floor of his chariot.

**6.90.19**  Seeing the teacher sorely afflicted, King Duryodhana himself and the son of Droṇa, both in fury, rushed upon Bhīmasena.

**6.90.20**  Beholding the two of them bearing down upon him, like Death and Yama at the end of time, the mighty-armed Bhīmasena swiftly seized his mace.

**6.90.21**  Leaping down at once from his chariot, he stood unmoving as a mountain, raising aloft in battle his heavy mace, like the rod of Yama.

**6.90.22**  Seeing him with mace upraised, like Kailāsa with its peak, the Kaurava and the son of Droṇa together charged upon him.

**6.90.23**  As those two foremost among the strong rushed on together in haste, Vṛkodara too, hurrying, ran toward them with great speed.

**6.90.24**  Beholding him bearing down, enraged and terrible to look upon, the great chariot-warriors of the Kauravas swiftly ran to meet him.

**6.90.25**  All of them, with the son of Bharadvāja at their head, in their desire to slay Bhīmasena, let fall weapons of every kind upon Bhīma's breast, and together oppressed the Pāṇḍava on every side.

**6.90.26**  Seeing that great chariot-warrior brought into peril and hard pressed, the great warriors of the Pāṇḍavas led by Abhimanyu rushed to his rescue, casting aside life so hard to cast away.

**6.90.27**  The valiant lord of the Anūpas, the dear friend of Bhīma, Nīla, dark as a rain-cloud, in fury fell upon the son of Droṇa — that mighty archer who ever vied with Droṇa's son.

**6.90.28**  Drawing his great bow, he pierced the son of Droṇa with a feathered shaft, even as Śakra in olden days, O great king, pierced the Dānava —

**6.90.29**  Vipracitti, hard to assail, terror of the gods, by whom in his wrath the three worlds were affrighted through his own splendor.

**6.90.30**  Thus pierced by Nīla with a keen-pointed winged shaft, and with his blood gushing forth, the son of Droṇa was filled with wrath.

**6.90.31**  Drawing his wondrous bow, loud as the thunderbolt of Indra, that foremost of the wise set his mind upon the destruction of Nīla.

**6.90.32**  Then, fitting to his bow bright broad-headed arrows tempered by the smith, he slew his four steeds and brought down his banner.

**6.90.33**  And with a seventh broad-headed shaft he pierced Nīla in the breast; sorely wounded and in anguish, he sank down upon the floor of his chariot.

**6.90.34**  Seeing King Nīla, dark as a mass of clouds, swooning away, Ghaṭotkaca too, in fury, surrounded by his brothers,

**6.90.35**  rushed with speed upon the son of Droṇa, splendid in battle; and likewise the other Rākṣasas, frenzied in war, charged after him.

**6.90.36**  Beholding that Rākṣasa of fearful aspect bearing down upon him, the splendid son of Bharadvāja swiftly rushed to meet him.

**6.90.37**  And in his wrath he slew the terrible-looking Rākṣasas who, enraged, stood foremost as the vanguard of the Rākṣasa.

**6.90.38**  Seeing them turned away by the arrows loosed from the bow of Droṇa's son, the huge-bodied Ghaṭotkaca, son of Bhīmasena, blazed up in anger.

**6.90.39**  That lord of Rākṣasas, master of illusion, conjured up a great magic, terrible of form and most dreadful, bewildering the son of Droṇa in the fray.

**6.90.40**  Then all your warriors, turned to flight by that illusion, beheld one another lying hewn upon the earth, writhing in piteous plight and drenched with blood —

**6.90.41**  Droṇa, Duryodhana, Śalya, and Aśvatthāman, and for the most part the great archers and the chief among the Kauravas.

**6.90.42**  All the chariot-warriors seemed shattered, the elephants struck down, and the horses with their riders cut to pieces by the thousand.

**6.90.43**  Seeing this, your army fled toward the camp, O king, while I and Devavrata cried aloud to them —

**6.90.44**  "Fight on, do not flee! This is but Rākṣasa illusion wrought in battle by Ghaṭotkaca!" — yet, bewildered, they would not stand their ground, nor in their terror did they heed the words of us two as we spoke.

**6.90.45**  Seeing them in full flight, the Pāṇḍavas, victorious now, together with Ghaṭotkaca raised lion-roars, and the blare of conches and the boom of drums resounded loudly on every side.

**6.90.46**  Thus, at the hour of the sun's setting, all your host was broken by the wicked son of Hiḍimbā and scattered to the quarters.

**6.91.1**  Saṃjaya said: In that great tumult of battle, King Duryodhana then drew near to the son of Gaṅgā, and bowing to him with reverence,

**6.91.2**  he began to recount to him all that had passed — the victory of Ghaṭotkaca and his own defeat.

**6.91.3**  The unconquerable one told his tale, sighing again and again, and then, O king, he spoke to Bhīṣma, the grandsire of the Kurus:

**6.91.4**  "Relying upon you, even as the foe rely upon Vāsudeva, I have begun this terrible strife with the Pāṇḍavas, O lord.

**6.91.5**  The eleven armies that are reckoned mine stand, together with me, under your command, O scorcher of foes.

**6.91.6**  Yet I, O tiger of the Bharatas, have been vanquished in battle by the Pāṇḍavas led by Bhīmasena, who took Ghaṭotkaca for their stay.

**6.91.7**  This burns my limbs as fire burns a withered tree. Therefore I desire, O illustrious one, by your grace, O scorcher of foes,

**6.91.8**  to slay that vile Rākṣasa myself, O grandsire, with you for my refuge, unconquerable one — this you ought to grant me to do."

**6.91.9**  Hearing this speech of the king, O best of the Bharatas, Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu spoke these words to Duryodhana:

**6.91.10**  "Hear my word, O king, which I shall speak to you, O Kaurava — how you ought to bear yourself, O great king, O scorcher of foes.

**6.91.11**  Your own self must be guarded in battle, dear son, in every plight, O subduer of foes; your warfare, O ever sinless one, should be waged against the King of Righteousness,

**6.91.12**  or with Arjuna, or the twins, or again with Bhīmasena; for, observing the duty of kings, a king should meet a king in combat.

**6.91.13**  I, and Droṇa, Kṛpa, the son of Droṇa, and Kṛtavarman of the Sātvatas, and Śalya, and the son of Somadatta, and Vikarṇa the great chariot-warrior,

**6.91.14**  and your valiant brothers led by Duḥśāsana — we shall fight for your sake against that mighty Rākṣasa.

**6.91.15**  But if you cherish a great longing in your heart against that fierce lord of Rākṣasas, then let King Bhagadatta, the equal of Purandara in battle, go forth to fight that evil-minded one in the fray."

**6.91.16**  Having said so much to the king, that master of speech then addressed Bhagadatta in the presence of the lord of kings:

**6.91.17**  "Go swiftly, O great king, and check in battle the war-frenzied son of Hiḍimbā, striving with all your might before the eyes of all the bowmen — that Rākṣasa of cruel deeds, even as Indra of old checked Tāraka.

**6.91.18**  Yours are celestial weapons and prowess too, O scorcher of foes, and in days gone by you have met in combat with many Asuras.

**6.91.19**  You, O tiger among kings, are his match in the great battle; girt by your own host, O king, slay that bull among Rākṣasas."

**6.91.20**  Hearing this word of Bhīṣma, the commander of the host, he set forth swiftly with a lion-roar straight toward the foe.

**6.91.21**  Beholding him rushing on, thundering like a rain-cloud, the enraged great warriors of the Pāṇḍavas advanced against him —

**6.91.22**  Bhīmasena and Abhimanyu, and the Rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca, the sons of Draupadī, Satyadhṛti, and Kṣatradeva, O venerable one,

**6.91.23**  the lord of the Cedis and Vasudāna, and the lord of the Daśārṇas as well; and upon all these Bhagadatta too charged with his elephant Supratīka.

**6.91.24**  Then there arose a battle horrible of form and dreadful between the Pāṇḍavas and Bhagadatta, swelling the realm of Yama.

**6.91.25**  The arrows let fly by the chariot-warriors, of dread speed and well sharpened, fell, O great king, upon the elephants and the chariots.

**6.91.26**  And the great elephants in rut, well trained by their drivers, meeting one another, fell upon each other without fear.

**6.91.27**  Blinded by rut and seized with rage, in that great battle they gored one another with the points of their tusks and smote with their pestle-like tusks as they closed in.

**6.91.28**  And the horses, decked with chowries and mounted by riders with lances in hand, urged swiftly on by their riders, fell upon one another.

**6.91.29**  And the foot-soldiers, struck by hosts of footmen with darts and javelins, fell then to the ground by hundreds and by thousands.

**6.91.30**  And the chariot-warriors too, O king, slaying heroes in the fray with barbed and reed-shafted arrows, sent forth their lion-roars.

**6.91.31**  While that hair-raising battle thus went on, Bhagadatta the great archer rushed upon Bhīmasena,

**6.91.32**  upon his elephant in rut, pouring forth its ichor in seven streams, like a mountain pouring down its waters on every side,

**6.91.33**  scattering thousands of arrows as he rode upon Supratīka's head, O sinless one, like Maghavat upon Airāvata pouring down showers of rain.

**6.91.34**  That king smote Bhīma with streams of arrows, as a thundercloud in the rains beats upon a mountain with its torrents of water.

**6.91.35**  But Bhīmasena, that great archer, in his wrath slew more than a hundred of the foot-guards with showers of arrows, enraged.

**6.91.36**  Seeing them slain, the mighty Bhagadatta in his fury urged his lordly elephant against the chariot of Bhīmasena.

**6.91.37**  That elephant, sent on by him like an arrow loosed from the bowstring, rushed with speed upon Bhīmasena, the subduer of foes.

**6.91.38**  Seeing it bearing down, the great warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, with Bhīmasena at their head, advanced against it with speed —

**6.91.39**  the Kekayas and Abhimanyu and all the sons of Draupadī, the valiant lord of the Daśārṇas and Kṣatradeva, O venerable one, the lord of the Cedis and Citraketu — all of them enraged.

**6.91.40**  Displaying their celestial weapons of the highest order, those mighty ones in their wrath surrounded that one elephant on every side.

**6.91.41**  Pierced by many arrows, that great elephant shone forth, with the gush of its flowing blood, like a lordly mountain bright with its mineral hues.

**6.91.42**  And the lord of the Daśārṇas too, mounted on his own elephant huge as a mountain, rushed against the elephant of Bhagadatta.

**6.91.43**  And that lord of elephants, Supratīka, withstood that elephant bearing down upon him in battle, as the shore withstands the ocean, the abode of sea-monsters.

**6.91.44**  Seeing the lordly elephant of the high-souled king of the Daśārṇas thus held in check, the armies of the Pāṇḍavas applauded it, crying "Well done, well done!"

**6.91.45**  Then the lord of Prāgjyotiṣa, in his wrath, hurled fourteen javelins full at the front of that elephant, O best of kings.

**6.91.46**  Rending its armor and its face-guard adorned with gold, they entered swiftly, as serpents enter an anthill.

**6.91.47**  That elephant, sorely pierced and in anguish, O best of the Bharatas, its rut suddenly stilled, swiftly turned and wheeled away in haste.

**6.91.48**  And it fled with great speed, trumpeting a fearful cry, crushing its own host as the wind in its might crushes the trees.

**6.91.49**  When that elephant was put to flight, the great warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, roaring aloud their lion-roars, drew up once more for battle.

**6.91.50**  Then, with Bhīma at their head, they rushed upon Bhagadatta, scattering arrows of many kinds and weapons of many sorts.

**6.91.51**  Hearing the terrible din of those enraged and wrathful warriors bearing down, O king, Bhagadatta the great archer, his fear gone in his fury, urged his elephant on against them.

**6.91.52**  Goaded by the hook and the thumb, that best of elephants in battle became in that moment like the fire of universal dissolution.

**6.91.53**  Chariot-hosts and elephants, horses together with their riders, and foot-soldiers too, the enraged warrior crushed by hundreds and by thousands, O king, charging this way and that across the battlefield.

**6.91.54**  Churned and harried by him, that great host of the Pāṇḍavas shrank back, O great king, like a hide drawn tight in the fire.

**6.91.55**  Seeing his own host broken by the wise Bhagadatta, Ghaṭotkaca then, filled with wrath, rushed upon Bhagadatta.

**6.91.56**  A fearsome figure, O king, with blazing face and blazing eyes, assuming a terrifying form, he seemed to flame with fury.

**6.91.57**  He seized a massive spear that could rend even mountains, and, eager to slay the elephant, the mighty one suddenly hurled it, wreathed all around with sparks and flames.

**6.91.58**  Seeing it rushing suddenly upon him in the fray, wreathed in flame, that king loosed a bright, keen crescent-headed arrow and with that swift shaft cleft the enormous spear in two.

**6.91.59**  Cut in two, the gold-adorned spear fell, like a great thunderbolt loosed by Śakra and hurtling fallen from the sky.

**6.91.60**  Seeing the spear fallen, cleft in two, that king took up a great dart with a golden shaft, like a tongue of fire, and hurled it at the rākṣasa, crying, "Stand! Stand!"

**6.91.61**  Beholding it rushing down upon him like a thunderbolt poised in the sky, the rākṣasa leapt up at once, caught it, and roared aloud.

**6.91.62**  And swiftly, setting it upon his knee, he broke it, O Bhārata, before the very eyes of that lord of kings; a marvel, it seemed, came to pass.

**6.91.63**  Beholding that deed done by the mighty rākṣasa, the gods in heaven, with the gandharvas and the sages, were filled with wonder.

**6.91.64**  And the Pāṇḍava great archers, with Bhīmasena at their head, made the earth resound with the cry, "Well done! Well done!"

**6.91.65**  But hearing that great shout of the exultant high-souled ones, the great archer Bhagadatta, full of valor, could not bear it.

**6.91.66**  Drawing his great bow, whose twang was like the roar of Indra's thunderbolt, he rushed with speed upon the great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, loosing bright, keen iron shafts that gleamed like flame.

**6.91.67**  He pierced Bhīma with one arrow, the rākṣasa with nine shafts, Abhimanyu with three, and the Kekayas with five.

**6.91.68**  With a winged, golden-nocked shaft loosed from a fully drawn bow, he pierced the right arm of Kṣatradeva in the battle, and suddenly his excellent bow, with its arrow, fell from his grasp.

**6.91.69**  Then he struck the five sons of Draupadī with five arrows, and in his wrath he slew the horses of Bhīmasena.

**6.91.70**  He cut down his lion-bannered standard with three barbless shafts, and with three other winged arrows he pierced through his charioteer.

**6.91.71**  Deeply wounded and stricken, Viśoka sank down upon the chariot-seat, O best of the Bharatas, struck by Bhagadatta in the fight.

**6.91.72**  Then Bhīma, O great king, that foremost of chariot-warriors, now bereft of his chariot, seized his mace and sprang swiftly down from the great car.

**6.91.73**  Seeing him with mace upraised, like a mountain with its peak, a dreadful terror came upon your warriors, O Bhārata.

**6.91.74**  At that very moment, O great king, the Pāṇḍava whose charioteer was Kṛṣṇa came up, slaying his foes by the thousand.

**6.91.75**  He came to where those two tigers among men, father and son, scorchers of the foe, Bhīmasena and Ghaṭotkaca, were locked in struggle with the lord of Prāgjyotiṣa.

**6.91.76**  Seeing those great chariot-warriors locked in combat, O king, the Pāṇḍava, O best of the Bharatas, came there in haste, scattering his shafts.

**6.91.77**  Then King Duryodhana, that great chariot-warrior, in his haste swiftly urged on his host, thronged with chariots, elephants, and horses.

**6.91.78**  Against that great Kaurava army rushing suddenly upon him, the Pāṇḍava borne by white horses charged with speed.

**6.91.79**  Bhagadatta too in the battle, O Bhārata, crushing the Pāṇḍava host with that elephant, rushed upon Yudhiṣṭhira.

**6.91.80**  Then there was a tumultuous battle, O honored sir, between Bhagadatta and the Pāñcālas, the Sṛñjayas, and the Kekayas with weapons upraised.

**6.91.81**  And Bhīmasena in the battle made both Keśava and Arjuna hear, just as it had happened, of the grievous slaying of Irāvat.

**6.92.1**  Saṃjaya said: Hearing that his son Irāvat had been slain, Dhanaṃjaya, overcome with great grief, hissed like a serpent.

**6.92.2**  In the midst of the battle, O king, he spoke these words to Vāsudeva: "Surely it was this that the deeply wise Vidura foresaw of old—

**6.92.3**  the terrible destruction of the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas; and so that great-minded one strove to restrain Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the lord of men.

**6.92.4**  Many heroes who ought never to have been slain, O slayer of Madhu, have been struck down in this battle by the Kauravas, and slain by us as well.

**6.92.5**  For the sake of wealth, O best of men, this contemptible deed is done. Cursed be that wealth, for whose sake such a destruction of kinsmen is wrought!

**6.92.6**  Better death in poverty than wealth won by the slaying of kin. What shall we gain, O Kṛṣṇa, by slaying our kinsmen gathered here?

**6.92.7**  Through the offense of Duryodhana, of Śakuni the son of Subala, and through the evil counsel of Karṇa, the kṣatriyas go to their destruction.

**6.92.8**  And now I understand, O slayer of Madhu, how rightly the king acted, O mighty-armed one, when he begged of Suyodhana but half the kingdom, or even five villages—yet that evil-minded one would not grant it.

**6.92.9**  For seeing the valiant kṣatriyas lying upon the face of the earth, I bitterly reproach myself: a curse upon the warrior's calling!

**6.92.10**  These kṣatriyas will think me powerless in battle. Yet for me, O slayer of Madhu, this warring against my own kinsmen is no fit thing.

**6.92.11**  Drive the horses swiftly toward the host of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son. With my two arms I shall cross to the far shore of this vast ocean of war. This, O Mādhava, is no time anywhere for faintness of heart.

**6.92.12**  Thus addressed by Pārtha, Keśava, slayer of enemy heroes, urged on those white horses, swift as the wind.

**6.92.13**  Then a great roar arose from your army, O Bhārata, like that of the ocean swelling at the full moon, lashed to fury by the wind.

**6.92.14**  In the afternoon, O great king, a battle joined between Bhīṣma and the Pāṇḍavas, with a din like that of the storm-cloud.

**6.92.15**  Then, O king, your sons rushed upon Bhīmasena, surrounding Droṇa in the fray as the Vasus surround Vāsava.

**6.92.16**  Then Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu, and Kṛpa that best of chariot-warriors, and Bhagadatta and Suśarman rushed upon Dhanaṃjaya.

**6.92.17**  Hārdikya and Bāhlika both rushed upon Sātyaki, while the king Ambaṣṭhaka held off Abhimanyu.

**6.92.18**  The rest of the great chariot-warriors, O great king, engaged the rest in turn; and so there began a battle, dreadful of aspect, fraught with terror.

**6.92.19**  But Bhīmasena, beholding your sons, O lord of men, blazed up in the battle in his wrath, like the fire that bears the oblation fed with ghee.

**6.92.20**  And your sons covered the son of Kuntī with their arrows, O great king, as the rain-clouds in the wet season veil a mountain.

**6.92.21**  Covered on every side by your sons, O lord of the people, that hero licked the corners of his mouth, like a tiger swollen with pride.

**6.92.22**  Then Bhīma, O king, with a keen razor-headed shaft, brought down Vyūḍhoraska, and he was bereft of life.

**6.92.23**  And with another broad-headed shaft, yellow and whetted sharp, he struck down Kuṇḍalin, as a lion brings down a lesser beast.

**6.92.24**  Then, swiftly, he took up seven well-whetted yellow shafts, O honored sir, when he had come upon your sons.

**6.92.25**  Those arrows, sped by Bhīmasena of the firm bow, struck down your sons, mighty chariot-warriors, from their cars.

**6.92.26**  Anādhṛṣṭi, Kuṇḍabheda, Vairāṭa, Dīrghalocana, Dīrghabāhu, Subāhu, and likewise Kanakadhvaja—

**6.92.27**  those heroes, as they fell, shone forth, O bull of the Bharatas, like mango trees in spring, dappled with blossoms, brought down to the ground.

**6.92.28**  Then the rest of your sons fled away, O lord of the people, deeming the mighty Bhīmasena to be Death itself.

**6.92.29**  But Droṇa, as that hero was consuming your sons in the battle, showered him on every side with arrows, as a mountain is drenched by streams of rain.

**6.92.30**  There we beheld the marvelous prowess of the son of Kuntī, for though held back by Droṇa, he still went on slaying your sons.

**6.92.31**  As a mighty bull endures the rain falling from the sky, so Bhīma bore up under the shower of arrows loosed by Droṇa.

**6.92.32**  And a wondrous deed, O great king, did Vṛkodara perform there, for he slew your sons in battle and fought with Droṇa as well.

**6.92.33**  Among your heroic sons the elder brother of Arjuna sported, O great king, ranging like a mighty tiger among deer.

**6.92.34**  And as a wolf in the midst of a herd would scatter the beasts, so Vṛkodara scattered your sons in the battle.

**6.92.35**  The son of Gaṅgā, and Bhagadatta, and Gautama that great chariot-warrior held off in the fight the impetuous Pāṇḍava Arjuna.

**6.92.36**  Warding off their missiles with his own, that surpassing chariot-warrior in the battle sent the chief heroes of your armies to their death.

**6.92.37**  And Abhimanyu with his shafts made the king Ambaṣṭha, renowned through the world, that best of chariot-warriors, bereft of his car.

**6.92.38**  Bereft of his chariot and hard-pressed by the illustrious son of Subhadrā, that lord of men, in shame, swiftly leapt down from his car.

**6.92.39**  He hurled his sword in the battle at the high-souled son of Subhadrā, and then mounted the chariot of the high-souled Hārdikya.

**6.92.40**  But the son of Subhadrā, slayer of enemy heroes, skilled in the ways of war, by his nimbleness made that flying sword miss its mark.

**6.92.41**  Seeing the sword turned aside by the son of Subhadrā in the battle, then a great cry of "Well done! Well done!" arose from the armies, O lord of the people.

**6.92.42**  The others, with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at their head, fought against your army, and in the same way all your warriors fought against the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.92.43**  There arose a great clamor among yours and among theirs, O Bhārata, as they smote one another fiercely and wrought deeds hard to do.

**6.92.44**  For the warriors in the battle, O honored sir, seizing one another by the hair, fought with nails, with teeth, with fists, and with knees.

**6.92.45**  And with arms, and with open palms, and with well-whetted swords, finding an opening, they sent one another to the abode of Yama.

**6.92.46**  Father slew son and son slew father in the battle; their purpose utterly distracted, the men fought there.

**6.92.47**  In the battle, O Bhārata, beautiful bows with golden backs, flung from the hands of the slain, and costly quivers lay scattered.

**6.92.48**  Sharpened arrows, with nocks of gold and silver, washed with oil, gleamed forth like serpents that had cast their skins.

**6.92.49**  Swords with hilts of ivory, adorned with gold, and shields with golden backs, flung away from the bowmen, lay strewn about.

**6.92.50**  Barbed darts wrought with gold, pikes adorned with gold, lances all of gold, and spears bright with the gleam of gold,

**6.92.51**  all cut down and fallen, lay there—and heavy maces, and iron clubs, and pikes, and slings too, O honored sir.

**6.92.52**  There lay fallen lances too, and gay caparisons wrought with gold of many shapes, and fans made of yak-tail whisks.

**6.92.53**  Having let fall their weapons of every kind, the men lay there; and the great chariot-warriors, though their breath was gone, seemed as if still living.

**6.92.54**  Their limbs crushed by maces, their skulls split open by clubs, trampled by elephants, horses, and chariots, the men lay strewn upon the earth.

**6.92.55**  And so with the bodies of horses, men, and elephants the earth then shone, O king, covered over on every side as though with mountains.

**6.92.56**  With spears, darts, arrows, and lances fallen in the fray, with swords, spiked maces, javelins, iron pikes, and battle-axes,

**6.92.57**  with bludgeons, slings, and engines that slay a hundred, and with bodies cleft by weapons, the earth was overspread.

**6.92.58**  Strewn with the dead — some voiceless, some scarcely moaning, all bathed in floods of blood — the earth shone forth, O slayer of foes, covered with the lifeless.

**6.92.59**  With arms still bearing their leather guards and armlets, anointed with sandal, hewn off and like the trunks of elephants, and with the severed thighs of the mighty,

**6.92.60**  and with the fallen heads of bull-eyed heroes, bound about with their crest-jewels and still wearing their earrings, the earth shone, O Bhārata.

**6.92.61**  With golden corslets smeared with blood and scattered all about, the ground gleamed exceedingly, as with fires whose flames had died down.

**6.92.62**  With quivers flung asunder and bows fallen down, and with arrows golden-feathered scattered on every side,

**6.92.63**  with many chariots broken, garlanded with nets of little bells, and with slain horses strewn about, their tongues lolling out, smeared with blood,

**6.92.64**  with chariot-poles, pennons, quivers, and banners, and with the great white conches of the foremost warriors scattered abroad,

**6.92.65**  and with elephants lying with trunks drooping, the earth shone, decked with ornaments of every shape, like a lovely woman richly adorned.

**6.92.66**  With other tusked elephants there, pierced by spears and in sore anguish, sending forth cries with their trunks and again and again a spray of foam, that field of battle gleamed as though with mountains being blown upon.

**6.92.67**  With blankets and housings of many hues belonging to the elephants, and with fair goads fallen, their shafts set with beryl gems,

**6.92.68**  with the bells of the lordly elephants fallen on every side, and with their gay caparisons torn apart, and with rugs of antelope-hide,

**6.92.69**  with neck-bands of curious make and golden girths, and with engines hacked to pieces, and with lances and javelins,

**6.92.70**  with the breastplates of horses, dust-grey and overlaid with gold, and with the hewn-off arms of riders fallen down, still bearing their armlets,

**6.92.71**  with javelins bright and keen, and with shining darts as well, and with turbans cut off and flung here and there,

**6.92.72**  with gaudy crescent-headed shafts adorned with gold, and with horse-cloths and coverlets of antelope-hide trodden underfoot,

**6.92.73**  with the crest-jewels of kings, curious and of great worth, and with parasols flung aside, and with yak-tail fans,

**6.92.74**  and with the faces of heroes bright as the lotus and the moon, with lovely earrings and trimmed beards, exceedingly adorned,

**6.92.75**  flung down, O great king, with earrings glittering with gold — the earth became like the sky dappled with planets and stars.

**6.92.76**  Thus, O Bhārata, were those two great hosts ground down there, yours and theirs, when they had closed with one another in the fight.

**6.92.77**  And when those men were weary, broken, and worn down, O Bhārata, dread night came on, and we could no longer see the battle.

**6.92.78**  Then the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas drew off their armies, while the dread and dire onset of night, fearful to behold, came on.

**6.92.79**  Having thus withdrawn, the Kurus and Pāṇḍavas together, going each to his own camp, settled down as the hour required.

**6.93.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then King Duryodhana, and Śakuni the son of Subala, and your son Duḥśāsana, and the invincible son of the charioteer —

**6.93.2**  having come together, O great king, took counsel on what they wished to say: how the sons of Pāṇḍu, with their hosts, were to be vanquished in war.

**6.93.3**  Then King Duryodhana addressed all those counsellors, speaking to the son of the charioteer and to the mighty son of Subala.

**6.93.4**  "Droṇa, Bhīṣma, Kṛpa, Śalya, and the son of Somadatta do not press the Pārthas hard in battle — and I do not know the reason for it.

**6.93.5**  Unslain themselves, the Pāṇḍavas waste away my force; and so, Karṇa, my strength is dwindling and my weapons are spent in the fight.

**6.93.6**  Worsted by the heroic Pāṇḍavas, who cannot be slain even by the gods, I am fallen into doubt: how shall I carry on the war?"

**6.93.7**  To him, O great king, the son of the charioteer answered the lord of men: "Grieve not, O best of the Bharatas; I shall do what pleases you.

**6.93.8**  Let Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, withdraw at once from the great battle; and once the son of Gaṅgā has retired from the fight and laid down his arms, O Bhārata,

**6.93.9**  I shall slay the Pārthas with all their kin and all the Somakas, before the very eyes of Bhīṣma in battle — this I swear to you by my truth, O king.

**6.93.10**  Bhīṣma, O king, ever shows compassion toward the Pāṇḍavas; and Bhīṣma is unable to conquer these great chariot-warriors in battle.

**6.93.11**  Bhīṣma is proud in war and ever loves the fray; how then, dear lord, shall he conquer the Pāṇḍavas when they stand arrayed in battle?

**6.93.12**  Do you, then, go swiftly hence to the camp of Bhīṣma, and having won his leave from the battle, make him lay down his arms, O Bhārata.

**6.93.13**  When Bhīṣma has laid down his arms, then behold the Pāṇḍavas slain — by me alone in battle, O king, with all their friends, their hosts, and their kinsmen."

**6.93.14**  Thus addressed by Karṇa, your son Duryodhana spoke this word there to his brother Duḥśāsana:

**6.93.15**  "See, Duḥśāsana, that all the retinue is made ready on every side; quickly arrange the whole of it."

**6.93.16**  Having spoken thus, O king, the lord of men said to Karṇa: "When I have won leave of Bhīṣma in the battle, that best of men,

**6.93.17**  I shall then come quickly to your side, O subduer of foes; and then, O tiger among men, you shall wage the war."

**6.93.18**  Then your son set forth at once, O lord of peoples, together with all his brothers, like Indra of the hundred sacrifices amid the gods.

**6.93.19**  Then his brother Duḥśāsana swiftly mounted that tiger among kings, whose valour matched a tiger's, upon his horse.

**6.93.20**  Wearing armlets, with crown bound on and bracelets upon his arms, the king, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, shone, O great king, like the great Indra.

**6.93.21**  Anointed with sandal-paste, fragrant and of priceless worth, glowing like the bhāṇḍī blossom and resembling refined gold,

**6.93.22**  clad in spotless raiment, moving with the sportive gait of a lion, the king shone bright and radiant, like the sun in autumn.

**6.93.23**  As that tiger among men went forth toward the camp of Bhīṣma, the mighty archers, the bowmen of all the world, followed him, and his brothers, great archers all, as the gods follow Vāsava.

**6.93.24**  Some mounted on horses, some on elephants, O Bhārata, and others, those best of men, on chariots, surrounded him on every side.

**6.93.25**  And his friends, weapons in hand, gathered together to guard the lord of earth, even as the immortals gather about Śakra in heaven.

**6.93.26**  Honoured by the Kurus, that great chariot-warrior of the Kauravas went on, O king, to the tent of the renowned son of Gaṅgā, the king followed all about by his brothers together.

**6.93.27**  That dexterous prince, raising at the fitting moment his own right arm — well-trained, like the trunk of an elephant, the crusher of every foe —

**6.93.28**  received the folded palms of men raised toward him from every quarter, and heard the sweet words of those who dwelt in diverse lands.

**6.93.29**  Praised by bards and minstrels, that man of great renown, lord over all the lords of the world, in turn paid honour to them all.

**6.93.30**  With golden lamps there, fed with fragrant oil and blazing on every side, they surrounded that high-souled one.

**6.93.31**  Encircled by those fair golden lamps, the king shone like the moon attended by the blazing great planets.

**6.93.32**  There the ushers in tunics and turbans, with staves and rattles in their hands, gently moved the throng aside on every quarter.

**6.93.33**  Then, having reached the fair dwelling of Bhīṣma, the king, that lord of men, alighted from his horse and came before Bhīṣma.

**6.93.34**  Having saluted Bhīṣma, he sat down upon an excellent seat, golden, fair on every side, spread with a priceless covering, and with folded palms, his throat choked with sobs and his eyes brimming with tears, he said to Bhīṣma:

**6.93.35**  "Relying upon you in the fray, O slayer of foes, we should dare to conquer in battle even the gods and demons together with Indra —

**6.93.36**  how much more the heroic sons of Pāṇḍu, with their friends, their hosts, and their kinsmen! Therefore, O son of Gaṅgā, you should show me your grace, O lord: slay the heroic sons of Pāṇḍu as the great Indra slew the Dānavas.

**6.93.37**  You said before, O mighty-armed one: 'I shall slay the Somakas and the Pāñcālas, together with the Pāṇḍavas and the Karūṣas,' so you spoke, O Bhārata.

**6.93.38**  Let that word of yours be made true: slay the Pārthas arrayed against us and the Somakas, those mighty archers — be true to your word, O Bhārata.

**6.93.39**  But if out of compassion, O king, or out of hatred toward me, my lord, or because of my own ill fortune, you spare the Pāṇḍavas,

**6.93.40**  then give leave in the battle to Karṇa, who is glorious in war; he will conquer in the fight the Pārthas with their friends, their hosts, and their kinsmen."

**6.93.41**  Having said only this much, the king, your son Duryodhana, spoke no further word to Bhīṣma of terrible prowess.

**6.94.1**  Saṃjaya said: Though pierced through by your son with those shafts of speech, the grandsire, filled with a great grief, yet spoke not even a trifle that was unkind.

**6.94.2**  For a long while he brooded, filled with grief and wrath, sighing like a serpent goaded with a pointed rod.

**6.94.3**  Rolling his eyes in anger, as if to burn up the world with its gods, demons, and gandharvas, O Bhārata, that best of those who know the worlds spoke this word to your son, beginning gently:

**6.94.4**  "Why, Duryodhana, do you thus pierce me with shafts of speech, when I am striving to the best of my power and doing what pleases you, offering up my very life in battle out of desire for your good?

**6.94.5**  When the heroic Pāṇḍava, at the Khāṇḍava forest, sated the fire, having vanquished Śakra in battle — that should be proof enough.

**6.94.6**  And when you, O mighty-armed one, were carried off by the gandharvas by force, the son of Pāṇḍu set you free — that should be proof enough.

**6.94.7**  When the heroes fled — your own brothers, O lord, and the son of the charioteer, Rādheya, with them — that should be proof enough.

**6.94.8**  And when, in the city of Virāṭa, against us all gathered together he rose up single-handed — that should be proof enough.

**6.94.9**  Having overpowered in that battle Droṇa enraged in the fight, and me, and Karṇa, and you, and the son of Droṇa, and Kṛpa, that mighty chariot-warrior, he carried off our garments — that should be proof enough.

**6.94.10**  The Nivātakavacas, hard to conquer in battle even by Vāsava, the Pārtha vanquished in the fray — that should be proof enough.

**6.94.11**  For who is able to conquer in battle that Pāṇḍava, impetuous in war? But you, in your delusion, do not know, Suyodhana, what should and should not be said."

**6.94.12**  For a man about to die sees all the trees as turned to gold; even so do you, O son of Gāndhārī, behold all things inverted.

**6.94.13**  Having yourself kindled this great enmity with the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas, fight them now in battle. Let us watch—be a man!

**6.94.14**  As for me, O tiger among men, I shall slay all the Somakas and the Pāñcālas who are gathered here, sparing only Śikhaṇḍin.

**6.94.15**  Either slain by them in battle I shall go to the abode of Yama, or, having slain them in the fray, I shall give you joy.

**6.94.16**  For Śikhaṇḍin was born a woman at first within the royal house; by the boon she became a man—yet she is still that woman, Śikhaṇḍinī.

**6.94.17**  Her I shall not slay, O Bhārata, even at the cost of my own life; she whom the Creator first fashioned thus is verily that woman, Śikhaṇḍinī.

**6.94.18**  Sleep at ease, O son of Gāndhārī; tomorrow I shall wage such a great battle as men will recount for as long as the earth endures.

**6.94.19**  Thus addressed, O lord of men, your son departed; bowing his head in reverence to the elder, he went away to his own dwelling.

**6.94.20**  Having come there, the king dismissed the great throng, and that bringer of doom to his foes swiftly entered his lodging; and so entered, the prince passed that night away.

**6.95.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the night had brightened into dawn, the king rose at morning and commanded the kings, "Array the army! Today Bhīṣma, wrathful in battle, shall slay the Somakas."

**6.95.2**  Bhīṣma, hearing all of Duryodhana's lament uttered in the night, O king, regarded it as a rebuke directed against himself.

**6.95.3**  Falling into utter despondency, censuring the slander of others, the son of Śaṃtanu brooded long, eager to do battle with Arjuna in the field.

**6.95.4**  Discerning by signs what the son of Gaṅgā was pondering, Duryodhana, O great king, urged on Duḥśāsana.

**6.95.5**  "Duḥśāsana, let the chariots that guard Bhīṣma be yoked at once, and urge forward all the two-and-thirty divisions."

**6.95.6**  "For now is at hand that which we have pondered through many years: the slaughter of the Pāṇḍavas with their hosts, and the winning of the kingdom."

**6.95.7**  "In this I deem the protection of Bhīṣma to be the chief task; guarded by us he would bring us comfort, and would slay the sons of Pṛthā in the fray."

**6.95.8**  "And that pure-souled one declared, 'I will not slay Śikhaṇḍin, for he was born a woman before; therefore he must be spared by me in battle.'"

**6.95.9**  "'The world knows this—that I, O mighty-armed one, to do my father's pleasure, of old renounced a flourishing kingdom and the company of women.'"

**6.95.10**  "'Never in any wise would I slay a woman, nor one who was a woman before, in battle, O best of men; this truth I tell you.'"

**6.95.11**  "'This Śikhaṇḍin, O king, was a woman before, if you have heard the tale told in the Udyoga—even so was Śikhaṇḍinī born.'"

**6.95.12**  "'Having been a maiden, she was born a man, and he shall fight, O Bhārata; yet against his face I would in no way loose my arrows.'"

**6.95.13**  "'But all the other Kṣatriyas, dear one, who seek the Pāṇḍavas' victory and come within range of my arrows, I shall slay in battle.'"

**6.95.14**  "Thus did the son of Gaṅgā, best of the Bharatas, knower of the scriptures, speak to me; therefore I deem the guarding of Bhīṣma to be our whole concern."

**6.95.15**  "For a wolf, if the lion be left unguarded, might slay the lion in the great forest; let us not, as it were by a wolf, let the tiger be slain by Śikhaṇḍin."

**6.95.16**  "Let my uncle Śakuni, and Śalya, Kṛpa, Droṇa, and Viviṃśati, vigilant, guard the son of Gaṅgā; when he is protected, victory is sure."

**6.95.17**  Hearing this speech of Duryodhana, the kings then surrounded the son of Gaṅgā on every side with a host of chariots.

**6.95.18**  And your sons, encircling the son of Gaṅgā, advanced with joy, making the earth and sky to tremble and throwing the Pāṇḍavas into confusion.

**6.95.19**  With those well-yoked chariots and with elephants, the great warriors, mailed for battle, encircled Bhīṣma and took their stand.

**6.95.20**  As in the war of gods and demons the thirty surrounded the Wielder of the Thunderbolt, so did they all stand guarding that mighty warrior.

**6.95.21**  Then king Duryodhana spoke again to his brother: "Yudhāmanyu guards Arjuna's left wheel and Uttamaujas his right; these two are Arjuna's protectors, and Arjuna in turn is Śikhaṇḍin's."

**6.95.22**  "Though guarded by Pārtha and thus shunned by us, see to it, O Duḥśāsana, that he—Śikhaṇḍin—does not slay our Bhīṣma."

**6.95.23**  Hearing his brother's words, your son Duḥśāsana, placing Bhīṣma at the forefront, advanced together with the army.

**6.95.24**  But Arjuna, foremost of chariot-warriors, seeing Bhīṣma there encircled by that host of chariots, spoke thus to Dhṛṣṭadyumna:

**6.95.25**  "O tiger among men, O sinless prince of the Pāñcālas, station Śikhaṇḍin this day at the very front, facing Bhīṣma; and I shall be his guardian."

**6.95.26**  Then Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, sallied forth with the army, and arrayed for the battle a great formation, the Sarvatobhadra, facing on every side.

**6.95.27**  Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman, and the great warrior of the Śaibyas, Śakuni and the lord of the Sindhus, and Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja—

**6.95.28**  all these, together with Bhīṣma and your sons, O Bhārata, took their stand in front of all the troops, at the head of the formation.

**6.95.29**  Droṇa, Bhūriśravas, Śalya, and Bhagadatta, O venerable one, mailed for war, took post upon the right wing of the formation.

**6.95.30**  Aśvatthāman, Somadatta, and the two great warriors of Avantī, joined by a mighty host, held the left wing.

**6.95.31**  Duryodhana, O great king, surrounded on all sides by the Trigartas, stood in the midst of the formation, facing the Pāṇḍavas, O Bhārata.

**6.95.32**  Alambusa, foremost of chariot-warriors, and the great warrior Śrutāyus, both clad in mail, took their stand at the rear of all the troops, behind the formation.

**6.95.33**  Having thus drawn up their array, O Bhārata, your warriors, fully armed, appeared like blazing fires.

**6.95.34**  In like manner king Yudhiṣṭhira and the Pāṇḍava Bhīmasena, and Nakula and Sahadeva, the two sons of Mādrī, mailed for war, took their stand in front of all the troops, at the head of the formation.

**6.95.35**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Virāṭa, and the great warrior Sātyaki, destroyers of the enemy's ranks, stood with a mighty host.

**6.95.36**  Śikhaṇḍin and Vijaya, and the Rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca, the mighty-armed Cekitāna and the valiant Kuntibhoja, took their stand in the battle, O great king, encompassed by a mighty host.

**6.95.37**  Abhimanyu the great archer, and the mighty warrior Drupada, and the five Kekaya brothers, stood arrayed in mail for battle.

**6.95.38**  Thus too did the heroic Pāṇḍavas, having drawn up against them their great and well-nigh invincible array, stand ready for battle, O venerable one.

**6.95.39**  And your kings, vigilant in the field, together with their hosts, advanced against the sons of Pṛthā in battle, placing Bhīṣma at their head, O king.

**6.95.40**  In like manner the Pāṇḍavas, O king, with Bhīmasena at their front, eager for victory in the fray, advanced against Bhīṣma, who longed to do battle.

**6.95.41**  Sounding their war-cries and shrill clamors, their krakacas and cow-horns, their kettledrums, tabors, and tom-toms, and their drum-faces, the Pāṇḍavas rushed forward, raising terrible roars.

**6.95.42**  With the din of kettledrums, tabors, conches, and great war-drums, with lion-roars raised on high and prancings of many kinds—

**6.95.43**  we, answering them with our own clamor, advanced upon them in haste, all at once and full of wrath; and there arose a great tumult.

**6.95.44**  Then, rushing upon one another, they fell to the clash of arms; and at that mighty roar the earth trembled.

**6.95.45**  The birds wheeled about, crying out most dreadfully; and the sun, though risen in splendor, grew dim of light.

**6.95.46**  Tumultuous winds blew, foretelling some most terrible fear; and there the jackals, dread and dread-howling, set up their cries, O great king, announcing that a great carnage was come.

**6.95.47**  The quarters of the sky blazed forth, O king, and a rain of dust fell down, and likewise a rain of bones mingled with blood.

**6.95.48**  From the eyes of the weeping beasts of burden water fell, and they let flow their dung and urine, sunk in brooding, O lord of peoples.

**6.95.49**  Unseen, O bull of the Bharatas, mighty cries were heard—the terrible roars of man-devouring Rākṣasas as they bellowed.

**6.95.50**  Jackals, herons, and crows were seen flocking together; and dogs, barking with cries of many sorts, stood there about.

**6.95.51**  And great blazing meteors, striking against the sun, fell suddenly upon the earth, announcing some great terror.
