**6.95.52**  At that great uprising, in the meeting of the Pāṇḍavas and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the mighty armies shone forth with the blare of conches and tabors, like forests set atremble by the wind.

**6.95.53**  In that ill-boding hour, as the hosts—thronged with kings, elephants, and horses—came on, there arose a tumultuous roar of the armies, like that of oceans uplifted by the wind.

**6.96.1**  Saṃjaya said: Abhimanyu, splendid upon his chariot, with his excellent tawny steeds, that fiery one, charged against the great host of Duryodhana, scattering showers of arrows as a raincloud scatters torrents of water.

**6.96.2**  Your warriors, the bulls among the Kurus, could not even check in the battle the wrathful son of Subhadrā, that slayer of foes, as with his flood of weapons he plunged into the unfathomable ocean of the army.

**6.96.3**  The shafts loosed by him in the battle, O king, crushers of foes, led heroic Kṣatriyas to the dwelling of the king of the dead.

**6.96.4**  Arrows dreadful as the rod of Yama, like blazing venomous serpents, did the son of Subhadrā, wrathful in the fray, send forth.

**6.96.5**  The chariot-warrior from his car, the horseman from his horse's back, and the elephant-riders together with their elephants—all these did the son of Phalguna swiftly bring down.

**6.96.6**  As he wrought that great and wondrous deed in battle, the kings, rejoicing, did him honor and extolled the son of Phalguna.

**6.96.7**  Routing those ranks, the son of Subhadrā shone exceedingly, like the wind that scatters a heap of cotton in every direction.

**6.96.8**  Driven in rout by him, your troops, O Bhārata, found no protector, like elephants sunk fast in the mire.

**6.96.9**  Having routed all your troops, that best of men, Abhimanyu, stood, O king, blazing like a smokeless fire.

**6.96.10**  And all your warriors could no more endure that slayer of foes than moths, driven by their doom, can endure a blazing fire.

**6.96.11**  Striking down all his foes, that great archer and mighty warrior of the Pāṇḍavas appeared like the Thunderbolt-bearer with his thunderbolt in hand.

**6.96.12**  His gold-backed bow was seen, as he ranged through the quarters, gleaming like flashes of lightning amid the rainclouds, O king.

**6.96.13**  And his sharp, keen-whetted shafts darted forth in the battle, O king, like swarms of bees from a forest of trees in full bloom.

**6.96.14**  As that high-souled son of Subhadrā ranged about thus, upon his thunder-roaring chariot, men could discern no interval in his action.

**6.96.15**  Bewildering Kṛpa and Droṇa, and Droṇa's son, and Bṛhadbala, and the great archer who was lord of the Sindhus, he ranged about lightly and well.

**6.96.16**  We beheld his bow drawn ever into a circle, O venerable one, resembling the orb of the sun, as he scorched your host.

**6.96.17**  Beholding him scorching all with the flames of his arrows, the heroic Kṣatriyas, from his deeds, deemed this world to have two Phalgunas.

**6.96.18**  Tormented by him, O great king, that great army of the Bharatas reeled this way and that, like a woman in the grip of drink.

**6.96.19**  Having routed that army and shaken its great chariot-warriors, he gladdened his friends, as Vāsava once did when he conquered Maya.

**6.96.20**  Driven before him in the battle, your forces raised a dreadful cry of anguish, like the roar of a thundercloud.

**6.96.21**  Hearing that terrible uproar of your army, my lord — like the sea swollen by the wind's force at the time of the full moon — King Duryodhana then spoke to the son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga.

**6.96.22**  This son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, a mighty archer, a second Phalguna as it were, routs our host in his fury, as Vṛtra once routed the army of the gods.

**6.96.23**  I see no other great remedy for him in this battle save you, O best of rākṣasas, master of all the arts.

**6.96.24**  Go swiftly, then, and slay this hero, the son of Subhadrā, in the fray; and we, with Bhīṣma and Droṇa at our head, shall destroy the Pārthas.

**6.96.25**  Thus addressed, that mighty and majestic lord of the rākṣasas advanced swiftly into the fight at your son's command, roaring a great roar like a stormcloud in the rains.

**6.96.26**  At his mighty cry, O king, the great host of the Pāṇḍavas was set trembling on every side, like the ocean swelling to the full.

**6.96.27**  And many men, O king, terrified by that roar, gave up their dear lives and fell to the ground.

**6.96.28**  And the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, filled with joy, his bow grasped tight, as though dancing upon his chariot-platform, rushed upon that rākṣasa.

**6.96.29**  Then that enraged rākṣasa, on reaching the son of Arjuna in the battle and taking his stand not far from him, set his host to flight.

**6.96.30**  That great host of the Pāṇḍavas, slaughtered in the battle, advanced against the rākṣasa in the fray, as the army of the gods once marched against Bali.

**6.96.31**  A great carnage befell that army, my lord, as it was slaughtered in the battle by the rākṣasa of dreadful form.

**6.96.32**  Then with thousands of shafts the rākṣasa routed that great host of the Pāṇḍavas in the battle, displaying his prowess.

**6.96.33**  And so the army of the Pāṇḍavas, slaughtered by the rākṣasa of dreadful form, fled the battle in fear.

**6.96.34**  Having crushed that army, as an elephant crushes a bed of lotuses, he then rushed in the battle upon the mighty sons of Draupadī.

**6.96.35**  But those sons of Draupadī, enraged, mighty archers and fierce strikers, all charged the rākṣasa, as the five planets bear down upon the sun.

**6.96.36**  Hard-pressed by those valiant warriors, that best of rākṣasas was like the moon beset by five planets at the dread ending of an age.

**6.96.37**  Then the mighty Prativindhya swiftly pierced the rākṣasa with whetted arrows, all of iron and unblunted at the point.

**6.96.38**  His armor cleft by these, that best of rākṣasas shone splendid, like a great cloud shot through with the rays of the sun.

**6.96.39**  With the gold-shafted arrows clinging to him, the son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga shone, O king, like a mountain with blazing peaks.

**6.96.40**  Then those five brothers pierced the lord of the rākṣasas in that great fray with whetted arrows adorned with gold.

**6.96.41**  Pierced by those dreadful arrows, as if by enraged serpents, Alambusa, O king, grew fiercely wrathful, like a king of elephants.

**6.96.42**  Sorely pierced, O great king, and hard-pressed by those great chariot-warriors, for a moment, my lord, he sank into a long darkness.

**6.96.43**  Then, regaining his senses and redoubled in his fury, he cut down with his arrows their banners and their bows.

**6.96.44**  And smiling, as it were, Alambusa, that great chariot-warrior, struck each of them with three arrows, as though dancing upon his chariot-platform.

**6.96.45**  And in haste and full of wrath, the mighty rākṣasa, enraged, slew the horses of those great-souled warriors and their charioteers as well.

**6.96.46**  And exulting greatly, he pierced them again with well-whetted arrows of many shapes, by hundreds and by thousands.

**6.96.47**  Having stripped those mighty archers of their chariots, the rākṣasa, that ranger of the night, rushed upon them with speed, eager to slay.

**6.96.48**  Seeing them tormented in the battle by that evil-souled rākṣasa, the son of Arjuna rushed upon the rākṣasa in the fray.

**6.96.49**  Between the two there arose a combat like that of Vṛtra and Vāsava; and all your men and all the Pāṇḍava chariot-warriors looked on.

**6.96.50**  Those two, met in the great combat and blazing with wrath against each other, mighty warriors both, O great king, with eyes red with rage, eyed one another in the fight, like the very fire that ends an age.

**6.96.51**  Dreadful and bitter in its outcome was the encounter between the two, as in the war of gods and asuras was that of Śakra and Śambara.

**6.97.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How, O Saṃjaya, did Alambusa give battle to the valiant son of Arjuna, that great chariot-warrior, as he was striking down our men in the combat?

**6.97.2**  And how did the son of Subhadrā, slayer of enemy heroes, fight the son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga? Tell me truly all that befell in the battle.

**6.97.3**  And what did Dhanaṃjaya do among my forces, O Saṃjaya, or Bhīma, best of the strong, or the rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca?

**6.97.4**  Or Nakula, or Sahadeva, or the great chariot-warrior Sātyaki? Tell me all this, for you are skilled in the telling, O Saṃjaya.

**6.97.5**  Saṃjaya said: Come then, I shall recount to you that hair-raising combat, my lord, even as it passed between the lord of rākṣasas and the son of Subhadrā.

**6.97.6**  And how Arjuna in the fray, and Bhīmasena the Pāṇḍava, and Nakula and Sahadeva, displayed their prowess in the battle.

**6.97.7**  And likewise how all your men, with Bhīṣma and Droṇa at their head, performed wondrous and varied feats without fear.

**6.97.8**  Now Alambusa, in the battle, roaring a mighty roar and threatening Abhimanyu, that great chariot-warrior, again and again, rushed upon him with speed and cried out, 'Stand, stand!'

**6.97.9**  And the son of Subhadrā too, O king, roaring again and again like a lion in the battle, faced the son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga, that mighty archer, the bitter foe of his father.

**6.97.10**  Then the man and the rākṣasa, those two foremost of chariot-warriors, met swiftly in the fray upon their cars, like a god and a dānava — the best of rākṣasas, master of illusion, and the son of Phalguna, knower of celestial weapons.

**6.97.11**  Then the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, O great king, having pierced the son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga with three whetted shafts in the battle, struck him again with five.

**6.97.12**  And Alambusa too, full of wrath, swiftly pierced the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister in the breast with nine swift arrows, as one goads a great elephant with hooks.

**6.97.13**  Then, swift in action, that ranger of the night tormented the son of Arjuna in the combat with a thousand shafts, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.97.14**  Then Abhimanyu, enraged, loosed ninety whetted arrows with smooth-bent joints into the broad chest of the rākṣasa.

**6.97.15**  Swiftly they entered his body, piercing it to the vital parts; and with all his limbs thus pierced, that best of rākṣasas shone, O king, like a mountain strewn with kiṃśuka trees in bloom.

**6.97.16**  Bearing those gold-feathered arrows, that mighty best of rākṣasas blazed forth like a mountain wreathed in flame.

**6.97.17**  Then, enraged, O great king, the mighty son of Ṛśyaśṛṅga covered the son of Kṛṣṇa's sister, who was the equal of great Indra, with feathered shafts.

**6.97.18**  The keen arrows loosed by him, like the rod of Yama, pierced clean through Abhimanyu and entered the surface of the earth.

**6.97.19**  And likewise the gold-adorned arrows loosed by the son of Arjuna pierced clean through Alambusa and entered the surface of the earth.

**6.97.20**  But the son of Subhadrā, closing with the rākṣasa in the battle, with smooth-jointed arrows turned him from the fight, as Śakra once turned Maya in combat.

**6.97.21**  And the rākṣasa, turned from the fight and slaughtered in the battle by his foe — that scorcher of enemies — then conjured up a great illusion of darkness.

**6.97.22**  Then all upon that field were swallowed up in the darkness; they could see neither Abhimanyu, nor their own, nor the enemy in the battle.

**6.97.23**  And Abhimanyu, beholding that vast and dreadful darkness, that joy of the Kurus brought forth the most terrible weapon of the sun.

**6.97.24**  Then all the world grew bright, O lord of the earth, and he destroyed that illusion of the evil-souled rākṣasa.

**6.97.25**  And full of wrath, that best of men of mighty valor covered the lord of the rākṣasas in the battle with smooth-jointed arrows.

**6.97.26**  And many other illusions wrought by that rākṣasa the son of Phalguna, knower of all weapons and of measureless spirit, turned aside.

**6.97.27**  Then the rākṣasa, his illusions undone and himself smitten with arrows, abandoned his chariot on the very spot and fled in great fear.

**6.97.28**  When that rākṣasa, that fighter by trickery, had been swiftly vanquished, the son of Arjuna crushed your army in the battle, as a wild king of elephants, blind with rut, crushes a lotus-pond together with its lotuses.

**6.97.29**  Then Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, seeing the army routed, surrounded the son of Subhadrā with a great array of chariots.

**6.97.30**  Penning in that hero, the great chariot-warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's party, many against one, hewed at him hard with their arrows in the fight.

**6.97.31**  Yet that hero among the chariot-warriors, equal in prowess to his father, was the peer of Vāsudeva in valor and in might.

**6.97.32**  Deeds worthy of both his father and his maternal uncle, in many forms, did he perform in the battle, that best of all who bear arms.

**6.97.33**  Then Dhanaṃjaya, O king, striking down your warriors, came upon Bhīṣma in the battle, unable to bear it and longing to reach his son.

**6.97.34**  And likewise in the battle, O king, your father Devavrata came upon Pārtha in the fray, as Svarbhānu comes upon the sun.

**6.97.35**  Then your sons, O lord of peoples, with their chariots, elephants, and horses, surrounded Bhīṣma in the battle and guarded him on every side.

**6.97.36**  And likewise the Pāṇḍavas, O king, surrounding Dhanaṃjaya, stood armored and arrayed for the great battle, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.97.37**  Then the son of Śaradvat, O king, covered Arjuna, who stood facing Bhīṣma, with five and twenty arrows.

**6.97.38**  Coming up against him, Sātyaki pierced him with keen arrows, for the sake of pleasing the Pāṇḍavas, as a tiger falls upon an elephant.

**6.97.39**  And the son of Gautama too, in haste and full of wrath, pierced the Mādhava in the breast with nine arrows fledged with heron feathers.

**6.97.40**  Then the grandson of Śini, that great chariot-warrior, sorely pierced and enraged, took up a dreadful arrow meant to make an end of Gautama's son.

**6.97.41**  As it sped toward him, gleaming like the thunderbolt of Śakra, the son of Droṇa, exceedingly wrathful, cut it in two.

**6.97.42**  Then the grandson of Śini, leaving the son of Gautama, that best of chariot-warriors, rushed upon the son of Droṇa in the battle, as Rāhu rushes upon the moon in the sky.

**6.97.43**  The son of Droṇa cut his bow in two, O descendant of Bharata, and then, his bow severed, struck him with arrows.

**6.97.44**  Taking up another bow, foe-slaying and equal to its burden, he struck the son of Droṇa, O great king, with sixty shafts upon the arms and breast.

**6.97.45**  Pierced and shaken, and for a moment overcome with faintness, he sank down upon his chariot-platform, leaning against the staff of his banner.

**6.97.46**  Then, regaining his senses, the majestic son of Droṇa, full of wrath, smote the descendant of the Vṛṣṇis in the battle with an iron shaft.

**6.97.47**  Piercing clean through the grandson of Śini, it entered the surface of the earth, as a young serpent in the springtime enters strongly into its hole.

**6.97.48**  Then with another broad-headed shaft the son of Droṇa cut down Mādhava's lofty banner in the battle, and roared aloud the lion's roar.

**6.97.49**  And once more, O Bhārata, he covered him over with dreadful arrows, as at the end of the hot season a cloud veils the sun, O great king.

**6.97.50**  But Sātyaki, O great king, struck down that net of arrows and swiftly fell upon the son of Droṇa with manifold nets of arrows in turn.

**6.97.51**  And Śaineya, slayer of enemy heroes, scorched the son of Droṇa as the sun scorches when freed from a covering mass of clouds.

**6.97.52**  And as he rose up again, the mighty Sātyaki covered him over with a thousand arrows, and let forth a roar.

**6.97.53**  Seeing his son thus seized, as the moon is seized by Rāhu, the valiant son of Bharadvāja rushed against Śaineya.

**6.97.54**  And in that great fray he pierced him with a keenly sharpened shaft, longing, O king, to rescue his own son who was being scorched by the Vārṣṇeya.

**6.97.55**  But Sātyaki, having vanquished in battle the preceptor's son, that great car-warrior, pierced Droṇa with twenty arrows all made wholly of iron.

**6.97.56**  In that interval the son of Kuntī of immeasurable spirit, he of the white steeds, that great car-warrior, rushed in fury against Droṇa in the battle.

**6.97.57**  Then Droṇa and Pārtha closed together in the great fray, even as Budha and Śukra meet, O great king, in the vault of heaven.

**6.98.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How did Droṇa the mighty archer and the Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya, those two heroes, encounter each other in battle? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.98.2**  For the Pāṇḍava is ever dear to the wise son of Bharadvāja, and the preceptor too is ever dear to Pārtha in battle, Saṃjaya.

**6.98.3**  Those two car-warriors, fierce in the field like two haughty lions—how did the son of Bharadvāja and Dhanaṃjaya come together in the fight?

**6.98.4**  Saṃjaya said: In battle Droṇa did not regard Pārtha as one dear to himself, nor did Pārtha so regard his teacher in the fray, each setting the warrior's duty foremost.

**6.98.5**  Warriors, O king, do not spare one another in battle; they fight without restraint even against fathers and brothers.

**6.98.6**  In the battle, O Bhārata, Droṇa was pierced by Pārtha with three arrows, yet he gave no thought to those shafts loosed from Pārtha's bow in the fight.

**6.98.7**  Again Pārtha covered him over with a rain of arrows in the battle, and Droṇa blazed up with wrath like a fire risen in a thicket.

**6.98.8**  Thereupon, O lord of kings, Droṇa checked Arjuna in the battle with smooth-jointed arrows, and that almost at once, O Bhārata.

**6.98.9**  Then King Duryodhana urged on Suśarman, O king, that he might guard Droṇa's rear in the battle.

**6.98.10**  And the king of the Trigartas in wrath, drawing his bow hard, covered Pārtha over in the battle with iron-faced arrows.

**6.98.11**  The arrows loosed by those two, O king, shone in the mid-air like wild geese, O great king, in the autumn sky.

**6.98.12**  Reaching the son of Kuntī, those arrows all entered him, O lord, as birds settle upon a sweet-fruited tree bowed down with the weight of its fruit.

**6.98.13**  But Arjuna, foremost of car-warriors, raising a war-cry in the battle, pierced the king of the Trigartas together with his sons with his arrows.

**6.98.14**  Smitten by Pārtha as if by Time at the dissolution of an age, and resolved upon death, they turned upon Pārtha himself and loosed a rain of arrows against the Pāṇḍava's chariot.

**6.98.15**  That rain of arrows the Pāṇḍava then received with a downpour of his own arrows, O lord of kings, as a mountain receives a downpour of water.

**6.98.16**  There we beheld a marvel, the lightness of Bībhatsu's hand, as he met that irresistible rain of weapons loosed by many heroes.

**6.98.17**  For alone he warded it off, as the wind scatters the masses of clouds; at that feat of Pārtha the gods and dānavas rejoiced.

**6.98.18**  Then in wrath, O Bhārata, Pārtha loosed against the Trigartas in the battle the weapon of Vāyu, O great king, at the head of the host.

**6.98.19**  Thereupon a wind arose, churning the vault of heaven, felling clusters of trees and smiting down the soldiers.

**6.98.20**  Then Droṇa, on beholding that most dreadful weapon of Vāyu, loosed in answer another terrible weapon, the weapon of the mountain, O great king.

**6.98.21**  When that weapon had been loosed by Droṇa in the great fray, the wind was stilled, and the quarters of the sky grew clear.

**6.98.22**  Then the heroic son of Pāṇḍu made the ranks of the Trigarta cars dispirited in the battle, turned away in flight and stripped of their valor.

**6.98.23**  Then King Duryodhana, and Kṛpa the foremost of car-warriors, and Aśvatthāman, and Śalya, and Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja,

**6.98.24**  and Vinda and Anuvinda of Avantī, and Bāhlika with his Bāhlika host, blocked off the quarters around Pārtha with a great array of chariots.

**6.98.25**  In the same way Bhagadatta and the mighty Śrutāyus, those two, with their host of elephants blocked off the quarters around Bhīma.

**6.98.26**  Bhūriśravas and Śala, and the son of Subala, O lord of peoples, swiftly hemmed in the two sons of Mādrī with manifold floods of arrows.

**6.98.27**  But Bhīṣma, joined by all the soldiers of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, closed in upon Yudhiṣṭhira and surrounded him on every side.

**6.98.28**  Seeing the host of elephants bearing down upon him, the heroic Vṛkodara, son of Pṛthā, licked the corners of his mouth like the king of beasts in the forest.

**6.98.29**  Then that foremost of car-warriors, taking up his mace in the great battle, leaped down swiftly from his chariot and struck terror into your army.

**6.98.30**  Beholding him mace in hand, those elephant-riders, intent on the fight, surrounded Bhīmasena on every side in the battle.

**6.98.31**  And the Pāṇḍava, come into the midst of the elephants, shone forth like the sun set in the midst of a great mass of clouds.

**6.98.32**  That bull among the Pāṇḍavas scattered the host of elephants with his mace, as the wind scatters a vast unbroken expanse of mighty clouds.

**6.98.33**  Those tusked beasts, slaughtered by the mighty Bhīmasena, raised cries of anguish in the battle, roaring like rain-clouds.

**6.98.34**  Gashed in many places there by the tusks of the elephants, the son of Pṛthā, resembling a blossoming aśoka tree, shone splendid at the head of the battle.

**6.98.35**  Seizing a tusker by the tusk, he wrenched that tusk away, and with that very tusk smiting the elephant upon its frontal globe, he struck it down in the battle like Death wielding his rod.

**6.98.36**  Bearing his blood-smeared mace, his hue made of fat and marrow, with armlets fashioned of gore, he appeared like Rudra to behold.

**6.98.37**  Thus slaughtered, the great elephants that survived the slaughter fled in all directions, O king, trampling their own forces underfoot.

**6.98.38**  By those great elephants fleeing on every side, O bull of the Bharatas, the whole army of Duryodhana was once again turned to flight.

**6.99.1**  Saṃjaya said: At midday, O great king, there arose a fierce battle, destroyer of the world, between Bhīṣma and the Somakas.

**6.99.2**  The son of Gaṅgā, foremost of car-warriors, scattered the host of the Pāṇḍavas with his whetted arrows by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**6.99.3**  And your father Devavrata crushed that army as herds of cattle crush a heap of reaped grain.

**6.99.4**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍin, and Virāṭa and Drupada, closing with Bhīṣma in the battle, smote that great car-warrior with their arrows.

**6.99.5**  Then, having pierced Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Virāṭa with three arrows, he sped an iron shaft at Drupada, O Bhārata.

**6.99.6**  Pierced by Bhīṣma, the tormentor of foes, those mighty archers blazed with wrath in the battle, O king, like serpents trodden underfoot.

**6.99.7**  Śikhaṇḍin pierced that grandsire of the Bharatas; but the steadfast one, reflecting in his mind that this was one born of woman, struck no blow against him.

**6.99.8**  But Dhṛṣṭadyumna, blazing like fire with wrath in the battle, planted three arrows in the grandsire's arms and breast.

**6.99.9**  Drupada with twenty-five, Virāṭa with ten arrows, and Śikhaṇḍin with twenty-five shafts pierced Bhīṣma.

**6.99.10**  Sorely pierced, O great king, by those high-souled warriors in the field, Bhīṣma shone like a red aśoka in spring, dappled with blossoms.

**6.99.11**  The son of Gaṅgā pierced them in return, each with three straight-flying shafts, and with a broad-headed arrow he cut Drupada's bow, O honored one.

**6.99.12**  Taking up another bow, he pierced Bhīṣma with five arrows and his charioteer with three well-whetted shafts at the head of the battle.

**6.99.13**  Then Bhīma, O great king, and the five sons of Draupadī, and the five Kekaya brothers, and Sātyaki of the Sātvata line,

**6.99.14**  rushed against the son of Gaṅgā, seeking Yudhiṣṭhira's welfare and wishing to protect the Pāñcāla Dhṛṣṭadyumna who led them in the battle.

**6.99.15**  And in like manner all your warriors, set upon guarding Bhīṣma, advanced with their hosts against the Pāṇḍava army, O ruler of men.

**6.99.16**  There was a vast and confused battle between your men and theirs, of men and horses and chariots and elephants, swelling the realm of Yama.

**6.99.17**  A car-warrior, closing with a car-warrior, sent him to the abode of Yama; so too with others, the riders of men and elephants and horses, closing in,

**6.99.18**  led them to the other world with smooth-jointed arrows and with manifold dreadful weapons, here and there, O lord of peoples.

**6.99.19**  Chariots bereft of their warriors and with their charioteers slain, their horses bolting away, ran in all directions over the battlefield.

**6.99.20**  Crushing men and very many horses in the battle, O king, and rushing like the wind, they were seen resembling the cities of the gandharvas.

**6.99.21**  Car-warriors bereft of their chariots, clad in armor and endowed with splendor, all wearing earrings and turbans and adorned with golden chains and armlets,

**6.99.22**  in form like the sons of the gods, in valor in battle like Śakra, surpassing Vaiśravaṇa in wealth and Bṛhaspati in statecraft,

**6.99.23**  those lords of all the world, those heroes, were seen fleeing here and there, O lord of peoples, like common men.

**6.99.24**  And the elephants, O best of men, deprived of their excellent riders, trampling their own ranks, rushed about, hurrying toward every sound.

**6.99.25**  With their armor and yak-tail fans and parasols and banners, O honored one, with their girths and goads and bells and lances likewise,

**6.99.26**  all torn and shattered, they were seen running through the ten directions, those elephants like mountains or clouds, their cries like the rumble of a rising rain-cloud.

**6.99.27**  In the same way we beheld elephant-riders bereft of their tuskers running about, O lord of peoples, in the confusion of your men and theirs.

**6.99.28**  And horses arisen from many lands, decked with gold, rushing like the wind, I saw by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**6.99.29**  Horsemen whose mounts were slain, grasping their swords, we saw on every side fleeing and being driven in flight in the battle.

**6.99.30**  An elephant, charging upon a fleeing elephant in the great battle, rushed on, crushing in its course the foot-soldiers and the horses.

**6.99.31**  And likewise an elephant, O king, crushed chariots in the battle; and a chariot too, bearing down upon foot-soldier and horse alike,

**6.99.32**  crushed horses and men in the battle, O king. Thus did they, O king, in manifold ways trample one another.

**6.99.33**  While that savage and terrifying battle raged on, there arose a ghastly river whose waves were blood and entrails.

**6.99.34**  Heaps of bones were its rafts, hair its moss and grass; chariots were its pools, arrows its whirlpools, horses its fish—an unfordable stream.

**6.99.35**  Strewn with severed heads for stones, teeming with elephants for crocodiles, foaming with armor and turbans, with bows for islands and swords for turtles.

**6.99.36**  Rich with banners and standards for trees, sweeping away the banks of men, thronged with packs of carrion-eaters, swelling the realm of Yama.

**6.99.37**  That river, O king, the brave warriors crossed in their multitudes by rafts of horses, elephants, and chariots, casting off fear in the great battle.

**6.99.38**  It bore away the cowards in the fray, overcome with stupefaction, as the Vaitaraṇī bears the dead toward the city of the king of the dead.

**6.99.39**  Beholding that great slaughter, the warriors there cried aloud: "Through Duryodhana's offense the Kauravas are going to ruin!"

**6.99.40**  "How could that lord of men, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, evil of soul and deluded by greed, conceive such hatred for the virtuous sons of Pāṇḍu?"

**6.99.41**  Words of many kinds such as these were heard there, O Bhārata—praising the Pāṇḍavas and most harsh toward your sons.

**6.99.42**  Hearing those words then uttered by all the warriors, your son Duryodhana—the offender against the whole world—

**6.99.43**  spoke to Bhīṣma, Droṇa, Kṛpa, and Śalya, O Bhārata: "Fight without arrogance! Why do you delay so long?"

**6.99.44**  Then, O king, there broke out between the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas that most dreadful carnage, born of the game of dice.

**6.99.45**  That which you would not restrain of old, though warned by the great-souled ones—O son of Vicitravīrya, behold now its fruit, just such as this.

**6.99.46**  For neither the sons of Pāṇḍu with their armies and followers, nor the Kauravas, O lord of peoples, spared their own lives in that battle.

**6.99.47**  From this cause, O tiger among men, that terrible destruction of men came to pass—whether by fate, O king, or by your own misrule.

**6.100.1**  Saṃjaya said: But Arjuna, O tiger among men, with his sharpened arrows led the kings headed by Suśarman to the abode of the king of the dead.

**6.100.2**  Suśarman too then pierced Pārtha with arrows in the fray, striking Vāsudeva with seventy and Pārtha again with nine.

**6.100.3**  Warding them off with a flood of arrows, the son of Śakra, that mighty car-warrior, sent Suśarman's fighters in battle to the dwelling of Yama.

**6.100.4**  Slaughtered by Pārtha as if by Time at the dissolution of the age, those mighty car-warriors fled the battle, O king, when terror seized them.

**6.100.5**  Some abandoning their horses, some their chariots, O venerable one, and others forsaking their elephants, fled in all ten directions.

**6.100.6**  Others, goading on their horses, elephants, and chariots, fled from the battle with the utmost haste, O lord of peoples.

**6.100.7**  And the foot-soldiers too, casting away their weapons in that great battle, ran heedlessly this way and that, O Bhārata.

**6.100.8**  Though restrained again and again by the Trigarta Suśarman and by the other foremost kings, they would not stand firm in the conflict.

**6.100.9**  Seeing that force in flight, your son Duryodhana, setting Bhīṣma before him in battle—Bhīṣma whom all the host placed at its head—

**6.100.10**  with a great and total effort rushed upon Dhanaṃjaya, for the sake of the Trigarta lord's life, O lord of peoples.

**6.100.11**  He alone stood firm in the fight, scattering arrows of many kinds, together with all his brothers, while the rest of the men had fled away.

**6.100.12**  Likewise the Pāṇḍavas, O king, mail-clad with their whole strength, advanced for Phalguna's sake to where Bhīṣma had taken his stand.

**6.100.13**  Though they knew the terrible valor in battle of the wielder of Gāṇḍīva, their ardor roused by cries of dismay, they assailed Bhīṣma on every side.

**6.100.14**  Then the hero of the palm-tree banner covered the host of the Pāṇḍavas in battle with arrows whose joints were smoothly bent.

**6.100.15**  Then, gathered into one, all the Kurus together with the Pāṇḍavas fought on, O great king, when the sun had reached its zenith.

**6.100.16**  Sātyaki, having pierced Kṛtavarman with five iron shafts, stood his ground in the fray, that hero scattering arrows by the thousand.

**6.100.17**  Likewise King Drupada, piercing Droṇa with sharp arrows, struck him again with seventy and his charioteer with seven.

**6.100.18**  And Bhīmasena, having pierced King Bāhlika, his great-grandsire, roared a mighty roar like a tiger in the forest.

**6.100.19**  Arjuna's son, pierced by Citrasena with many swift shafts, struck Citrasena deep in the heart with three arrows.

**6.100.20**  Those two great champions, met together in battle, shone forth like the two most dread planets in the sky, O king—Mercury and Saturn.

**6.100.21**  Having slain his four horses and his charioteer with nine arrows, the son of Subhadrā, slayer of enemy heroes, roared a mighty roar.

**6.100.22**  Then that mighty car-warrior, swiftly leaping down from his horseless chariot, swiftly mounted the car of Durmukha, O lord of peoples.

**6.100.23**  And Droṇa, having pierced Drupada with arrows of smoothly bent joints, in his haste, that valiant one, struck his charioteer as well.

**6.100.24**  Hard pressed then at the head of the host, King Drupada withdrew with his swift horses, recalling their ancient enmity.

**6.100.25**  But Bhīmasena, in but a moment, stripped King Bāhlika of horses, charioteer, and car, before the eyes of the whole army.

**6.100.26**  In confusion, O great king, fallen into the gravest peril, Bāhlika, that best of men, leaping down from his mount, swiftly mounted the chariot of Lakṣmaṇa, that mighty car-warrior.

**6.100.27**  Sātyaki, that mighty car-warrior, having warded off Kṛtavarman with arrows of many kinds, O king, closed upon the grandsire.

**6.100.28**  Having pierced the Bhārata with sixty keen feathered shafts, he seemed to dance upon his chariot-platform, shaking his great bow.

**6.100.29**  At him the grandsire then hurled a great iron lance, gold-adorned, of mighty speed, beautiful as a serpent-maiden.

**6.100.30**  As it suddenly flew at him, death-like and keenly bright, the Vārṣṇeya of great renown shattered it by his deftness.

**6.100.31**  Failing to reach the Vārṣṇeya, that most dreadful lance fell upon the surface of the earth like a great meteor whose light had failed.

**6.100.32**  Then the Vārṣṇeya, O king, seizing his own lance of dreadful aspect, hurled it with speed toward the grandsire's chariot.

**6.100.33**  Driven by the force of the Vārṣṇeya's arm, in that great battle it rushed swiftly on like the Night of Doom upon a man.

**6.100.34**  As it suddenly flew at him, he cut it in two, O Bhārata, with two razor-tipped, exceedingly sharp arrows, and it was scattered upon the ground.

**6.100.35**  Having severed the lance, the son of Gaṅgā, wrathful yet laughing, that harrier of foes, struck Sātyaki in the chest with nine arrows.

**6.100.36**  Then the Pāṇḍavas, O elder of the Pāṇḍu line, with their chariots, elephants, and horses, surrounded Bhīṣma in battle for the protection of the Mādhava.

**6.100.37**  Then there broke out a tumultuous, hair-raising battle between the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus, each longing for victory in the fray.

**6.101.1**  Saṃjaya said: Seeing Bhīṣma wrathful in battle and hemmed in by the Pāṇḍavas, O great king, as the sun in the sky is wrapped by clouds at the end of the hot season—

**6.101.2**  Duryodhana, O great king, spoke to Duḥśāsana: "Here is the hero, the great archer Bhīṣma, slayer of foes,

**6.101.3**  covered on every side by the brave Pāṇḍavas, O bull of the Bharatas. The protection of that exalted one must be your task, O hero.

**6.101.4**  For if guarded in battle, Bhīṣma our grandsire would slay the striving Pāñcālas together with the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.101.5**  Therein I deem the duty to be the safeguarding of Bhīṣma himself, for this great archer Bhīṣma, our grandsire, is our protector."

**6.101.6**  "Let you, then, with all the host, surround the grandsire and guard him as he works his hard deeds in battle."

**6.101.7**  Thus addressed in the battle, your son Duḥśāsana took his stand encircling Bhīṣma, surrounded by a great host.

**6.101.8**  Then the son of Subala, with a hundred thousand horsemen bearing gleaming javelins in hand, wielding spears and lances—

**6.101.9**  proud, swift, mighty, and bannered, attended by trained men foremost among men, skilled in war—

**6.101.10**  held back Nakula and Sahadeva, and the Pāṇḍava Dharmarāja, foremost of men, surrounding them on every side.

**6.101.11**  Then King Duryodhana sent ten thousand brave horsemen to hold back the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.101.12**  As they charged in, swift as Garuḍa in the battle, the earth, struck by their hooves, O king, both trembled and roared.

**6.101.13**  And the mighty sound of the horses' hooves was then heard like that of a great bamboo forest burning upon a mountain.

**6.101.14**  And the great dust raised up by their galloping there, reaching the path of the day-maker, veiled the sun.

**6.101.15**  By those swift horses the Pāṇḍava force was thrown into turmoil, as a great lake is by geese descending at high speed; and amid the sound of their neighing nothing could be discerned.

**6.101.16**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī swiftly beat back the onrush of the horsemen in battle.

**6.101.17**  As the shore checks the surging flood of the great ocean, swollen by the rainy season and risen at the full moon, O great king.

**6.101.18**  Then those car-warriors, O king, with arrows of smoothly bent joints, severed the heads of the horsemen from their bodies.

**6.101.19**  They fell, O great king, slain by those stout bowmen, like great elephants felled by elephants in a mountain cave.

**6.101.20**  They too, ranging through all ten directions, severed the heads of their foes with keen javelins and arrows of smoothly bent joints.

**6.101.21**  Drawing very close, the horsemen with their spears cut off the heads of their foes, O bull of the Bharatas, as one plucks fruits from a great tree.

**6.101.22**  Horses with their riders, O king, were slain here and there—fallen and falling by the hundreds and the thousands.

**6.101.23**  Those horses, being slaughtered and stricken with terror, fled away, as deer that, meeting lions, flee bent on saving their lives.

**6.101.24**  And the Pāṇḍavas, O great king, having vanquished their foes in that great battle, blew their conches and beat their drums in the fray.

**6.101.25**  Then Duryodhana, seeing his army standing crestfallen, O best of the Bharatas, spoke these words to the king of the Madras:

**6.101.26**  "This eldest son of Pāṇḍu, O maternal uncle, having conquered my men, even as we look on, O mighty-armed one, the strong one routs my army.

**6.101.27**  Hold him back, O mighty-armed one, as the shore holds back the abode of sea-monsters; for you are renowned beyond measure as one of unbearable strength and prowess."

**6.101.28**  Hearing that speech of your son, the majestic Śalya advanced with his line of chariots to where King Yudhiṣṭhira was.

**6.101.29**  As Śalya's vast host came rushing on all at once, sweeping forward like the rush of a mighty flood, the son of Pāṇḍu held it back in the battle.

**6.101.30**  And the king of righteousness, that great chariot-warrior, swiftly struck the king of the Madras full in the chest with ten arrows in the fight; and Nakula and Sahadeva struck him each with three straight-flying shafts.

**6.101.31**  The king of the Madras in turn smote them all, each with three arrows, and pierced Yudhiṣṭhira again with sixty whetted shafts, and struck the two furious sons of Mādrī with two arrows apiece.

**6.101.32**  Then mighty-armed Bhīma, vanquisher of foes, seeing the king fallen into the power of the lord of the Madras in the battle, as if caught in the very jaws of death, rushed to Yudhiṣṭhira's side in the fray.

**6.101.33**  Then a most terrible and savage combat broke out, while the sun, blazing, had taken its stand in the western quarter.

**6.102.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then your father, filled with wrath, with his keen and excellent shafts struck the sons of Pṛthā along with their armies on every side in the battle.

**6.102.2**  He pierced Bhīma with twelve arrows and Sātyaki with nine shafts, Nakula with three darts and Sahadeva with seven,

**6.102.3**  and into Yudhiṣṭhira's arms and breast he drove twelve shafts; then, having pierced Dhṛṣṭadyumna, the mighty one roared aloud.

**6.102.4**  In return Nakula pierced the grandsire with six, and Mādhava with three shafts, Dhṛṣṭadyumna with seventy and Bhīmasena with five, while Yudhiṣṭhira struck back the grandsire with twelve.

**6.102.5**  And Droṇa, having pierced Sātyaki, then struck Bhīmasena, smiting each with five sharp arrows like the rod of Yama.

**6.102.6**  And those two struck him back, each with three straight-flying shafts, as men goad a great elephant with hooks—Droṇa, that bull among brāhmaṇas.

**6.102.7**  The Sauvīras, the Kitavas, the men of the east and the west and the north, the Mālavas, the Abhīṣāhas, the Śūrasenas, the Śibis, and the Vasātis, though cut down by sharp arrows, did not abandon Bhīṣma in the fight.

**6.102.8**  Likewise others too, though being slain by the high-souled men of the Pāṇḍava side, advanced against the Pāṇḍavas with weapons of every kind in their hands; and even so, O king, the Pāṇḍavas encircled the grandsire.

**6.102.9**  Surrounded on every side by floods of chariots, yet unconquered, he blazed forth like a fire let loose in a thicket, consuming the foe.

**6.102.10**  His chariot was the fire-altar, his bow the flame, swords, lances, and maces its fuel, his arrows the sparks—and that fire that was Bhīṣma burned up the bulls among the warriors.

**6.102.11**  With gold-feathered arrows fledged with vulture-plumes and finely sharpened, with barbed shafts, nālīka darts, and iron bolts, he covered over that host.

**6.102.12**  With his keen shafts he brought down banners and chariot-warriors alike, and made the ranks of chariots like groves of palm-trees stripped bare of their crowns.

**6.102.13**  Chariots, elephants, and horses he left without men upon them in the battle, O king—that mighty-armed one, the foremost of all who bear arms.

**6.102.14**  Hearing the roar of his bowstring against the leather guard, like the crash of a thunderbolt, all creatures trembled, O Bhārata.

**6.102.15**  For your father's arrows fell never in vain, O bull of the Bharatas; the shafts loosed from Bhīṣma's bow were not stayed by any armor.

**6.102.16**  Chariots whose heroes had been slain, yet yoked still to swift horses, we beheld, O great king, being dragged across the field of battle.

**6.102.17**  Fourteen thousand of the Cedis, Kāśis, and Karūṣas—renowned as great chariot-warriors, sons of noble houses, ready to give up their lives, all unflinching, with banners wrought of gold—

**6.102.18**  having come upon Bhīṣma in the battle, like Death with jaws agape, sank down into the other world together with their horses, chariots, and elephants.

**6.102.19**  Chariots with axles and frames shattered, some with their wheels broken all about, we beheld, O king, by the hundreds and by the thousands.

**6.102.20**  With broken chariots and their canopies, with fallen chariot-warriors, with shattered fine coats of mail and arrows and spears, O lord of peoples,

**6.102.21**  with maces and pestles, with swords and bee-shaped shafts, with chariot-poles and quivers and broken wheels, O honored one,

**6.102.22**  with arms and bows and swords, with heads still bearing their earrings, with leathern wrist-guards and finger-guards, with fallen banners, and with bows cut to pieces in many places—with all these the earth was strewn over.

**6.102.23**  Elephants whose riders had been slain and horses whose mounted men were dead ran wildly to and fro there, O king, by the hundreds and the thousands.

**6.102.24**  And those heroes, though they strove their utmost, could not check the great chariot-warriors as they fled, tormented by Bhīṣma's arrows.

**6.102.25**  Cut down by him whose valor equaled great Indra's, the mighty host broke and scattered, O great king, so that not even two men fled together.

**6.102.26**  With its chariots, elephants, and horses thrown into confusion, its banners and chariot-poles fallen, the army of the sons of Pāṇḍu became one cry of woe, bereft of its senses.

**6.102.27**  There, in the rout, father slew son and son slew father, and friend slew beloved friend, each driven on by the force of fate.

**6.102.28**  Others among the son of Pāṇḍu's soldiers cast off their armor and, with their hair flying loose, were seen running away, O Bhārata.

**6.102.29**  Like a herd of cattle in a stampede, its chariots and elephants scattering in panic, the army of the son of Pāṇḍu was then to be seen, raising a cry of distress.

**6.102.30**  Seeing the army being broken to pieces, the delight of the Yādavas reined in that excellent chariot and spoke to Pārtha, the Bībhatsu:

**6.102.31**  "This is the very hour, O Pārtha, that you have longed for; strike now at this man, O tiger among men—unless from bewilderment your purpose fails you.

**6.102.32**  What you yourself once declared, O hero, before the assembled kings in Virāṭa's city, O Pārtha, in the presence of Saṃjaya—

**6.102.33**  'All the warriors of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, with Bhīṣma and Droṇa at their head, together with their kinsmen, I shall slay—whoever shall give me battle in the fight'—

**6.102.34**  make good that word, O son of Kuntī, O subduer of foes; remembering the duty of a kṣatriya, do battle, O bull of the Bharatas."

**6.102.35**  Thus addressed by Vāsudeva, the Bībhatsu, with sidelong glance and downcast face, as though against his will, spoke these words:

**6.102.36**  "By slaying those who ought not to be slain, what good can come to me—whether a kingdom that leads but to hell, or the sorrows of life in the forest?

**6.102.37**  Drive on the horses to where Bhīṣma stands; I shall do your bidding. I shall bring down the unassailable old grandsire of the Kurus."

**6.102.38**  Then Mādhava urged on the horses, white as silver, toward where Bhīṣma stood—who, O king, was hard to gaze upon, like the radiant sun.

**6.102.39**  Then Yudhiṣṭhira's great host turned back again, seeing the mighty-armed Pārtha rising up against Bhīṣma in the battle.

**6.102.40**  Then Bhīṣma, foremost of the Kurus, roaring again and again like a lion, swiftly overwhelmed Dhanaṃjaya's chariot with showers of arrows.

**6.102.41**  In an instant that chariot of his, with its horses and its driver, was so covered by the great rain of arrows that nothing of it could be made out at all.

**6.102.42**  But Vāsudeva of the Sātvatas, unshaken, holding firm to his steadiness, urged on those horses that were wounded by Bhīṣma's shafts.

**6.102.43**  Then Pārtha, taking up his divine bow that sounded like a thundercloud, cut Bhīṣma's bow with his keen shafts and brought it down.

**6.102.44**  His bow thus severed, that scion of Kuru, your father, in but the span between two blinks of an eye strung another great bow.

**6.102.45**  With both arms he drew that bow that roared like a thundercloud; but this one too the wrathful Arjuna cut through.

**6.102.46**  The son of Śaṃtanu praised that swiftness of his: "Well done, O mighty-armed Pārtha! Well done, O son of Kuntī!"

**6.102.47**  Having so addressed him, Bhīṣma took up another splendid bow and loosed his arrows in the battle against Pārtha's chariot.

**6.102.48**  Vāsudeva showed forth surpassing skill in the driving of the horses, making the other's arrows fall in vain by displaying his wheeling circles.

**6.102.49**  Those two tigers among men, Bhīṣma and Pārtha, gashed by arrows, shone resplendent, like two furious bulls scored and marked by each other's horns.

**6.102.50**  But Vāsudeva, beholding the gentleness of Pārtha's fighting, and Bhīṣma pouring forth showers of arrows unceasingly in the battle,

**6.102.51**  blazing like the sun as he stood between the two armies, striking down one after another the best of the son of Pāṇḍu's warriors,

**6.102.52**  making it seem as though the end of an age had come upon Yudhiṣṭhira's host—that Bhīṣma the mighty-armed Mādhava, slayer of hostile heroes, could no longer endure.

**6.102.53**  Letting go of Pārtha's horses, white as silver, O honored one, that great master of yoga, in his wrath, leapt down from the great chariot, and the mighty one rushed at Bhīṣma with only his arms for weapons.

**6.102.54**  Goad in hand, blazing with splendor, roaring again and again like a lion, that lord of the world seemed to rend the very earth with his feet.

**6.102.55**  His eyes red with wrath, eager to slay, of measureless brilliance, Kṛṣṇa seemed in that great battle to devour the very spirits of your men.

**6.102.56**  Seeing Mādhava in the tumult rising up against Bhīṣma in the fight, the warriors there cried out, "Bhīṣma is slain! Bhīṣma is slain!" and all those men fled away in terror of Vāsudeva.

**6.102.57**  Wrapped in yellow silk, dark as a sapphire, Janārdana, as he rushed upon Bhīṣma, shone resplendent like a thundercloud garlanded with lightning.

**6.102.58**  As a lion springs upon an elephant, or the bull of the herd upon a rival bull, so did that splendid bull of the Yādavas rush forward, roaring.

**6.102.59**  Beholding the lotus-eyed one rushing upon him in the battle, Bhīṣma, unperturbed, drew his great bow in the fray and spoke to Govinda with an untroubled heart:

**6.102.60**  "Come, come, O lotus-eyed one, O God of gods, homage be to you! Strike me down today, O best of the Sātvatas, in this great battle!

**6.102.61**  For if I be slain by you in the battle, O sinless god, the highest good alone shall be mine, O Kṛṣṇa, both in that world and in this; I am honored, O Govinda, this day before the three worlds, in the fight."

**6.102.62**  Then close behind, the mighty-armed Pārtha, running after Keśava, caught hold of him, seizing him fast with both his arms.

**6.102.63**  Though held back by Pārtha, the lotus-eyed Kṛṣṇa, that supreme of men, dragging Arjuna along with him, still pressed forward with great force.

**6.102.64**  But Pārtha, slayer of hostile heroes, planting his feet firmly with all his strength, with great effort restrained Hṛṣīkeśa at the tenth step.

**6.102.65**  Then Arjuna, slayer of hostile heroes, in his distress spoke to him whose eyes were troubled with wrath, who hissed like a serpent:

**6.102.66**  "Turn back, O mighty-armed one! You must not make a falsehood of what you yourself declared before, that you would not fight, O Keśava.

**6.102.67**  The world will call you a breaker of your word, O Mādhava. This whole burden is mine; I shall slay him who is steadfast in his vow.

**6.102.68**  I swear, O Mādhava, by our friendship, by truth, and by my good deeds, that I shall make an end of the foes, O harrier of foes.

**6.102.69**  This very day behold the unassailable one, mighty in his vows, brought low—like the lord of the stars when, at the hour of doom, of itself it wanes and falls."

**6.102.70**  And Mādhava, hearing the words of the high-souled Phalguna, said nothing, but, still wrathful, mounted the chariot again.

**6.102.71**  Upon those two tigers among men, standing in their chariot, Bhīṣma the son of Śāntanu once more rained a rain of arrows, as a cloud pours its torrents upon two mountains.

**6.102.72**  And your father Devavrata took the lives of the warriors as the sun with its rays draws up the dews at winter's end.

**6.102.73**  Even as the Pāṇḍava had broken the armies of the Kurus in the fight, so did your father break the armies of the Pāṇḍavas in the battle.

**6.102.74**  Their soldiers slain and put to flight, dispirited and bereft of their senses, they could not bear even to look upon the peerless Bhīṣma in the battle, blazing with his own splendor like the sun arrived at its zenith.

**6.102.75**  Slain by Bhīṣma as if by Time itself at the dissolution of an age, the Pāṇḍavas, oppressed with fear, looked about them, O great king.

**6.102.76**  They could find no deliverer, like cattle mired in the mud, like ants trampled underfoot — the weak crushed by the strong in battle.

**6.102.77**  O Bhārata, upon that mighty car-warrior, unassailable, pouring forth floods of arrows and scorching the kings, upon Bhīṣma they could not so much as look — blazing with shafts for rays like the burning sun.

**6.102.78**  As he ground down the host of the Pāṇḍavas, the thousand-rayed one went to his setting; then in the armies, worn out by toil, the mind turned toward withdrawal.

**6.103.1**  Saṃjaya said: While they were still fighting, when the sun had reached its setting, a dreadful twilight came on, and we could no longer see the battle.

**6.103.2**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, O Bhārata, beholding the twilight and his host being slaughtered by Bhīṣma, slayer of foes,

**6.103.3**  its weapons let fall, turned about and bent on flight, and Bhīṣma raging in the fight, pursuing the mighty car-warriors —

**6.103.4**  and seeing the Somakas vanquished and the great car-warriors dispirited, having pondered long and reflected, he resolved upon a withdrawal.

**6.103.5**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira ordered the withdrawal of his troops; and in like manner there was a withdrawal of your troops as well at that time.

**6.103.6**  Then, having drawn back the troops, the great car-warriors there encamped, O best of the Kurus, hacked and wounded in the fray.

**6.103.7**  But the Pāṇḍavas, brooding on Bhīṣma's deeds in the battle, could find no peace then, sorely harried by Bhīṣma.

**6.103.8**  Bhīṣma too, having conquered in battle the Pāṇḍavas together with the Sṛñjayas, honored by your sons and extolled, O Bhārata,

**6.103.9**  encamped together with the Kurus, who were joyful on every side; then came the night, that bewilders all living beings.

**6.103.10**  In that dread nightfall the Pāṇḍavas, together with the Vṛṣṇis and the unassailable Sṛñjayas, sat down to take counsel.

**6.103.11**  All those mighty ones, skilled in resolving deliberations, took counsel, unperturbed, upon what would be best for them and fitting to the hour.

**6.103.12**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, having deliberated long, O king, looked up at Vāsudeva and spoke these words:

**6.103.13**  "Behold, Kṛṣṇa, the great-souled Bhīṣma of terrible prowess, crushing my host as an elephant crushes thickets of reeds.

**6.103.14**  We dare not even look upon that great-souled one, who licks among the armies like a fire grown high.

**6.103.15**  As the dread great serpent Takṣaka, swollen with venom, so is Bhīṣma in battle, O Kṛṣṇa — sharp-weaponed and full of fiery might.

**6.103.16**  With bow in hand in battle, loosing his whetted shafts — Yama enraged might be conquered, and the thunderbolt-wielding king of the gods,

**6.103.17**  or Varuṇa bearing his noose, or the lord of wealth with his mace — but Bhīṣma in his fury cannot be conquered in great battle.

**6.103.18**  So am I, things having come to this pass, O Kṛṣṇa, sunk in an ocean of grief, having encountered Bhīṣma in the fight through my own weakness of judgment.

**6.103.19**  I shall go to the forest, O unassailable one — there indeed lies my welfare. I take no joy in this war, Kṛṣṇa, for Bhīṣma slays us without ceasing.

**6.103.20**  As a moth rushing upon a blazing fire goes straight to its death, so have I come against Bhīṣma.

**6.103.21**  I am brought to ruin, O scion of Vṛṣṇi, valiant though I am, for the sake of a kingdom; and my heroic brothers are sorely tormented by his shafts.

**6.103.22**  For my sake, out of brotherly love, they have been cast down from the kingdom; and Kṛṣṇā too has been brought to grief for my sake, O slayer of Madhu.

**6.103.23**  Life I hold dear, for life today is hard to keep; with the remnant of life that is left me this day I will follow the highest dharma.

**6.103.24**  If I, together with my brothers, am worthy of your grace, O Keśava, then declare it to me, Keśava, in a manner not at odds with my own dharma."

**6.103.25**  Hearing this his speech, uttered at length out of compassion, Kṛṣṇa then replied, comforting Yudhiṣṭhira:

**6.103.26**  "Son of Dharma, true to your covenant, do not give way to despair — you whose brothers are heroes, hard to vanquish, slayers of foes:

**6.103.27**  Arjuna and Bhīmasena, equal in splendor to Wind and Fire, and the two valiant sons of Mādrī, like lords among the gods.

**6.103.28**  Or set me to the task, out of our friendship, O Pāṇḍava, and I will fight with Bhīṣma; for, charged by you, O king, what would I not do in the great battle?

**6.103.29**  I will challenge and slay in battle that bull among men, Bhīṣma, before the very eyes of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's sons — if Phalguna is unwilling.

**6.103.30**  If, O king, you see victory in Bhīṣma's death, O Pāṇḍava, I will this day, with a single car, slay the aged grandsire of the Kurus.

**6.103.31**  Behold my prowess in the fight, O king, as it were great Indra's: loosing mighty weapons, I will hurl him down from his car.

**6.103.32**  Whoever is an enemy of the sons of Pāṇḍu is my enemy too, beyond all doubt; my cause is yours, and those who are mine are yours alone.

**6.103.33**  Your brother is my friend, my kinsman, and indeed my pupil; for Arjuna's sake, O lord of earth, I would cut off my own flesh and give it.

**6.103.34**  And this tiger among men would for my sake give up his life; this is our compact, dear one — that we should deliver each other across. So set me to the task, O lord of kings, that I may be your refuge.

**6.103.35**  That which was vowed before, at Upaplavya, by Pārtha in Ulūka's presence — 'I will bring about the death of the son of Gaṅgā' —

**6.103.36**  that word of the wise Pārtha I am bound to keep; and what Pārtha has consented to must be done by me, beyond all doubt.

**6.103.37**  Or else this is a burden well within Phalguna's measure in battle: he will slay in the fray Bhīṣma, conqueror of hostile cities.

**6.103.38**  For Pārtha, once roused, could accomplish even the impossible in battle: Arjuna might in combat slay the gods themselves, arrayed together with Daityas and Dānavas — how much more Bhīṣma, O lord of men!

**6.103.39**  Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, mighty in valor, is surely now turned awry, his spirit gone, his life but short, and no longer perceives what ought to be done."

**6.103.40**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "So it is, O mighty-armed one, even as you say, O Mādhava; for none of these is a match to withstand your onset.

**6.103.41**  Assuredly I shall attain all that I desire, I for whom you, O tiger among men, mighty in strength, are protector.

**6.103.42**  With you as my protector, O best of the victorious, I could conquer in battle the very gods together with Indra, O Govinda — how much more Bhīṣma in the great fight!

**6.103.43**  Yet I will not, for the weight of my own interest, make your word a falsehood. Without yourself doing battle, render the aid that was promised, O Mādhava.

**6.103.44**  A certain compact, O Mādhava, was made with me by Bhīṣma: 'I will counsel you for your good, but I will in no way fight for you; for Duryodhana's sake I shall fight' — truly this he said, O lord.

**6.103.45**  For he is the giver to me both of the kingdom and of counsel, O Mādhava. Therefore let us all, together with you, once more ask Devavrata himself the means of his own undoing, O slayer of Madhu.

**6.103.46**  So let us go together at once to Bhīṣma, that best of men, and — if it please you, O scion of Vṛṣṇi — ask the Kaurava for his counsel.

**6.103.47**  He will speak words salutary and true, O Janārdana; and as he shall direct, Kṛṣṇa, so shall I do in the battle.

**6.103.48**  He, firm in his vow, is the giver to us both of victory and of counsel; orphaned children bereft of our father, we were reared by him.

**6.103.49**  If I must wish to slay that aged grandsire, O Mādhava — my father's beloved father — then a curse upon the warrior's calling!"

**6.103.50**  Saṃjaya said: Then, O great king, the scion of Vṛṣṇi said to the delight of the Kurus: "Ever does your speech please me, O mighty-armed one.

**6.103.51**  Devavrata Bhīṣma, accomplished, could consume one with a mere glance. Let us go to ask the son of the ocean-bound river the means of his death; he is bound to tell the truth, especially when asked by you.

**6.103.52**  So let us go there to question the grandsire of the Kurus; bowing our heads to him, let us ask his counsel, O Mādhava. By the counsel he shall give us we will fight our foes."

**6.103.53**  Having thus taken counsel, the heroic Pāṇḍavas, O elder of Pāṇḍu, went all together, and the valiant Vāsudeva with them, their weapons and armor laid aside, to the tent of Bhīṣma.

**6.103.54**  Entering then, the Pāṇḍavas approached Bhīṣma and saluted him with their heads, doing him honor, O great king, O bull of the Bharatas; and bowing their heads to him, they took refuge with Bhīṣma.

**6.103.55**  To them the mighty-armed Bhīṣma, grandsire of the Kurus, said: "Welcome to you, O scion of Vṛṣṇi; welcome to you, Dhanaṃjaya; welcome to the son of Dharma, to Bhīma, and to the twins as well.

**6.103.56**  What service may I do you this day to increase your gladness? I will do it with my whole heart, even were it most hard to accomplish."

**6.103.57**  To the son of Gaṅgā, who spoke thus again and again with affection, the son of Dharma, Yudhiṣṭhira, sore at heart, said these words:

**6.103.58**  "How may we conquer, O knower of dharma? How may we win the kingdom? How may there be no destruction of the people? Tell me that, O lord.

**6.103.59**  Tell us yourself the means of your own undoing; for how, O king, can we withstand you in battle?

**6.103.60**  For there is in you not even the slightest gap, O grandsire of the Kurus; with your bow ever drawn into a full circle you are seen in the fight.

**6.103.61**  We cannot mark you taking up, nor nocking, nor drawing your bow, O mighty-armed one, stationed on your car like the sun.

**6.103.62**  Slayer of men and horses, chariots and elephants, destroyer of hostile heroes — what man could presume to slay you, O bull of the Bharatas?

**6.103.63**  By you, O best of men, pouring down your great showers of arrows, my mighty host has been brought to ruin.

**6.103.64**  How I may conquer you in battle, how the kingdom may be mine, and how there may be peace for my army — tell me that, O grandsire."

**6.103.65**  Then the son of Śaṃtanu said to the Pāṇḍavas, O elder of Pāṇḍu: "In no way, O son of Kuntī, while I live, will victory be seen for you in battle — this truth I tell you.

**6.103.66**  But once I am overcome in the fight, you will surely conquer the Kauravas. Strike at me quickly, if you desire victory in battle; I grant you leave, O sons of Pṛthā — strike as you please.

**6.103.67**  Thus indeed I deem it well done by you, for I am known to you; when I am slain, all is slain. Therefore let it be so ordained."

**6.103.68**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "Tell us then the means by which we may conquer in battle you, who in your wrath are like Death himself, rod in hand.

**6.103.69**  The wielder of the thunderbolt may be conquered, and Varuṇa, and Yama too; but you cannot be vanquished in battle even by gods and demons with Indra at their head."

**6.103.70**  Bhīṣma said: "True is this, O mighty-armed one, even as you say, O Pāṇḍava: I cannot be conquered in battle even by gods and demons with Indra at their head,

**6.103.71**  while in battle I stand armed and intent, my excellent bow in hand. But once I have laid down my weapons, O king, the great car-warriors may strike me in the fight.

**6.103.72**  Against one whose weapons are cast down, who has fallen, whose armor and banner are flung off, who is fleeing or afraid, or who says 'I am yours' —

**6.103.73**  against a woman, or one bearing a woman's name, against the maimed, against one who is the only son, or unborn-of-issue, or unsightly — to fight with these pleases me not.

**6.103.74**  And hear this resolve of mine, O Pārtha, long ago conceived: beholding an inauspicious standard, I would in no way give battle.

**6.103.75**  This son of Drupada in your host, O king, the great car-warrior Śikhaṇḍin, eager for battle, a hero victorious in assemblies of war —

**6.103.76**  how he was a woman before and afterward attained to manhood — you yourselves know all this exactly as it befell.

**6.103.77**  Let the heroic Arjuna in battle, putting Śikhaṇḍin in the forefront, mailed and swift, rush upon me with his arrows.

**6.103.78**  Against one who bears that inauspicious banner, and above all against one who was formerly a woman, I have no wish to strike, nor in any way to take up my arrows.

**6.103.79**  Seizing that opening, let the Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya swiftly strike me down with his arrows from every side, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.103.80**  In all the worlds I see no one who could slay me when I stand ready for battle, save the illustrious Kṛṣṇa, or the Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya.

**6.103.81**  Therefore, placing some other before me in the van, let Bībhatsu strike me down — thus shall victory be yours.

**6.103.82**  Do this, son of Kuntī, as I have said in my words; then in battle you shall conquer the assembled sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**6.103.83**  Saṃjaya said: Granted leave, the sons of Pṛthā then went toward their own camp, having saluted the great-souled Bhīṣma, grandsire of the Kurus.

**6.103.84**  When the son of Gaṅgā, now consecrated for the other world, had spoken thus, Arjuna, burning with grief, said this with shame:

**6.103.85**  With my teacher, the elder of our house, the wise and discerning grandsire — how shall I fight him in battle, O Mādhava?

**6.103.86**  For in my childhood, Vāsudeva, while I played with my limbs all smeared with dust, I roughly handled that high-minded, great-souled one.

**6.103.87**  Climbing onto his lap as a child, O elder brother of Gada, I would call him 'Father,' though he was the father of my own father, the great-souled Pāṇḍu.

**6.103.88**  'I am not your father, but your father's father, O Bhārata' — so he said to me in my childhood. How is such a one to be slain by me?

**6.103.89**  Let my army be slain if it must — I will not fight the great-souled one. Be it victory or death for me, what is your judgment, O Kṛṣṇa?

**6.103.90**  The blessed Kṛṣṇa said: Having vowed before, O Jiṣṇu, to slay Bhīṣma in battle, and standing fast in the duty of a warrior, O Pārtha, how will you not slay him?

**6.103.91**  Cast him down from his chariot, O Pārtha, like a tree struck by the thunderbolt. Without slaying the son of Gaṅgā in battle, no victory shall be yours.

**6.103.92**  This was ordained of old by the gods, and shall come to pass beyond your control: 'The slayer of Bhīṣma shall be the former Indra' — and that cannot be otherwise.

**6.103.93**  For Bhīṣma, unassailable, like Death with gaping jaws — none other than you could slay him, not even the wielder of the thunderbolt himself.

**6.103.94**  Slay Bhīṣma, mighty-armed one, and hear this word of mine, even as the deeply wise Bṛhaspati once spoke to Śakra:

**6.103.95**  'Even an elder, O Śakra, even one endowed with virtues, if he comes as an assassin bent on murder, one may challenge and slay him.'

**6.103.96**  This is the eternal, established law of warriors, O Dhanaṃjaya: that without malice they must fight, must protect, and must offer sacrifice.

**6.103.97**  Arjuna said: Śikhaṇḍin shall surely be the death of Bhīṣma, O Kṛṣṇa; for the moment Bhīṣma sees the Pāñcāla prince, he always turns away.

**6.103.98**  So we, stationing Śikhaṇḍin at the forefront before him, shall cast down the son of Gaṅgā by this stratagem — such is my thought.

**6.103.99**  I shall hold off the other great archers with my shafts, while Śikhaṇḍin, foremost of fighters, advances against Bhīṣma alone.

**6.103.100**  You have heard the chief of the Kurus say, 'I will not slay Śikhaṇḍin, for he was born a maiden of old and only afterward became a man.'

**6.103.101**  Saṃjaya said: Having reached this resolve, the Pāṇḍavas together with Mādhava, those bulls among men, each repaired to his own couch.

**6.104.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How did Śikhaṇḍin advance against the son of Gaṅgā in battle, and how did the Pāṇḍavas advance against Bhīṣma? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.104.2**  Saṃjaya said: Then, when the clear dawn came toward the rising of the sun, while kettledrums, tabors, and war-drums were being sounded,

**6.104.3**  and while curd-white conches were being blown on every side, the Pāṇḍavas, placing Śikhaṇḍin at their head, marched forth to battle.

**6.104.4**  Having formed an array, great king, that could crush every foe, Śikhaṇḍin stood at the forefront of all the troops, O lord of peoples.

**6.104.5**  As guardians of his chariot-wheels were Bhīmasena and Dhanaṃjaya, and behind came the sons of Draupadī and the valiant son of Subhadrā.

**6.104.6**  Sātyaki and Cekitāna, the great car-warrior, were their protectors; and behind came Dhṛṣṭadyumna, guarded by the Pāñcālas.

**6.104.7**  Then King Yudhiṣṭhira, the lord, together with the twins, advanced, making the field resound with his lion's roar, O bull of the Bharatas.

**6.104.8**  Behind them came Virāṭa, surrounded by his own army, and after him, great king, Drupada pressed forward.

**6.104.9**  The five Kekaya brothers and the valiant Dhṛṣṭaketu guarded the rear of the Pāṇḍava host, O Bhārata.

**6.104.10**  Having thus arrayed their great army, the Pāṇḍavas charged upon your host in battle, casting aside their very lives.

**6.104.11**  In like manner the Kurus, O king, placing the mighty Bhīṣma at the head of all their troops, marched out against the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.104.12**  Guarded by your sons, unassailable and of great might, came Droṇa the great archer, and his son the great car-warrior;

**6.104.13**  behind them Bhagadatta, surrounded by a host of elephants, and Kṛpa and Kṛtavarman, devoted followers of Bhagadatta.

**6.104.14**  Behind them the mighty king of the Kāmbojas, Sudakṣiṇa, and Jayatsena of Magadha, and the son of Subala, and Bṛhadbala.

**6.104.15**  And the other great archers, kings led by Suśarman, guarded the rear of your army, O Bhārata.

**6.104.16**  Day after day, as it came, Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu formed in battle arrays after the manner of the Asuras, the Piśācas, and the Rākṣasas.

**6.104.17**  Then began the battle between your men and theirs, O Bhārata, as they struck one another down, O king — a battle that swelled the realm of Yama.

**6.104.18**  The sons of Pṛthā, led by Arjuna, placing Śikhaṇḍin before them, advanced upon Bhīṣma in battle, scattering arrows of every kind.

**6.104.19**  There, O Bhārata, your warriors, sorely pressed by Bhīma's arrows and drenched in streams of blood, went then to the other world.

**6.104.20**  Nakula and Sahadeva and Sātyaki the great car-warrior, falling upon your army, harried it with their might.

**6.104.21**  Being slaughtered in the battle, your men, O bull of the Bharatas, were unable to hold back the great force of the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.104.22**  Then your army, cut down on every side, O king, fled in all directions, driven on by the great car-warriors.

**6.104.23**  Your men found no rescuer, O bull of the Bharatas, as they were struck down by the sharp arrows of the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas.

**6.104.24**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Seeing his army hard pressed by the sons of Pṛthā, what did the valiant Bhīṣma do in his wrath upon the field? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.104.25**  And how did that scorcher of foes advance against the Pāṇḍavas in battle, striking down the heroic Somakas? Tell me that, Saṃjaya.

**6.104.26**  Saṃjaya said: I will tell you, great king, what the grandsire did when your son's army was hard pressed by the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas.

**6.104.27**  With gladdened hearts the heroic Pāṇḍavas, O elder of Pāṇḍu's line, advanced, smiting your son's host.

**6.104.28**  That destruction of men, elephants, and horses, O lord of men — that slaughter of his army by the foe in battle — Bhīṣma could not then endure.

**6.104.29**  That unassailable great archer charged upon the Pāṇḍavas and the Pāñcālas together with the Sṛñjayas, casting aside his own life.

**6.104.30**  He held off the five foremost car-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, O king, who stood ready with weapons raised in battle, with his shafts — with iron arrows, with calf-tooth darts, and with sharp crescent-headed barbs.

**6.104.31**  In his wrath he slew in battle countless elephants and horses beyond measure, and that bull among men hurled the chariot-warriors down from their cars, O king.

**6.104.32**  He cast the riders from the backs of their horses and the foot-soldiers that closed with him, and the elephant-riders from their elephants, spreading terror among the foe.

**6.104.33**  Against that single Bhīṣma, the great car-warrior pressing swiftly on in battle, the Pāṇḍavas closed in, as the Asuras against the wielder of the thunderbolt.

**6.104.34**  Loosing sharp arrows whose touch was like Indra's thunderbolt, he was seen in every quarter, bearing a terrible form.

**6.104.35**  As he fought in the battle, his great bow, like the bow of Indra, was seen forever drawn into a perfect circle.

**6.104.36**  Seeing that feat in battle, your sons, O lord of peoples, were filled with the highest wonder and did honor to the grandsire.

**6.104.37**  The sons of Pṛthā, grown downcast, gazed upon your sire, that hero fighting in the field, as the immortals once gazed upon Vipracitti; nor could they stay him, who was like Death with gaping jaws.

**6.104.38**  When the tenth day had come, he consumed Śikhaṇḍin's host of chariots with his sharp arrows, as the fire whose path is black consumes a forest.

**6.104.39**  Śikhaṇḍin pierced him with three arrows between the breasts — him who was like an enraged venomous serpent, like Death sent forth by Time.

**6.104.40**  Pierced thus most grievously, Bhīṣma, looking upon Śikhaṇḍin, though unwilling and wroth, said this with a laugh:

**6.104.41**  "Strike me if you will or do not — in no wise shall I fight you; for you remain the very Śikhaṇḍinī that the Creator made you."

**6.104.42**  Hearing that speech of his, Śikhaṇḍin, swooning with rage, licking the corners of his mouth, said to Bhīṣma in the battle:

**6.104.43**  "I know you, mighty-armed one, as the bringer of doom to the warriors; and I have heard of your battle with the son of Jamadagni.

**6.104.44**  This divine might of yours I have heard of many times; yet, knowing your might, I shall this day do battle with you.

**6.104.45**  Doing what pleases the Pāṇḍavas and myself, O best of men, this day I shall fight you in battle, O most excellent of men.

**6.104.46**  And surely I shall slay you — I swear it by truth before your face. Having heard this word of mine, do whatever you deem fit."

**6.104.47**  "Strike me if you will or do not — you shall not escape me alive. Look well upon this world, O Bhīṣma, victor in battle!"

**6.104.48**  Having spoken thus, he then pierced Bhīṣma in the battle, O king, with five straight-jointed arrows, after first driving home his shafts of words.

**6.104.49**  Hearing those words of his, the ambidextrous one, scorcher of foes, thinking 'This is the hour,' urged Śikhaṇḍin on:

**6.104.50**  "I shall follow close behind you, scattering the foe with my arrows. Charge, in furious wrath, upon Bhīṣma of terrible valor.

**6.104.51**  For the mighty one is not able to do you harm in battle; therefore this day, O mighty-armed hero, charge upon Bhīṣma.

**6.104.52**  If without slaying Bhīṣma in battle you turn away, good sir, you shall become, along with me, the laughingstock of this world.

**6.104.53**  That we may not become objects of mockery, O hero, in this great battle, so put forth your effort in the fight: bring down the grandsire.

**6.104.54**  I shall be your guard in the battle, O scorcher of foes, holding off all the chariot-warriors: bring down the grandsire.

**6.104.55**  Droṇa and Droṇa's son, and Kṛpa, and Suyodhana, and Citrasena and Vikarṇa, and the lord of Sindhu, Jayadratha,

**6.104.56**  Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, and Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja, and the heroic Bhagadatta, and the Māgadha, the great car-warrior —"

**6.104.57**  Saumadatti, the hero in battle, and Ārśyaśṛṅgi the rākṣasa, and the king of the Trigartas in the fray, together with all their great chariot-warriors—these I shall hold back, as the shore holds back the abode of sea-monsters.

**6.104.58**  And all the Kurus assembled, and whatever soldiers of theirs stand ready, I shall ward off in the battle; do you go after the grandsire.

**6.105.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How did Śikhaṇḍin, the Pāñcāla, enraged in battle, rush upon the son of Gaṅgā, the grandsire, that righteous soul of steadfast vows?

**6.105.2**  Who among the great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍava host, hastening in the hour of haste and eager for victory, guarded Śikhaṇḍin as he raised his weapon?

**6.105.3**  How did Bhīṣma, son of Śāntanu, that mighty hero, fight on that tenth day against the Pāṇḍavas together with the Sṛñjayas?

**6.105.4**  I cannot bear that Bhīṣma in battle was confronted by Śikhaṇḍin coming against him. Surely there was no breaking of his chariot, nor did his bow shatter as he shot?

**6.105.5**  Saṃjaya said: His bow did not shatter, nor was there any breaking of his chariot, O bull of the Bharatas, while Bhīṣma fought in the engagement, slaying his foes in battle with arrows whose joints were well bent.

**6.105.6**  Many hundreds of thousands of your great chariot-warriors, O king, with their throngs of chariots and elephants and their well-caparisoned horses, advanced to battle, placing the grandsire at their head.

**6.105.7**  True to his vow, O Kaurava, Bhīṣma the conqueror in assemblies wrought a ceaseless slaughter among the hosts of the Pārthas.

**6.105.8**  As that great archer fought, striking down his enemies with arrows, the Pāñcālas together with the Pāṇḍavas, one and all, sought to ward him off.

**6.105.9**  When the tenth day had come, he scorched the army of his foes, strewing it with whetted shafts by hundreds and by thousands.

**6.105.10**  For the Pāṇḍavas, O ancestor of Pāṇḍu's line, were unable to vanquish in battle the great archer Bhīṣma, who was like Death with noose in hand.

**6.105.11**  Then, O great king, Savyasācin the scorcher of foes came forward, the unconquered Bībhatsu, terrifying all the chariot-warriors.

**6.105.12**  Roaring aloud like a lion, twanging his bowstring again and again, loosing floods of arrows, the Pārtha ranged through the battle like Time itself.

**6.105.13**  Terrified by his roar, O bull of the Bharatas, your men fled in great fear, O king, like deer before a lion.

**6.105.14**  Seeing the Pāṇḍava triumphant and your army hard pressed, Duryodhana, himself sorely afflicted, then spoke to Bhīṣma:

**6.105.15**  "This son of Pāṇḍu, father, with his white horses and Kṛṣṇa for his charioteer, consumes all my men as fire, whose path is black, consumes a forest.

**6.105.16**  Behold, O son of Gaṅgā, the armies fleeing in every direction, driven off in the conflict, O best of warriors, by the Pāṇḍava.

**6.105.17**  As a herdsman drives his herds of cattle through the forest, so is this army of mine driven, O scorcher of foes.

**6.105.18**  Broken by Dhanaṃjaya's arrows, it flees this way and that; and this irresistible Bhīma scatters my forces.

**6.105.19**  Sātyaki and Cekitāna and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī, and valiant Abhimanyu—they burn up my host.

**6.105.20**  And the hero Dhṛṣṭadyumna, and the rākṣasa Ghaṭotkaca, those two of mighty strength, have suddenly put my army to flight.

**6.105.21**  For this army, slaughtered by all these mighty ones, I see no other refuge to hold its ground and fight, O Bhārata,

**6.105.22**  save you, O tiger among men, whose prowess equals the gods'. You are equal to the task—be swiftly the refuge of the afflicted!"

**6.105.23**  Thus addressed, O great king, your father Devavrata, son of Śāntanu, having pondered a moment and made his resolve within himself, spoke to your son to steady him:
