**6.114.55**  "With a touch like the thunderbolt and the lightning, sharp-pointed, driven deep, loosed without break—these are not the arrows of Śikhaṇḍin.

**6.114.56**  Cutting through my vital parts, piercing my stout armor, they strike me like iron clubs—these are not the arrows of Śikhaṇḍin."

**6.114.57**  "Their touch is like the rod of Brahmā, their force like the thunderbolt, and they are not to be withstood; they tear at my very life-breath. These are no arrows of Śikhaṇḍin."

**6.114.58**  "Like enraged serpents, tongues flickering, swollen with venom, they enter into my vital parts. These are no arrows of Śikhaṇḍin."

**6.114.59**  "They destroy my life-breath like the hostile messengers of Yama; their touch is like that of mace and iron club. These are no arrows of Śikhaṇḍin."

**6.114.60**  "They cut into my limbs as the cold cuts into cattle in the month of Māgha. These are the arrows of Arjuna; they are no arrows of Śikhaṇḍin."

**6.114.61**  "All the other kings together could not bring me such pain—none but that hero, the wielder of the Gāṇḍīva, victorious Jiṣṇu of the ape-bannered car."

**6.114.62**  So speaking, the son of Śāntanu, as though he would burn the Pāṇḍava to ashes, hurled a spear that scattered sparks and blazed at its point, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.114.63**  But Arjuna, cutting it with three shafts into three pieces, brought it down before the eyes of all the Kuru heroes there, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.114.64**  Then the son of Gaṅgā took up a shield adorned with gold and a sword as well, resolved either upon death or upon victory at its threshold.

**6.114.65**  But Arjuna shattered that shield of the armored warrior into a hundred fragments while he had not yet descended from his car; and that was a thing of wonder.

**6.114.66**  Roaring aloud like a lion, Bhīṣma urged on his own troops: "Rush upon the son of Gaṅgā! Let not even the smallest fear possess you for my sake!"

**6.114.67**  Then on every side, with javelins, with lances, with floods of arrows, with battle-axes and swords and weapons of every kind,

**6.114.68**  with calf-tooth darts and broad-headed shafts, they all rushed upon him alone; and a terrible lion-roar arose then from the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.114.69**  And likewise your sons, O king, longing for Bhīṣma's victory, closed about that one man and raised their lion-roars.

**6.114.70**  There a tumultuous battle arose between your men and the foe, O lord of kings, on the tenth day, in the encounter of Bhīṣma and Arjuna.

**6.114.71**  For a moment it was like the whirlpool of the Gaṅgā, like that of the ocean, among the warring armies as they struck one another down.

**6.114.72**  The earth, drenched with blood, became impassable in appearance, and one could no longer tell level ground from broken.

**6.114.73**  Having slain ten thousand warriors on that tenth day, Bhīṣma stood firm in battle, though his vital parts were being pierced through.

**6.114.74**  Then, stationed at the head of that host, Pārtha Dhanaṃjaya drove the army into rout through the midst of the Kuru ranks.

**6.114.75**  We, terrified of him of the white horses, Dhanaṃjaya the son of Kuntī, sorely pressed by his keen weapons, fled from the great battle.

**6.114.76**  The Sauvīras, the Kitavas, the men of the East, the West, and the North, the Mālavas, the Abhīṣāhas, the Śūrasenas, the Śibis, and the Vasātis,

**6.114.77**  the dependents of the Śālvas, the Trigartas, the Ambaṣṭhas together with the Kekayas—these twelve peoples, though tormented by arrows and afflicted with wounds, did not in that battle forsake Bhīṣma as he fought with Arjuna the diademed.

**6.114.78**  Then the many, surrounding that one man on every side and driving back all the Kurus, overwhelmed him with showers of arrows.

**6.114.79**  "Strike him down! Seize him! Pierce him! Drag him out!"—such was the tumultuous outcry, O king, around the chariot of Bhīṣma.

**6.114.80**  Smitten by floods of arrows by the hundreds and the thousands, there was not upon his body so much as a finger's breadth left unpierced.

**6.114.81**  Thus, O lord, your father, his body torn to pieces by the keen-pointed shafts of Phalguna, fell from his chariot in battle, his head toward the east, while a little of the day yet remained, before the eyes of your sons.

**6.114.82**  "Alas! Alas!"—a mighty cry arose on every side, from the gods in heaven and from the kings on earth, as Bhīṣma fell from his car.

**6.114.83**  Beholding that great-souled grandsire fall, the hearts of us all fell down together with Bhīṣma.

**6.114.84**  The mighty-armed one fell, making the earth resound, like the banner of Indra let loose—he, the ensign of all the bowmen; yet, thickly studded with masses of arrows, he did not touch the ground.

**6.114.85**  As that bull among men, the great archer, lay fallen from his car upon a bed of arrows, a divine presence entered into him.

**6.114.86**  The rain-cloud poured down and the earth trembled; and as he fell he saw the sun shrunken in its course.

**6.114.87**  The hero regained his consciousness, reflecting upon the time, O descendant of Bharata, and he heard from the sky a divine voice on every side.

**6.114.88**  "How shall the great-souled son of Gaṅgā, foremost of all who bear arms, that tiger among men, meet his end now that the sun's southward course has come?"

**6.114.89**  Hearing that utterance, the son of Gaṅgā answered, "I yet abide," and held fast his life-breaths, though fallen upon the ground—Bhīṣma, grandsire of the Kurus, awaiting the sun's northward turning.

**6.114.90**  Knowing that resolve of his, Gaṅgā, daughter of Himavat, sent thither great sages in the form of swans.

**6.114.91**  Then the swift swans, dwellers of Lake Mānasa, came flying together to see Bhīṣma, grandsire of the Kurus, there where that best of men, the grandsire, lay upon his bed of arrows.

**6.114.92**  And those sages in the guise of swans, drawing near to Bhīṣma, beheld him, the grandsire of the Kurus, lying upon his bed of arrows.

**6.114.93**  Seeing that great-souled son of Gaṅgā, foremost of the Bharatas, and making their reverent circuit about him, with the sun upon their right,

**6.114.94**  those wise ones, taking counsel one with another, said there: "Bhīṣma, great-souled as he is, would yet make his end while the sun runs its southward course!"

**6.114.95**  Having so spoken, the swans set forth toward the southern quarter; and the deeply wise one, gazing after them and reflecting, O descendant of Bharata,

**6.114.96**  the son of Śāntanu said to them: "By no means shall I depart while the sun is turned to the south. This is settled in my mind."

**6.114.97**  "I shall go to my own abode that was mine of old when the sun has turned to the north. O swans, I tell you the truth."

**6.114.98**  "I shall hold fast my life-breaths in longing for the northward course, for I am master over the laying down of those breaths, fixed in my purpose. Therefore I shall hold them, ready to die when the sun moves northward."

**6.114.99**  "And the boon that was granted me by that great-souled father of mine—that death should come at my own will—let that boon of his stand fulfilled."

**6.114.100**  "I shall hold my breaths until the appointed hour for their release." Having so spoken to those swans, he lay there upon his bed of arrows.

**6.114.101**  When Bhīṣma of mighty splendor, the very summit of the Kurus, had thus fallen, the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas raised their lion-roar.

**6.114.102**  When that one of great courage, peerless among the Bharatas, had been slain, your sons knew not what to do, O bull of the Bharatas, and a tumultuous bewilderment fell then upon the Kurus.

**6.114.103**  The kings, with Duryodhana at their head, sighed and then wept, and out of grief stood for a long while as though robbed of their senses.

**6.114.104**  They brooded, O great king, and set not their minds upon the fight; and seized as if by cramp in the thighs, they did not rush upon the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.114.105**  When the son of Śaṃtanu, deemed unslayable, the splendid Bhīṣma, had been slain, a vast and unrelenting ruin came upon the Kurus, O king.

**6.114.106**  Our champions slain, ourselves cut down by keen arrows, vanquished by Savyasācin, we knew not what was to be done.

**6.114.107**  But the Pāṇḍavas, having won the victory and the supreme goal in the world beyond, those heroes with arms like iron bars all blew their great conches; and the Somakas with the Pāñcālas rejoiced greatly, O lord of men.

**6.114.108**  Then, while a thousand trumpets sounded, the mighty Bhīmasena slapped his arms loudly and danced for joy.

**6.114.109**  When the son of Gaṅgā had been brought down, the heroes of both armies laid aside their weapons and gave themselves up to reflection on every side.

**6.114.110**  Some cried aloud, others fell down, and still others swooned away; some reviled the warrior order, while others did honor to Bhīṣma.

**6.114.111**  The seers and the ancestors praised him of the great vow; and those who were the forefathers of the Bharatas, they too praised him.

**6.114.112**  And taking his stand upon the great secret doctrine and upon yoga, the valiant and wise son of Śāntanu remained murmuring his prayers, awaiting his hour.

**6.115.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How fared the warriors then, O Saṃjaya, bereft of Bhīṣma—the mighty one, godlike, who kept his celibate vow for his elder's sake?

**6.115.2**  I count the Kurus and the other kings as slain already from that moment when Bhīṣma, out of compassion, would not strike at the son of Drupada.

**6.115.3**  What grief could ever befall worse than this, when today I hear that my father has been slain—I, the evil-minded one?

**6.115.4**  Surely my heart is made of iron, O Saṃjaya, that, hearing of Bhīṣma's slaying, it does not break asunder into a hundred pieces.

**6.115.5**  Again and again I cannot endure it—that Devavrata has been slain in battle, he whom of old not even the son of Jamadagni could slay with his celestial weapons.

**6.115.6**  Tell me, O Saṃjaya, what that lion among men, Bhīṣma, did in his desire for victory before he was slain in battle this day.

**6.115.7**  Saṃjaya said: At the close of day the aged grandsire of the Kurus fell to the earth, plunging the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra into despair and giving joy to the Pāñcālas.

**6.115.8**  He lay upon his bed of arrows, not touching the earth, Bhīṣma, fallen from his car, cast down upon the ground.

**6.115.9**  "Alas! Alas!"—a tumultuous cry went up from all beings when the boundary-tree of the Kurus fell at the breaking of their host.

**6.115.10**  Fear came upon the warriors of both armies, O king; seeing Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, with shattered armor and standard, the Kurus turned about, and the Pāṇḍavas as well, O lord of peoples.

**6.115.11**  The sky was wrapped in darkness, and the radiance of the sun was no more, and the earth itself groaned aloud when Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, was slain.

**6.115.12**  "This is the foremost of the knowers of Brahman; this is the refuge of the knowers of Brahman"—so the beings spoke of that bull of the Bharatas as he lay there.

**6.115.13**  "This bull among men, knowing of old that his father Śaṃtanu was tormented by desire, made of himself one whose seed was drawn upward in chastity."

**6.115.14**  Thus the seers, together with the Siddhas and Cāraṇas, hastened about him, the peerless one of the Bharatas, as he lay upon his bed of arrows.

**6.115.15**  When Bhīṣma, son of Śāntanu, grandsire of the Bharatas, was slain, your sons knew not what to do, O descendant of Bharata.

**6.115.16**  Their faces grew pale and their splendor departed, O descendant of Bharata; they stood abashed, overcome with shame, their faces bent downward.

**6.115.17**  But the Pāṇḍavas, having won the victory and standing at the forefront of the battle, all blew their great conches adorned with nets of gold.

**6.115.18**  And while the trumpets were loudly sounding, O sinless one, we saw in the battle, O king, the mighty Bhīmasena, son of Kuntī, sporting and filled with exceeding joy.

**6.115.19**  Having slain in battle his foes endowed with great strength—and a tumultuous bewilderment then fell upon the Kurus.

**6.115.20**  Karṇa and Duryodhana too sighed again and again; and when Bhīṣma, the chief upholder of the Kauravas, had thus fallen, all became a cry of woe, and everything passed beyond restraint.

**6.115.21**  And seeing Bhīṣma fallen, your son Duḥśāsana, taking on the utmost speed, rushed toward the division of Droṇa.

**6.115.22**  Sent forth by his brother, that hero, mailed and amid his own host, that tiger among men advanced, urging on his troops.

**6.115.23**  Beholding him approach, the Kurus gathered round Duḥśāsana, O great king, wondering, "What will he have to say?"

**6.115.24**  Then the Kaurava announced to Droṇa that Bhīṣma had been slain; and Droṇa, hearing those unwelcome tidings, fell at once from his chariot.

**6.115.25**  Then, regaining his senses, the valiant son of Bharadvāja held back his own divisions at that moment, O honored one.

**6.115.26**  Seeing the Kurus withdraw, the Pāṇḍavas too, through messengers mounted on swift horses, caused their own troops to retire from the field.

**6.115.27**  When the armies had everywhere withdrawn in succession, all the kings, their armor laid aside, went to Bhīṣma.

**6.115.28**  Then, ceasing from battle, the warriors in their hundreds of thousands stood attending upon that great-souled one, as the immortals attend Prajāpati.

**6.115.29**  Approaching Bhīṣma, that bull of the Bharatas, as he lay there, the Pāṇḍavas together with the Kurus paid him homage and took their places around him.

**6.115.30**  Then to the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus, who stood bowing before him, the righteous Bhīṣma, son of Śantanu, spoke.

**6.115.31**  "Welcome to you, illustrious ones; welcome to you, great chariot-warriors. I rejoice at the sight of you, who are like the immortals."

**6.115.32**  Having greeted them thus, with his head hanging down he said: "My head hangs too low; let a pillow be given to me."

**6.115.33**  Then the kings brought choice pillows, thin and soft; but the grandsire would not have them.

**6.115.34**  And that tiger among men said to those kings, as if smiling: "These, O princes, are not fitting for a hero's bed."

**6.115.35**  Then, looking upon that best of men, the Pāṇḍava Dhanaṃjaya, the long-armed great chariot-warrior of all the worlds, he addressed him.

**6.115.36**  "O Dhanaṃjaya, mighty-armed, this head of mine hangs down; let such a pillow be given as you here deem fitting."

**6.115.37**  Setting aside his great bow and saluting the grandsire, with his two eyes brimming with tears he spoke these words.

**6.115.38**  "Command me, O best of the Kurus, foremost of all wielders of weapons. I am your servant, O unassailable one; what shall be done, grandsire?"

**6.115.39**  The son of Śantanu said to him: "My head hangs down, my son. O best of the Kurus, Phalguna, fetch me a pillow; quickly, O hero, give me one befitting this bed of mine."

**6.115.40**  "For you, O Pārtha, mighty-armed, are the foremost of all bowmen, knower of the kṣatriya's law, endowed with the virtues of wisdom and resolve."

**6.115.41**  Phalguna, saying "So be it," swift and forward in resolve, took up the Gāṇḍīva and, with sacred utterance, his keen-jointed arrows.

**6.115.42**  Having sought the leave of that great-souled, peerless one among the Bharatas, with three sharp, swift-flying arrows he upheld his head.

**6.115.43**  When his intent was understood by Savyasācin, the righteous one, Bhīṣma, that best of the Bharatas, knower of the truths of dharma and artha, was well pleased.

**6.115.44**  With the pillow thus given, he praised Dhanaṃjaya, the son of Kuntī, the best of fighters, the increaser of his friends' delight.

**6.115.45**  "O Pāṇḍava, you have provided what befits one who lies thus. Had you acted otherwise, I would have cursed you in my wrath."

**6.115.46**  "This is so, O mighty-armed one, established firmly in the laws: thus must a kṣatriya sleep in battle, lying upon a bed of arrows."

**6.115.47**  Having spoken thus to Bībhatsu, he addressed these words to all those kings and princes who stood gathered with the Pāṇḍava.

**6.115.48**  "I shall lie upon this bed until the sun turns back in its course. Those kings who shall then survive will indeed behold me."

**6.115.49**  "When the sun, the day-maker, blazing and scorching the worlds with his chariot of supreme splendor, shall pass to the quarter ruled by Vaiśravaṇa, then shall I release my life-breaths, dear though my friends be to me."

**6.115.50**  "Let a trench be dug here, O kings, around my dwelling-place; thus pierced by a hundred arrows, I shall worship Vivasvat. Desist from battle, O princes, and cast off your enmities."

**6.115.51**  Then physicians came near, skilled in the drawing-out of arrows, furnished with all their instruments, expert and well-trained.

**6.115.52**  Seeing them, the son of Jāhnavī then spoke these words: "Let the physicians be honored, given their due, and dismissed."

**6.115.53**  "In this pass there is now no need of physicians for me, for I have attained the supreme goal, lauded by the kṣatriya's law."

**6.115.54**  "This is not the way, O guardians of the earth, for me who lie upon a bed of arrows; with these very arrows, O kings, I must be burned at the end."

**6.115.55**  Hearing those words of his, your son Duryodhana dismissed the physicians, having honored them as they deserved.

**6.115.56**  Then the lords of diverse realms were filled with wonder, beholding the supreme steadfastness in dharma of Bhīṣma of boundless splendor.

**6.115.57**  Then, having given a pillow to your father, O lord of men, all the Pāṇḍavas together and the Kuru great chariot-warriors—

**6.115.58**  —approaching that great-souled one as he lay upon his auspicious bed, saluted Bhīṣma and circled him with reverence.

**6.115.59**  Having set guards about Bhīṣma on every side, all those heroes, deep in thought and sorely grieved, went toward their own camps at evening to take their rest, drenched in blood.

**6.115.60**  And to the Pāṇḍava great chariot-warriors, encamped and rejoicing, gladdened by the fall of Bhīṣma, the Yādava drew near at the fit time and spoke to Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Dharma.

**6.115.61**  "By good fortune you triumph, O Kaurava; by good fortune Bhīṣma is laid low—he, the great chariot-warrior, true to his word, unslayable by mortals."

**6.115.62**  "Or rather, O Pārtha, he who was master of all weapons and missiles, even by the gods could not be slain—but, encountering you whose very glance destroys, he was consumed by your terrible eye."

**6.115.63**  Thus addressed, the King of Righteousness replied to Janārdana: "Through your grace comes victory, through your wrath comes defeat. For you, O Kṛṣṇa, are our refuge, the giver of fearlessness to your devotees."

**6.115.64**  "No wonder is victory for those whose protector in battle you ever are, O Keśava, ever devoted to their good. Having gained you in every way, that there should be no wonder—such is my conviction."

**6.115.65**  Thus addressed, Janārdana replied, smiling: "This speech befits none but you, O best of kings."

**6.116.1**  Saṃjaya said: When the night had passed, O great king, all the princes—the Pāṇḍavas and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra—went forth to the grandsire.

**6.116.2**  That hero, best of the Kurus, as he lay upon his hero's bed, the kṣatriyas saluted and attended upon, that bull among kṣatriyas.

**6.116.3**  Maidens with sandal-powder and parched grain and garlands on every side, women, children, and the aged, and onlookers of the common folk, drew near the son of Śantanu as living beings draw near the dispeller of darkness.

**6.116.4**  Musicians, courtesans, and likewise actors and dancers danced and sang before the aged grandsire of the Kurus.

**6.116.5**  Ceasing from their battles, loosing off their armor, and laying down their weapons, the Kurus and Pāṇḍavas together—

**6.116.6**  —sat attending upon the unassailable Devavrata, that subduer of foes, affectionate toward one another as before, each according to his age.

**6.116.7**  That assembly of the Bharatas, thronged with a hundred princes and graced by Bhīṣma, shone resplendent like the orb of the sun blazing in the sky.

**6.116.8**  And that gathering of kings waiting upon the grandsire shone forth like the gods waiting upon the lord of gods, the Grandsire of all.

**6.116.9**  But Bhīṣma, O bull of the Bharatas, mastering his pain through fortitude, sore-tormented by the arrows, spoke with a mind not over-glad.

**6.116.10**  "My body is scorched by the arrows, and I swoon with the burning of their wounds; I long for water"—thus he spoke to those kings.

**6.116.11**  Then those kṣatriyas, O king, brought from all around foods of every kind and jars of cool water.

**6.116.12**  Seeing what was brought, Bhīṣma, son of Śantanu, said: "It is not possible for me now, my son, to take any human enjoyments."

**6.116.13**  "For I, removed from men and come to a bed of arrows, remain awaiting the turning-back of the moon and the sun."

**6.116.14**  Having spoken thus to all the princes in a feeble voice, the son of Śantanu addressed the mighty-armed Dhanaṃjaya, O Bhārata.

**6.116.15**  Then the mighty-armed one, approaching and saluting the grandsire, stood with joined palms, bowing, and said: "What shall I do?"

**6.116.16**  Seeing that Pāṇḍava, O king, standing before him with salutation, the righteous Bhīṣma, well pleased, addressed Dhanaṃjaya.

**6.116.17**  "This body of mine burns; I am pierced through and through by great arrows; my vital points are aflame, and my mouth runs dry."

**6.116.18**  "To cool my body, give me water, O Arjuna; for you, great archer, are able to bring forth water in the proper way."

**6.116.19**  Arjuna, saying "So be it," the valiant one, mounted his chariot and, drawing the Gāṇḍīva mightily to the string, twanged the bow.

**6.116.20**  At the sound of his bowstring and palm-guard, like the crashing of a thunderbolt, all beings, hearing it, trembled, and so did all the kings.

**6.116.21**  Then that best of charioteers, having circled in his chariot that best of the Bharatas, foremost of all wielders of weapons, as he lay there—

**6.116.22**  —and fixing a blazing arrow, consecrating it with sacred utterance, the illustrious Pārtha joined it to the Parjanya weapon and, before the eyes of all the world, pierced the earth at Bhīṣma's right side.

**6.116.23**  Thereupon there sprang up a pure and blessed jet of water, cool and like ambrosia, of divine fragrance and savor.

**6.116.24**  Then Pārtha, of divine deeds and prowess, refreshed with that cool jet of water Bhīṣma, the bull of the Kurus.

**6.116.25**  At that deed of Pārtha, who wrought wonders like Śakra himself, the lords of the earth then fell into the utmost amazement.

**6.116.26**  Beholding that superhuman and marvelous deed of Bībhatsu, the Kurus shook all over, like cattle tormented by cold.

**6.116.27**  In their wonder the kings waved their upper garments on every side, and with the blare of conches and kettledrums a tumult arose all around.

**6.116.28**  And the son of Śantanu, his thirst quenched, O king, spoke to Bībhatsu in the presence of all the princely heroes, as if honoring him.

**6.116.29**  "This is no marvel in you, O mighty-armed delight of the Kurus; by Nārada you were declared to be an ancient seer of boundless luster."

**6.116.30**  "With Vāsudeva as your ally, you shall accomplish a mighty deed that even Indra, lord of the gods, together with the gods, surely could not dare."

**6.116.31**  "Those who know such things know you, O Pārtha, to be the destroyer of all the kṣatriya race; alone among bowmen, you are the foremost of men upon the earth."

**6.116.32**  "Among the creatures of the world men are the best; of birds Garuḍa is supreme; of waters the ocean is best; of four-footed beasts the cow is highest."

**6.116.33**  "The sun is best of the radiant ones, Himavat the highest of mountains, the brāhmaṇa the best of castes—and you are the best of bowmen."

**6.116.34**  "The counsel given by Vidura was not heeded by the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, nor that of Droṇa and Rāma and Janārdana, urged again and again, and spoken too by Saṃjaya."

**6.116.35**  "For Duryodhana, his mind deranged and well-nigh senseless, took no joy in my words; transgressor of the scriptures, struck down by Bhīma's might, he shall lie slain for ages to come."

**6.116.36**  Then, hearing those words, the lord of the Kurus, Duryodhana, became dejected in heart; and the son of Śantanu, looking upon him, said: "Hear me, O king, and let your anger pass."

**6.116.37**  "You have seen, Duryodhana, how by the wise Pārtha this jet of cool water, fragrant as ambrosia, was brought forth; of such a deed there is none other in this world who can be the doer."

**6.116.38**  "The weapons of Agni, of Varuṇa, of Soma, of Vāyu, and then of Viṣṇu, of Indra, of Paśupati, of Brahmā, of Parameṣṭhin and Prajāpati, of Dhātṛ, of Tvaṣṭṛ, and of Savitṛ—all the divine missiles—"

**6.116.39**  "In all the world of men, Dhanaṃjaya alone knows them, or Kṛṣṇa the son of Devakī—no other knows them at all. The Pāṇḍavas, my son, cannot in any wise be conquered in battle."

**6.116.40**  Superhuman are these deeds of that great-souled one; with him, the resolute, the valiant, who shines in combat and is accomplished in war, O king — let a peace be made with him, my son.

**6.116.41**  While mighty-armed Kṛṣṇa is still amenable in the assembly of the Kurus, even now, my son, let a peace be made with the valiant Pārtha.

**6.116.42**  While Arjuna has not yet destroyed what remains of your army with his straight-jointed arrows, even now, my son, let a peace be made with him.

**6.116.43**  While your own brothers, the survivors of the slaughter, and many kings still stand in the battle, O king, even now let a peace be concluded.

**6.116.44**  While Yudhiṣṭhira, his eyes blazing with wrath, has not yet burned up your army, even now, my son, let a peace be made with him.

**6.116.45**  While Nakula and Sahadeva, and Bhīmasena the son of Pāṇḍu, have not yet destroyed your army utterly, O great king, even now, my son, let brotherhood with the Pāṇḍavas find favor with you.

**6.116.46**  Let this war end with me, my son; be reconciled with the Pāṇḍavas. May the words I have spoken to you find favor with you, O blameless one. This I judge to be for the welfare of yourself and of your lineage.

**6.116.47**  Cast off your wrath and make peace with the Pārthas; enough is the slaughter that Phalguna has wrought. With the passing of Bhīṣma let there be friendship between you; reunion is good, O king — be gracious.

**6.116.48**  Let half the kingdom be given to the Pāṇḍavas; let the King of Righteousness rule from Indraprastha. Do not become a betrayer of friends, the basest of kings, lest you win an evil fame, O lord of the Kauravas.

**6.116.49**  With my end let there be peace for the peoples; let the kings come together in gladness. Let father go to son, and nephew to uncle, and brother to brother, O king.

**6.116.50**  But if, sunk in delusion and folly, you do not heed these timely words of mine, then with the end of Bhīṣma all of you shall meet your end — this true word do I declare, O Bhārata.

**6.116.51**  Having out of affection made the Bhārata hear these words in the midst of the kings, the son of the river fell silent, his vitals seared by the darts, mastering himself and holding back his pain.

**6.117.1**  Saṃjaya said: Then, when Bhīṣma the delight of Śantanu had fallen silent, O great king, all those kings went back again to their own dwellings.

**6.117.2**  But when Rādheya, the bull among men, heard that Bhīṣma had been struck down, seized with a touch of fear he came swiftly to him.

**6.117.3**  He beheld the great-souled one lying then upon his bed of arrows, like the god Kārttikeya, the lord, lying upon the couch of his birth.

**6.117.4**  That hero lay with his eyes closed; and the Bull, his throat choked with tears, of great splendor, drew near and fell down at his feet.

**6.117.5**  "I am Rādheya, O best of the Kurus, ever before your eyes, hated by you utterly though I had done no wrong" — thus he spoke to him.

**6.117.6**  Hearing this, the aged Kuru, forcing open his closed eyes, gazed up slowly and with affection spoke these words.

**6.117.7**  Seeing the place was secluded, and having sent away the guards, the son of Gaṅgā embraced him with one arm, as a father embraces a son.

**6.117.8**  "Come, come to me, you who set yourself against me, contending with me. Had you not come to me, surely it would not have been well for you.

**6.117.9**  You are the son of Kuntī, not the son of Rādhā — this I know from Nārada, and from Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana, and from Keśava too; of this there is no doubt.

**6.117.10**  I bear you no hatred, my son — I speak the truth to you; it was only to curb your fierce energy that I uttered harsh words.

**6.117.11**  You hate the Pāṇḍavas without cause — such was my thought; and for that reason I goaded you again and again with rough speech, O son of the Sun.

**6.117.12**  I know your prowess in battle, hard for foes to bear, and your reverence for the brahmins, your heroism, and your supreme excellence in giving.

**6.117.13**  There is none among men your equal, O peer of the immortals; yet, fearing a division of the family, I always spoke to you harshly.

**6.117.14**  In archery and missile-craft, in bearing strain, in swiftness, and in the might of your weapons, you are the equal of Phalguna and of the great-souled Kṛṣṇa.

**6.117.15**  O Karṇa, going to the royal city, you alone, bearing your bow, crushed kings in battle for the sake of that king of the Kurus.

**6.117.16**  And likewise the mighty king Jarāsaṃdha, hard to assail and proud of his warring, proved no match for you in combat.

**6.117.17**  Devoted to the brahmins and truthful in speech, in splendor like a second sun, sprung from a god, unconquered in battle — on earth you surpass all men.

**6.117.18**  Gone now is the wrath I once nursed against you. Fate cannot be overcome by human effort.

**6.117.19**  The heroic Pāṇḍavas are your own brothers, born of the same womb, O slayer of foes. Join with them, mighty-armed one, if you would do what pleases me.

**6.117.20**  Let this enmity be ended with me, O son of the Sun; let all the kings on earth this day be free of affliction."

**6.117.21**  Karṇa said: I know all this, O greatly wise one; there is no doubt of it. As you say, O unassailable one, I am the son of Kuntī and not born of a charioteer.

**6.117.22**  Yet I was cast off by Kuntī and raised by a charioteer; and having enjoyed Duryodhana's bounty, I cannot now prove false to it.

**6.117.23**  My wealth, my body, and my noble fame — all of it I have surrendered for Duryodhana's sake, O giver of abundant gifts; and, taking my stand with Suyodhana, I have ever provoked the Pāṇḍavas.

**6.117.24**  That which is destined to be cannot be turned aside; who could venture to reverse fate by human effort?

**6.117.25**  The omens foretelling the destruction of the earth, O grandsire, have been perceived by you and declared in the assembly.

**6.117.26**  The Pāṇḍavas and Vāsudeva are known to me in every way as unconquerable by other men; yet even against them we will dare to strive.

**6.117.27**  Give me your leave, my father, in this war, ever with a glad heart; with your leave, O hero, it is my resolve to fight.

**6.117.28**  Whatever harsh or perverse word I spoke amiss, out of anger or recklessness, whatever offense I have done — you ought to forgive it.

**6.117.29**  Bhīṣma said: If this most dreadful enmity cannot be cast off, then I give you leave, O Karṇa — fight, in your longing for heaven.

**6.117.30**  Free of wrath, free of agitation, do the king's work, according to your strength and your zeal, abiding by the conduct of the good.

**6.117.31**  I give you my leave; attain what you desire. You shall surely win the worlds conquered by the duty of the warrior — of this there is no doubt.

**6.117.32**  Fight without pride, relying on your strength and prowess; for there is no higher duty known for a warrior than righteous battle.

**6.117.33**  Long and again long have I striven for peace, yet I have not been able to make it; for where there is righteousness, there is victory.

**6.117.34**  Saṃjaya said: When the son of Gaṅgā had thus spoken, Rādheya saluted him and won his grace, then mounted his chariot and set out toward your son.
